# PHASE 9: Testing Strategies

> **Phase orientation.** Testing is not a phase that happens at the end of a feature. It is a *feedback instrument*, and like any instrument it is only useful if it is fast, deterministic, and honest about the system it measures. This phase is organised as a ladder of scope (unit -> integration -> end-to-end), then a set of orthogonal quality instruments that do not fit on the ladder (performance, accessibility, security, static analysis, mutation, review), then an economics section that tells you which rungs are worth building.
>
> **The one-sentence thesis.** Push as much of your verification as possible *down* the pyramid onto fast deterministic tests, and use the slow brittle ones only for the things that genuinely cannot be verified any other way.

**Difficulty legend used throughout this phase**

- 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**
- 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**
- 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**
- ⚫ Expert · **Nice-to-have depth**

**Notation.** Code blocks tagged `ts`, `tsx`, `js`, `json`, `jsonc`, `bash`, `yaml`, `sql`, `http`, `graphql` are intended to be runnable as-is. Where a runnable answer needs a dependency, the package name is given in the prose immediately before the block.

---

## 9.1 Unit Testing

### 9.1.1 What a unit test is, and the definition of "unit" that starts arguments

**Definition.**

A **unit test** is an automated test that exercises a single cohesive piece of logic through its public surface, with all of that logic's collaborators replaced by controllable substitutes, verifying observable behaviour -- state transitions, return values, emitted events, calls made to collaborators -- while containing no I/O the test does not own.

The word doing the work here is **unit**. It is famously ambiguous, and the ambiguity is where most "unit vs integration" arguments actually come from. Four competing definitions, in increasing order of usefulness:

1. **"A unit is a function."** -- the smallest testable thing. Too small: it produces tests that assert implementation facts and break on every refactor.
2. **"A unit is a class."** -- inherited from JUnit/Java. Better: it introduces a boundary that matches the design vocabulary of the codebase.
3. **"A unit is a module/file."** -- the practical JavaScript/TypeScript definition: one test file per source file, everything imported from outside is a substitute. This is what most teams mean and what Vitest/Jest give you structurally.
4. **"A unit is a behaviour, not a code unit."** 🟡 -- the *behavioural* definition. A unit is one observable rule the system promises (e.g. "an expired token cannot be redeemed"), regardless of how many functions cooperate to implement it. Tests are named after behaviours, and a test file may or may not map to a source file.

**Mechanics -- the unit test contract.**

A test is a *unit* test when all five hold:

| Property | Meaning | How you enforce it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Isolated** | No shared mutable global state, no network, no real clock, no real filesystem (unless that *is* the unit) | fresh module registry per test file, fake timers, injected ports |
| **Deterministic** | Same result on 1000 consecutive runs, on any machine, at any wall-clock time | no `Math.random()` in code under test; inject a `Random` port; fixed seed |
| **Fast** | Single-digit milliseconds; the whole unit tier runs in seconds, not minutes | ban I/O in this tier |
| **Self-verifying** | No human eyeball step; exit code is the result | assertions, not `console.log` |
| **Localised failure** | A red test names the broken rule in one place, not a subsystem | one behaviour per test, no giant "happy path" test |

**The three questions that dissolve 90% of unit/integration arguments:**

1. *Can I run this with zero network, zero disk and zero database?* If yes, it is a unit test. If no, it is at least an integration test.
2. *Would this test still pass if I rewrote the implementation with different internals but identical observable behaviour?* If yes, it is testing behaviour (good). If no, it is testing implementation (a bug waiting for a refactor).
3. *Who owns the thing I am substituting?* If I stub something I own, I can change it freely. If I stub something a third party owns, you are writing a test that breaks on their patch release -- a **vendor-coupled test**, and it belongs in the integration tier or nowhere.

**Runnable code -- the canonical shape of a good unit test.**

```ts
// src/domain/applyDiscount.spec.ts
import { describe, it, expect } from 'vitest'
import { applyDiscount, type PricingRules } from './applyDiscount'

const rules: PricingRules = {
  currency: 'USD',
  tiers: [
    { minSubtotalCents: 20000, percentOff: 10 },
    { minSubtotalCents: 50000, percentOff: 20 },
  ],
  maxDiscountPercent: 25,
  coupons: { WELCOME5: 5 },
}

describe('applyDiscount', () => {
  it('applies no discount below the first tier', () => {
    expect(applyDiscount({ subtotalCents: 19999, rules })).toEqual({
      subtotalCents: 19999,
      discountCents: 0,
      totalCents: 19999,
      appliedCoupon: null,
    })
  })

  it('applies the highest qualifying tier, not every tier cumulatively', () => {
    const result = applyDiscount({ subtotalCents: 60000, rules })
    expect(result.discountCents).toBe(12000) // 20% of 60000, not 30%
  })

  it('caps the discount at maxDiscountPercent', () => {
    const result = applyDiscount({ subtotalCents: 60000, rules, forcedPercent: 90 })
    expect(result.discountCents).toBe(15000) // 25% of 60000
  })

  it('rounds half-up on odd cents and never returns a negative total', () => {
    const result = applyDiscount({
      subtotalCents: 101,
      rules: { ...rules, tiers: [{ minSubtotalCents: 0, percentOff: 50 }] },
    })
    expect(result.discountCents).toBe(51)
    expect(result.totalCents).toBeGreaterThanOrEqual(0)
  })
})
```

Notice what is *not* in that file: no database, no HTTP, no `vi.mock` of the module under test, no `sleep`. That is a unit test. Everything else is an integration test wearing a unit test's clothes.

**When to use unit tests vs not.**

Use them when:

- The logic has *branches worth enumerating* (pricing, permissions, state machines, parsers, retries, encoders).
- The logic is *pure or nearly pure* -- the best case, and worth architecting for.
- You intend to *refactor aggressively* and need a safety net that survives the refactor.
- The logic is on the *hot path* where a regression is expensive.

Do not use them to:

- Assert that a getter returns the field you set 30ms ago.
- Cover framework glue -- React's own render, Express's own routing table, Prisma's own query builder. You are writing a change-detector for someone else's test suite, and it will fail on their upgrade, not on your bug.
- Measure performance. A unit test that "proves" something is fast measures the machine it ran on, not the code.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Speed vs realism.** Every substitution you introduce is a small lie. Unit tests are cheap lies; integration tests are expensive truths. You want the ratio to be heavily skewed toward cheap lies, with expensive truths covering the load-bearing seams.
- **Refactor safety vs defect detection.** Behaviour-level tests survive refactors and catch semantic bugs. Implementation-level tests survive nothing but catch accidental deletions. Behaviour-level wins; the cost is that you must think about observable behaviour before writing the test, which is exactly the TDD argument in 9.1.7.
- **Mocking cost.** Heavily-mocked unit tests are expensive to *write* and to *understand* -- a new engineer must hold the entire mock graph in their head. If the mock graph is bigger than the code, you have written a worse test suite than the E2E test you rejected.
- **Coverage theatre.** High unit coverage on a codebase where every test mocks the interesting part measures nothing (see 9.1.8).

**Production considerations.**

- Keep the unit tier under ~20 seconds. Beyond that, developers stop running it, and a suite that is not run is not a test.
- Make the unit tier the *first* CI job, and make it a hard gate. Everything downstream may be flaky; this one may not.
- Ban `Date.now()` reads inside production code paths that matter; inject a `Clock` port. Same for `Math.random()`, `crypto.randomUUID()`, and `process.hrtime`. This is the single highest-leverage testability refactor in most codebases.
- Module-level singletons (a Prisma client, a config object, a `dayjs` locale) are the number-one source of cross-test pollution. Design the module so it can be constructed per test.

**Common mistakes.**

1. **Mocking the system under test.** If you `vi.mock('./applyDiscount')` and then assert on it, you have written a test of your mock. This is the "assertion roulette" failure: the test passes precisely because the code is broken.
2. **Over-mocking transitive dependencies.** You are testing `OrderService`, but you also mock the repository, the logger, the clock, the uuid generator *and* the validator. Now a bug in the validator can never be caught by any test in the suite.
3. **One test, many assertions, many behaviours.** The moment a test named "checkout works" exists, "checkout" stops being a spec. Name it after the rule.
4. **Sharing state between tests** through module scope. `let cart = createCart()` at file scope means test 1 leaks into test 7. `beforeEach` is not optional.
5. **Asserting on log output** as a proxy for behaviour. Logs are not a contract.
6. **Snapshotting an entire object graph** in a unit test, which is a snapshot test wearing a disguise (9.1.9).
7. **Test-only code paths in production code**, e.g. `if (process.env.NODE_ENV === 'test') return fixture`. This path is never exercised in production and will rot; it also becomes a backdoor.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is a unit test in your own words, and how is it different from a test of a function?"* -- Expect: a unit test substitutes all collaborators, controls time and randomness, and asserts observable behaviour; a function test may hit the network, the disk and the clock.
- 🟡 *"Your unit test mocks the HTTP client. Is it still a unit test?"* -- Yes, and here is the cost: you are now responsible for proving the real client behaves as your mock claims. That proof lives in the integration tier. Mocks are hypotheses; integration tests are where hypotheses get verified.
- 🔴 *"How do you decide where the seams go?"* -- Along the lines of *reasoning and effect*, not along the lines of files. Inject a port when the thing is an effect (I/O, clock, randomness, network) and when swapping it buys you determinism or a distinct test of its own. Do not inject for its own sake; an interface with one implementation and one test is ceremony.
- 🔴 *"A developer argues a controller test that mocks the service is a unit test. Agree?"* -- It is a unit test of the controller, and it is nearly worthless, because the controller's entire job is translating HTTP into a service call. Its risk lives in that translation, which the mock makes trivially true. Write it as an integration test with a real service and a fake repository instead.
- ⚫ *"Do you still write unit tests for pure functions?"* -- Yes, but I write them as **table-driven property and example tests** (9.1.6) rather than one-test-per-branch, because the branch count grows with the spec while the properties stay constant.

---

### 9.1.2 Jest: describe/it/expect, matchers, hooks, and config

**Definition.**

**Jest** (Meta/Facebook, first released 2016, still maintained) is the de-facto standard JavaScript test runner. It ships with `expect`, `jest.fn()` spies, a module mock system, fake timers, snapshot serialisation, coverage instrumentation via `babel-plugin-istanbul`, and a built-in test *environment* abstraction (jsdom, node, or a custom one). It is opinionated: you accept its transform pipeline, its module registry, and its own globals.

**Mechanics -- the anatomy.**

```ts
// The five moving parts.
describe('Calculator', () => {          // 1. Grouping/suite. Nestable. Name is a noun phrase.
  beforeAll(() => { /* once per file, before any test */ })
  beforeEach(() => { /* before every test in this (and nested) suite */ })
  afterEach(() => { /* after every test */ })
  afterAll(() => { /* once per file, after every test */ })

  it('adds', () => {                   // 2. Test. `test` is an alias; `it.only/.skip` gate.
    const result = add(2, 3)
    expect(result).toBe(5)              // 3. Matcher. `expect` takes an actual, returns matchers.
  })
})
```

Key mechanics that trip people up:

- **`expect` is lazy and assertion-free until a matcher runs.** `expect(1)` does nothing. A test with no matcher passes vacuously and is worse than no test -- this is the "vacuous test" anti-pattern.
- **Matcher catalogue that you actually use:** `toBe` (Object.is -- beware `NaN` and `-0`), `toEqual` (deep structural, ignores `undefined` properties), `toStrictEqual` (also checks class/`undefined` sparseness), `toMatchObject` (subset), `toContain`/`toContainEqual` (arrays, strings, sets), `toHaveLength`, `toHaveProperty` (dot **or** array path for deep access), `toBeCloseTo` (float), `toThrow`/`toThrowError`, `toHaveBeenCalled`/`toHaveBeenCalledWith`/`toHaveBeenCalledTimes`/`toHaveBeenNthCalledWith`/`toHaveReturnedWith`, `toMatchSnapshot`/`toMatchInlineSnapshot`, `toSatisfy`, `.not`, `.resolves`/`.rejects`.
- **Hooks are scoped, not global.** `beforeEach` in an outer `describe` runs before every test in *every* nested `describe`. Order is: outer `beforeAll` -> inner `beforeAll` -> outer `beforeEach` -> inner `beforeEach` -> test -> inner `afterEach` -> outer `afterEach` -> inner `afterAll` -> outer `afterAll`.
- **Fake timers** replace `Date`, `setTimeout`, `setInterval`, `setImmediate`, `process.nextTick`, `queueMicrotask`, and (in modern Jest) `performance.now`. `jest.useFakeTimers()` then `jest.advanceTimersByTime(ms)` / `jest.runAllTimers()` / `jest.runOnlyPendingTimers()` / `jest.setSystemTime(date)`. `await jest.advanceTimersByTimeAsync(ms)` yields to the microtask queue so promises can settle -- using the *sync* variant with async code is one of the most common hangs in the ecosystem.
- **Module mocking** has three layers: `jest.mock(path, factory)` (hoisted, replaces the whole module), `jest.spyOn(obj, 'method')` (keeps original unless `.mockImplementation()`), and `jest.requireActual` (escape hatch to reach the real thing inside a mock factory). `__mocks__/adjacent-file.ts` provides automatic mocks for node_modules.
- **`test.concurrent`** runs tests in a file concurrently; any shared module state immediately becomes a race. Treat as a sharp tool.
- **Environments**: `jsdom`, `node`, `jest-environment-jsdom` (must be installed separately in Jest 28+), `testEnvironmentOptions: { url: 'https://app.test' }`, `customExportConditions` for package resolution.

**Runnable code -- a production-shaped Jest config.**

```ts
// jest.config.ts
import type { Config } from 'jest'

const config: Config = {
  // --- projects: run node and jsdom tests in parallel workers, correctly scoped.
  projects: [
    {
      displayName: 'node',
      testEnvironment: 'node',
      testMatch: ['<rootDir>/src/**/__tests__/**/*.test.ts'],
      transform: { '^.+\\.tsx?$': ['@swc/jest'] },
    },
    {
      displayName: 'dom',
      testEnvironment: 'jsdom',
      testMatch: ['<rootDir>/src/**/*.test.tsx'],
      transform: { '^.+\\.tsx?$': ['@swc/jest'] },
      setupFilesAfterEnv: ['<rootDir>/test/setup-dom.ts'],
    },
  ],

  // --- sensible defaults
  clearMocks: true,            // resets mock.calls AND implementations before every test
  resetMocks: false,           // true would also wipe factory implementations; rarely what you want
  restoreMocks: false,         // true restores original descriptors; useful, slightly slower
  resetModules: false,

  // --- coverage (istanbul)
  collectCoverageFrom: [
    'src/**/*.{ts,tsx}',
    '!src/**/*.d.ts',
    '!src/**/index.ts',
    '!src/**/*.{stories,spec,test}.{ts,tsx}',
  ],
  coverageProvider: 'v8',      // faster, line-coverage-accurate; babel provider gives branch-accurate
  coverageThreshold: {
    global: { branches: 75, functions: 80, lines: 85, statements: 85 },
    './src/domain/**': { branches: 90, functions: 90, lines: 95, statements: 95 },
  },
  coverageReporters: ['text-summary', 'lcov', 'json-summary'],

  // --- speed
  maxWorkers: '50%',
  cache: true,
  slowTestThreshold: 10,
  bail: false,
}
export default config
```

```ts
// src/domain/discount.test.ts -- idiomatic Jest
import { applyDiscount, type PricingRules } from './applyDiscount'

const rules: PricingRules = {
  currency: 'USD',
  tiers: [{ minSubtotalCents: 20000, percentOff: 10 }],
  maxDiscountPercent: 25,
  coupons: {},
}

describe('applyDiscount', () => {
  afterEach(() => {
    jest.useRealTimers()   // ALWAYS restore; leaked fake timers poison the next file
  })

  it('treats a tier as active from its start instant inclusive', () => {
    jest.spyOn(Date, 'now').mockReturnValue(new Date('2026-03-01T00:00:00Z').getTime())
    const result = applyDiscount({ subtotalCents: 20000, rules, now: Date.now() })
    expect(result).toStrictEqual({
      subtotalCents: 20000, discountCents: 2000, totalCents: 18000, appliedCoupon: null,
    })
  })

  it('exposes a sale that ended one millisecond ago', () => {
    const onSale = applyDiscount({ subtotalCents: 20000, rules, now: 1_772_000_000_000 })
    const afterSale = applyDiscount({ subtotalCents: 20000, rules, now: 1_772_000_000_001 })
    expect(onSale.discountCents).toBeGreaterThan(0)
    expect(afterSale.discountCents).toBe(0)
  })
})

// Assertion of "nothing happened": the only honest way to say it.
it('never calls the payment gateway when the cart is empty', () => {
  const gateway = { charge: jest.fn() }
  checkout({ cart: [], gateway })
  expect(gateway.charge).not.toHaveBeenCalled()
})
```

**When to use Jest vs not.**

Use Jest when: the repo is already Jest; you need the enormous plugin ecosystem (`jest-axe`, `msw`, `jest-localstorage-mock`); you are on a legacy project with heavy `jest.mock` usage; you need `jsdom` behaviours that Vitest's DOM shim does not perfectly reproduce.

Prefer Vitest when: greenfield TypeScript; you want ESM-native speed and type-aware coverage; you want one tool to replace Jest, jsdom, ts-jest/babel, and the bundler (9.1.3). The migration is usually mechanical.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Jest's transform pipeline is its main tax.** Babel or `ts-jest` compiling every file per worker, then a jsdom instance per test file, is why Jest suites take 30-120s on projects where Vitest takes 4s. `@swc/jest` recovers most of it by swapping Babel for the Rust-based SWC compiler.
- **The global environment is a feature and a bug.** `describe`/`it`/`expect` as globals avoids imports (nice, terse), but makes implicit dependencies and name collisions (`test` from a test helper) likely, and makes it easy to forget you are in a special runtime.
- **`clearMocks`/`resetMocks` semantics are a classic footgun.** `resetMocks: true` clears factory implementations passed to `jest.fn(impl)`, turning your configured mock into a no-op that returns `undefined`, and the failure surfaces 200 lines away in some unrelated assertion.
- **`jest-environment-jsdom` is a separate install in Jest 28+.** A missing devDependency produces a confusing "Test environment jest-environment-jsdom cannot be found".

**Production considerations.**

- Never set `testTimeout` high globally to paper over a hang. If a test needs 30s, it is doing I/O and belongs in the integration tier.
- Fail the build on coverage *regression* (diff-cover, or a `coverageThreshold` you ratchet upward), not on an absolute number you set once and never move.
- Pin `@swc/jest` (or `ts-jest` `isolatedModules`) in production repos: type-checking belongs in `tsc --noEmit`, not inside the test transform, where it makes every run 5x slower and reports type errors as test failures.

**Common mistakes.**

1. `expect(x).toBeTruthy()` on a non-boolean, which produces "expected 3 to be truthy" -- a useless failure message. Use `toBe(true)` or a specific assertion.
2. Asserting argument order with `toHaveBeenCalledWith` when order is irrelevant -> use `expect.objectContaining({...})`.
3. `expect(...).toBeCalled()` (alias) in a codebase that standardises on `toHaveBeenCalled()` -- pick one; aliases hide intent in reviews.
4. Forgetting `await` on an async assertion. `expect(p).rejects.toThrow()` without `await` passes even if the promise rejects. Enable `expect.assertions(n)`.
5. Using `jest.useFakeTimers()` globally in `setupFiles` for tests that need real microtask behaviour, causing infinite hangs in promise chains.
6. `expect.hasAssertions()` omitted in tests that mock everything, so a silently short-circuited code path yields a green test.
7. Putting `describe` blocks with side effects at module scope -- the *collection* phase runs even with `--testNamePattern` filtering, so a network call in a `describe` body happens even for skipped tests.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"Walk me through the lifecycle of a test in Jest."* -- Discovery/collection (module top-level code, `describe` bodies, `beforeAll`) -> per-test: `beforeEach` -> test body -> `afterEach` -> finally `afterAll`; then report. `beforeAll` runs once per *worker per file*, not per test, and never across files.
- 🟡 *"How do you test code that calls `setTimeout` and a callback?"* -- `jest.useFakeTimers()` in the test, trigger, then `await jest.advanceTimersByTimeAsync(1000)` and assert the callback fired. Restore with `afterEach(() => jest.useRealTimers())`.
- 🔴 *"How do you make fake timers not break third-party async libraries?"* -- Do not fake timers for code that uses real `setTimeout` inside a library whose behaviour you do not control. Scope `useFakeTimers()` to the specific test; pass a real scheduler into your own code; and prefer dependency injection over timer faking for your own scheduling.
- 🔴 *"`resetMocks: true` broke our suite. Explain."* -- It calls `mockReset()` on every mock before each test, which removes any implementation, so a mock created with `jest.fn(() => 42)` becomes `jest.fn()` returning `undefined`. Use `clearMocks: true` instead, or re-establish implementations in `beforeEach`.
- ⚫ *"When is `@swc/jest` not appropriate?"* -- When you rely on Babel-specific plugins in test transforms, or when you need SWC's decorator behaviour to match a legacy `experimentalDecorators` setup. Also never use it to avoid a genuine type error.

---

### 9.1.3 Vitest: why it is faster, and migration from Jest

**Definition.**

**Vitest** (VoidZero, 2022, Vite-native) is a test runner whose defining choice is to reuse **Vite's** module graph, transform pipeline and dev server for tests. Import a file and you get the same transform, the same aliases (`@/`), the same plugins, the same module caching semantics you already have in dev. Its API is intentionally Jest-compatible: `describe/it/expect/vi.fn/vi.spyOn/beforeEach/...` and `.toBe`/`.toEqual`/`.toHaveBeenCalledWith` all exist with the same meanings.

**Mechanics -- why it is faster (four independent reasons, each measurable).**

1. **Native ESM, no CJS interop.** Jest requires `transform` to CJS for `require()` interop. Every file pays a transpile, and every mock factory crosses a CJS boundary. Vitest keeps native ESM and uses Vite's SSR transform, which is a rename plus shim, not a module rewrite.
2. **esbuild (Rust) transform.** Vite transforms TS/JSX in single-digit milliseconds per file with esbuild's parser, versus Babel's much slower pure-JS transform and ts-jest's full type-checking compiler. `@swc/jest` narrows the gap but is still a second pipeline.
3. **Threads pool (Tinypool), with browser mode available.** `pool: 'threads'` (default) or `'forks'`; browser mode (`browser.enabled: true`) runs tests *inside a real browser* via Vitest Browser Mode with a Playwright provider, which removes the jsdom fidelity gap entirely.
4. **Shared, warm server and smart module invalidation.** A watch-mode re-run only re-transforms changed files; the runner keeps the rest of the graph in memory. Jest must re-read and re-transform a file per worker on every run.

Measured in practice on a mid-size TypeScript monorepo: full suite 90s (Jest) -> 6s (Vitest); watch-mode single re-run 8s -> 0.4s. The *dev-loop* number is the one that changes engineering behaviour: tests get run after every edit instead of once before pushing.

Additional Vitest-only mechanics worth knowing:

- **`vi.mock` is hoisted and works with ESM**, with `importOriginal` for partial mocks.
- **`vi.hoisted()`** to share a value between the hoisted `vi.mock` factory and the test body (the classic "mock out of variable scope" problem Jest solves with a prefix-`mock` variable convention).
- **Mocking a class with `{ spy: true }`** to keep the original implementation while recording calls.
- **`vi.stubEnv` / `vi.stubGlobal` / `vi.unstubAllEnvs`** -- first-class environment stubbing with automatic restore, no `process.env` mutation leaks.
- **`onConsoleLog`** in config to fail tests on `console.error` (a cheap, very effective production-hygiene gate).
- **Coverage via `@vitest/coverage-v8` or `-istanbul`**, plus `coverage.all: true` so untested files count.
- **`test.sequence.shuffle`** to randomise test order -- the single best way to *find* hidden inter-test coupling.
- **`expect.soft()`** for assertions that report but do not halt, and `test.fails()` for known-broken tests.
- **`vitest --typecheck`** runs `tsc` on `*.test-d.ts` files (type-level testing with `expectTypeOf`/`tsd`) in the same run as your unit tests.

**Runnable code -- Vitest config and a migrated test.**

```ts
// vitest.config.ts
import { defineConfig } from 'vitest/config'
import react from '@vitejs/plugin-react'
import { fileURLToPath } from 'node:url'

export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [react()],
  resolve: {
    alias: { '@': fileURLToPath(new URL('./src', import.meta.url)) },
  },
  test: {
    globals: true,                       // describe/it/expect without imports
    environment: 'node',                 // 'node' | 'jsdom' | 'happy-dom' | 'edge-runtime'
    setupFiles: ['./test/setup.ts'],
    include: ['src/**/*.test.{ts,tsx}'],
    exclude: ['**/node_modules/**', '**/dist/**', '**/e2e/**'],

    // Speed / isolation
    pool: 'threads',                     // 'threads' | 'forks' | 'vmThreads' | 'vmForks'
    isolate: true,                       // fresh module registry per file
    fileParallelism: true,
    sequence: { shuffle: false, concurrent: false },
    testTimeout: 5_000,
    hookTimeout: 10_000,
    teardownTimeout: 10_000,

    // Coverage
    coverage: {
      provider: 'v8',
      reporter: ['text', 'lcov', 'json-summary'],
      reportsDirectory: './coverage',
      all: true,
      include: ['src/**/*.{ts,tsx}'],
      exclude: ['src/**/*.d.ts', 'src/main.ts', 'src/**/index.ts'],
      thresholds: {
        lines: 85, functions: 80, branches: 75, statements: 85,
        perFile: true,
        'src/domain/**': { lines: 95, branches: 90 },
      },
    },

    // Gates
    onConsoleLog(log) {
      if (log === 'error') throw new Error('console.error was called during the test')
    },
    dangerouslyIgnoreUnhandledErrors: false,
  },
})
```

```ts
// Before (Jest)
jest.mock('./mailer', () => ({ send: jest.fn() }))
import { send } from './mailer'
import { register } from './register'

// After (Vitest)
import { afterEach, beforeEach, describe, expect, it, vi } from 'vitest'
import { register } from './register'
import { send } from './mailer'

vi.mock('./mailer', () => ({ send: vi.fn() }))
const sendMock = vi.mocked(send)   // fully typed mock -- the big ergonomic win

describe('register', () => {
  beforeEach(() => { sendMock.mockReset() })
  afterEach(() => { vi.unstubAllEnvs(); vi.restoreAllMocks() })

  it('emails a welcome message containing the display name', async () => {
    vi.stubEnv('APP_URL', 'https://app.test')
    const result = await register({ email: 'a@b.test', displayName: 'Ada' })

    expect(result.status).toBe('created')
    expect(sendMock).toHaveBeenCalledWith(
      expect.objectContaining({ to: 'a@b.test', template: 'welcome' }),
    )
  })
})
```

**Migration mechanics (the boring checklist that actually makes it painless).**

| Jest | Vitest | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `jest.fn()` | `vi.fn()` | identical semantics |
| `jest.spyOn` | `vi.spyOn` | |
| `jest.mock(p, f)` | `vi.mock(p, f)` | hoisted in both; use `importOriginal` in Vitest |
| `jest.requireActual` | `await importOriginal()` | inside the factory |
| `jest.useFakeTimers()` | `vi.useFakeTimers()` | defaults differ -- see below |
| `jest.advanceTimersByTime` | `vi.advanceTimersByTime` | |
| `jest.setSystemTime` | `vi.setSystemTime` | |
| `jest.clearAllMocks` | `vi.clearAllMocks()` | |
| `jest.restoreAllMocks` | `vi.restoreAllMocks()` | |
| `jest.requireMock` | `vi.importMock` (async) | |
| `moduleNameMapper` | `resolve.alias` | |
| `testEnvironment: 'jsdom'` | `environment: 'jsdom'` | plus `happy-dom` as a faster alternative |
| `setupFilesAfterEach` | `setupFiles` | runs before *each* test file |
| `globalSetup` | `globalSetup` | identical |
| `collectCoverageFrom` | `coverage.include` | |
| `--detectOpenHandles` | `pool: 'forks'` + isolate | different mechanisms |
| `expect.extend` | `expect.extend` | |
| `jest-image-snapshot` | `@vitest/image-snapshot` | |
| `jest-playwright-preset` | Playwright provider | |

Three *semantic* differences that bite:

1. **Fake timer defaults.** Vitest fakes *more* APIs by default (`performance`, `queueMicrotask`, `nextTick`). `vi.useFakeTimers({ toFake: ['setTimeout', 'clearTimeout', 'Date'] })` gives Jest-like narrowness, and is the right call for library code.
2. **`moduleNameMapper` vs `alias`:** `alias` is applied to *all* imports including those inside dependencies you do not control; `server.deps.inline` controls whether a dependency is transformed. Mocking a `node_modules` package often needs `test.server.deps.inline: ['that-pkg']` in addition to `vi.mock`.
3. **ESM mocks are per-module-graph, not per-require-call.** With native ESM, a module imported by two specifiers is instantiated once; `vi.doMock` + dynamic `import()` + `vi.resetModules()` is the only reliable "mock only for this test" pattern:

```ts
it('handles a network blip', async () => {
  vi.resetModules()
  vi.doMock('./api', () => ({ get: vi.fn().mockRejectedValue(new Error('ECONNRESET')) }))
  const { load } = await import('./load')
  await expect(load()).rejects.toThrow('ECONNRESET')
  vi.doUnmock('./api')
})
```

**When to use Vitest vs Jest.**

Vitest when: greenfield; TypeScript-heavy; you already use Vite; you want the tight watch loop; you want one tool for unit + browser-mode DOM + coverage + type tests. Jest when: an existing large Jest suite with mature custom matchers/transformers; you need a plugin that only exists for Jest; or an organisational constraint.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Vitest is younger.** Its config surface has had breaking changes (v1->v2->v3: `environmentMatchGlobs` removed in favour of `projects`, `deps.optimizer` moved, coverage `all` renamed). Pin the major version and read the migration guide when you bump.
- **The Vite dependency is a feature and a coupling.** Test semantics become coupled to your bundler. If you later move off Vite, you move test runner too.
- **V8 coverage differs from Istanbul coverage.** V8 is line-oriented and fast; Istanbul instruments the AST and gives function and branch counts that can be *more* accurate for default parameters. Both are approximations; neither is ground truth.
- **Browser mode** is excellent but heavier per worker; it is not always the right default.

**Production considerations.**

- Set `isolate: true` (default) and only disable it when you have proven a suite is 3x faster and still deterministic -- a non-isolated suite will eventually produce ghost failures under `fileParallelism`.
- Turn on `onConsoleLog` error-throwing for application code (not libraries; libraries legitimately warn).
- Adopt `--typecheck` and write `*.test-d.ts` files for your public API surface; this is cheap, high-value, and catches breaking type changes CI would otherwise ship.
- Run Vitest in `pool: 'forks'` in CI if you have native addons (better-sqlite3, sharp, canvas) or if you see `worker_threads` flakiness.
- Keep `test.server.deps.inline` minimal; over-inlining blows up transform time.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Assuming `vi.mock` hoisting lets you reference a `const` declared in the test file -- it cannot; the factory runs before initialisation. Use `vi.hoisted()`.
2. Setting `environment: 'jsdom'` globally and thereby slowing the entire suite and hiding that your node-tier tests are not really node-tier. Use per-file `// @vitest-environment jsdom` docblocks or `projects`.
3. Importing `test` from `@jest/globals` in a mixed repo and wondering why the runner cannot find it.
4. Migrating `jest.mock('../fixtures', ...)` path style without checking Vite's resolution.
5. Assuming `vi.useFakeTimers()` restores automatically. It does not; you must call `vi.useRealTimers()`.
6. Expecting the jsdom global `window.matchMedia` / `ResizeObserver` / `IntersectionObserver` to exist. They do not -- polyfill in setup or use `happy-dom`.
7. Comparing `toHaveBeenCalledWith` against an object created with `new Date()` when you meant `expect.any(Date)`.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"Why is Vitest faster than Jest?"* -- It reuses Vite's esbuild-based transform and native ESM graph instead of Babel/ts-jest compiling to CJS, keeps a warm server for watch mode, and can pool in threads while offering a real browser environment; the practical effect is a 10x faster edit-to-result loop.
- 🟡 *"You migrated and one test now hangs. What are the likely causes?"* -- (1) fake timers faking `queueMicrotask`/`nextTick` by default so a promise chain never advances -- narrow `toFake`; (2) a `node_modules` dependency not inlined, so its ESM/CJS interop differs; (3) an import that now resolves through a Vite alias to a different build (`src` vs `dist`); (4) a top-level `await` in an imported module that Jest tolerated.
- 🔴 *"How do you migrate 900 Jest tests safely?"* -- Codemod the API names; add aliases in a `test/setup.ts` (`globalThis.jest = vi`) so the suite compiles; run both runners in CI side-by-side for one week comparing result sets; delete `jest.mock` factories that can become dependency injection; then remove Jest. Never migrate a suite in the same PR as a feature change.
- 🔴 *"When would you prefer Jest's jsdom over Vitest's?"* -- When you need exact jsdom quirks (custom elements, `getComputedStyle` edge cases), or an existing ecosystem plugin. Vitest's `happy-dom` is faster; `jsdom` remains the compatibility king.
- ⚫ *"Does Vitest share state between test files?"* -- With `isolate: true` (default) each test file runs in an isolated environment with a fresh module registry, so no. With `isolate: false`, module state *is* shared -- that is exactly how you turn an accidental global into a flaky ordering dependency you can observe.

---

### 9.1.4 Test doubles: mock, stub, spy, fake, and when each is honest

**Definition.**

A **test double** is any object that stands in for a real dependency in a test. The taxonomy (Meszaros, *xUnit Test Patterns*, the vocabulary the industry actually uses):

| Double | What it does | Defined behaviour | How you make one in Vitest |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Dummy** | Passed to satisfy a signature; does nothing | Nothing (returns `undefined`) | `vi.fn()` |
| **Stub** | Returns canned data so the SUT can run | Provided | `vi.fn().mockReturnValue(x)` |
| **Spy** | Delegates to the real thing, then records | Real, but observable | `vi.spyOn(obj, 'm')` |
| **Mock** | Returns canned data *and* asserts it was called correctly | Wrong on wrong call (**verification-driven**) | `vi.fn(impl)` + `toHaveBeenCalledWith` |
| **Fake** | A working but simplified implementation | Real semantics, reduced fidelity | a hand-written class implementing the port |

The critical distinction is between **state verification** (assert on outputs the SUT produced) and **behaviour verification** (assert on which collaborators the SUT called). Martin Fowler's canonical framing: prefer *state* verification; use *behaviour* verification only when the interaction *is* the requirement. A "mock-heavy" suite is a suite where the mocks' return values *are* the specification -- and the specification is then wrong the moment the interface changes.

**Mechanics -- an honest hierarchy of substitution, from best to worst.**

1. **Real value object** (use the actual `Money` type).
2. **Real in-memory implementation** -- e.g. an `InMemoryOrderRepository` backed by a `Map`, a hand-written `FakeClock` with a settable `now`.
3. **Fake** -- a deliberately simplified but semantically real implementation (`FakePaymentGateway` that succeeds unless you hand it a declining card number).
4. **Spy on a real object** -- great for non-critical collaborators (`logger`, `metrics`).
5. **Stub** -- only for a collaborator whose return value is an input to your assertion.
6. **Mock with verification** -- only when *the call itself* is the behaviour under test (e.g. "publishes an `order.created` event to Kafka with this payload").
7. **Total mock of a system boundary in a unit test** -- the smell; belongs in the integration tier with MSW or Testcontainers.

**Runnable code -- all five doubles, side by side, in one test file.**

```ts
import { describe, expect, it, vi, beforeEach } from 'vitest'
import { CheckoutService } from './checkout'
import type { PaymentGateway, OrderRepo, Logger } from './ports'

// ---- 5. FAKE: semantically real, deliberately simplified. Preferred substitute.
class FakePaymentGateway implements PaymentGateway {
  readonly attempts: { cardId: string; amountCents: number }[] = []
  private readonly declinedCards = new Set<string>(['card_declined'])

  constructor(private readonly ledger: { totalCents: number } = { totalCents: 0 }) {}

  async charge(cardId: string, amountCents: number): Promise<{ txId: string }> {
    this.attempts.push({ cardId, amountCents })
    if (this.declinedCards.has(cardId)) throw new Error('card_declined')
    this.ledger.totalCents += amountCents
    return { txId: `tx_${this.attempts.length}` }
  }
}

// ---- 1. STUB: canned data, no assertions on it.
const stubbedAddressBook = {
  lookup: vi.fn(async (id: string) =>
    id === 'known' ? { line1: '1 Main St', country: 'US' } : null),
}

// ---- 2. SPY: real implementation, calls recorded.
const logger: Logger = { info: vi.fn(), error: vi.fn(), warn: vi.fn() }
const metrics = { increment: vi.fn(), histogram: vi.fn() }

describe('CheckoutService', () => {
  let repo: OrderRepo
  let gateway: PaymentGateway
  let bus: { publish: (type: string, payload: unknown) => void }

  beforeEach(() => {
    repo = { save: vi.fn(async (o) => o), findById: vi.fn(async () => null) } as unknown as OrderRepo
    gateway = new FakePaymentGateway()
    bus = { publish: vi.fn() }
    vi.clearAllMocks()
  })

  // ---- State verification: assert on the outcome. Refactor-tolerant.
  it('charges the total and persists a paid order', async () => {
    const service = new CheckoutService({ repo, gateway, addressBook: stubbedAddressBook, logger, metrics, bus })
    const order = await service.checkout({ cartId: 'c1', cardId: 'card_ok' })

    expect(order.status).toBe('paid')
    expect(repo.save).toHaveBeenCalledWith(expect.objectContaining({ status: 'paid' }))
    // The fake gives you a *queryable* world:
    expect((gateway as FakePaymentGateway).attempts).toEqual([{ cardId: 'card_ok', amountCents: 4200 }])
  })

  // ---- Behaviour verification: the call IS the requirement (an integration event).
  it('emits exactly one order.paid domain event', async () => {
    const service = new CheckoutService({ repo, gateway, addressBook: stubbedAddressBook, logger, metrics, bus })

    await service.checkout({ cartId: 'c1', cardId: 'card_ok' })

    expect(bus.publish).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(1)          // exactly once -- the actual rule
    expect(bus.publish).toHaveBeenCalledWith(
      'order.paid',
      expect.objectContaining({ orderId: expect.any(String) }),
    )
  })

  // ---- Negative verification: the highest-value assertion class in unit tests.
  it('does not charge the card when persistence fails', async () => {
    const failingRepo = {
      save: vi.fn(async () => { throw new Error('out_of_stock') }),
      findById: vi.fn(),
    } as unknown as OrderRepo
    const service = new CheckoutService({ repo: failingRepo, gateway, addressBook: stubbedAddressBook, logger, metrics, bus })

    await expect(service.checkout({ cartId: 'c1', cardId: 'card_ok' })).rejects.toThrow('out_of_stock')

    expect((gateway as FakePaymentGateway).attempts).toEqual([])   // no side effect leaked
    expect(bus.publish).not.toHaveBeenCalled()
  })

  // ---- Spy assertion on a non-critical side effect.
  it('does not log at error level on the happy path', async () => {
    const service = new CheckoutService({ repo, gateway, addressBook: stubbedAddressBook, logger, metrics, bus })
    await service.checkout({ cartId: 'c1', cardId: 'card_ok' })
    expect(logger.error).not.toHaveBeenCalled()
  })
})
```

**Fakes that pay for themselves.** A `FakeClock` is the single highest-value fake in a TypeScript codebase:

```ts
export interface Clock {
  now(): Date
  sleep(ms: number): Promise<void>
  setTimeout(fn: () => void, ms: number): number
  clearTimeout(handle: number): void
}

export class FakeClock implements Clock {
  private currentMs: number
  private seq = 1
  private readonly pending = new Map<number, { fn: () => void; at: number }>()

  constructor(start: number | string | Date = '2026-01-01T00:00:00.000Z') {
    this.currentMs = typeof start === 'number' ? start : new Date(start).getTime()
  }

  now = (): Date => new Date(this.currentMs)

  sleep = async (ms: number): Promise<void> => {
    this.currentMs += ms
    await Promise.resolve()
  }

  setTimeout = (fn: () => void, ms: number): number => {
    const id = this.seq++
    this.pending.set(id, { fn, at: this.currentMs + ms })
    return id
  }

  clearTimeout = (handle: number): void => { this.pending.delete(handle) }

  /** Fire everything due within `ms`, draining microtasks between callbacks. */
  async advance(ms: number): Promise<void> {
    const target = this.currentMs + ms
    for (;;) {
      const due = [...this.pending.entries()]
        .filter(([, v]) => v.at <= target)
        .sort((a, b) => a[1].at - b[1].at)
      if (due.length === 0) break
      const [id, entry] = due[0]!
      this.pending.delete(id)
      this.currentMs = entry.at
      entry.fn()
      await Promise.resolve()
      await Promise.resolve()
    }
    this.currentMs = target
  }

  get pendingCount(): number { return this.pending.size }
  get time(): number { return this.currentMs }
}
```

Injecting this means token-expiry, retry backoff, and scheduled-job tests become instant and exact -- no fake timers, no `advanceTimersByTimeAsync` subtleties. Fakes beat mocks whenever the SUT is time-dependent, because time is the collaborator that most often lies.

**When to use each / when not to.**

- **Mock** -- when the interaction with a boundary (message published, email sent, third-party API called) *is* the contract, and you do not want a network call. Otherwise it is a liability.
- **Stub** -- when a return value is merely an input; never assert on the stub's calls.
- **Spy** -- for logging, metrics, caching layers, and to assert "did not write to the audit log".
- **Fake** -- for repositories, clocks, random, ID generation, and payment/email providers. Highest readability, lowest maintenance, best default.
- **None at all** -- for pure functions, value objects, and code you own end-to-end.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Fakes are code you must maintain.** A fake that drifts from the real interface is a second source of truth. Mitigate with a **contract test**: run the *same* test suite against the fake and the real adapter in the integration tier.
- **Mocks are brittle to refactors.** Renaming a method breaks tests that never asserted business behaviour.
- **Verification style changes failure messages.** Behaviour-verification failures say "expected publish to be called with X, was not called", which points at wiring, not semantics. State-verification failures say "expected status paid, received pending" -- much closer to the bug.
- **Deep mocks (`vi.mock('./deep/module')`)** couple the test to a private detail of another module. This is how a codebase ends up unable to delete a file because 40 tests mock it.

**Production considerations.**

- Enforce "no mocking your own domain" with a lint rule: `no-restricted-syntax` targeting `vi.mock` paths under `src/domain`. If you find yourself mocking a domain module, your seam is in the wrong place.
- Prefer **constructor injection** over module-level mocking. It makes doubles explicit in the signature, removes `vi.mock` hoisting hazards, and makes the production composition root the only place that knows about real implementations.
- Track your "mock ratio" as a smell metric: doubles created / lines under test. Above ~0.3, redesign the seam.
- When you introduce a fake for a third-party adapter, put it in `test-support/` and require it to satisfy the same `interface` as the real one, enforced by TypeScript.

**Common mistakes.**

1. **Mocking `Date.now()` but not `new Date()`** -- a code path that does `new Date()` still gets the real clock. `vi.setSystemTime` fixes both.
2. **Asserting on the mock's identity**: `expect(gateway).toBe(gatewayMock)` -- asserts the test, not the code.
3. **Using `vi.fn()` as both stub and assertion target in a test with 6 other reasons to fail** -- you cannot tell which behaviour regressed.
4. **A fake that always succeeds.** Your tests then never exercise the decline path in a realistic combination; you end up testing the fake.
5. **Reusing a `vi.fn()` across tests without `clearAllMocks`** -- call counts leak and `toHaveBeenCalledTimes(1)` becomes order-dependent.
6. **Mocking a getter.** `vi.spyOn(obj, 'status', 'get')` is required; `vi.spyOn(obj, 'status')` silently does nothing and your "assert" passes vacuously.
7. **Over-mocking your own validator/pure helper** so the test can never catch a real logic bug in it.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is the difference between a mock and a stub?"* -- A stub returns canned data and does not care how it is called; a mock also verifies the calls. Stubs are inputs; mocks are expectations.
- 🟡 *"When is a fake better than a mock?"* -- When the code under test queries the dependency more than once, branches on its responses, or when you want to assert on accumulated state (a fake ledger, a fake queue). Fakes support state verification, which survives refactors; mocks push you into behaviour verification.
- 🔴 *"How do you prevent mocks from rotting?"* -- (1) Contract-test the fake against the real adapter; (2) keep fakes in a single `test-support` package owned by the module; (3) type fakes as `implements Port` so a signature change is a compile error; (4) forbid mocking your own domain via lint; (5) measure and cap the ratio of mocks to code under test.
- 🔴 *"Your test asserts `gateway.charge` was called with these args. The third arg is now an options object. Did the test catch a bug?"* -- No. It caught a signature change, which is a refactor, not a defect. That is the argument for state verification and fakes; interaction assertions should be reserved for genuinely contractual interactions like published events.
- ⚫ *"How do you test code with non-determinism: random and concurrency?"* -- Inject `Random` (a port with `next()`, seeded and reproducible) and a `Scheduler`. For concurrency, inject a `Yield`/deferred helper and assert on ordering explicitly, or model the race as a state machine and assert the *invariant* that holds under all interleavings, rather than asserting one interleaving.

---

### 9.1.5 AAA pattern, arrange helpers, and test data factories

**Definition.**

**AAA** = **Arrange, Act, Assert** -- the structural convention that makes a unit test readable and diffable. One behaviour per test implies exactly one Act and one logical Assert (a compound `expect` that reads as a single statement is acceptable and often clearer than splitting it). The pattern is *narrative*: given a precondition, when an event occurs, then a consequence. The AAA comment blocks are optional but the structure is not -- the value is that a reader can locate each phase with their eyes, and that a failing test names the phase it died in.

Around AAA sit two supporting tools:

- **Arrange helpers** -- functions that build the precondition. Their job is to make the *only* thing that varies visible in the test body.
- **Test data factories / builders** -- parameterised constructors that produce valid-by-default objects, overridable field by field, with **relational integrity** (a `customer()` that yields a customer with a `defaultCard` referencing *that* customer's id, not a hard-coded UUID).

**Mechanics -- the rules that make AAA and factories actually work.**

1. **One Act per test.** Two acts means two behaviours; if the second depends on the first's state, you cannot assert the first in isolation.
2. **The Act is the smallest possible call.** Act on one function, not on a chain that also parses, validates and persists.
3. **Assertions read as sentences.** `expect(result.totalCents).toBe(4200)` is a sentence. `expect(result).toEqual({...17 fields...})` is a change-detector: it fails on every irrelevant field addition and buries the real assertion.
4. **Arrange helpers must be pure and default-rich.** A helper that requires 6 arguments is not a helper; make 5 of them default.
5. **Factories must be deterministic.** Fixed IDs, fixed timestamps via an injected clock, no `Math.random()` -- otherwise failures are unreproducible and you cannot use snapshot diffing.
6. **Factories live in test code, not production code.** Exporting `createUser` from `src` and calling it in tests means your production bundle contains test-only code paths.
7. **Override, don't mutate.** Builders return new objects; a helper that mutates a shared constant produces order-dependent tests.

**Runnable code -- AAA with an arrange helper and a factory.**

```ts
// test-support/factories.ts
import type { User, Order, Address, Money } from '@/domain/types'

// A tiny, typed factory. Note: every field has a valid default; nothing random; nothing shared.
export function userFactory(over: Partial<User> = {}): User {
  return {
    id: 'usr_00000001',
    email: 'ada@example.test',
    displayName: 'Ada Lovelace',
    role: 'member',
    createdAt: '2026-01-01T00:00:00.000Z',
    defaultAddressId: 'adr_00000001',
    ...over,
  }
}

export function addressFactory(over: Partial<Address> = {}): Address {
  return {
    id: 'adr_00000001', line1: '1 Main St', city: 'London',
    country: 'GB', postcode: 'E1 6AN', ...over,
  }
}

export function orderFactory(over: Partial<Order> = {}): Order {
  return {
    id: 'ord_00000001',
    userId: 'usr_00000001',
    // relational integrity by construction
    shippingAddress: addressFactory(),
    lines: [{ sku: 'SKU-1', qty: 1, unitPriceCents: 4200 }],
    status: 'draft',
    createdAt: '2026-01-01T00:00:00.000Z',
    ...over,
  }
}

export function moneyFactory(over: Partial<Money> = {}): Money {
  return { amountCents: 4200, currency: 'GBP', ...over }
}

/** Relational factory: a user wired to an address, correctly. */
export function userWithAddressFactory(over: Partial<User> = {}): { user: User; address: Address } {
  const address = addressFactory(over.defaultAddressId ? { id: over.defaultAddressId } : {})
  return { user: userFactory({ ...over, defaultAddressId: address.id }), address }
}
```

```ts
// src/domain/placeOrder.spec.ts
import { describe, expect, it, vi } from 'vitest'
import { placeOrder } from './placeOrder'
import { orderFactory } from '../../test-support/factories'
import type { Order } from './types'

const clockStub = () => ({ now: () => new Date('2026-02-01T09:00:00Z') })

// ---- Arrange helper: hides construction noise, keeps the *interesting* variation visible.
function setup({ order = orderFactory() }: { order?: Order } = {}) {
  const repo = { save: vi.fn(async (o: Order) => o) }
  const events = { publish: vi.fn() }
  const service = placeOrder({ repo, events, clock: clockStub() })  // composition happens here
  return { service, repo, events, order }
}

describe('placeOrder', () => {
  it('moves a draft order to placed', () => {
    // ---- Arrange
    const { service, repo } = setup({ order: orderFactory({ status: 'draft' }) })

    // ---- Act
    const result = service.place(orderFactory({ id: 'ord_1', status: 'draft' }))

    // ---- Assert
    expect(result.status).toBe('placed')
    expect(repo.save).toHaveBeenCalledWith(expect.objectContaining({ id: 'ord_1', status: 'placed' }))
  })

  it('rejects an order that is already placed', () => {
    // ---- Arrange
    const { service } = setup()

    // ---- Act
    const result = service.place(orderFactory({ status: 'placed' }))

    // ---- Assert
    expect(result).toEqual({ ok: false, error: { code: 'INVALID_TRANSITION', from: 'placed', to: 'placed' } })
  })

  it('publishes order.placed with the order id and the acting user', () => {
    // ---- Arrange
    const { service, events } = setup()

    // ---- Act
    service.place(orderFactory({ id: 'ord_99' }))

    // ---- Assert
    expect(events.publish).toHaveBeenCalledWith({
      type: 'order.placed',
      payload: { orderId: 'ord_99', userId: 'usr_00000001', at: '2026-02-01T09:00:00.000Z' },
    })
  })

  it('does not persist when the transition is illegal', () => {
    // ---- Arrange
    const { service, repo, events } = setup()

    // ---- Act
    const result = service.place(orderFactory({ status: 'shipped' }))

    // ---- Assert
    expect(result.ok).toBe(false)
    expect(repo.save).not.toHaveBeenCalled()
    expect(events.publish).not.toHaveBeenCalled()
  })
})
```

**Table-driven AAA.** When the only variation is input -> output, collapse the tests into a spec:

```ts
const STATUSES = ['draft', 'placed', 'shipped', 'cancelled'] as const

it.each([
  ['draft', 'placed', true],
  ['placed', 'shipped', true],
  ['shipped', 'placed', false],
  ['cancelled', 'placed', false],
  ['placed', 'cancelled', true],
])('transition %s -> %s allowed = %s', (from, to, allowed) => {
  // Arrange
  const service = placeOrder({ repo: { save: vi.fn() }, events: { publish: vi.fn() }, clock: clockStub() })
  // Act
  const result = service.place(orderFactory({ status: from as Order['status'] }))
  // Assert
  expect(result.ok).toBe(allowed)
  expect(allowed ? (result as { to?: string }).to : undefined).toBe(allowed ? to : undefined)
})
```

**When to use AAA / helpers / factories vs not.**

- Use AAA always in this tier. It is free.
- Use an arrange helper when 2 or more tests need the same wiring -- at that point the helper is free, and above ~3 lines of construction it is a win.
- Use a **builder** (fluent, `.withStatus('shipped').withLines([...])`) when there are many fields and tests vary several at once; use a **factory** (one call, overridable object) when tests vary one or two fields.
- Do not build an elaborate factory for a function with 2 parameters. Ceremony.

**Trade-offs.**

- A powerful factory becomes a **second domain model**. If `userFactory` can build a user the real system forbids, tests can pass against impossible states. Counter-measures: a production `User` type that is precise (branded ids, discriminated unions for status), and a test that asserts the factory output passes the production invariant validator once.
- Factories with many defaults hide the fact that a test is exercising 40 fields. Keep the default surface small; make unusual setups explicit.
- Helpers that hide the Act are worse than no helper. Never let an arrange helper call the method under test.

**Production considerations.**

- Locate factories in one folder (`test-support/factories`) and lint against importing them from `src/` (test-only code must not ship).
- If you use Prisma/Drizzle in integration tests, derive your factories from the *generated* types so a schema change breaks the factory, not the test.
- Add an ESLint rule or `boundaries` config so `test-support` cannot be imported by production entry points.
- Use a fixed default timestamp constant; if a test cares about a time delta, inject the clock.

**Common mistakes.**

1. **Arrange buried in the Act** -- building inputs inside the call under test, so the test reads as one long line and the failure message points nowhere.
2. **Arrange helper that also asserts** -- the failure is attributed to the wrong test.
3. **Mutating a shared fixture object** between tests (`order.status = 'x'` at test scope) -> order dependence.
4. **A factory with `as any` fields**, defeating the compiler's ability to tell you a field was renamed.
5. **Copy-pasting 30 lines of setup into 30 tests** -- the classic DRY violation that makes refactoring a merge conflict.
6. **Asserting a whole object literal** for a 40-field entity when you care about 1 field.
7. **Using real personal data in fixtures** -- a GDPR problem before it is a testing problem (9.4.8).

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"Explain AAA."* -- Arrange the preconditions, Act once on the subject, Assert one logical outcome. The value is a test that a stranger can navigate in three glances and that fails in an identifiable phase.
- 🟡 *"When do you extract an arrange helper?"* -- As soon as the same 3+ lines of wiring appear in a second test, and always before the setup grows past a screenful. The test body should be almost entirely the behaviour.
- 🔴 *"How do you prevent test factories from becoming a second, wrong domain model?"* -- Derive their types from the production types, keep the factory surface minimal, prefer explicit overrides to accumulating optional fields, add one test that the factory output passes the production invariant validator, and delete the factory the moment a third variant appears (switch to an inline literal).
- 🔴 *"Show me a test that is testing implementation details."* -- `expect(repo.save.mock.calls[0][0].lines[0].unitPriceCents).toBe(4200)` when the behaviour under test is "the order total is 4200". The first breaks when you add a `discountCents` field; the second survives.
- ⚫ *"What is a test smell you look for in AAA review?"* -- Multiple Acts; asserts on the arrange helper's internals; a test whose name starts with "test" or describes a method rather than a rule; and a helper whose name hides an action (`setupAndSave()`).

---

### 9.1.6 Pure functions and property-based testing

**Definition.**

A **pure function** is one whose output depends only on its inputs -- no I/O, no clock reads, no randomness, no mutation of arguments or globals, no network. Pure functions are the ideal unit of testing because they are trivially deterministic, trivially parallel, and trivially reproducible.

**Property-based testing (PBT)** flips the normal question. Example-based testing asks: *does this function return the right thing for this input?* PBT asks: *which properties hold for **every** input?* You provide **generators** (arbitraries) for the input domain and **properties** (invariants) that must hold for all generated values; the library shrinks any failing case to a minimal counterexample. The workflow: generate -> assert property -> on failure, shrink -> report minimal input plus seed.

The framework you will meet: **fast-check** (TypeScript, property and model-based testing). Use it.

**Mechanics -- properties worth testing (a catalogue).**

| Property | Statement | Example domain |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Round-trip / inverse** | `decode(encode(x)) === x` | codecs, serialisers, URL builders, base64, token signing |
| **Idempotence** | `f(f(x)) === f(x)` | normalisation, upsert, dedupe, slugify |
| **Commutativity** | `f(a, b) === f(b, a)` | set union, money sum across carts, stable sorts |
| **Associativity** | `f(f(a,b),c) === f(a,f(b,c))` | money arithmetic, string concat, reducers |
| **Monotonicity** | `a <= b` implies `f(a) <= f(b)` | tax brackets, size limits, pagination |
| **Involution** | `f(f(x)) === x` | negation, swap, percent-escape |
| **Distributivity** | `a*(b+c) === a*b + a*c` | pricing relationships |
| **Conservation** | `sum(inputs) === sum(outputs)` | splitting a payment into provider line items |
| **Totality** | never throws on a valid domain | parsers, validators |
| **Determinism** | `f(x)` deep-equals itself twice | catches hidden state |
| **Boundary invariance** | shrinking a value cannot increase a cost | rate limits, page sizes |
| **Order irrelevance** | result independent of input permutation | batching, aggregation |

**Runnable code -- fast-check over a money/pricing domain.**

```ts
// src/domain/money.property.spec.ts
import { describe, expect, it } from 'vitest'
import fc from 'fast-check'
import { addMoney, allocateMoney, applyDiscount, roundHalfUp } from './money'
import type { Money } from './types'

// A generator for a valid Money. Build arbitraries ONCE and reuse them.
const moneyArb = fc
  .record({
    amountCents: fc.integer({ min: 0, max: 10 ** 9 }),
    currency: fc.constantFrom<'GBP' | 'USD' | 'EUR'>('GBP', 'USD', 'EUR'),
  })
  .filter((m) => Number.isSafeInteger(m.amountCents)) as fc.Arbitrary<Money>

const sameCurrency = (...ms: Money[]) => new Set(ms.map((m) => m.currency)).size === 1

describe('addMoney -- properties', () => {
  it('is commutative', () => {
    fc.assert(
      fc.property(moneyArb, moneyArb, (a, b) => {
        if (!sameCurrency(a, b)) return true
        expect(addMoney(a, b)).toEqual(addMoney(b, a))
        return true
      }),
      { numRuns: 500, seed: 42 },
    )
  })

  it('is associative', () => {
    fc.assert(
      fc.property(moneyArb, moneyArb, moneyArb, (a, b, c) => {
        if (!sameCurrency(a, b, c)) return true
        expect(addMoney(addMoney(a, b), c)).toEqual(addMoney(a, addMoney(b, c)))
        return true
      }),
    )
  })

  it('has zero as an identity element', () => {
    fc.assert(
      fc.property(moneyArb, (a) => {
        expect(addMoney(a, { amountCents: 0, currency: a.currency })).toEqual(a)
        return true
      }),
    )
  })

  it('matches a reference implementation over 200 random operations (model-based)', () => {
    fc.assert(
      fc.property(
        fc.array(fc.integer({ min: -10_000, max: 10_000 }), { minLength: 1, maxLength: 200 }),
        (deltas) => {
          const model = deltas.reduce((s, d) => s + d, 0)
          const real = deltas.reduce<Money>(
            (m, d) => addMoney(m, { amountCents: d, currency: 'GBP' }),
            { amountCents: 0, currency: 'GBP' },
          )
          expect(real.amountCents).toBe(model)
          return true
        },
      ),
      { numRuns: 200 },
    )
  })
})

describe('allocateMoney -- the classic integer-splitting property', () => {
  it('never invents or loses a cent when splitting, and is fair', () => {
    fc.assert(
      fc.property(
        fc.integer({ min: 0, max: 10 ** 7 }),      // total to split
        fc.integer({ min: 1, max: 20 }),          // number of shares
        (totalCents, n) => {
          const shares = allocateMoney({ amountCents: totalCents, currency: 'GBP' }, n)

          expect(shares.reduce((s, x) => s + x.amountCents, 0)).toBe(totalCents)   // conservation
          expect(shares.every((s) => Number.isInteger(s.amountCents) && s.amountCents >= 0)).toBe(true)
          const amounts = shares.map((s) => s.amountCents)
          expect(Math.max(...amounts) - Math.min(...amounts)).toBeLessThanOrEqual(1)  // fairness
          expect(shares).toHaveLength(n)
          return true
        },
      ),
      { numRuns: 1000 },
    )
  })

  it('keeps total + discount === subtotal and never goes negative', () => {
    fc.assert(
      fc.property(
        fc.integer({ min: 0, max: 10 ** 7 }),
        fc.integer({ min: 0, max: 100 }),
        (subtotal, pct) => {
          const r = applyDiscount({
            subtotalCents: subtotal,
            percentOff: pct,
            rules: { currency: 'GBP', maxDiscountPercent: 100, tiers: [] },
          })
          expect(r.subtotalCents).toBe(subtotal)
          expect(r.discountCents).toBe(roundHalfUp((subtotal * pct) / 100))
          expect(r.totalCents + r.discountCents).toBe(r.subtotalCents)
          expect(r.totalCents).toBeGreaterThanOrEqual(0)
          return true
        },
      ),
    )
  })
})
```

```ts
// Shrink output on failure -- this is what makes PBT worth the effort.
it('parses ISO dates only when they are real', () => {
  fc.assert(
    fc.property(fc.string(), (raw) => {
      const parsed = parseIsoDate(raw)          // returns Date | null, never throws
      if (parsed === null) return true
      expect(formatIsoDate(parsed)).toMatch(/^\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}$/)
      expect(parseIsoDate(formatIsoDate(parsed))).toEqual(parsed)
      return true
    }),
    { numRuns: 5_000 },
  )
})
// On failure fast-check prints:
//   Counterexample: ["2026-13-45"]
//   Shrunk 12 steps -- the minimal input that breaks the property.
//   seed: -8123456789   (reproduce with { seed: -8123456789 })
```

**The other half of pure-function testing: exhaustive enumeration for small domains.** When the input domain is small and enumerable, do not sample -- enumerate:

```ts
it('defines a legal transition for exactly these pairs', () => {
  const matrix = STATUSES.flatMap((from) =>
    STATUSES.map((to) => [`${from}->${to}`, isLegalTransition(from, to)] as const),
  )
  expect(Object.fromEntries(matrix)).toMatchInlineSnapshot(`
    {
      "cancelled->cancelled": false,
      "cancelled->draft": false,
      "cancelled->placed": false,
      "cancelled->shipped": false,
      "draft->cancelled": true,
      "draft->placed": true,
      "draft->shipped": false,
      "placed->cancelled": true,
      "placed->draft": false,
      "placed->placed": false,
      "placed->shipped": true,
      "shipped->cancelled": false,
      "shipped->draft": false,
      "shipped->placed": false,
      "shipped->shipped": false,
    }
  `)
})
```

**When to use PBT vs example tests.**

Use PBT for: parsers/serialisers (round-trip), schedulers and allocators (conservation, fairness), state machines (model-based against a reference), crypto and adversarial input handling, reducers and fold logic, anything where a human would enumerate fewer than 20 cases but the space is 10^6.

Stay with example tests for: a specific business rule someone will ask about in UAT ("GBP 100 gets 10% off"), UI copy, and anything where the property is not obvious to a non-author. Properties are documentation only their author fully understands -- pair them with a couple of example tests.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Authoring cost.** Generators require thought about domain invariants (`filter` for cross-field constraints). Budget 2-5x a single example test.
- **Debugging cost is lower, paradoxically**, because the counterexample is minimal and the **seed** is printed -- you can reproduce with `fc.assert(prop, { seed: 1234 })`.
- **Flakiness risk from unseeded randomness.** Always fix the seed in CI, or use `fc.configureGlobal({ seed: 0 })`; a "flaky" PBT failure is almost always a real bug the previous run happened not to find.
- **Smaller `numRuns` in CI for slow properties**, and a separate nightly job with `numRuns: 50_000` to mine deeper.

**Production considerations.**

- Persist the failing seed to CI artefacts so a developer can reproduce locally; keep a `property-failures/` directory of counterexamples promoted to deterministic regression tests.
- Run PBT in a dedicated `test:property` script; it is the slowest tier and does not need to gate every commit.
- Combine with **fuzzing** for parsers: `fc.string()` with a large `maxLength` and a byte-level arbitrary (`fc.uint8Array`) gets close for codec code.
- For crypto, use a **known-answer test (KAT)** from a published vector *in addition to* round-trip properties; round-trip alone will not catch a key-order mistake.

**Common mistakes.**

1. **Asserting a property the type system already guarantees** -- wasted budget.
2. **Properties that are trivially true because of the implementation** (asserting `x === x`).
3. **Forgetting cross-field preconditions** and letting the generator produce a currency mismatch, then "fixing" it by returning early in every property so the property never runs. Watch the `numRuns` output and count early returns.
4. **Unbounded `numRuns` in CI** -- a property over a big arbitrary can take minutes; the whole suite must stay fast.
5. **Not shrinking** because you used `fc.check` instead of `fc.assert` and lost the report.
6. **Property tests that are also unit tests for the generator** -- a 200-line arbitrary with its own `describe` is a sign you are testing fast-check.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is property-based testing?"* -- You assert invariants that must hold for all generated inputs rather than specific expected outputs; the framework generates, checks, and on failure shrinks to a minimal counterexample.
- 🟡 *"Give me a good property for a payment-splitting function."* -- Conservation: the parts sum to the whole; non-negativity; integrality; and fairness (max minus min is at most 1 cent). Four properties, each catching a different real bug.
- 🔴 *"A property test failed once in CI and never again. What now?"* -- PBT failures are almost never flaky; the run found a real bug. Reproduce with the printed seed, write it as a deterministic example test so it can never regress silently, fix the bug, keep the property.
- 🔴 *"Where does PBT sit relative to unit tests?"* -- It *is* a unit-test technique, and a complement: properties explore a large space, examples pin the specific rules stakeholders care about. Ship both.
- ⚫ *"How do you PBT a React component?"* -- You do not primarily. Generate **state** (a list of items, a form model) and assert **invariants** with RTL queries: no duplicate keys, no unlabelled input, focus order follows DOM order, the accessible name of a control always contains its visible label. Property-generating component tests are the frontier, not the mainstream.

---

### 9.1.7 TDD: red, green, refactor, and where it actually pays off

**Definition.**

**TDD** (Kent Beck, 1999; Beck's *Test-Driven Development: By Example*, 2003) is a *discipline*, not a testing technique: you write a failing test first, make it pass in the simplest way, then improve the design without changing behaviour, repeating in a small loop. The canonical cycle is **Red -> Green -> Refactor**. Uncle Bob's framing: "I write no production code unless there is a failing test that fails for the right reason."

The formal alternative is **BDD** (Dan North, 2006): the same loop expressed in *behaviour* language -- `given/when/then` or `context/when/it` -- with a translation layer (Cucumber, Behave) and, more commonly, the "BDD-flavoured TDD" where the `describe` string is the scenario and the `it` string is the rule. The differences that matter in practice are the vocabulary and the intent that scenarios are shared with non-programmers.

**Mechanics -- the loop, with the failure mode at each step.**

**Red.** Write the smallest test that expresses one rule. Run it. Confirm it fails **for the expected reason** (not `TypeError: x is not a function` for an unrelated reason). A red test is not a bug report you file; it is your specification, written as an executable sentence.
- *Failure mode:* a test that fails because the function does not exist yet is fine. A test that fails because your import is wrong is not -- you have learned nothing.
- *Failure mode:* writing 12 red tests before any green. You have written a design document; you lose the incremental feedback that makes TDD valuable.

**Green.** Make it pass in the **simplest way that could possibly work** -- hardcode, stub, return a constant. This feels like cheating and is the most misunderstood step. The point is to establish that the test can actually pass, i.e. that you have a *working feedback loop*, before investing in the real implementation.
- *Failure mode:* the classic "I wrote the real implementation in the first green." You skip the "does my test work at all?" check and can end up with a test that passes for the wrong reason.

**Refactor.** Only with the test green. Remove duplication, extract names, invert dependencies, reduce coupling, delete dead code. The test suite is the safety net that makes this safe; the refactor is where TDD pays its rent, because design improvements that would be terrifying to make "for safety" become free.
- *Failure mode:* refactoring without a net, or refactoring so aggressively you change the public contract while the test stays green (only possible if your test was weak -- which TDD's discipline should have prevented).
- *Failure mode:* "refactor" as a euphemism for "rewrite". If behaviour changes, that is a new green, and you need a new red.

**Refactor-only-with-tests (the honest admission).** TDD is genuinely hard to apply to: legacy code with no tests (you write characterisation tests first, then vertical slices), UI layout work (you spike, then lock in with visual/E2E tests), spike-then-delete work, and anything where the *design is the unknown*.

**Runnable code -- one full cycle, showing the throwaway green.**

```ts
// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// STEP 1 -- RED
// Rule: "A 25-year-old member gets free standard shipping."
it('gives free standard shipping to members over 25', () => {
  // Arrange
  const member = userFactory({ role: 'member', age: 25 })

  // Act
  const shipping = quoteShipping({ cart: cartFactory(), customer: member, address: addressFactory() })

  // Assert
  expect(shipping.costCents).toBe(0)
  expect(shipping.method).toBe('standard')
})
// -> FAIL: expected 599 to be 0. Correct failure reason. The test is a real spec. ✅

// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// STEP 2 -- GREEN (throwaway, on purpose)
export function quoteShipping(_args: ShippingArgs): Quote {
  return { costCents: 0, method: 'standard', etaDays: 3 }   // hardcode: proves the test can pass
}
// -> PASS ✅  The feedback loop is now *proven*.

// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// STEP 2b -- GREEN (real, minimally)
export function quoteShipping({ cart, customer, address }: ShippingArgs): Quote {
  const weightKg = cart.lines.reduce((w, l) => w + l.weightKg * l.qty, 0)
  const over25Member = customer.role === 'member' && customer.age >= 25
  const method = over25Member ? 'standard' : weightKg > 20 ? 'freight' : 'standard'
  const costCents = over25Member ? 0 : method === 'freight' ? 4500 : 599
  return { costCents, method, etaDays: method === 'freight' ? 7 : 3, country: address.country }
}
// -> PASS ✅

// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// STEP 3 -- REFACTOR (test stays green throughout)
export interface Quote {
  costCents: number
  method: 'standard' | 'freight'
  etaDays: number
  country: string
}

const RATE_TABLE = {
  standard: { baseCents: 599, perKgCents: 10, etaDays: 3 },
  freight: { baseCents: 4500, perKgCents: 35, etaDays: 7 },
} as const

function chooseMethod(weightKg: number, c: Customer): 'standard' | 'freight' {
  if (c.role === 'member' && c.age >= 25) return 'standard'
  return weightKg > 20 ? 'freight' : 'standard'
}

function isFreeShipping(c: Customer, method: 'standard' | 'freight'): boolean {
  return c.role === 'member' && c.age >= 25 && method === 'standard'
}

export function quoteShipping({ cart, customer, address }: ShippingArgs): Quote {
  const weightKg = cart.lines.reduce((w, l) => w + l.weightKg * l.qty, 0)
  const method = chooseMethod(weightKg, customer)
  const rate = RATE_TABLE[method]
  return {
    costCents: isFreeShipping(customer, method) ? 0 : rate.baseCents + Math.ceil(weightKg * rate.perKgCents),
    method,
    etaDays: rate.etaDays,
    country: address.country,
  }
}
```

Note that adding `country` to `Quote` did not require changing the test, because the test asserted a *rule*, not a shape. Had the test been `expect(quoteShipping(...)).toEqual({ costCents: 0, method: 'standard' })`, the refactor would have gone red and you would have learned nothing useful -- you would have "fixed" the test. **This is the entire argument for behaviour-focused tests, and it is why TDD and mock-heavy suites are in tension.**

**Characterisation tests: how to start TDD on legacy code (Michael Feathers' seam-first approach).**

```ts
// Step 1: pin current behaviour WITHOUT judgement, including current bugs.
it('CHARACTERISATION: rounds 1.005 down to 1.00 (we believe this is a bug)', () => {
  expect(legacyRound(1.005)).toBe(1.0)   // green on arrival by definition
})

// Step 2: find a seam -- inject the effects.
export function computeInvoice(input: InvoiceInput, deps: { now: Clock; tax: TaxProvider }) { /* ... */ }

// Step 3: write a red test for the *correct* behaviour, in the new seam.
it('rounds 1.005 up to 1.01', () => {
  expect(roundMoney(1.005)).toBe(1.01)
})

// Step 4: you now have one test documenting the bug and one documenting the intent.
//         The migration becomes "delete legacy, keep the new" -- mechanical.
```

**When TDD pays off vs not.**

**TDD has a large, well-documented positive return when:**
- the domain has **rules and edge cases** (finance, insurance, logistics, permissions, compliance);
- the rules are **stable** and the **implementation will be rewritten** (framework migration, language change, algorithm replacement);
- the code is **hot** -- a bug costs real money or real incidents;
- you work **solo or in a small pair** -- the loop's cost is low;
- the team has **continuous integration**, so the suite runs constantly and is therefore trustworthy.

**TDD is a poor economic fit when:**
- you are **exploring** an unknown design (spike first, throw the spike away, then TDD the shape you kept);
- the "unit" is mostly **framework wiring** whose value is in the integration, not the logic;
- **UI layout** is the deliverable -- you need eyes on pixels;
- you are on a **hard deadline with fixed scope** and the only question is whether it works end to end;
- you are in a team of more than ~4 on the same code and coordination cost dominates;
- your tests would be **thrown away** by the next framework migration (a real risk for heavy `jest.mock` suites).

**Trade-offs.**

- **TDD's cost is paid in the first 20% of the work** and recovered in the remaining 80% (faster feedback, no debugging phase, design pressure). Studies are mixed on *defect counts* but consistently positive on *debug time* and *API design quality*.
- **The discipline is a social contract, not a tool.** A CI gate cannot tell you whether you wrote the test first. The proof is in the git history: alternating red/green commits.
- **Bad tests make TDD actively harmful** -- a green suite protecting the wrong behaviour, with the *additional* cost of having written the test first. TDD raises the value of test quality and lowers tolerance for bad tests.
- **"Test-first" is not "test-all-first."** The rule is a *small* red step.

**Production considerations.**

- Keep the loop under ~10 minutes; if a single TDD cycle takes an hour, the design is too coupled.
- Track cycle time (edit -> green) as a team metric.
