- If you adopt TDD on an existing codebase, do it **vertical-slice**: one new feature end-to-end, TDD, then the rest of the team observes.
- Have the reviewer ask "which test did you write first, and can I see that it failed?" for non-trivial changes. Make it a review comment, not a debate.
- In AI-assisted coding, TDD is *more* valuable, not less: models generate plausible implementations at high volume; a failing test is the only reliable specification and the only thing that catches a confidently-wrong first draft.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Writing the test after the implementation, then calling it TDD in the PR description.
2. **Green-by-hardcode** left in production ("we'll fix it in the refactor step") -- and the refactor step never comes.
3. Writing 3 tests that fail for 3 unrelated reasons, then fixing them in one commit, so you learn nothing about which behaviour drove which design.
4. Refactoring during the red step ("let me just restructure this first").
5. TDD-ing a one-line pure function to the point of ceremony, then skipping TDD for the 200-line orchestrator. The wrong allocation of discipline.
6. Using a mock's return value to *define* the expected output, so the test asserts the mock, not the logic.
7. Abandoning the refactor step entirely, which yields a codebase that is well-tested and structurally a mess.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is TDD?"* -- A cycle of write a failing test, make it pass simply, then refactor with the test as a safety net, repeated in small steps; the test is written before the code, and it is an executable specification.
- 🟡 *"Why hardcode in the green step?"* -- To prove the harness and the assertion work, isolating a failure of the test from a failure of the implementation, and to reach green in seconds so the loop's cost stays low.
- 🔴 *"When would you NOT use TDD?"* -- Exploratory spikes, pure-UI layout work, throwaway prototypes, hard-deadline throwaway code, and legacy code where you must first write characterisation tests. Also pure glue with no logic; there is nothing to specify.
- 🔴 *"How do you introduce TDD to a team?"* -- One team, one bounded area, a real example, and a CI signal: require that any *bug fix* ships with a test that fails without the fix. That is enforceable, valuable, and does not require anyone to believe in purity.
- ⚫ *"How do you know a test was genuinely written first?"* -- Evidence, not assertion: separate commits (red test, then green), a CI run on the test commit that shows failure, or `git log -p` showing the test file's first version referencing a non-existent export. Note that `--fixup` workflows erase this evidence, so keep tests in their own commit when you care.

---

### 9.1.8 Coverage: what it measures, what it misses, and the coverage traps

**Definition.**

**Code coverage** is a static measure of which *executable constructs* your test suite actually executed, produced by **instrumenting** the source and recording hits. It is a *meta-metric about the test suite*, not a measure of quality. Four families:

| Metric | Definition | What it catches | Trap |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Statement / line** | % of executable lines hit | a whole function never called | the "line is hit" illusion: `if (x) { dangerousCall() }` -- the line ran, the branch did not |
| **Branch** | % of `if/else`, ternary, `switch` arms, `??`, `?.` taken | untaken branches | a branch "covered" by a test that hits it accidentally |
| **Function** | % of functions invoked | an unexported helper nobody calls | dead code detection (its main real value) |
| **Istanbul (statement/branch/function/line)** | AST-based, precise | -- | counts generated/transpiled code unless configured to skip |

Providers: **Istanbul** (`babel-plugin-istanbul`, TS via `ts-jest`/`@vitest/coverage-istanbul`) instruments the AST -- accurate but slower; **V8** (Vitest v8 provider, `c8`, Node's `NODE_V8_COVERAGE`) uses the runtime's own counters -- much faster, but coverage maps are coarser (default args, `&&`/`||` chains and some transpiler-generated branches are not distinguished).

**Mechanics -- what is *not* measured, which is the important part.**

1. **Assertions.** A test that calls a function and asserts nothing is 100% coverage of that function. Coverage cannot see that `expect` is missing. The mitigations are `expect.assertions(n)` and `expect.hasAssertions()`, not coverage.
2. **Correctness of expectations.** 100% coverage with wrong assertions is the worst possible state: full confidence, zero signal.
3. **Behaviour outside the executed paths.** Mutation testing exists precisely to measure this (9.4.4).
4. **Interleavings and concurrency.** A race in a message consumer has 0% "missing lines" and a real bug.
5. **The system.** Coverage of unit tests says nothing about whether the deployed configuration, DNS, or migration is right.
6. **Test quality per line.** Two tests can hit the same line; the second may assert nothing.
7. **Timing/performance/memory.** Never measured by coverage.

**The coverage traps, enumerated.**

- **The 100% gate.** A codebase at 100% coverage with vacuous assertions is less trustworthy than one at 60% with strong property tests. 100% is almost always achieved by adding `it('returns a value', () => { expect(f(1)).toBeDefined() })`.
- **Coverage of generated code.** Instrumenting `dist/`, `node_modules`, `.d.ts`, barrel `index.ts` re-exports, and framework glue distorts every number. Exclude them explicitly.
- **Coverage of the *test* files themselves.** Always exclude `*.spec.ts`; otherwise your metric rewards writing more test lines.
- **Uncovered does not mean untestable.** Some lines are defensive (`catch { /* istanbul ignore next */ }`), unreachable by design, or dead. The interesting metric is the *delta* -- new uncovered code in the PR.
- **Coverage as a target rather than a signal.** Teams hit a number and stop writing meaningful assertions. Goodhart's law with a very short fuse.
- **A single global threshold on a monorepo**, which lets an untested critical package borrow coverage from a trivially-covered utility package.
- **Coverage measured on the wrong provider for your pipeline** (e.g. V8 coverage on heavily-transpiled TS with `downlevelIteration`), producing numbers that do not match the source you read.
- **Comparing coverage across frameworks** (Jest vs Vitest, Istanbul vs V8) -- the numbers are not commensurable.
- **Ignoring branch coverage of error paths**, which is where coverage is most valuable (the `catch`, the `if (!user) throw`, the retry-exhausted branch) and most frequently missing.

**Runnable code -- coverage config that resists Goodhart, plus diff coverage in CI.**

```jsonc
// package.json (relevant scripts)
{
  "scripts": {
    "test": "vitest run",
    "test:watch": "vitest",
    "test:cov": "vitest run --coverage",
    "test:cov:diff": "vitest run --coverage && diff-cover coverage/lcov.info --compare-branch=origin/main --fail-under=90",
    "test:property": "vitest run --project property",
    "test:mutation": "stryker run"
  }
}
```

```ts
// vitest.config.ts -- per-package thresholds and explicit exclusions
coverage: {
  provider: 'v8',
  reporter: ['text', 'json-summary', 'lcov', 'html'],
  all: true,
  include: ['src/**/*.{ts,tsx}'],
  exclude: [
    'src/**/*.d.ts',
    'src/main.ts',                  // bootstrapping, no logic
    'src/**/index.ts',              // re-exports only
    'src/**/migrations/**',
    'src/**/*.stories.tsx',
    'src/**/*.spec.{ts,tsx}',
    'src/vendor/**',
  ],
  thresholds: {
    lines: 70, functions: 70, branches: 65, statements: 70,
    'src/domain/**': { lines: 90, functions: 95, branches: 90 },
    'src/application/**': { lines: 80, branches: 75 },
    'src/infrastructure/**': { lines: 55, branches: 45 },
  },
  clean: true,
  cleanOnRerun: true,
}
```

```yaml
# .github/workflows/ci.yml -- the gate that actually helps: no *new* uncovered lines.
- name: Diff coverage gate
  run: |
    npx vitest run --coverage --reporter=default --reporter=json-summary
    npx diff-cover coverage/lcov.info \
      --compare-branch="origin/${{ github.base_ref }}" \
      --fail-under=90 \
      --exclude-lines="^\s*(export |const |function |type |interface |import )" \
      --markdown-report diff-cover.md
- name: Upload diff report
  if: always()
  uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
  with: { name: diff-cover, path: diff-cover.md }
```

```ts
// The mitigation for "coverage cannot see assertions": assert your asserts.
it('degrades gracefully when the recommendations service is unavailable', async () => {
  expect.assertions(4)                       // if the early-return path is hit, this test FAILS
  const result = await buildHomeFeed({ deps: depsWithFailingRecommendations() })
  expect(result.items).toHaveLength(3)       // curated items still shown
  expect(result.banner).toBe('recommendations-unavailable')
  expect(result.usedFallback).toBe(true)
  expect(result.items.every((i) => i.source === 'curated')).toBe(true)
})
```

**When to chase coverage vs not.**

Chase it for: detecting **dead code** (uncovered exported functions are usually dead), spotting **untested error paths** (`catch` blocks at 0%), and finding **new** untested code in a PR. Use diff coverage as a gate.

Do not chase it for: quality assurance, defect prediction (a weak predictor once you control for suite size), or as an optimisation target for engineers (it produces assertion-free code and is associated with more churn).

**Trade-offs.**

- **Instrumentation cost.** Istanbul roughly doubles the transform time of the unit tier; V8 is nearly free but coarser. On a large codebase, choosing V8 is a speed/precision trade you should make explicitly.
- **High water mark.** Once at 90%, the remaining 10% is often the hardest 10% (error paths, races, platform branches), and getting there can take weeks and produce low-value tests. Set thresholds per package, not globally.
- **Diff coverage** requires a base branch reference and can be gamed by pushing untested code in a separate commit; it is still markedly better than absolute.
- **Coverage as a merge gate** slows small PRs and creates pressure to add "coverage tests". The alternative -- reporting coverage, alerting on large drops, reviewing in the PR -- is slower to improve the codebase but healthier.

**Production considerations.**

- Never enforce coverage on `main` retroactively with a 100% gate; you will ship a hostile change and lose goodwill.
- Track **mutation score** (9.4.4) as the real quality metric and coverage as the cheap proxy.
- Publish the HTML report to CI artefacts; a coverage number in a CI log is never inspected.
- If you use V8 coverage, verify with one known untested branch -- if it reports 100% branches with a real gap, switch to Istanbul.
- Exclude `/* c8 ignore next */` sparingly and require a comment explaining why; an unexplained ignore is hidden test debt.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Setting a 100% global threshold, then hitting it with `toBeDefined()` assertions.
2. Including the coverage report of the test files themselves.
3. Not excluding transpiler output, so `__awaiter`/`__generator` helper branches dominate the number.
4. Using `expect.assertions(0)` in a test that "should never run" instead of `it.skip` plus a tracked ticket.
5. Measuring coverage on a `--changed` run, which only instruments touched files and reports a meaningless 100%.
6. Treating an uncovered line as a bug to fix *now* instead of triaging: dead code? defensive? or a real missing test?
7. Comparing your 95% to a competitor's 95% when one uses V8 and the other Istanbul.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What does code coverage measure?"* -- Which executable constructs (lines, branches, functions, statements) your suite executed. It is a measure of the *suite*, not of correctness or quality.
- 🟡 *"A function is at 100% coverage. Is it tested?"* -- Not necessarily. It may be called by one test that asserts nothing meaningful, or all its `if` branches may be untested while the lines were executed. Check assertions and branch coverage; the real test is mutation score.
- 🔴 *"How do you use coverage in a PR gate without encouraging assertion-free tests?"* -- Diff coverage with a high bar (90% of changed lines), per-package thresholds rather than a global 100%, mutation testing as the quality signal, and `expect.assertions(n)` / `expect.hasAssertions()` in the setup so vacuous tests fail.
- 🔴 *"What are the worst coverage traps?"* -- 100% gates, coverage of generated/test code, and using coverage as a productivity target. All three are Goodhart failures: they optimise the number, not the defect rate.
- ⚫ *"Your repo shows 88% branch coverage and shipped a null-dereference bug in an error path. Explain and fix."* -- Branch coverage counted the `catch` as taken (it was entered) but the assertion inside was a smoke test. Add `expect.assertions`, mutation testing on that module, and a rule that error paths need a *named* test asserting the *error contract* (type/code/message), not just "throws".

---

### 9.1.9 Snapshot testing: the good use, the bad use, and obsolete-snapshot rot

**Definition.**

A **snapshot test** runs code, serialises the result to a deterministic string, and compares it to a stored artefact (a `.snap` file, an inline snapshot, or a committed PNG). If it differs, the test fails and shows a diff; the developer reviews the diff and, if the change is intended, updates the snapshot. Snapshot tests trade precise assertions for *reviewable diffs*, and they are the only practical way to assert on a large, low-stakes, high-churn output -- rendered markup, serialised state, generated code, API responses, and (a different tool) rendered pixels.

| Flavour | Artefact | Update | Best for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **External** `.snap` | `__snapshots__/x.snap` | `-u` | large, multi-fixture outputs |
| **Inline** `.toMatchInlineSnapshot()` | the test file itself | `-u` writes into source | small, reviewable-in-PR values |
| **Image** | `__image_snapshots__/*.png` | `-u` | visual regression (9.3.7) |
| **DOM** `toMatchSnapshot()` | HTML string | `-u` | component markup -- usually a mistake (9.1.11) |
| **API response** | JSON of the server's response | manual | contract documentation |

**Mechanics -- the mechanism, and its sharp edges.**

- **Serialisation must be deterministic.** `JSON.stringify` is insertion-order dependent; object keys from a `Map`, `Set`, `Promise.all` completion order, `Date`, and class instances all serialise unpredictably. You almost always need a **snapshot serializer** that normalises: sorted keys, replaced ids/uuids/dates/timestamps, truncated hashes, sorted arrays where order is not semantically meaningful.
- **`propertyMatchers`** (Jest) and **`matchers`** (Vitest) let you ignore noisy fields, e.g. `expect.addSnapshotSerializer({ test: (v) => typeof v === 'string' && /^[0-9a-f]{24}$/.test(v), print: () => 'HexString' })`.
- **`-u` is the most dangerous flag in the toolchain.** It rewrites the artefact without asking. A reviewer must read snapshot diffs as carefully as code diffs -- a *process* requirement, not a technical one.
- **Obsolete snapshots** (no test references them any more) linger. In CI you must set `updateSnapshot: 'none'` and fail on obsolete entries. Unreferenced `.snap` entries should be deleted, not left to rot.
- **Snapshots are not reviewable when huge.** A 4000-line `.snap` diff is not reviewed; it is rubber-stamped. The moment the snapshot is too big to read, it has lost its value and should be replaced by explicit assertions.

**Runnable code -- the *good* use: normalised serialisation of a generated artefact.**

```ts
// test-support/snapshotSerializers.ts -- installed in setup.ts for ALL tests.
import { expect } from 'vitest'

// 1. Normalise volatile primitives.
expect.addSnapshotSerializer({
  test: (v) => typeof v === 'string' && /^\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}T[\d:.]+Z$/.test(v),
  print: () => '"<ISO-DATE>"',
})
expect.addSnapshotSerializer({
  test: (v) => typeof v === 'string' && /^[0-9a-f]{8}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{12}$/i.test(v),
  print: () => '"<UUID>"',
})
expect.addSnapshotSerializer({
  test: (v) => typeof v === 'string' && /\b[0-9a-f]{24}\b/.test(v),
  print: () => '"<MONGO_OID>"',
})
expect.addSnapshotSerializer({
  test: (v) => v instanceof Date,
  print: (v) => `"<DATE:${(v as Date).toISOString().slice(0, 10)}>"`,   // keep day granularity when it matters
})
expect.addSnapshotSerializer({
  test: (v) => v instanceof Error,
  print: (v) => `"<Error ${(v as Error).name}: ${(v as Error).message}>"`,
})
// 2. Deterministic key order for plain objects.
expect.addSnapshotSerializer({
  test: (v) => v !== null && typeof v === 'object' && !Array.isArray(v) && v.constructor === Object,
  print: (v, serialize) =>
    serialize({ ...Object.fromEntries(Object.entries(v as object).sort(([a], [b]) => a.localeCompare(b))) }),
})
```

```ts
// GOOD: an OpenAPI document generated from route definitions. Large, churny, human-reviewable diff.
//       Any accidental change to a required field, an error code, or a security scope is visible.
it('generates a stable OpenAPI document', () => {
  expect(buildOpenApiDocument(routes)).toMatchSnapshot()
})

// GOOD: a state-machine trace. Small, human-meaningful, and the diff tells the whole story.
it('replays the subscription lifecycle and records every transition', () => {
  const sm = createSubscriptionMachine()
  const trace: string[] = []
  for (const event of ['trial_started', 'trial_ended', 'activated', 'payment_failed', 'canceled', 'reactivated']) {
    const next = sm.send(event)
    trace.push(`${sm.state} --${event}--> ${next.state}`)
  }
  expect(trace).toMatchInlineSnapshot(`
    [
      "inactive --trial_started--> trialing",
      "trialing --trial_ended--> grace",
      "grace --activated--> active",
      "active --payment_failed--> past_due",
      "past_due --canceled--> canceled",
      "canceled --reactivated--> trialing",
    ]
  `)
})
```

```ts
// BAD: snapshotting a component's full DOM. Every class-name refactor is a red PR;
//      nobody reads 300 lines of HTML; and it asserts implementation, not behaviour.
it('renders the cart', () => {
  const { container } = render(<Cart items={items} />)
  expect(container).toMatchSnapshot()   // 🔴 do not do this
})
```

```ts
// BAD: snapshotting the whole response of a big endpoint with volatile fields
//      not covered by a serializer -> the snapshot fails on every request.
it('matches the user profile response', async () => {
  const res = await request(app).get('/api/me').set(auth).expect(200)
  expect(res.body).toMatchSnapshot()   // 🔴 contains lastLoginAt, sessionId, order-sensitive arrays
})
```

**Vitest's obsolete-snapshot handling and a CI guard.**

```ts
// vitest.config.ts
test: {
  snapshotOptions: {
    updateSnapshot: process.env.CI ? 'none' : 'new',   // CI must never rewrite snapshots
  },
}
```

```bash
# Practical CI guard that works regardless of runner version:
set -o pipefail
npx vitest run --update=false 2>&1 | tee vitest.log
if grep -qE '[0-9]+ snapshots? obsolete' vitest.log; then
  echo "::error::Obsolete snapshots present. Run 'npx vitest -u' locally and commit the result."
  exit 1
fi
```

**The rot-prevention protocol (this is the part most teams skip).**

1. **Small and inline by default.** If a snapshot does not fit in a PR diff you would actually read, it does not go in.
2. **Serializers cover every volatile field**, and you add a serializer *the moment* a snapshot fails for a non-behavioural reason. This keeps the "review noise" ratio near zero.
3. **Explicit assertions for the things that matter.** Snapshot = "everything else did not change". Then add 3-5 targeted `expect`s for invariants that must hold (auth required, this field present, this status code). Snapshots detect *change*; explicit assertions detect *correctness*. You need both.
4. **`-u` is a code review artefact.** A PR whose diff is 900 lines of `.snap` change is a signal to add a serializer or an explicit assertion, not to approve.
5. **Delete on removal.** When you delete a test, run `-u` and commit the deletion of the orphaned entry. Configure the runner to fail on obsolete entries.
6. **Version-controlled, never stored in a database.** Snapshots belong in git, next to the code, reviewable in the same PR.
7. **Snapshot-only PRs get extra scrutiny.** If the diff contains only `.snap` changes, the reviewer should ask "which change is intended?" and get a specific answer.

**When to use snapshots vs not.**

**Use for:** generated artefacts (OpenAPI, GraphQL schema, i18n key coverage, code-gen output), large stable-ish structures where explicitness is infeasible (serialised redux/Immer state, form models), state-machine traces, translation key sets, and visual output (pixels).
**Avoid for:** a single value you can assert precisely, error messages, business-critical numeric results, component DOM markup, anything a human must read to be useful, and anything with more than a handful of volatile fields.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Extremely low marginal cost to add a case, extremely low value per byte.** A snapshot test with 5 fixtures gives 5 reviews' worth of signal for the price of one test -- but only if a human actually reads the diff.
- **Snapshots are change-detectors, not correctness checks.** They will happily lock in a bug: a snapshot recorded from wrong output becomes the standard, and "the snapshot says so" is a powerful and wrong argument in a review.
- **They can be used to hide work.** A large unexplained snapshot diff in a PR is a red flag for "I just pressed -u".
- **Determinism costs engineering.** Each volatile field needs a serializer or a deterministic fixture; without it you will churn.

**Production considerations.**

- Never auto-update snapshots in CI. `updateSnapshot: 'none'` and fail on obsolete.
- Store image snapshots with a tolerance and a pixel-diff review UI (Playwright's `toHaveScreenshot`).
- In a monorepo, put snapshots beside the test or in `__snapshots__`, and make sure your clean/build script does not delete them.
- If a snapshot fails only because of an unrelated id, that is a bug in your *fixture*, and the fix is a deterministic fixture, not a looser snapshot.

**Common mistakes.**

1. `-u` in CI. It converts a red build into a green one with no review, then the next run passes forever.
2. Snapshotting a component's DOM.
3. Snapshotting an object that contains a `Date`, an id, or a timestamp, and then adding `--ci` so it passes "sometimes".
4. Never deleting obsolete entries, so `.snap` files grow to megabytes and reviewing any change becomes impossible.
5. Using snapshots to avoid thinking about what should be asserted.
6. Snapshotting in a loop over 50 fixtures with a shared mutable object.
7. Storing snapshots in S3 or a shared database, so PRs cannot show a diff.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"When is a snapshot test a good idea?"* -- For large, deterministic, reviewable outputs where writing explicit assertions would be enormous and unmaintainable: generated schemas, serialised state, state-machine traces, rendered pixels.
- 🟡 *"How do you stop snapshots becoming noise?"* -- Serialisers normalise volatile fields (ids, dates, hashes), keep snapshots small and inline, pair each snapshot with a few explicit invariant assertions, never update in CI, and fail on obsolete snapshots.
- 🔴 *"A PR changed 400 lines of `.snap`. What do you do?"* -- Do not approve. Ask which change is intended; add serialisers for the volatile parts; add explicit assertions for the important invariants; and require the author to explain the semantic change in the PR description. Large snapshot diffs hide real behaviour changes and occasionally lock in bugs.
- 🔴 *"Snapshots are 100% covered but the app broke. How?"* -- Snapshots assert that output is *unchanged*, not that it is *correct*. They have no notion of assertions, so `expect(fn())` with `.toMatchSnapshot()` still passes. Pair with mutation testing.
- ⚫ *"Inline vs external snapshots?"* -- Inline keeps the expected value in the test file, so the diff appears in the same review as the code and the value cannot drift out of sync with the test; external is better for very large or many-fixture outputs where file-level organisation matters.

---

### 9.1.10 Async code and timers: the four ways tests lie about time

**Definition.**

Async tests lie about time in four distinct ways, and each has a distinct symptom:

1. **The test finishes before the async work does** -- you assert on a promise that has not resolved; `await` is missing or on the wrong promise. Symptom: intermittent "expected undefined", or a test that passes even when the code is broken.
2. **Real time is slower than the test's patience** -- the test uses a real `setTimeout(50)` and CI is loaded. Symptom: the classic "works locally, fails in CI".
3. **Fake time is advanced but the microtask queue is not** -- `advanceTimersByTime` (synchronous) fires the timer callback but any `await` inside it is queued as a microtask that never runs before your assertion. Symptom: the assertion sees the state from *before* the callback completed. This is the single most common timer bug.
4. **The system under test schedules a timeout but the test never flushes it, or flushes it and leaks it** -- the test finishes with a pending timer, and Jest prints "A worker process has failed to exit gracefully" or the next test is affected. Symptom: open handles, "Cannot log after tests are done", memory growth.

**Mechanics -- the correct tool for each.**

```ts
// ── 1. Await the right promise.
it('rejects a duplicate signup', async () => {
  await expect(register({ email: 'dup@example.test' })).rejects.toThrow(AlreadyRegistered)
  // and if you must not await, prove you waited:  expect.assertions(1)
})

// ── 2. Eliminate real time entirely. Two valid approaches:

//    (a) Fake timers (Vitest/Jest)
vi.useFakeTimers({ toFake: ['setTimeout', 'clearTimeout', 'Date'] })   // narrow: do not fake nextTick
afterEach(() => { vi.useRealTimers() })

it('retries with exponential backoff', async () => {
  const fetchImpl = vi.fn()
    .mockRejectedValueOnce(new Error('503'))
    .mockRejectedValueOnce(new Error('503'))
    .mockResolvedValueOnce({ ok: true })
  const client = createClient({ fetch: fetchImpl, backoffMs: (n) => 2 ** n * 100, maxRetries: 3 })

  const promise = client.get('/thing')

  // Async variant: advances the clock AND drains the microtask queue between timers.
  await vi.advanceTimersByTimeAsync(700)   // 100 + 200 (+ slack)
  const res = await promise

  expect(res.ok).toBe(true)
  expect(fetchImpl).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(3)
})

//    (b) Inject the clock (preferred for your own code -- no global patching at all)
it('expires the session after 30 days', () => {
  const clock = new FakeClock('2026-01-01T00:00:00Z')
  const sessions = new SessionStore({ clock, ttlDays: 30 })
  const s = sessions.issue('usr_1')

  clock.advance(29 * 24 * 3600_000)
  expect(sessions.get('usr_1')).toBeDefined()
  clock.advance(2 * 24 * 3600_000)
  expect(sessions.get('usr_1')).toBeNull()
})

// ── 3. If you are stuck with fake timers, remember the difference:
//      vi.advanceTimersByTime(1000)          -> fires callbacks synchronously, no promise resolution
//      await vi.advanceTimersByTimeAsync(1000) -> fires callbacks AND awaits the microtask queue
it('flushes the debounced search', async () => {
  vi.useFakeTimers()
  const user = userEvent.setup({ advanceTimers: vi.advanceTimersByTime })
  render(<SearchBox />)
  await user.type(screen.getByRole('searchbox'), 'ada')
  await vi.advanceTimersByTimeAsync(300)
  expect(await screen.findByText(/3 results/)).toBeInTheDocument()
})

// ── 4. Never leak. Detect leaks.
it('does not leave a debounce timer pending after unmount', () => {
  vi.useFakeTimers()
  const clearSpy = vi.spyOn(globalThis, 'clearTimeout')
  const { unmount } = render(<SearchBox />)
  fireEvent.change(screen.getByRole('searchbox'), { target: { value: 'a' } })
  unmount()
  expect(clearSpy).toHaveBeenCalled()
  expect(vi.getTimerCount()).toBe(0)      // the definitive check
})
```

**The four lie-modes, matched to their fixes, in one table.**

| The lie | Symptom | Root cause | Correct fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Not awaiting | Passes with broken code; "expected undefined" | `await` missing / on the wrong promise | `await` the returned promise; `expect.assertions(n)` |
| Real time in test | Flaky ~1% in CI | real timers + loaded machine | inject a `Clock`/scheduler, or fake timers with the async advance variant |
| Sync timer advance | Assertion sees pre-callback state | microtask queue not drained | `await vi.advanceTimersByTimeAsync(ms)` |
| Leaked timers/handles | "worker failed to exit", cross-test pollution | no `clearTimeout`/unsubscribe | production code must clear on teardown; assert `getTimerCount() === 0`; `afterEach` restores real timers |

**Runnable code -- testing a debounced, abortable, promise-returning function properly.**

```ts
// src/lib/autocomplete.ts
export interface AbortableSearch { (q: string, signal: AbortSignal): Promise<string[]> }

export interface Scheduler {
  setTimeout(fn: () => void, ms: number): number
  clearTimeout(handle: number): void
}

export function createAutocomplete(deps: {
  search: AbortableSearch
  debounceMs: number
  clock: Scheduler
}) {
  return function autocomplete(q: string, signal: AbortSignal): Promise<string[]> {
    return new Promise<string[]>((resolve, reject) => {
      const handle = deps.clock.setTimeout(() => {
        deps.search(q, signal).then(resolve, reject)
      }, deps.clock && deps.debounceMs)
      signal.addEventListener('abort', () => {
        deps.clock.clearTimeout(handle)
        reject(new DOMException('Aborted', 'AbortError'))
      }, { once: true })
    })
  }
}
```

```ts
// src/lib/autocomplete.test.ts
import { describe, expect, it, vi } from 'vitest'
import { createAutocomplete } from './autocomplete'
import type { Scheduler } from './autocomplete'

const fakeScheduler = () => {
  const pending = new Map<number, { fn: () => void; at: number }>()
  let now = 0
  let seq = 1
  const s: Scheduler & { advance(ms: number): Promise<void>; count(): number } = {
    setTimeout: (fn, ms) => { const id = seq++; pending.set(id, { fn, at: now + ms }); return id },
    clearTimeout: (h) => { pending.delete(h) },
    async advance(ms: number) {
      const target = now + ms
      for (;;) {
        const due = [...pending.entries()].filter(([, v]) => v.at <= target).sort((a, b) => a[1].at - b[1].at)
        if (due.length === 0) break
        const [id, v] = due[0]!
        pending.delete(id)
        now = v.at
        v.fn()
        await Promise.resolve()
        await Promise.resolve()
      }
      now = target
    },
    count: () => pending.size,
  }
  return s
}

describe('createAutocomplete', () => {
  it('debounces: three keystrokes produce exactly one search, with the last value', async () => {
    const clock = fakeScheduler()
    const search = vi.fn().mockResolvedValue(['a'])
    const ac = createAutocomplete({ search, debounceMs: 250, clock })
    const ctl = new AbortController()

    ac('a', ctl.signal)
    ac('ab', ctl.signal)
    const p3 = ac('abc', ctl.signal)
    await clock.advance(250)

    expect(await p3).toEqual(['a'])
    expect(search).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(1)
    expect(search).toHaveBeenCalledWith('abc', ctl.signal)
  })

  it('cancels the pending debounce on abort and leaves no timer behind', async () => {
    const clock = fakeScheduler()
    const search = vi.fn()
    const ac = createAutocomplete({ search, debounceMs: 250, clock })
    const ctl = new AbortController()

    const p = ac('ada', ctl.signal)
    const rejection = expect(p).rejects.toThrow(/AbortError/)
    ctl.abort()
    await rejection

    expect(clock.count()).toBe(0)
    expect(search).not.toHaveBeenCalled()
  })

  it('propagates a search failure instead of hanging forever', async () => {
    const clock = fakeScheduler()
    const search = vi.fn().mockRejectedValue(new Error('upstream 500'))
    const ac = createAutocomplete({ search, debounceMs: 250, clock })

    const p = ac('ada', new AbortController().signal)
    const assertion = expect(p).rejects.toThrow('upstream 500')
    await clock.advance(250)
    await assertion
  })
})
```

**Tooling that finds these lies for you.**

```ts
// test/setup.ts
import { afterEach, expect, vi } from 'vitest'

// (1) Unhandled rejections become failures.
process.on('unhandledRejection', (reason) => {
  throw new Error(`Unhandled rejection in tests: ${String(reason)}`)
})

// (2) Detect leaked fake timers automatically when a test forgets to restore.
afterEach(() => {
  if (vi.isFakeTimers()) {
    const left = vi.getTimerCount()
    if (left > 0) {
      // Do not auto-clean: surfacing the leak teaches the author to fix production code.
      throw new Error(`${left} timer(s) still pending at end of test -- the code under test did not clear them`)
    }
    vi.useRealTimers()
  }
})
```

```ts
// vitest.config.ts -- narrow the faked API surface globally
test: {
  setupFiles: ['./test/setup.ts'],
  fakeTimers: { toFake: ['setTimeout', 'clearTimeout', 'setInterval', 'clearInterval', 'Date'] },
}
```

**When fake timers vs injected clock.**

- **Fake timers** when the code under test is *third-party* or uses globals directly and you cannot change it (a debounce inside a library, a `setInterval` in a component you do not own).
- **Injected clock** for your own code, always. It is faster, parallel-safe, has no microtask subtleties, produces better failures, and works identically in the browser. It is also the only approach that scales to *ordering* tests.
- **Never** fake timers globally for a whole test file that also contains pure synchronous tests -- you gain nothing and lose the ability to test real async I/O in the same file.

**Trade-offs.**

- Fake timers couple your tests to the *shape* of the code (it must use globals) and to the runner's timer implementation. Vitest's `@sinonjs/fake-timers` defaults differ from Jest's, so tests are not always portable.
- Injecting a clock is a design commitment: every module needs the port threaded through. That is a cost paid once and refunded forever, and it is the right investment in any system with a time rule (sessions, retries, billing, caches, jobs).
- `userEvent.setup({ advanceTimers: vi.advanceTimersByTime })` is required when using `user-event` with fake timers; forgetting it produces a 5-second-timeout flake.
- Async advancement (`advanceTimersByTimeAsync`) is slower but correct. Use it by default.

**Production considerations.**

- Production code that schedules anything **must** return a disposer, unsubscribe, or accept an `AbortSignal`, and must clear on teardown. This is a correctness requirement (memory leaks in a long-lived server) that the test enforces for free.
- Ban real `sleep` in tests above the unit tier. In the integration tier, prefer waiting for a *condition* with a deadline (polling a health endpoint, `await waitFor(() => expect(...))`) over `sleep(2000)`.
- Set a global `testTimeout` of 5s and treat a timeout as a *bug report*, not as a knob to turn.

**Common mistakes.**

1. `it('...', () => { expect(fn()).resolves.toBe(1) })` -- missing `async`/`await`; the assertion is never awaited and unhandled rejections leak.
2. `vi.advanceTimersByTime(1000)` with a promise-returning SUT.
3. `await Promise.all(promises.map((p) => p))` where `p` is a rejection -- becomes an unhandled rejection.
4. `vi.useFakeTimers()` in `beforeAll` and never restoring, so `Date.now()` is frozen for the whole file.
5. `fireEvent.change(...)` then `await waitFor(...)` without faking time, so the debounce's real 300ms makes the suite slow and flaky.
6. `setTimeout(done, 100)` instead of returning a promise; combined with fake timers it never fires and you get a timeout with no error.
7. `vi.runAllTimers()` on code with a `setInterval` -- infinite loop/hang. Use `runOnlyPendingTimers()` or `advanceTimersByTime`.
8. Awaiting `expect(p).rejects.toThrow()` before the timer that resolves `p` has fired -- the rejection assertion attaches to a still-pending promise.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you test code with a 5-minute timeout?"* -- Never with real time: fake the timers and advance them, or inject a `Clock`. The test finishes in milliseconds and is deterministic.
- 🟡 *"`advanceTimersByTime` vs `advanceTimersByTimeAsync`?"* -- The synchronous version only fires timer callbacks; any promise continuations inside them are queued as microtasks and have not run when it returns, so your assertion can see stale state. The async version drains the microtask queue between callbacks, which is what you need for async code.
- 🔴 *"A test is flaky ~2% in CI. Walk me through your diagnosis."* -- (1) Is it asserting on a real timer? (2) Is a promise awaited on the right promise? (3) Is a shared resource (file, port, module state) leaking between files? (4) Is it order dependent? (5) Run it 1000x locally and in a single-threaded, unloaded environment to separate real flakiness from load. (6) Check for open handles.
- 🔴 *"How do you test a debounced, abortable function?"* -- Inject the timer functions and the abort signal; assert that N rapid calls produce one fetch with the last value, that abort rejects with `AbortError` and leaves zero pending timers, and that a search failure propagates rather than hanging. Advance the fake clock with the async variant.
- ⚫ *"What is 'test pollution via timers'?"* -- A test leaves a real `setInterval` running; the next test's assertions observe the previous test's callbacks mutating shared state or writing to stdout, producing failures that move depending on order. Detect with `getTimerCount()` assertions or a teardown that fails on pending handles.

---

### 9.1.11 React component testing philosophy: implementation details vs user-visible behavior

**Definition.**

**Component testing** is the highest-volume category of unit test in a React codebase and the category most likely to go wrong. The governing principle, established by the Testing Library philosophy ("Your tests should resemble the way your users use your software"):

> **Test the component the way a user would use it: by its accessible role, its visible label, its placeholder, its text content. Not by its class names, its internal state, its data-testid, or its prop plumbing.**

React Testing Library (RTL) is the de-facto standard: it renders into a `Document`, exposes **queries that prioritise accessibility** (`getByRole`, `getByLabelText`, `getByPlaceholderText`, `getByText`, `getByDisplayValue`, `getByAltText`, `getByTitle`), and fights you if you reach for `container.querySelector`.

**Mechanics -- the query priority, and what each tier tells you.**

| Query | Accessibility-safe? | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `getByRole(role, { name })` | best | "there is a button labelled 'Submit'" -- exactly the user's contract |
| `getByLabelText` | yes | form control association |
| `getByPlaceholderText` | caution | disappears once the value is set; not an accessible name |
| `getByText` | yes | static text |
| `getByDisplayValue` | caution | input values; also matches non-form text nodes |
| `getByAltText` | yes | images |
| `getByTitle` | caution | mostly tooltips |
| `getByTestId` | last resort | "an element exists" with zero user meaning |
| `container.querySelector` | no | couples to markup; use mostly to assert *absence* |

```tsx
// ✅ Behaviour, via role and accessible name.
it('disables the submit button until the form is valid', async () => {
  const user = userEvent.setup()
  render(<SignupForm onSubmit={onSubmit} />)

  expect(screen.getByRole('button', { name: /create account/i })).toBeDisabled()

  await user.type(screen.getByRole('textbox', { name: /email address/i }), 'ada@example.test')
  await user.type(screen.getByLabelText(/^password$/i), 'correct horse battery staple')
  await user.click(screen.getByRole('checkbox', { name: /i accept the terms/i }))

  expect(screen.getByRole('button', { name: /create account/i })).toBeEnabled()
})

// ❌ Implementation details: breaks on any refactor, tests nothing about behaviour.
it('validates', () => {
  const { container } = render(<SignupForm onSubmit={onSubmit} />)
  expect(container.querySelector('.is-valid')).toBeNull()
  expect(container.querySelector('form input[name="email"]')).toBeTruthy()
  expect(screen.getByTestId('submit-btn')).toBeInTheDocument()
})
```

**The three testable layers of a component, and where to draw the line.**

1. **Behaviour (assert here).** What the user can do and observe: enabled/disabled, visible text, validation messages, focus movement, error announcements, `aria-*` state, the callback payload.
2. **Structure (assert sparingly).** Only when the structure *is* the requirement: a `<table>` with `<th scope>` for a data grid, a `<fieldset>`+`<legend>` group, a modal's `role="dialog"` and `aria-modal`.
3. **Implementation (never assert).** Hook call order, `useState` internals, `useMemo` dependency arrays, class names, conditional rendering branches, prop identity.

**What to mock in a component test -- the narrow line.**

- Mock: network (MSW, at the boundary -- see 9.2.6), the *parent* (render the component in isolation with a fake `onSubmit`), time and randomness, and heavy third-party widgets (a chart, a map, a payment iframe) where the test's subject is the surrounding layout, not the widget.
- Do not mock: your own hooks (`useCart`, `useUser`), your own presentational children, your own validators. Mocking them means the test cannot find a bug in them, and you end up with 40 tests that all pass while the app is broken. **If a hook needs mocking to be testable, extract the logic into a pure function and test that directly.**
- Do not mock: `react`, `react-dom`, the router provider, or `@tanstack/react-query` (MSW at the network layer tests the real query hook, which is what production uses).

**Runnable code -- the same component tested two ways, and the extract-a-hook refactor that saves the suite.**

```tsx
// ❌ BEFORE: 90-line component, 140-line test, 6 mocked hooks, 0 real coverage of the logic.
export function OrderSummary({ orderId }: { orderId: string }) {
  const { data, isLoading, error } = useOrder(orderId)
  const { taxRate, formatMoney } = usePricing()
  if (isLoading) return <Spinner />
  if (error) return <ErrorBox error={error} />
  return <div>{/* ... 80 more lines of JSX ... */}</div>
}

// test/OrderSummary.test.tsx
vi.mock('../hooks/useOrder')
vi.mock('../hooks/usePricing')
// ... and the test asserts on class names. Zero defect detection. 🔴
```

```tsx
// ✅ AFTER: extract the pure decision, test the decision exhaustively, test the component's contract.
export interface OrderSummaryView {
  state: 'loading' | 'error' | 'ready'
  heading: string
  lines: { label: string; value: string }[]
  total: string
  errorMessage?: string
}

export function buildOrderSummaryView(input: {
  order: Order | null
  taxRateBps: number
  formatMoney: (cents: number) => string
  error?: Error | null
  isLoading: boolean
}): OrderSummaryView {
  if (input.isLoading) return { state: 'loading', heading: 'Loading your order', lines: [], total: '' }
  if (input.error) {
    return {
      state: 'error',
      heading: 'We could not load this order',
      lines: [],
      total: '',
      errorMessage: 'Please refresh the page or contact support if this continues.',
    }
  }
  if (!input.order) {
    return { state: 'ready', heading: 'Nothing here yet', lines: [], total: input.formatMoney(0) }
  }
  const subtotal = input.order.lines.reduce((s, l) => s + l.unitPriceCents * l.qty, 0)
  const tax = Math.round((subtotal * input.taxRateBps) / 10_000)
  const total = subtotal + tax
  return {
    state: 'ready',
    heading: `Order ${input.order.id}`,
    lines: [
      { label: 'Subtotal', value: input.formatMoney(subtotal) },
      { label: `Tax (${input.taxRateBps / 100}%)`, value: input.formatMoney(tax) },
    ],
    total: input.formatMoney(total),
  }
}

export function OrderSummary({ orderId }: { orderId: string }) {
  const { data, isLoading, error } = useOrder(orderId)
  const { taxRateBps, formatMoney } = usePricing()
  const view = buildOrderSummaryView({ order: data ?? null, taxRateBps, formatMoney, error, isLoading })

  if (view.state === 'loading') return <Spinner />
  if (view.state === 'error') return <ErrorBox error={view.errorMessage!} />

  return (
    <section aria-labelledby="order-summary-heading">
      <h2 id="order-summary-heading">{view.heading}</h2>
      <dl>
        {view.lines.map((l) => (
          <div key={l.label}>
            <dt>{l.label}</dt>
            <dd>{l.value}</dd>
          </div>
        ))}
      </dl>
      <p>Total: {view.total}</p>
    </section>
  )
}
```

```ts
// The decision is now a pure function: table-test it exhaustively, no mocks at all.
describe('buildOrderSummaryView', () => {
  const gbp = (cents: number) => `GBP ${(cents / 100).toFixed(2)}`

  it('shows the loading state before any data exists', () => {
    const view = buildOrderSummaryView({ order: null, taxRateBps: 2000, formatMoney: gbp, isLoading: true })
    expect(view.state).toBe('loading')
  })

  it('never leaks a raw error message to the user', () => {
    const view = buildOrderSummaryView({
      order: null, taxRateBps: 2000, formatMoney: gbp, isLoading: false,
      error: new Error('ECONNREFUSED 10.0.0.5:5432'),
    })
    expect(view.errorMessage).not.toMatch(/ECONNREFUSED/)
    expect(view.errorMessage).toBe('Please refresh the page or contact support if this continues.')
  })

  it('computes tax on the subtotal, rounded to the nearest penny', () => {
    const view = buildOrderSummaryView({
      order: orderFactory({ lines: [{ sku: 'A', qty: 3, unitPriceCents: 333 }] }),
      taxRateBps: 1750, formatMoney: gbp, isLoading: false,
    })
    expect(view.lines).toEqual([
      { label: 'Subtotal', value: 'GBP 9.99' },   // 999 cents
      { label: 'Tax (17.5%)', value: 'GBP 1.75' },// round(999*0.175) = 174.825 -> 175
    ])
    expect(view.total).toBe('GBP 11.74')
  })
})

// The component test is now 3 assertions about what the user sees.
it('renders the order total and the tax breakdown', async () => {
  server.use(http.get('/api/orders/ord_1', () => HttpResponse.json(orderFactory({ id: 'ord_1' }))))
  render(<OrderSummary orderId="ord_1" />, { wrapper: createTestProviders() })

  expect(await screen.findByRole('heading', { name: 'Order ord_1' })).toBeInTheDocument()
  expect(screen.getByText('Total: GBP 42.00')).toBeInTheDocument()
  expect(screen.getAllByRole('term')).toHaveLength(2)
})

it('tells the user to refresh when the order cannot be loaded', async () => {
  server.use(http.get('/api/orders/ord_1', () => new HttpResponse(null, { status: 503 })))
  render(<OrderSummary orderId="ord_1" />, { wrapper: createTestProviders() })

  expect(await screen.findByRole('alert')).toHaveTextContent(/we could not load this order/i)
})
```

**Async component testing: the four things you must get right.**

1. **Use `findBy*` (or `await waitFor`) for anything that arrives asynchronously.** `getBy*` on async content throws immediately.
2. **Use `userEvent`, not `fireEvent`, for user interactions.** `userEvent` dispatches the full event sequence (`pointerdown`, `mousedown`, `focus`, `input`, `keyup`) and honours `disabled`, `readonly`, and pointer-events. `fireEvent.change` skips half of it and will pass when the real interaction fails.
3. **Wrap stateful components in a real provider** (MSW server, `QueryClientProvider` with `retry: false`, a memory router). Do not mock the data layer.
4. **Assert the accessible outcome, then assert the absence of the failure state.** `expect(screen.queryByRole('alert')).not.toBeInTheDocument()` after a successful submit catches the "double error banner" class of bug.

**When to use component tests vs not.**

Use them when: the component has branching behaviour worth enumerating (form validation, permission-gated rendering, error and empty states, disabled/enabled logic, keyboard interaction). Use them **sparingly** for pure layout (that is visual regression's job) and **never** for "does the router work" (that is E2E's job).

**Trade-offs.**

- **Component tests are slower and flakier than unit tests** but far faster than E2E; the sweet spot is 5-20 assertions per behaviour cluster, not one per line.
- **Accessibility-first queries double as accessibility tests.** A component you cannot query by role is usually a component a screen-reader user cannot use -- the test suite becomes an a11y smoke test for free.
- **Over-abstraction for testability** (prop-drilling `formatMoney` everywhere, splitting every component so it can be rendered alone) can damage the design. The `buildOrderSummaryView` pattern works because the *view model* is a legitimate domain object, not because everything became a prop.
- **Snapshot testing components** is fast to write and near-worthless to maintain. Resist.

**Production considerations.**

- Configure `testEnvironmentOptions: { url: 'http://localhost' }` so relative URLs and `window.location` behave.
- Set `userEvent.setup({ delay: null })` for speed when you do not need realistic typing delays; use a real delay only when testing debounce behaviour (with fake timers).
- Wrap every `render` in your app's providers via a custom `renderWithProviders` so tests cannot accidentally run without them:
  ```tsx
  export function renderWithProviders(ui: ReactElement, { route = '/' } = {}) {
    const user = userEvent.setup()
    return {
      user,
      ...render(ui, {
        wrapper: ({ children }) => (
          <QueryClientProvider client={new QueryClient({ defaultOptions: { queries: { retry: false } } })}>
            <MemoryRouter initialEntries={[route]}>{children}</MemoryRouter>
          </QueryClientProvider>
        ),
      }),
    }
  }
  ```
- Add `jest-axe`/`vitest-axe` for automated a11y assertions in the component tier (see 9.4.2).
- Enforce a lint rule banning `container.querySelector` and `getByTestId` in spec files unless the line has an explicit `// testing-implementation-detail` comment.

**Common mistakes.**

1. `getByTestId` everywhere, producing a suite that survives a completely broken UI (a `<div>` with a test id passes).
2. `fireEvent.click(button)` on an element covered by an overlay, or on a `pointer-events: none` element -- it dispatches where a real user cannot click.
3. Asserting on state: `expect(result.current.isOpen).toBe(true)` instead of `expect(screen.getByRole('dialog')).toBeVisible()`.
4. Not awaiting: `render(<Async />); expect(await screen.findByText('x'))` mixed with `screen.getByText('x')` two lines later.
5. Mocking your own hooks so the test only exercises the JSX. The component becomes a "render assert" with no logic coverage.
6. Duplicating a bug in both the component and its test copy-pasted from a previous test.
7. Using `waitFor` with a long default timeout to paper over a race; the right fix is `findBy*` with the right query, not a 5s wait.
8. Forgetting `cleanup` (RTL auto-cleans when `globals: true`; if you set `globals: false` you must call it in `afterEach`).

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you query a button in a React test?"* -- `screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Submit' })`, which asserts both the role and the accessible name. That is what a screen reader user perceives.
- 🟡 *"`userEvent` vs `fireEvent`?"* -- `userEvent` simulates the full interaction sequence a browser produces (pointer, focus, keyboard, input) and respects `disabled`/`pointer-events`, so it reproduces real failures. `fireEvent` dispatches a single synthetic event, so it can pass where the real user would fail. Use `fireEvent` only for events users cannot produce (e.g. `scroll`, `resize`).
- 🔴 *"Your component is 400 lines of JSX with `useState` and three hooks. How do you test it?"* -- Do not mock the hooks. Extract the decision logic into a pure view-model function and table-test it exhaustively; leave the component test with 3-5 assertions about roles, names and visible text; put the layout in visual regression.
- 🔴 *"When is a `data-testid` justified?"* -- When there is no user-visible or accessible handle at all: a canvas, a WebGL region, a custom drag handle with no role, or a third-party widget you cannot modify. Then give it a semantic name (`data-testid="order-map"`, not `data-testid="div7"`) and add a comment.
- ⚫ *"How do you test a component that uses a router?"* -- Render inside `MemoryRouter` with `initialEntries`, do not mock `react-router`. Then use E2E for real URL transitions, because `MemoryRouter` cannot catch a misconfigured server rewrite or a broken `base` path.

---

### 9.1.12 Hook testing and testing custom hooks

**Definition.**

A **custom hook** is a function whose name starts with `use` that composes React primitives. Testing a custom hook means testing a *unit of behaviour that has no markup*: state transitions, effects, memoisation, subscriptions, and the contract with whatever the hook manages. Because a hook cannot be called outside a component, hook tests need a host component -- either a probe component you render, or `renderHook` from `@testing-library/react`.

**Mechanics -- the three ways, and when each is correct.**

```ts
// 1. `renderHook` (React Testing Library >= 13.1). The idiomatic choice.
import { renderHook, act, waitFor } from '@testing-library/react'
import { useDebouncedValue } from './useDebouncedValue'

it('debounces the value by the given delay', async () => {
  vi.useFakeTimers()
  const { result, rerender } = renderHook(
    ({ value, delay }) => useDebouncedValue(value, delay),
    { initialProps: { value: 'a', delay: 300 } },
  )

  expect(result.current).toBe('a')                      // initial value is immediate

  rerender({ value: 'ab', delay: 300 })
  rerender({ value: 'abc', delay: 300 })
  expect(result.current).toBe('a')                      // still the old value: debounce works

  await act(async () => { await vi.advanceTimersByTimeAsync(300) })
  expect(result.current).toBe('abc')                    // settled

  vi.useRealTimers()
})

// 2. A probe component. Use when the hook's effect touches the DOM, or when you want
//    to assert on rendered output as well as hook state.
function Probe({ delay, onValue }: { delay: number; onValue: (v: string) => void }) {
  const v = useDebouncedValue('typed', delay)
  onValue(v)
  return <span data-testid="out">{v}</span>
}
it('updates the rendered output after the debounce', async () => { /* render <Probe/>, waitFor */ })

// 3. `renderHook` with a wrapper, to supply providers.
const { result } = renderHook(() => useUser('usr_1'), { wrapper: createTestProviders() })
```

**Mechanics -- `act`, and the single most common hook-test bug.**

React batches state updates; asserting on `result.current` immediately after an update that happened inside an effect or an event handler reads a stale value. The fix is `act`:

```ts
// ❌ Stale read: the effect's setState has not been flushed when you assert.
const { result } = renderHook(() => useOnlineStatus())
expect(result.current).toBe(true)          // usually passes by luck; fails when the effect is async

// ✅
const { result } = renderHook(() => useOnlineStatus())
await waitFor(() => expect(result.current).toBe(true))
```

Rules of thumb:
- Wrap state mutations you perform yourself in `act`: `act(() => result.current.setOpen(true))`, `act(() => window.dispatchEvent(new Event('online')))`.
- Never wrap `render` in `act` -- RTL already does.
- `waitFor` beats `act(async () => { await sleep(50) })`; prefer `findBy*` for DOM and `waitFor` for hook state.
- "An update to X inside a test was not wrapped in `act(...)`" usually means something outside React (a timer, a promise, an event listener) is calling a setter. Either the code should be restructured, or the test should `act` around the trigger.

**The subscribe/cleanup contract -- the highest-value hook test.**

Every hook that subscribes must unsubscribe. This is the test that catches real memory leaks in long-lived SPAs:

```ts
it('unsubscribes from the socket on unmount', () => {
  const handlers: Array<(payload: unknown) => void> = []
  const on = vi.fn((_event: string, cb: (p: unknown) => void) => { handlers.push(cb) })
  const off = vi.fn()
  vi.mock('./socket', () => ({ socket: { on, off } }))

  const { unmount } = renderHook(() => useLivePrice('BTC-GBP'))

  expect(on).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(1)
  expect(off).not.toHaveBeenCalled()

  unmount()
  expect(off).toHaveBeenCalledWith('price:BTC-GBP', expect.any(Function))

  // And the handler really is dead: pushing a payload must not call setState (React would warn).
  handlers[0]!({ price: 1 })
  expect(off).toHaveBeenCalled()      // sanity: the callback was removed, not merely called once
})
```

And the effect-cleanup / cancellation test:

```ts
it('cancels an in-flight request when the query key changes', async () => {
  const signals: AbortSignal[] = []
  vi.mock('./api', () => ({
    fetchUser: vi.fn((_id: string, signal: AbortSignal) => {
      signals.push(signal)
      return new Promise(() => {})     // never resolves: proves the abort path is taken
    }),
  }))

  const { rerender } = renderHook(({ id }) => useUser(id), { initialProps: { id: 'a' } })
  rerender({ id: 'b' })

  expect(signals[0]!.aborted).toBe(true)
  expect(signals[1]!.aborted).toBe(false)
})
```

**State-machine hooks deserve table-driven tests over their transition table.**

```ts
type State =
  | { status: 'idle' }
  | { status: 'loading' }
  | { status: 'ready'; data: User }
  | { status: 'error'; code: 'NOT_FOUND' | 'UNAVAILABLE' }

it('satisfies the async state machine contract', async () => {
  // Arrange: a fake data source we can drive
  const source = new ControllableUserSource()
  // Act + Assert: the invariants that must hold in every reachable state
  for (const script of [
    { emits: [], final: 'idle' },
    { emits: ['load'], final: 'ready' },
    { emits: ['load', 'load'], final: 'ready' },            // no double-fetch
    { emits: ['load', 'notFound'], final: 'error/NOT_FOUND' },
    { emits: ['load', 'failure', 'retry', 'load'], final: 'ready' },
  ] as const) {
    const { result } = renderHook(() => useRemoteUser(source))
    for (const e of script.emits) await act(() => source.emit(e))
    expect(`${result.current.status}/${'code' in result.current ? result.current.code : ''}`)
      .toBe(script.final.replace('idle', 'idle/').replace('ready', 'ready/').replace('error/', 'error/'))
  }
})
```

**The "logic in a hook" anti-pattern and its fix.** When a hook contains more than ~30 lines of branching logic, the logic is not a React concern and is nearly untestable in isolation. Extract it:

```ts
// ❌ 60 lines of useState + useMemo + branching inside a hook: no way to test the branching
//    without rendering, so the team mocks the hook instead and the logic is untested.

// ✅ A pure reducer, exhaustively table-tested, plus a thin hook wrapper.
export type State = { status: 'idle' | 'saving' | 'saved' | 'error'; error?: string }
export type Action =
  | { type: 'EDIT'; field: string; value: string }
  | { type: 'SUBMIT' }
  | { type: 'RESOLVE'; ok: boolean; error?: string }

export function profileReducer(state: State, action: Action): State {
  switch (action.type) {
    case 'EDIT': return { status: 'idle' }
    case 'SUBMIT': return state.status === 'saving' ? state : { status: 'saving' }
    case 'RESOLVE': return action.ok ? { status: 'saved' } : { status: 'error', error: action.error }
    default: return state
  }
}

export function useProfileForm(initial: Profile) {
  const [state, dispatch] = useReducer(profileReducer, { status: 'idle' })
  const submit = useCallback(async () => {
    dispatch({ type: 'SUBMIT' })
    const res = await saveProfile(initial)
    dispatch({ type: 'RESOLVE', ok: res.ok, error: res.error })
  }, [initial])
  return { state, submit, dirty: state.status === 'idle' }
}

it('ignores a duplicate submit while saving', () => {
  let s = profileReducer({ status: 'idle' }, { type: 'SUBMIT' })
  s = profileReducer(s, { type: 'SUBMIT' })
  s = profileReducer(s, { type: 'RESOLVE', ok: true })
  expect(s).toEqual({ status: 'saved' })     // one save, not two
})
```

**When to use hook tests vs not.**

Test a hook directly when: it owns a non-trivial state machine, it has cleanup/abort/retry semantics, it is used by three or more components (so its contract matters), or it encapsulates a bug-prone interaction with an external system (WebSocket, IndexedDB, IntersectionObserver, ResizeObserver).

Do not test a hook that is a one-line `useState` wrapper, or one that only composes other hooks you are already testing. Do not re-test the library under it (`useEffect`'s behaviour, `useMemo`'s memoisation).

**Trade-offs.**

- **`renderHook` tests are close to unit tests but not quite**: they need a DOM (or `react-test-renderer`/a no-DOM renderer), they are subject to React's concurrent rendering rules, and they cannot be run in a pure node environment. This is why extracting logic into pure functions is strictly better where you can.
- **Hook tests couple to the hook's API**, so a signature change breaks them -- but that is a *good* coupling, because the API *is* the contract.
- **Effect timing is a source of frustration**: `useEffect` does not run during render, and in StrictMode it runs twice in development. Test with `renderHook` in the default (non-StrictMode) test entry point, and enable StrictMode selectively in one test to assert idempotent mount/unmount.
- **`renderHook` is React-only.** Svelte, Vue and Solid need different harnesses (`@testing-library/svelte`, `mount` from `@vue/test-utils`, `createRoot` from `solid-js/web`); the *philosophy* transfers, the API does not.

**Production considerations.**

- Standardise on `renderHook` and export a `renderHookWithProviders` from your test utilities, so every hook test gets the real providers.
- Enforce the "logic out of the hook" rule by convention and review: hooks over 40 lines of branching get a `*.reducer.ts` beside them.
- Add a lint rule or CI check for `useEffect` bodies longer than N lines, or a `/* eslint-disable */`-free heuristic; the real enforcement is code review.
- Test cleanup for every hook that subscribes; memory leaks in a single-page app manifest as a slow, mysterious memory growth over a long session, and this test is the cheapest possible detector.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Asserting `result.current` immediately after an effect-driven update, without `act` or `waitFor`.
2. Wrapping `render` in `act` ("the act(...) was passed to render" warning) -- RTL already acts.
3. Not testing unmount cleanup, so a `setInterval` keeps running in production.
4. Testing `useMemo`'s memoisation ("the factory was called once") -- that is testing React, not your code.
5. Mocking `useState` or `useReducer` to assert calls -- you are testing your own test.
6. Writing `await act(async () => { await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 100)) })` instead of `waitFor`, which is slower and can still be wrong.
7. Forgetting that in StrictMode, effects run twice on mount, so a test that counts effect invocations must either not use StrictMode or account for 2x.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you test a custom hook?"* -- `renderHook` from React Testing Library, assert on `result.current`, wrap your own state mutations in `act`, and use `waitFor`/`findBy*` for anything asynchronous.
- 🟡 *"Why does my hook test see a stale value?"* -- The state update happened in an effect or event handler and React has not re-rendered/committed yet. Wrap the trigger in `act`, or use `waitFor` to let React flush.
- 🔴 *"How do you test that a hook cleans up properly?"* -- Render, capture the subscribe callback, unmount, then assert the unsubscribe was called with the same function reference, and (stronger) invoke the captured callback afterwards and assert no state update warning occurs. For fetch, assert the `AbortSignal` was aborted.
- 🔴 *"Your hook has 60 lines of branching logic and the team wants to mock it in every test. What do you do?"* -- Extract the logic into a pure reducer and table-test every transition; keep the hook a 10-line wrapper. Then the component tests use the real hook and can catch wiring bugs, because the logic is already covered in isolation.
- ⚫ *"Does StrictMode break hook tests?"* -- It double-invokes render and effects in development to surface impurity, so effect-invocation counts double. Keep the default test entry point non-StrictMode for count-based assertions, and add one dedicated StrictMode test per important hook to assert mount/unmount idempotency.

---

## 9.2 Integration Testing

### 9.2.1 What integration means and where the boundary sits

**Definition.**

An **integration test** verifies that two or more components work together correctly, including their *real* boundaries: a real database, a real HTTP server, a real message queue, a real cache. It differs from a unit test by not substituting the things on the seam, and from an end-to-end test by not crossing the *user-facing* boundary (the browser, the deployed system, third-party services).

**The boundary rule, stated precisely.** Integration tests start where the substitution stops. Concretely, for a typical web app the seams are:

```
[ browser ]  --E2E boundary-->  [ your server process ]  --integration boundary-->  [ Postgres, Redis, S3 (emulated), queue ]
                                                                        |
                                                              [ third-party APIs via MSW ]
```

- **Unit tier:** your server process, with `OrderRepo`, `EmailSender`, `Clock` substituted.
- **Integration tier:** your server process plus a *real* Postgres, *real* Redis, a *real* HTTP server bound to an ephemeral port, and MSW intercepting the outbound third-party calls. Requests are made over TCP with Supertest/undici, not by calling handlers directly.
- **E2E tier:** the browser talking to a real deployed-ish server, which talks to real infrastructure.

**The "one real thing" heuristic.** An integration test needs exactly one (or a very small number of) *real* dependency of each kind, and substitutes everything else. The most valuable integration tests are the ones where a real dependency catches a class of bug that is invisible otherwise:

| Real dependency | Bug class only it can catch |
| --- | --- |
| **SQL database** | constraint violations, transaction semantics, isolation levels, `NULL` semantics, index-driven query plans, migration drift, ORM escape hatches |
| **Redis** | TTL behaviour, atomicity of Lua scripts, cache stampede, serialisation format compatibility, eviction |
| **Message queue** | at-least-once delivery, consumer idempotency, ack/nack timeouts, poison messages, ordering |
| **Real HTTP server** | middleware ordering, body parsing, CORS, cookie flags, header casing, content negotiation, 100-continue, graceful shutdown |
| **Real filesystem (tmpfs)** | atomic rename, file locking, encoding assumptions, path traversal |
| **Real clock (or fake with a real scheduler)** | TTL boundaries, race conditions, ordering |
| **MSW in front of third parties** | that your code actually speaks the third party's protocol correctly (shape, auth header, retry semantics) |

**Mechanics -- the three properties every integration suite must have.**

1. **Isolation.** Each test starts from a known database state and leaves no residue that affects another test. Options: transaction-per-test with rollback (fastest, 9.2.4), truncate-and-reseed per test (simple, order-independent, slower), or a fresh schema/database per worker (parallel-safe, needs a template database or container).
2. **Realism of the code path.** Do not import a handler and call it. Start the server, make an HTTP request. Every layer above the database must be the production layer: the real router, the real middleware chain, the real DI container.
3. **Speed.** A suite that takes 20 minutes will be skipped. Target 30-120 seconds for the whole integration tier by using an ephemeral in-process database, connection pooling, and parallelism across workers.

**Runnable code -- the shape of an integration test versus a unit test of the same feature.**

```ts
// ❌ This is a unit test with a database. It tests the handler, not the integration.
import { createOrder } from './orderHandlers'
it('creates an order', async () => {
  const order = await createOrder({ prisma, id: genId(), body: validBody })
  expect(order.status).toBe('draft')
})

// ✅ This is an integration test: real HTTP, real router, real middleware, real DB.
it('POST /api/orders creates a draft order and returns 201 with a Location header', async () => {
  const res = await request(app).post('/api/orders').set(authHeader()).send(validBody).expect(201)

  expect(res.body.status).toBe('draft')
  expect(res.headers.location).toBe(`/api/orders/${res.body.id}`)

  // The behaviour lives in the *system of record*, not in the response:
  const row = await prisma.order.findUniqueOrThrow({ where: { id: res.body.id } })
  expect(row.status).toBe('draft')
  expect(row.lines).toHaveLength(1)
})
```

**Where the boundary sits -- four judgement calls, with the reasoning.**

1. **Do you use a real database or a fake repository?** Real. An in-memory fake repo cannot catch a missing `NOT NULL`, an index, a transaction boundary, or a `timestamptz` timezone bug, and those are the bugs that reach production. The cost (startup time) is paid once with Testcontainers (9.2.5) or PGlite (an in-process WASM Postgres, which is not a fake -- it is the real engine).
2. **Do you mock third-party APIs?** Yes, at the *network* boundary with MSW (9.2.6), because a real third-party call in CI is slow, flaky, billable, and (for sign-up flows) ethically fraught. But mock at HTTP, not at your own adapter's method, so your adapter's serialisation is still under test.
3. **Do you use the production DI container?** Yes, with a *test* composition root that swaps only adapters you are deliberately substituting. If your test builds the object graph by hand, the test will not catch a missing binding in `main.ts` -- which is a real and common bug.
4. **Do you test HTTP handlers through Supertest or by calling the framework?** Through Supertest, over a real listening socket. Calling `app.handle(req, res)` skips the HTTP parser, and HTTP parsing is a real source of bugs (chunked bodies, duplicate headers, `Transfer-Encoding`).

**When to use integration tests vs not.**

Use them for: repositories and queries, transactions and rollback, migrations, auth/session middleware, API route contracts (status codes, headers, error shapes), event publish/consume, caching behaviour, rate limiting, and anything where a bug is expensive and unit tests cannot see it.

Do not use them for: pure business rules (unit + property tests are faster and better), UI rendering (component/visual tests), and third-party behaviour (you can only mock it; if you need to know, use a sandbox account against the real sandbox and run that suite on a schedule, not per-PR).

**Trade-offs.**

- **Cost.** Integration tests are 10-100x slower per test than unit tests. The whole pyramid argument is that you need *few* of them.
- **Confidence.** An integration test that passes with a real database and a real HTTP stack is close to the highest confidence available without a browser. This is where the majority of production-defects-per-dollar live.
- **Flakiness risk.** Real infrastructure means real timing, real resource contention, real port conflicts, real cleanup bugs. The mitigation is relentless standardisation: one harness, one lifecycle, no bespoke setup in individual tests.
- **Speed of authoring.** You must build a harness (container startup, seeding, request helpers, auth tokens) before you can write a single test. Budget a day, and amortise it.
- **Debugging.** A failing integration test tells you *that* the system is broken, not *where*. Add structured logging or a `DEBUG=1` mode that prints the SQL, headers, and body of the failing request.

**Production considerations.**

- Run the integration tier in every PR, but on a schedule *and* on demand for main. If it takes more than ~5 minutes, split it: a "fast" integration set (in-process Postgres via PGlite or a pre-warmed container) for every PR, and a "full" set (Testcontainers with real Postgres version, Redis, a real queue) nightly and pre-release.
- Use the **same database engine and major version** as production. Testing against SQLite and shipping Postgres is one of the great integration-testing illusions.
- Apply **real migrations**, not `db push`/`synchronize`. `prisma migrate deploy` in the test harness is the only way to catch a broken migration.
- Assert on the *side effects in the system of record* (database rows, published events, cache entries), not just on the HTTP response. A test that only checks the response body will pass while the row was never written.
- Pin container images by digest in CI for reproducibility, and by tag locally for convenience.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Calling handlers directly instead of over HTTP, so middleware, CORS, and body parsing are untested.
2. Using `sqlite`/`in-memory` in tests and Postgres in production.
3. Using `prisma db push` instead of `migrate deploy`, so migrations are never tested until deploy day.
4. Asserting only the response body, not the persisted state.
5. Building the object graph by hand in each test file instead of using one harness, so a missing production binding goes unnoticed.
6. Sharing one database across parallel workers with no namespacing, causing a 5% flake rate that gets "fixed" with retries.
7. A single `beforeAll` that seeds once and no per-test cleanup, so tests pass alone and fail together.
8. Using `expect(res.status).toBe(200)` only, with no assertion on the error-shape for the 4xx paths -- the most common place production APIs break their consumers.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is an integration test?"* -- A test that exercises two or more real components together, crossing at least one real boundary (usually a database or an HTTP server), to verify they interoperate correctly.
- 🟡 *"Unit test or integration test for a repository's `findByUserWithOrders` query?"* -- Integration. The risk is SQL semantics -- joins, nullability, ordering, pagination, indexes -- and a mocked repo cannot see any of it. A unit test with a fake array proves nothing about the query.
- 🔴 *"Where do you draw the line between mocking a service and mocking a network call?"* -- Mock the network, not your own code. If you mock `PaymentGateway.charge`, your adapter's URL, headers, payload shape, and error mapping are untested. MSW at the HTTP boundary tests all of that, and keeps the seam where your system actually has one.
- 🔴 *"How do you keep integration tests fast enough to run on every PR?"* -- In-process Postgres (PGlite) or a pre-warmed container reused across workers, transaction-per-test rollback, parallel workers with per-worker schema names, a minimal migration set, and no external network calls. If you cannot get under ~3 minutes, split into fast-PR and full-nightly sets.
- ⚫ *"How do you debug a failing integration test?"* -- Reproduce it alone (`--repeat-each 10 --shuffle`) to separate flakiness from a real failure; print the request/response pair and the SQL for the failing test; check the container logs; and assert on system-of-record state so you can inspect the data after the failure with a SQL client. Build a `--keep-db` debug flag into the harness.

---

### 9.2.2 Supertest and testing HTTP endpoints end to end

**Definition.**

**Supertest** is a small library that starts (or attaches to) an HTTP server, issues a real HTTP request, and gives you a fluent assertion API on status, headers, and body. It is the standard way to integration-test an Express/Fastify/Koa/Hono app. In a TypeScript codebase, the modern equivalent is often **`undici`'s `request`** plus **`light-my-request`**, or `@hono/testing` for Hono, or the framework's own test client (Fastify's `app.inject`, Nest's `supertest`).

**Mechanics -- the harness that makes Supertest pleasant.** The three things that matter: (1) build the app exactly once per suite, (2) have a real auth helper, (3) have typed response bodies.

```ts
// test/integration/helpers/app.ts
import { buildApp } from '../../src/app'          // returns { app, container }
import { createContainer, type Container } from '../../src/container'

export interface TestHarness {
  app: ReturnType<typeof buildApp>
  container: Container
  prisma: Container['prisma']
  request: (path?: string) => import('supertest').Test
  api: (path?: string) => import('supertest').Test   // alias, for readability
  auth: (over?: { userId?: string; role?: string; scopes?: string[] }) => { Authorization: string }
  close: () => Promise<void>
}

export async function createHarness(): Promise<TestHarness> {
  const container = await createContainer({ env: { NODE_ENV: 'test' } })
  const { app } = buildApp(container)

  const base = supertest(app)
  const wrap = (path = '') => base(path)

  // Real tokens, signed by the test key. No auth mocking: the middleware is under test.
  const auth = (over: { userId?: string; role?: string; scopes?: string[] } = {}) => {
    const token = container.tokens.sign({
      sub: over.userId ?? 'usr_00000001',
      role: over.role ?? 'member',
      scopes: over.scopes ?? ['orders:read', 'orders:write'],
      exp: Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000) + 3600,
    })
    return { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` }
  }

  return {
    app, container, prisma: container.prisma,
    request: wrap, api: wrap, auth,
    close: async () => { await container.close() },
  }
}
```

**The full test surface, not just the happy path.** A production-grade endpoint suite covers: happy path, every documented error code, authorisation boundaries, and the *representation* (headers, status, content type).

```ts
// test/integration/orders.api.test.ts
import { describe, it, expect, beforeAll, afterAll, beforeEach } from 'vitest'
import supertest from 'supertest'
import { createHarness, type TestHarness } from '../helpers/app'
import { orderFactory } from '../../test-support/factories'

let h: TestHarness

beforeAll(async () => { h = await createHarness() })
afterAll(async () => { await h.close() })
beforeEach(async () => { await h.prisma.order.deleteMany() })   // or rely on rollback

const validBody = () => ({
  lines: [{ sku: 'SKU-1', qty: 2, unitPriceCents: 1250 }],
  currency: 'GBP',
})

describe('POST /api/orders', () => {
  it('creates a draft order, persists it, and returns 201 with Location', async () => {
    // Arrange
    const body = validBody()

    // Act
    const res = await h.api('/api/orders').post(h.auth()).send(body)

    // Assert
    expect(res.status).toBe(201)
    expect(res.headers['content-type']).toMatch(/application\/json/)
    expect(res.headers.location).toBe(`/api/orders/${res.body.id}`)
    expect(res.body).toMatchObject({ status: 'draft', currency: 'GBP', subtotalCents: 2500 })

    // System of record -- the assertion that actually matters
    const row = await h.prisma.order.findUniqueOrThrow({ where: { id: res.body.id } })
    expect(row.status).toBe('draft')
    expect(row.lines).toHaveLength(1)
    expect(row.userId).toBe('usr_00000001')
  })

  it('returns 400 with a field-level error object for an invalid body', async () => {
    const res = await h.api('/api/orders').post(h.auth()).send({ lines: [{ sku: '', qty: -1 }] })
```
