```ts
    expect(res.status).toBe(400)
    expect(res.body).toEqual({
      error: {
        code: 'VALIDATION_FAILED',
        message: 'Request body failed validation',
        fields: [
          { path: 'lines.0.sku', message: 'must not be empty' },
          { path: 'lines.0.qty', message: 'must be a positive integer' },
        ],
        requestId: expect.any(String),
      },
    })
  })

  it('returns 401 without a token and 403 with an insufficient scope', async () => {
    const anonymous = await h.api('/api/orders').post().send(validBody())
    expect(anonymous.status).toBe(401)
    expect(anonymous.body.error.code).toBe('UNAUTHENTICATED')

    const wrongScope = await h.api('/api/orders').post(h.auth({ scopes: ['orders:read'] })).send(validBody())
    expect(wrongScope.status).toBe(403)
    expect(wrongScope.body.error.code).toBe('FORBIDDEN')
  })

  it('returns 409 on a duplicate idempotency key and does not create a second order', async () => {
    const key = 'idem_abc123'
    const first = await h.api('/api/orders').post(h.auth()).set('Idempotency-Key', key).send(validBody())
    expect(first.status).toBe(201)

    const second = await h.api('/api/orders').post(h.auth()).set('Idempotency-Key', key).send(validBody())
    expect(second.status).toBe(409)
    expect(second.body.error.code).toBe('IDEMPOTENCY_CONFLICT')

    expect(await h.prisma.order.count()).toBe(1)
  })

  it('returns 415 for an unsupported content type and 413 for an oversized body', async () => {
    const wrongType = await h.api('/api/orders')
      .post(h.auth())
      .set('Content-Type', 'text/plain')
      .send('not json')
    expect(wrongType.status).toBe(415)

    const huge = await h.api('/api/orders').post(h.auth()).send({ lines: Array.from({ length: 5000 }, () => validBody().lines[0]) })
    expect(huge.status).toBe(413)
  })

  it('adds a request id to the response and echoes it back on error', async () => {
    const res = await h.api('/api/orders').post(h.auth()).set('X-Request-Id', 'req_fixed_1').send({})
    expect(res.headers['x-request-id']).toBe('req_fixed_1')
  })
})

describe('GET /api/orders/:id', () => {
  it('supports cursor pagination deterministically and never duplicates or drops a row', async () => {
    // Arrange: 25 orders with deterministic createdAt values
    await h.prisma.order.createMany({
      data: Array.from({ length: 25 }, (_, i) => ({
        id: `ord_${String(i).padStart(3, '0')}`,
        userId: 'usr_00000001',
        status: 'draft',
        currency: 'GBP',
        createdAt: new Date(Date.UTC(2026, 0, 1, 0, i)),
      })),
    })

    // Act: walk the whole cursor chain
    const seen: string[] = []
    let cursor: string | undefined
    for (let page = 0; page < 10; page++) {
      const q = cursor ? `?limit=7&cursor=${encodeURIComponent(cursor)}` : '?limit=7'
      const res: { body: { items: { id: string }[]; nextCursor: string | null } } =
        await h.api('/api/orders').get(q).set(h.auth())
      expect(res.status).toBe(200)
      seen.push(...res.body.items.map((i) => i.id))
      cursor = res.body.nextCursor ?? undefined
      if (!cursor) break
    }

    // Assert
    expect(seen).toHaveLength(25)
    expect(new Set(seen).size).toBe(25)
    expect(seen).toEqual([...seen].sort())       // stable ordering
  })

  it('returns 404 for an unknown id and never leaks whether it exists to another user', async () => {
    const missing = await h.api('/api/orders/ord_does_not_exist').get(h.auth())
    expect(missing.status).toBe(404)

    const someoneElses = await h.api('/api/orders/ord_of_other_user').get(h.auth())
    expect(someoneElses.status).toBe(404)     // 404, not 403: do not confirm existence
  })
})
```

**Fastify's `inject` and Hono's `testClient` -- when they are better than Supertest.** They do not open a socket (faster, no port conflicts) but they *do* run the full router, hooks, and serialiser pipeline.

```ts
// Fastify
const res = await app.inject({ method: 'POST', url: '/api/orders', payload: validBody(), headers: h.auth() })
expect(res.statusCode).toBe(201)
expect(res.json()).toMatchObject({ status: 'draft' })

// Hono
const res = await app.request('/api/orders', { method: 'POST', body: JSON.stringify(validBody()), headers })
expect(res.status).toBe(201)
```

The trade-off: `inject` bypasses the Node HTTP layer, so it will not catch a real socket-level problem (a header you can only set via a real client, `Transfer-Encoding` handling, keep-alive). For most apps that is an acceptable trade for a 2x speedup; keep one Supertest suite as the "over the wire" proof.

**When to use Supertest vs not.**

Use it for: every route's happy path, every documented error, authorisation boundaries, content negotiation, idempotency, pagination, rate limiting, and header contracts. Use it as the *definition* of your API's behaviour, because it is executable documentation that cannot go stale.

Do not use it for: business rules (unit test them), and do not use it to assert on SQL structure (assert on the returned data and the persisted row, not on the query text).

**Trade-offs.**

- **Speed.** Each `supertest(app)` call starts an ephemeral server per request. For large suites, create one server and reuse it (Supertest handles this internally when you pass the same `app`).
- **Header/cookie fidelity.** Supertest uses a real `http.ClientRequest`, so cookies, `Set-Cookie`, and `Authorization` behave as in production -- a genuine advantage over `inject`.
- **Async leaks.** If your app has a `setInterval` or an open DB pool, the test process will not exit. You must call `h.close()`. A suite that "hangs after tests pass" is the most common Supertest complaint.
- **No browser semantics.** No cookies-with-`SameSite` enforcement, no CORS preflight unless you ask for `OPTIONS`, no service worker. Those are E2E or explicit-header tests.

**Production considerations.**

- One harness module, imported by every integration spec. Never build the app inside a test.
- Use **real** auth (sign a real token with the test secret) rather than a bypass header like `x-test-user`; the bypass header is a permanent backdoor in production and is the single most dangerous thing in this list.
- Assert on the **error envelope** for every 4xx/5xx, not just the status code. The error shape is a contract your clients depend on.
- Add a `--keep-db` mode and print the failing request/response to make triage possible.
- In CI, run the integration suite with `maxWorkers` tuned to the number of DB connections, not to the number of CPU cores, or you will exhaust the pool and generate fake flakes.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Forgetting `await` on the Supertest promise (Supertest is thenable, so this mostly works, but `expect(app.get(...).expect(200))` does not).
2. Not closing the app, so the process hangs and CI times out with no failure message.
3. Mocking the auth middleware, so the entire auth path is untested and a broken middleware passes CI.
4. Asserting only the status code, and shipping an error shape that breaks every client.
5. Using a `x-test-user` bypass header.
6. Not seeding the user the token refers to, so the test fails on a foreign key in a way that looks like an application bug.
7. Sharing a `beforeAll` database across parallel workers.
8. `expect(res.body).toEqual({...})` on an endpoint that returns a timestamp, so the test fails at midnight and nobody knows why.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you test an Express endpoint?"* -- With Supertest against the real app: `request(app).post('/x').set('Authorization', ...).send(body).expect(201)`, then assert on the response and on the persisted row. Never call the handler directly.
- 🟡 *"Why real tokens instead of mocking the auth middleware?"* -- Because the middleware is where authentication bugs live (expiry, signature, scope, algorithm confusion). Mocking it means the test passes while production rejects every request, or worse, accepts requests it should reject.
- 🔴 *"Your integration suite is 12 minutes long. How do you get it under 3?"* -- In-process Postgres or a reused container, transaction-per-test rollback instead of re-migrating, parallel workers sized to the connection pool, drop redundant `beforeAll` seeding, and split into a fast PR set and a full nightly set.
- 🔴 *"Supertest or `app.inject`?"* -- `inject` is faster and avoids ports but bypasses the Node HTTP layer; Supertest crosses a real socket and therefore tests cookies, `Set-Cookie`, keep-alive and header fidelity. Default to Supertest for contract fidelity, and use `inject` for high-volume smoke tests.
- ⚫ *"The suite hangs after all tests pass. Where do you look?"* -- Open handles: the DB pool, a `setInterval` from a job scheduler, an unclosed Redis client, or a `--experimental-vm-modules` worker. Add `afterAll(close)`, and Vitest's `pool: 'forks'` plus `teardownTimeout` will surface it.

---

### 9.2.3 Database seeding, fixtures, and test isolation

**Definition.**

**Seeding** is the process of putting a known, valid state into a database before a test. **Fixtures** are the data definitions; **seeding** is the act of loading them. **Test isolation** is the guarantee that each test starts from a known state and cannot be affected by another test. These three are inseparable in practice: isolation is a *strategy* (transaction rollback, truncate, template database, per-worker schema) and seeding is *what runs under that strategy*.

**Mechanics -- the four isolation strategies, with costs.**

| Strategy | Speed | Parallelism | Realism | Use when |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Transaction per test, rollback after** | fastest (sub-10ms) | safe (one transaction per test, one connection) | high (real transactions, real isolation) | the default for Postgres |
| **Truncate + reseed per test** | fast (50-200ms for small tables) | safe if per-worker database | high | legacy suites, when a transaction cannot be used (async callbacks, background jobs) |
| **Template database + CREATE DATABASE per test** | slow (100-500ms) | safe | highest (fresh DB each time) | tests that change schema or roles |
| **Fresh container per test** | very slow (2-10s) | limited | highest | rarely; only for "does it work on a virgin database" |

**Why transaction-per-test usually works and when it does not.** A transaction per test gives real transactional semantics, real locks, real isolation, and instant cleanup (`ROLLBACK` instead of `DELETE`). It does **not** work when:
- your code under test **commits** inside the test (a `prisma.$transaction` nested call, a separate connection, a `COMMIT` in raw SQL) -- then the test's rollback cannot undo it;
- your code uses **two connections** concurrently (a queue worker polling while the request runs) -- the worker's changes are not in your transaction;
- you need to test **`READ COMMITTED` vs `SERIALIZABLE`** behaviour with two actors;
- the code uses **`LISTEN/NOTIFY`, advisory locks, or advisory-based coordination** across connections.

**Runnable code -- the transaction-per-test harness with Prisma, with the escape hatches for the cases above.**

```ts
// test/integration/helpers/db.ts
import { PrismaClient } from '@prisma/client'
import type { Container } from '../../../src/container'

const TEST_DATABASE_URL = process.env.TEST_DATABASE_URL!
const MIGRATE_LOCK = new Map<string, Promise<void>>()

let prismaSingleton: PrismaClient | undefined

export function getPrisma(): PrismaClient {
  prismaSingleton ??= new PrismaClient({
    datasources: { db: { url: TEST_DATABASE_URL } },
    log: process.env.DEBUG_SQL ? [{ emit: 'event', level: 'query' }] : ['error'],
  })
  return prismaSingleton
}

/** Run migrations exactly once per process, before any test. */
export async function migrateOnce(): Promise<void> {
  let running = MIGRATE_LOCK.get(TEST_DATABASE_URL)
  if (!running) {
    running = (async () => {
      const { execSync } = await import('node:child_process')
      execSync(`npx prisma migrate deploy`, {
        env: { ...process.env, DATABASE_URL: TEST_DATABASE_URL },
        stdio: process.env.CI ? 'ignore' : 'inherit',
      })
    })()
    MIGRATE_LOCK.set(TEST_DATABASE_URL, running)
  }
  return running
}

/**
 * Transaction-per-test isolation.
 *
 * Contract:
 *  - `trx` is the ONLY Prisma client your code under test may use.
 *  - The container is built with `trx`, so a stray `new PrismaClient()` in production code
 *    is a detectable bug (see the assertion below) rather than a silent escape.
 *  - Everything rolls back on teardown, so tests may run in parallel against one database.
 */
export async function withRolledBackDb<T>(
  build: (trx: PrismaClient) => Promise<T>,
  opts: { useSecondConnection?: boolean } = {},
): Promise<T> {
  const prisma = getPrisma()
  await prisma.$transaction(
    async (trx) => {
      const result = await build(trx)
      // Optional: verify no out-of-band connection was used.
      if (!opts.useSecondConnection) {
        const [, inTx] = await prisma.$transaction(async (t) => {
          try {
            await t.$queryRaw`SELECT 1`
            return [0, true]
          } catch {
            return [1, false]
          }
        })
        void inTx
      }
      throw new ROLLBACK_SENTINEL
    },
    { timeout: 20_000, maxWait: 10_000, isolationLevel: 'ReadCommitted' },
  ).catch((e) => {
    if (e instanceof ROLLBACK_SENTINEL || (e as { code?: string }).code === 'P2028' || /rolled back/i.test(String(e))) {
      return undefined as never    // unreachable: we capture via closure below
    }
    throw e
  })
  throw new Error('unreachable')
}

const ROLLBACK_SENTINEL = Symbol('rollback')
```

The above is deliberately shown in its *awkward* raw form, because the honest, robust pattern needs a result channel. Here is the version you actually ship:

```ts
/** The shipping version: returns the value, always rolls back. */
export async function withRolledBackDb<T>(build: (trx: PrismaClient) => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
  const prisma = getPrisma()
  const box: { value?: T } = {}

  await prisma.$transaction(
    async (trx) => {
      box.value = await build(trx)
      throw new RollbackSignal()          // unwinds; Prisma issues ROLLBACK
    },
    { timeout: 30_000, maxWait: 10_000 },
  ).catch((e) => {
    if (e instanceof RollbackSignal) return
    if (isP2028(e)) throw new Error(`Test transaction timed out: ${e.message}`)
    throw e                              // a real failure inside the test -- surface it
  })

  return box.value as T
}

class RollbackSignal extends Error {
  constructor() { super('__ROLLBACK__'); this.name = 'RollbackSignal' }
}
const isP2028 = (e: unknown) => (e as { code?: string })?.code === 'P2028'
```

```ts
// The harness that ties it together, including the production-container guarantee.
import { beforeAll, afterAll, afterEach } from 'vitest'
import { createContainer } from '../../src/container'
import supertest from 'supertest'
import { buildApp } from '../../src/app'
import { getPrisma, migrateOnce, withRolledBackDb } from './db'

beforeAll(async () => { await migrateOnce() })
afterAll(async () => { await getPrisma().$disconnect() })

/**
 * Use inside each test. The container is rebuilt per test with the transaction client,
 * so no module-level singleton can leak data between tests.
 */
export function integrationTest(fn: (ctx: Ctx) => Promise<void>, opts: { noTransaction?: boolean } = {}) {
  it(fn.name ?? 'test', async () => {
    if (opts.noTransaction) {
      // escape hatch for tests that need commits / a second connection
      const prisma = getPrisma()
      await prisma.order.deleteMany()
      try {
        await fn(await makeCtx(prisma))
      } finally {
        await prisma.order.deleteMany()
      }
      return
    }
    await withRolledBackDb(async (trx) => {
      await fn(await makeCtx(trx))
    })
  }, 30_000)
}

async function makeCtx(prisma: PrismaClient) {
  const container = await createContainer({ env: { NODE_ENV: 'test' }, prismaOverride: prisma })
  const { app } = buildApp(container)
  const base = supertest(app)
  return {
    prisma,
    api: (p = '') => base(p),
    auth: (over: { sub?: string; role?: string; scopes?: string[] } = {}) => ({
      Authorization: `Bearer ${container.tokens.sign({
        sub: over.sub ?? 'usr_00000001',
        role: over.role ?? 'member',
        scopes: over.scopes ?? ['orders:read', 'orders:write'],
        exp: Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000) + 3600,
      })}`,
    }),
    seed: makeSeeder(prisma),
  }
}
```

**Seeders: minimal, relational, and derived from production types.**

```ts
// test/integration/helpers/seed.ts
import type { PrismaClient } from '@prisma/client'

/** Each seeder returns the ids it created, so tests wire relationships explicitly. */
export function makeSeeder(db: PrismaClient) {
  return {
    async user(over: Partial<{ id: string; email: string; role: string; createdAt: Date }> = {}) {
      const data = {
        id: over.id ?? 'usr_00000001',
        email: over.email ?? 'ada@example.test',
        role: over.role ?? 'member',
        createdAt: over.createdAt ?? new Date('2026-01-01T00:00:00Z'),
      }
      return db.user.create({ data })
    },
    async order(userId: string, over: Partial<{ id: string; status: string; lines: number }> = {}) {
      const id = over.id ?? 'ord_00000001'
      await db.orderLine.createMany({
        data: Array.from({ length: over.lines ?? 1 }, (_, i) => ({
          orderId: id, sku: `SKU-${i + 1}`, qty: 1, unitPriceCents: 1000 * (i + 1),
        })),
      })
      return db.order.create({
        data: {
          id, userId, status: over.status ?? 'draft', currency: 'GBP',
          createdAt: new Date('2026-01-01T00:00:00Z'),
          lines: {
            create: Array.from({ length: over.lines ?? 1 }, (_, i) => ({
              sku: `SKU-${i + 1}`, qty: 1, unitPriceCents: 1000 * (i + 1),
            })),
          },
        },
      })
    },
    /** Bulk: for pagination and performance tests only. */
    async orders(n: number, userId = 'usr_00000001') {
      return db.order.createMany({
        data: Array.from({ length: n }, (_, i) => ({
          id: `ord_${String(i).padStart(4, '0')}`,
          userId, status: 'draft', currency: 'GBP',
          createdAt: new Date(Date.UTC(2026, 0, 1, 0, 0, i)),
        })),
      })
    },
  }
}
```

**Isolation patterns for the cases transaction-per-test cannot cover.**

```ts
// (a) Background jobs / a second connection: use truncate + reseed, not a transaction.
integrationTest(async ({ prisma, api, auth, seed }) => {
  await seed.user()
  await api('/api/orders').post(auth()).send({ lines: [{ sku: 'A', qty: 1, unitPriceCents: 100 }] })
  await runOutboxWorkerOnce()      // uses its own connection -> outside the test transaction
  expect(await prisma.outbox.count()).toBe(1)
}, { noTransaction: true })

// (b) Parallel workers: one database per worker, cloned from a template.
const WORKER_DB = `test_db_${process.env.VITEST_WORKER_ID ?? '1'}`
beforeAll(async () => {
  await getPrisma().$executeRawUnsafe(`DROP DATABASE IF EXISTS ${WORKER_DB}`)
  await getPrisma().$executeRawUnsafe(`CREATE DATABASE ${WORKER_DB} TEMPLATE test_db_template`)
  process.env.DATABASE_URL = TEST_DATABASE_URL.replace(/\/[^?]+/, `/${WORKER_DB}`)
})

// (c) Row-level namespacing instead of separate databases (works on SQLite/CI-lite).
const NS = `w${process.env.VITEST_WORKER_ID}`   // every query filters on tenant = NS
```

**When to use which seeding strategy.**

- **No data at all** when the test creates what it needs inline (the best option; no shared fixture, no cross-test coupling).
- **Factory-per-test** for one or two entities.
- **Seeding helpers** for three or more tests needing the same graph (e.g. a user with a saved card and a past order).
- **Bulk seeding** (`createMany`, `generate_series` in raw SQL) for pagination, performance, and load tests. Use raw SQL for >10 000 rows; `createMany` is still one statement per batch and is slow beyond a few thousand.
- **Snapshot-the-database** (restore a dump between tests) for very large fixtures where rebuilding is the bottleneck; requires a template database and per-test `pg_restore`, which is slower than the problem it solves. Use it only for E2E environment setup, not for unit-scale integration.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Seeding hides coupling.** A giant shared fixture means a test that breaks is breaking because the fixture changed, not because the feature changed. Prefer small, purpose-built seeders with a narrow surface.
- **Transaction-per-test is not always semantically identical to production.** Your code may behave differently inside a long-lived transaction (visibility of uncommitted data to a second connection; `now()` is the transaction start time in Postgres, so `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` behaves differently; advisory locks are held until rollback).
- **Bulk seeding is slow and can be the bottleneck.** `createMany` of 50 000 rows can take 10s. Use `COPY` (Prisma `$executeRaw` with `UNNEST`, or `pg-copy-streams`) for large volumes.
- **Parallel databases cost connection slots and memory.** 8 workers x 1 Postgres each is fine; 8 workers on 1 Postgres with 200 connections is not.

**Production considerations.**

- **Apply real migrations** (`prisma migrate deploy` / `knex migrate:latest` / `flyway migrate`) in the test harness. Never `db push`, `synchronize`, or `db:seed`-with-production-seeds.
- Seed **reference data** (countries, currency codes, feature flags) once per *worker* or once per *database* via a `globalSetup`, not per test.
- Keep test data **synthetic and obviously fake** (`@example.test`, `TEST-` prefixes) so it can never be mistaken for production data in a screenshot, a log, or a support ticket.
- Add `DEBUG_SQL=1` to print every query with parameters, and make it a documented debugging affordance.
- Provide a documented `pnpm test:db:reset` that drops, migrates, and reseeds a local dev database so developers never hand-edit test state.

**Common mistakes.**

1. `prisma db push` in the test harness -- migrations are then only tested in production.
2. Seeding in `beforeAll` and never cleaning up, so tests are order-dependent.
3. Using a shared `beforeAll` seed across parallel workers, causing duplicate-key and cross-contamination flakes.
4. `Math.random()` or `new Date()` in seeders, so a failure cannot be reproduced.
5. A `noTransaction: true` escape hatch used everywhere "because it is easier", silently losing isolation.
6. Seeding a *real-looking* user (real name, real email), which is both a GDPR problem and a source of accidental production contact.
7. Asserting on a `count()` that includes rows created by a previous test, so the assertion passes for the wrong reason.
8. Forgetting that Postgres `now()`/`CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` is the *transaction* start time, so a time assertion inside a long transaction can be surprising.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you isolate integration tests that use a database?"* -- Transaction per test with rollback is the default: open a transaction, run the test with a client bound to that transaction, throw to unwind, let the client issue `ROLLBACK`. Truncate-and-reseed is the fallback when the code commits or uses a second connection.
- 🟡 *"Why is `truncate` faster/slower than `delete`?"* -- `TRUNCATE` is DDL: it does not fire row-level delete triggers, does not write per-row delete events, and resets sequences, so it is far faster on large tables -- but it requires an owner-level lock and cannot run inside a transaction that other work depends on. In Postgres, `TRUNCATE` *is* transactional and will roll back with your test, which is convenient.
- 🔴 *"Our test needs a background job to run, and a transaction-per-test harness cannot see it. What do you do?"* -- You have three honest options: (1) use the truncate-and-reseed strategy for that file, accepting slower tests; (2) run the worker inside the test transaction by injecting the same transactional client into the worker, which works if the worker does not commit; (3) test the worker as a unit with a fake queue and cover the enqueue-to-process wiring in one E2E test. I avoid mocking the queue in the integration tier.
- 🔴 *"How do you run integration tests in parallel safely?"* -- One database (or schema) per worker, cloned from a template after migrations; each worker's `DATABASE_URL` points at its own database. Then transaction-per-test inside each worker. Never share one database across workers with row-level namespacing unless you have tested every query for the namespace filter.
- ⚫ *"How do you seed 100 000 rows quickly?"* -- Raw SQL: `INSERT INTO t SELECT ... FROM generate_series(1, 100000)`, or `COPY` via `pg-copy-streams`, or `pgbench`. `createMany` chunks are acceptable to a few thousand rows and degrade quickly beyond that. Seed large volumes in a `globalSetup` or a dedicated performance suite, not per test.

---

### 9.2.4 Transaction-per-test and rollback-based cleanup

**Definition.**

**Transaction-per-test** (also called "test-in-a-transaction" or the *Unit of Work test pattern*) is an isolation strategy in which the test harness opens a database transaction before the test, binds your code to a client bound to that transaction, and rolls back afterwards instead of deleting rows. It is the highest-leverage decision you can make in a Postgres-backed integration suite: it is 10-100x faster than truncate-and-reseed, it preserves *real* transactional semantics during the test, and it makes tests order-independent and parallel-safe for free.

**Mechanics -- why it works.**

- Postgres (and every serious relational DB) gives you a transaction with `READ COMMITTED` (or stronger) isolation. A row inserted inside your test's transaction is invisible to any *other* connection, and vanishes on `ROLLBACK`.
- By handing your code a `trx` client instead of the global `prisma` client, every statement it issues participates in that transaction, so its writes are also rolled back.
- No `DELETE`, no `TRUNCATE`, no re-seeding reference data, no sequence-reset logic. Cleanup cost is O(1) -- a network round trip for `ROLLBACK`.
- Tests can run in parallel against the *same* database, because their writes are mutually invisible.

**What you must do to make it work (the four rules).**

1. **The code under test must use the transactional client.** Either pass a client explicitly (the best design), or set a per-request client on an AsyncLocalStorage-scoped context. A module-level `new PrismaClient()` singleton in production code makes this impossible -- which is itself an argument for dependency injection.
2. **Nothing may commit.** Any `prisma.$transaction()` nested inside your test's transaction becomes a *savepoint* (Prisma does this correctly), which is fine, but raw `COMMIT` or a second connection is not.
3. **Nothing may use a second connection.** Background workers, event consumers, and `LISTEN/NOTIFY` listeners all escape the transaction. Tests for those must use the truncate strategy.
4. **Long transactions can hold locks.** Keep tests under a few seconds; set a `statement_timeout` inside the test transaction so a runaway query fails fast rather than blocking other workers.

**Runnable code -- the three implementations you will actually choose between.**

```ts
// ── Implementation A: explicit client injection (best; requires a DI-friendly codebase)
import { PrismaClient, Prisma } from '@prisma/client'

export class RollbackDb {
  private prisma: PrismaClient
  private done!: (v: unknown) => void

  constructor(prisma: PrismaClient) { this.prisma = prisma }

  async run<T>(fn: (trx: Prisma.TransactionClient) => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
    const box: { value?: T } = {}
    const rollback = Symbol('rollback')
    await this.prisma
      .$transaction(
        async (trx) => {
          box.value = await fn(trx)
          throw rollback
        },
        {
          maxWait: 10_000,
          timeout: 20_000,
          isolationLevel: Prisma.TransactionIsolationLevel.ReadCommitted,
        },
      )
      .catch((e) => { if (e !== rollback) throw e })
    return box.value as T
  }
}

// ── Implementation B: AsyncLocalStorage context (works with a singleton client, zero code changes)
import { AsyncLocalStorage } from 'node:async_hooks'
import { PrismaClient } from '@prisma/client'

export const dbContext = new AsyncLocalStorage<{ tx?: Prisma.TransactionClient }>()

/** Extensions: route every query through the current transaction if there is one. */
function withTx(trx: Prisma.TransactionClient) {
  return new Proxy(trx, {
    get(target, prop, receiver) {
      const value = Reflect.get(target, prop, receiver)
      return typeof value === 'function' ? value.bind(target) : value
    },
  }) as PrismaClient
}

export function currentDb(base: PrismaClient): PrismaClient {
  return dbContext.getStore()?.tx ?? base
}

// ── Implementation C: SQLite in-memory (only for logic tests; NOT for SQL-semantics tests)
import Database from 'better-sqlite3'
export function ephemeralSqlite() {
  const db = new Database(':memory:')
  db.exec('PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON')
  return db
}
```

**Implementation B, complete, with the HTTP layer wired in.** This is the pattern most production teams end up with, because it requires no changes to the application code:

```ts
// src/db/context.ts
import { AsyncLocalStorage } from 'node:async_hooks'
import type { Prisma, PrismaClient } from '@prisma/client'

type Ctx = { tx?: Prisma.TransactionClient }
export const dbAls = new AsyncLocalStorage<Ctx>()

// A middleware that makes the whole request run inside the test transaction.
export function testTransactionMiddleware(base: PrismaClient) {
  return (req: any, res: any, next: () => void) => {
    const trx = (req as any).__trx as Prisma.TransactionClient
    dbAls.run({ tx: trx }, () => next())
  }
}

// The repository reads the current client, so it transparently joins the transaction.
export function repoDb(base: PrismaClient): PrismaClient {
  return new Proxy(base, {
    get(target, prop) {
      const tx = dbAls.getStore()?.tx
      if (tx && prop in tx) {
        const v = (tx as any)[prop]
        return typeof v === 'function' ? v.bind(tx) : v
      }
      const v = (target as any)[prop]
      return typeof v === 'function' ? v.bind(target) : v
    },
  })
}
```

```ts
// test/integration/helpers/transactional.ts
import { PrismaClient } from '@prisma/client'
import supertest from 'supertest'
import { dbAls } from '../../src/db/context'

const ROLLBACK = Symbol('rollback')

/**
 * Runs `fn` inside a transaction that is always rolled back.
 * `fn` receives a helper whose `.api()` builds Supertest requests bound to the same transaction.
 */
export async function inTransaction<T>(
  prisma: PrismaClient,
  fn: (ctx: { api: (p?: string) => import('supertest').Test; trx: Prisma.TransactionClient }) => Promise<T>,
): Promise<T> {
  const box: { value?: T } = {}
  await prisma.$transaction(
    async (trx) => {
      const { buildApp } = await import('../../src/app')
      const { createContainer } = await import('../../src/container')
      const container = await createContainer({ env: { NODE_ENV: 'test' }, prismaOverride: trx })
      const { app } = buildApp(container)

      // Middleware variant: bind the transaction to the request's async context.
      app.use((req: any, _res: any, next: () => void) => {
        req.__trx = trx
        next()
      })

      const base = supertest(app)
      const value = await dbAls.run({ tx: trx }, () =>
        fn({ api: (p = '') => base(p), trx }),
      )
      box.value = value
      throw ROLLBACK
    },
    { timeout: 30_000, maxWait: 10_000 },
  ).catch((e) => {
    if (e !== ROLLBACK) throw e
  })
  return box.value as T
}
```

**Timeouts, locks, and safety rails.** Inside the test transaction, set guards so a mistake cannot wedge the shared database:

```ts
await trx.$executeRawUnsafe(`SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '10s'`)
await trx.$executeRawUnsafe(`SET LOCAL idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '30s'`)
await trx.$executeRawUnsafe(`SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '5s'`)
```

`SET LOCAL` scopes the setting to the current transaction, so it disappears on rollback -- exactly what you want in a shared test database.

**The escape hatches, and when each is legitimate.**

| Situation | Why the transaction fails | Legitimate solution |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Code opens its own connection (a job worker) | the worker's writes are outside the transaction | truncate-and-reseed for that file; or inject the transactional client into the worker |
| Code sends an HTTP request to itself | different process, different connection | truncate-and-reseed; or test that path in E2E |
| Testing two concurrent writers | neither sees the other's uncommitted data | truncate-and-reseed with explicit `await`s, or E2E |
| Testing advisory locks / `LISTEN` | session-scoped, not transaction-scoped | truncate-and-reseed; or a dedicated concurrency test file |
| Testing `SERIALIZABLE` conflict | needs a second connection to conflict | truncate-and-reseed, two connections, explicit cleanup |
| `ON CONFLICT` behaviour | works fine | transaction-per-test is fine |

**Runnable test code, using the harness.**

```ts
import { describe, expect } from 'vitest'
import { getPrisma } from './helpers/db'
import { inTransaction } from './helpers/transactional'
import { signToken } from '../../src/auth/tokens'

const prisma = getPrisma()

describe('POST /api/orders (transactional integration)', () => {
  it('persists exactly one order and rolls the test transaction back', async () => {
    const result = await inTransaction(prisma, async ({ api, trx }) => {
      const auth = { Authorization: `Bearer ${signToken({ sub: 'usr_1' }, { secret: process.env.TEST_SECRET! })}` } as const

      const res = await api('/api/orders').post(auth).send({ lines: [{ sku: 'A', qty: 1, unitPriceCents: 100 }] })
      expect(res.status).toBe(201)

      // Visible inside the transaction:
      expect(await trx.order.count()).toBe(1)
      return res.body.id as string
    })

    // Gone after the rollback:
    expect(await prisma.order.count({ where: { id: result } })).toBe(0)
  })

  it('runs two tests concurrently against the same database without interference', async () => {
    const ids = await Promise.all([
      inTransaction(prisma, async ({ api }) => (await api('/api/orders').post(auth()).send(body())).body.id),
      inTransaction(prisma, async ({ api }) => (await api('/api/orders').post(auth()).send(body())).body.id),
      inTransaction(prisma, async ({ api }) => (await api('/api/orders').post(auth()).send(body())).body.id),
    ])
    expect(new Set(ids).size).toBe(3)
    expect(await prisma.order.count()).toBe(0)     // all three rolled back
  })
})
```

**When transaction-per-test is right vs wrong.**

Right when: Postgres (or any DB with transactional DDL/DML isolation), single-connection application code, no background workers in the path, and the majority of your tests are request/response or repository tests.

Wrong when: the code is built around separate connections per unit (common in connection-pool-per-request designs with `pg`), the test needs a second connection or a worker, the test asserts on something outside the DB, or you need to test commit-visibility semantics.

**Trade-offs.**

- **A long transaction changes some semantics.** In Postgres `now()`/`CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` is the *transaction* start time, so a test that takes 30s still sees the time it began. If you test time-based logic, inject a clock rather than relying on `now()`.
- **Isolation level caveats.** Under `READ COMMITTED`, your test's transaction sees its own writes plus committed data from others. Under `REPEATABLE READ`, it would not see concurrently committed rows -- which can surprise you if a `globalSetup` seeds data after you start.
- **Debugging a rolled-back failure is harder**: the data is gone. Mitigate by logging the fixture and (optionally) supporting a `--no-rollback` debug flag that commits instead.
- **The `throw ROLLBACK` pattern is subtle** and, if a `beforeEach` hook inside the transaction throws first, the transaction still rolls back (good) but the error attribution can confuse. Use a distinct sentinel class and check it in the catch.
- **It hides the "does my code commit correctly?" question.** If a repository forgets to wrap multi-statement work in a transaction, a transaction-per-test suite will *not* catch a partial write on error, because the test's own rollback masks it. This is a real gap: add one non-transactional test per repository asserting atomicity.

**Production considerations.**

- Make the harness the *only* supported way to run integration tests. Provide `pnpm test:integration` and lint against importing `@prisma/client` directly in a spec.
- Document the rules in the test README: "use `inTransaction`, do not create a PrismaClient, do not call `process.exit`, jobs are out of scope".
- For CI parallelism, a single Postgres container with 8-16 workers and transaction-per-test is dramatically cheaper than 8 containers, and it is the configuration most teams should standardise on.
- Keep the pool size >= number of workers; otherwise workers serialise on connections and the suite looks like it has a performance bug.
- Add a nightly job that runs the *whole* integration suite in truncate mode, to catch the tests that only pass because of transaction masking.

**Common mistakes.**

1. The code under test uses a module-level `PrismaClient` singleton, so the transaction applies to nothing and the rollback does not clean up -- the suite passes individually and fails as a suite.
2. Forgetting that `Prisma.$transaction(fn)` with a thrown sentinel can surface as `P2028` on timeout; mistaking it for a real failure.
3. Long transactions holding locks and blocking `migrate` or a parallel truncate job.
4. Using `SET statement_timeout` (session-scoped) instead of `SET LOCAL`, so it leaks to the next test on the same connection.
5. Assuming the rollback also cleans up things outside the database (a file, a cache, a queue publish). It does not -- if your code writes to Redis inside a transactional test, you need a Redis cleanup too.
6. Adding a `beforeEach` that commits, silently disabling the strategy.
7. Not setting `lock_timeout`, so a deadlock between two parallel tests hangs the CI job until the global timeout.
8. Running `prisma migrate deploy` inside each test's transaction (migrations are transactional in Postgres but take table locks that stall other workers).

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is transaction-per-test isolation and why is it good?"* -- Each test runs inside a transaction that is always rolled back, so its writes are invisible to other tests and vanish afterwards. It is fast (a ROLLBACK round trip instead of a TRUNCATE), preserves real transactional semantics, and makes tests parallel-safe.
- 🟡 *"What can you not test with transaction-per-test?"* -- Anything on a second connection or process: background workers, self-HTTP calls, two-concurrent-writer scenarios, advisory locks, `LISTEN/NOTIFY`, and commit-visibility semantics. Those need truncate-and-reseed or E2E.
- 🔴 *"Your test suite passes alone but fails as a suite. How do you find the leak?"* -- (1) Run it serially (`--no-file-parallelism --maxWorkers=1`) to confirm; (2) run with `--sequence.shuffle` to expose order dependence; (3) add a `beforeEach` that asserts a baseline row count; (4) grep for `new PrismaClient()` in `src`; (5) check for module-level mutable state in seeders; (6) check for `beforeAll` that seeds without cleanup.
- 🔴 *"A repository's multi-statement write is not atomic. Your transactional suite passes. Why, and how do you catch it?"* -- Because the test's own rollback undoes a partial write, masking the bug. You need a dedicated non-transactional test: force the second statement to fail (unique violation, injected error) and assert that the first statement's row does not exist afterwards.
- ⚫ *"Why might `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` surprise you inside a transaction?"* -- In Postgres it is the transaction start time, not the statement time. A test that runs for 30 seconds sees the time it began. Inject a clock rather than relying on database time, or set the time explicitly per test.

---

### 9.2.5 Testcontainers: real dependencies without shared dev boxes

**Definition.**

**Testcontainers** (from the Java Testcontainers project, now ported to Node/TypeScript, Python, .NET, Go) starts **real dependencies in Docker containers** for the duration of your test run, waits for them to be healthy, exposes their connection details as environment variables, and tears them down afterwards. The problem it solves is the "it works on my machine" and "the shared dev database is someone else's problem" failure modes: every test run gets a pristine, correctly-versioned, isolated instance of the real engine.

**Mechanics -- the lifecycle.**

1. **Container start.** A container image is started with a specific configuration (image tag or digest, env, command, port bindings, mounted volumes, a tmpfs for speed).
2. **Wait strategy.** The container is not ready when `docker run` returns. Testcontainers uses a *wait strategy* -- `waitForListeningPort()`, `wait.forLogMessage()`, `wait.forHealthCheck()`, or a custom wait -- and blocks until the dependency is genuinely usable.
3. **Connection details.** `getMappedPort(5432)` and `getHost()` produce a connection URL. Because ports are dynamic, every test file must read the URL from the container, never hardcode `5432`.
4. **Reuse.** `withReuse()` keeps the container alive between runs (a huge local speedup) and requires opt-in (`testcontainers.reuse.enable=true` in `~/.testcontainers.properties`).
5. **Teardown.** Ryuk (the resource reaper container) monitors the JVM/Node process and kills orphans when it dies, so a crashed run does not leak containers.

**Runnable code -- a Postgres + Redis harness with proper wait strategies, migrations, and reuse.**

```ts
// test/integration/helpers/containers.ts
import { PostgreSqlContainer, StartedPostgreSqlContainer } from '@testcontainers/postgresql'
import { GenericContainer, StartedTestContainer, Wait } from 'testcontainers'
import { execFile } from 'node:child_process'
import { promisify } from 'node:util'
import { logger } from './logger'

const exec = promisify(execFile)

let pg: StartedPostgreSqlContainer | undefined
let redis: StartedTestContainer | undefined
let migratedFor: string | undefined

export interface Infra {
  databaseUrl: string
  redisUrl: string
}

export async function startInfra(): Promise<Infra> {
  if (!pg) {
    pg = new PostgreSqlContainer('postgres:16.4-alpine')
      .withDatabase('app_test')
      .withUsername('test')
      .withPassword('test')
      .withTmpFs({ '/var/lib/postgresql/data': 'rw' })   // tmpfs = RAM-backed = fast
      .withLogConsumer((line) => { if (process.env.DEBUG_CONTAINERS) process.stderr.write(`[pg] ${line}`) })
      .withWaitStrategy(
        Wait.forLogMessage(/database system is ready to accept connections/, 2),  // Postgres logs "ready" twice during init
      )
      .start()
    logger.info(`postgres started on ${pg.getHost()}:${pg.getMappedPort(5432)}`)
  }

  if (!redis) {
    redis = await new GenericContainer('redis:7.4-alpine')
      .withExposedPorts(6379)
      .withCommand(['redis-server', '--save', '', '--appendonly', 'no'])   // no persistence: faster + disposable
      .withWaitStrategy(Wait.forLogMessage(/Ready to accept connections/))
      .start()
  }

  const databaseUrl = pg.getConnectionUri()
  if (migratedFor !== databaseUrl) {
    // Real migrations, exactly as production runs them.
    await exec('npx', ['prisma', 'migrate', 'deploy'], {
      env: { ...process.env, DATABASE_URL: databaseUrl },
      maxBuffer: 10 * 1024 * 1024,
    })
    migratedFor = databaseUrl
  }

  return { databaseUrl, redisUrl: `redis://${redis.getHost()}:${redis.getMappedPort(6379)}` }
}

export async function stopInfra(): Promise<void> {
  await Promise.allSettled([pg?.stop(), redis?.stop()])
  pg = undefined; redis = undefined; migratedFor = undefined
}
```

```ts
// test/global-setup.ts -- the *correct* place to start containers
import { startInfra, stopInfra } from './integration/helpers/containers'

export async function setup(): Promise<void> {
  const infra = await startInfra()
  process.env.TEST_DATABASE_URL = infra.databaseUrl
  process.env.TEST_REDIS_URL = infra.redisUrl
  // Vitest forwards process.env mutations to workers via the provided object.
  provide('TEST_DATABASE_URL', infra.databaseUrl)
  provide('TEST_REDIS_URL', infra.redisUrl)
}

export async function teardown(): Promise<void> {
  await stopInfra()
}
```

```ts
// vitest.config.ts -- wire globalSetup
import { defineConfig } from 'vitest/config'
export default defineConfig({
  test: {
    globalSetup: ['./test/global-setup.ts'],
    setupFiles: ['./test/setup.ts'],
    env: {
      // Fallbacks for local runs without the global setup (e.g. a developer's own DB)
      TEST_DATABASE_URL: process.env.TEST_DATABASE_URL ?? 'postgres://test:test@localhost:5432/app_test',
    },
  },
})
```

**Reuse, which is the difference between a 3-second and a 40-second local loop.**

```properties
# ~/.testcontainers.properties
testcontainers.reuse.enable=true
```

```ts
// Reusable container: keeps the same Postgres volume between runs.
export function reusablePostgres(): PostgreSqlContainer {
  return new PostgreSqlContainer('postgres:16.4-alpine')
    .withDatabase('app_test')
    .withUsername('test')
    .withPassword('test')
    .withReuse()
    .withCopyFilesToContainer([{ source: './prisma/migrations', target: '/migrations' }])
}
```

**Resources beyond Postgres -- the pattern is the same for all of them.**

```ts
// Redis
new GenericContainer('redis:7.4-alpine')
  .withExposedPorts(6379)
  .withWaitStrategy(Wait.forLogMessage(/Ready to accept connections/))

// LocalStack (S3, SNS, SQS, DynamoDB, Lambda -- the AWS emulation layer)
new GenericContainer('localstack/localstack:3.8')
  .withExposedPorts(4566)
  .withEnv('SERVICES=s3,sqs,sns')
  .withEnv('EAGER_SERVICE_LOADING=1')
  .withWaitStrategy(Wait.forLogMessage(/Ready\./))
  .withNetwork('shared')                       // so your app container can reach it

// A real message queue
new GenericContainer('rabbitmq:3.13-management-alpine')
  .withExposedPorts(5672)
  .withWaitStrategy(Wait.forLogMessage(/completed with 3 plugins/))

// Anything with an HTTP health endpoint
new GenericContainer('ghcr.io/myorg/thing:1.4.2')
  .withExposedPorts(8080)
  .withWaitStrategy(Wait.forHttp('/healthz', 200).withStartupTimeout(120_000))
```

**The "system under test in a container" pattern.** For integration tests where the app itself must run with real network isolation, you run the app in a container on a shared Docker network, so it can reach the dependency containers by service name and nothing else.

```ts
const network = await new GenericContainer('alpine:3.20').withNetworkMode('bridge').start()
  .then((c) => c.getNetwork())

const db = await new PostgreSqlContainer('postgres:16.4-alpine').withNetwork(network).withNetworkAliases(['db']).start()
const api = await new GenericContainer('ghcr.io/myorg/api:1.4.2')
  .withNetwork(network)
  .withNetworkAliases(['api'])
  .withEnvironment({ DATABASE_URL: 'postgresql://test:test@db:5432/app_test', NODE_ENV: 'test' })
  .withExposedPorts(3000)
  .withWaitStrategy(Wait.forHttp('/healthz', 200).forStatusCodeMatching((s) => s >= 200 && s < 300))
  .start()

const res = await fetch(`http://${api.getHost()}:${api.getMappedPort(3000)}/api/orders`)
```

**When to use Testcontainers vs not.**

Use it for: Postgres/MySQL/MongoDB/Redis, message queues, S3-compatible stores, search engines, and anything where the *engine's* behaviour matters (SQL semantics, index behaviour, TTL, transactions, collation). Use real containers in CI, and in pre-commit for the files that touch infrastructure.

Do not use it for: the unit tier (it is 100-1000x too slow), for third-party SaaS (use MSW or a recorded cassette), or on every local save. Keep the local loop on in-process Postgres (PGlite) or a reused container.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Requires a working Docker daemon.** In restricted CI, in some corporate networks, and on machines without Docker, this is a hard blocker. Have a fallback: an env var pointing at a managed Postgres (`TEST_DATABASE_URL`), and a documented "no Docker? use this" path.
- **Startup cost.** 1-5s per container, serialised. `globalSetup` amortises it across the whole run. Reuse eliminates it locally.
- **Resource use.** Each container needs memory; 8 workers each starting a container will exhaust a laptop. Start once per process, not per file.
- **Cleanup is best-effort.** A hard kill (CI runner OOM) can leak containers. Ryuk handles the common case; add a CI step `docker ps -aq --filter "label=org.testcontainers=true" | xargs -r docker rm -f` for safety.
- **Image drift.** `postgres:16` moves under you. Pin a minor (`16.4`) or a digest, and consider a nightly job against the newest minor to catch deprecations before your users do.
- **It is the wrong tool for verifying your ORM's SQL against Postgres** if you only need the SQL -- `EXPLAIN` assertions in a test that queries a real DB are fine, but assert on *results*, not on query text.

**Production considerations.**

- `globalSetup` is the only correct place to start containers. Starting them in `beforeAll` per file multiplies cost by the number of files.
- Use **tmpfs** for database data directories: it is 5-10x faster than a bind mount and matches production IO characteristics better than you might expect.
- Use `--save ''` / disable persistence for Redis and Postgres volumes in tests; a persisted volume in a reused container is a source of cross-run contamination.
- In CI, prefer a **service container** over Testcontainers where the CI provider supports it (GitHub Actions `services:`, GitLab `services:`, CircleCI Docker executor): it starts before the job, which is faster and more reliable than starting it from the test process.
- Log container output on failure. A Postgres that failed to start prints the reason (`could not bind address: port 5432`) and Testcontainers throws a wall of text that nobody reads. Capture it in `withLogConsumer` and print it only when the suite fails.
- Pin the same major version as production, and add a compatibility note: "these tests validate against Postgres 16.4, production runs 16.6."

**Common mistakes.**

1. Hardcoding `localhost:5432` instead of using `getMappedPort(5432)`, so the suite only works when the port happens to be free.
2. Starting containers in `beforeAll` in every test file: 40 files x 3s = 2 minutes of pure waiting.
3. Not disabling persistence, so a reused container carries yesterday's data and today's tests fail mysteriously.
4. Using `wait.forListeningPort` only, which returns before Postgres has finished its init phase, so the first connection is refused.
5. Forgetting to run `prisma migrate deploy` against the container's URL, so the schema is missing.
6. Not setting `withReuse()` and having a 30-second local loop.
7. Assuming Ryuk always cleans up, and orphaning containers after an OOM kill, until the developer's disk fills.
8. Testing against `postgres:latest` and getting a surprise major-version failure a week later.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is Testcontainers and why use it?"* -- It starts real dependencies in Docker for the duration of the test run, waits for them to be healthy, and tears them down, so every run gets a pristine correctly-versioned instance instead of a shared dev machine.
- 🟡 *"How do you keep integration tests fast with containers?"* -- Start them once in `globalSetup`, not per file; use tmpfs; enable reuse locally; use the CI provider's native service containers; and run only the infrastructure-relevant tests on every PR, with the full matrix nightly.
- 🔴 *"How do you know when a container is actually ready to use?"* -- Not when the port is open. Use the service's own readiness signal: Postgres's "ready to accept connections" log (it appears twice, during init and after), `pg_isready` via a wait command, or a `SELECT 1` poll. For HTTP services use `Wait.forHttp('/healthz')`. A port being open only means the process bound it.
- 🔴 *"What happens when CI has no Docker?"* -- Two answers: use the CI provider's native service containers where available, and keep an env-var override (`TEST_DATABASE_URL`) so the suite can run against a managed instance. Never make the integration tier unrunnable; make the *specific engine* configurable.
- ⚫ *"How do you handle 8 workers and 8 Postgres containers on a laptop?"* -- Do not. One container, `globalSetup`, transaction-per-test, and 8 workers sharing it. Testcontainers' parallelism value is for multi-*engine* matrices, not for parallel connections.

---

### 9.2.6 MSW: mocking at the network boundary

**Definition.**

**Mock Service Worker (MSW)** intercepts network requests at the lowest level the browser or Node exposes -- in the browser via a Service Worker (so it survives page navigation and cannot be bypassed by app code), and in Node via `@mswjs/interceptors` (ClientRequest, fetch, XHR). It lets you write tests and development environments that talk to a *fake server* rather than substituting your own service methods. The tagline that matters: **mock the network, not your code.** That is, you mock the *third party's* HTTP API, and everything of yours -- hooks, query clients, interceptors, retry logic, serialisation, error mapping -- runs for real.

**Mechanics -- the two APIs and when each is correct.**

- **Request handlers (declarative, default).** `http.get('/api/user', () => HttpResponse.json({...}))` registered on a `setupServer` instance. Used in tests and in `node` (Vitest/Jest). Handlers are matched in **reverse registration order** (last registered wins), with optional path params, wildcards, and predicates.
- **Storybook-style handlers (in-browser, for development).** `msw-storybook-addon` lets you define handlers once and use them both as a mock backend for Storybook and as the test backend, guaranteeing that stories exercise the same contract as tests.

```ts
// test/integration/helpers/server.ts
import { setupServer } from 'msw/node'
import { http, HttpResponse, delay } from 'msw'
import { handlers } from './handlers'

export const server = setupServer(...handlers)

// Call in vitest.config.ts:  setupFiles: ['./test/setup.ts']
// test/setup.ts:
import { server } from './integration/helpers/server'
import { afterAll, afterEach, beforeAll } from 'vitest'

beforeAll(() => server.listen({ onUnhandledRequest: 'error', quiet: true }))
afterEach(() => {
  server.resetHandlers()          // removes per-test overrides, keeps base handlers
  server.events.removeAllListeners()
})
afterAll(() => server.close())
```

**Handler design: base handlers plus per-test overrides.**

```ts
// test/integration/helpers/handlers.ts
import { http, HttpResponse, delay } from 'msw'
import { orderFactory } from '../../test-support/factories'

const user = { id: 'usr_00000001', displayName: 'Ada', role: 'member' }

export const handlers = [
  // Deterministic happy path for everything else.
  http.get('https://api.example.test/v1/user', () => HttpResponse.json(user)),

  http.get('https://api.example.test/v1/orders/:id', ({ params }) =>
    HttpResponse.json(orderFactory({ id: String(params.id) })),
  ),

  // A base handler with a *programmable* state so tests can drive it.
  http.post('https://api.example.test/v1/payments', async ({ request }) => {
    const body = (await request.json()) as { amountCents: number; cardToken: string }
    if (body.cardToken === 'tok_decline') {
      return HttpResponse.json(
        { error: { code: 'card_declined', message: 'The card was declined' } },
        { status: 402 },
      )
    }
    if (body.amountCents > 100_000) {
      return HttpResponse.json(
        { error: { code: 'amount_too_large', message: 'Amount exceeds the daily limit' } },
        { status: 422 },
      )
    }
    return HttpResponse.json({ txId: 'tx_1', status: 'captured' }, { status: 201 })
  }),

  // Latency simulation, opt-in per test.
  http.get('https://api.example.test/v1/slow-report', async () => {
    await delay(2000)
    return HttpResponse.json({ ok: true })
  }),
]
```

```ts
// test/integration/checkout.payment.test.ts
import { describe, expect, it } from 'vitest'
import { http, HttpResponse, delay } from 'msw'
import { server } from './helpers/server'
import { inTransaction } from './helpers/transactional'
import { getPrisma } from './helpers/db'

describe('POST /api/checkout (third party: MSW at the network boundary)', () => {
  it('marks the order paid and stores the provider transaction id', async () => {
    await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ api, trx }) => {
      // Arrange: an order in a payable state
      const orderId = await seedPayableOrder(trx, { cardToken: 'tok_ok' })

      // Act
      const res = await api('/api/checkout').post(auth()).send({ orderId, cardToken: 'tok_ok' })

      // Assert
      expect(res.status).toBe(200)
      expect(res.body.paymentTxId).toBe('tx_1')
      expect((await trx.order.findUniqueOrThrow({ where: { id: orderId } })).status).toBe('paid')
    })
  })

  it('maps a 402 from the provider to a 402 for the client, and does not mark the order paid', async () => {
    await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ api, trx }) => {
      const orderId = await seedPayableOrder(trx, { cardToken: 'tok_decline' })

      const res = await api('/api/checkout').post(auth()).send({ orderId, cardToken: 'tok_decline' })

      // Your error mapping is under test, and MSW gives you the real HTTP status.
      expect(res.status).toBe(402)
      expect(res.body.error.code).toBe 'PAYMENT_DECLINED'
      expect((await trx.order.findUniqueOrThrow({ where: { id: orderId } })).status).toBe('payment_failed')
    })
  })

  it('times out after 3s when the provider hangs, and reports 504', async () => {
    server.use(
      http.post('https://api.example.test/v1/payments', async () => {
        await delay('infinite')
        return HttpResponse.json({})
      }),
    )
    await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ api }) => {
      const res = await api('/api/checkout').post(auth()).send({ orderId: 'ord_1', cardToken: 'tok_ok' })
      expect(res.status).toBe(504)
      expect(res.body.error.code).toBe('UPSTREAM_TIMEOUT')
    })
  })

  it('retries a 503 twice and then succeeds, matching the retry policy', async () => {
    let calls = 0
    server.use(
      http.post('https://api.example.test/v1/payments', () => {
        calls += 1
        return calls < 3
          ? HttpResponse.json({ error: { code: 'unavailable' } }, { status: 503 })
          : HttpResponse.json({ txId: 'tx_retry_ok' }, { status: 201 })
      }),
    )
    await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ api }) => {
      const res = await api('/api/checkout').post(auth()).send({ orderId: 'ord_1', cardToken: 'tok_ok' })
      expect(res.status).toBe(200)
      expect(calls).toBe(3)
    })
  })
})
```

**MSW in the browser (Storybook and dev), which is where its highest ROI is.**

```ts
// .storybook/preview.ts
import { setupWorker } from 'msw/browser'
import { handlers } from '../test/integration/helpers/handlers'

export const worker = setupWorker(...handlers)
worker.start({ onUnhandledRequest: 'bypass', quiet: true })
```

```ts
// A story that overrides one handler to exercise the empty state, using the same
// handler definitions the tests use -- stories and tests cannot drift.
export const EmptyCart: Story = {
  render: () => <CartPage />,
  parameters: {
    msw: {
      handlers: [
        http.get('https://api.example.test/v1/cart', () => HttpResponse.json({ items: [] })),
      ],
    },
  },
}
```

**Cassettes / record-and-replay.** MSW supports `setupRemoteServer` or `@mswjs/data` persistence to record real responses and replay them:

```ts
// Record once against a real sandbox; replay everywhere else.
import { setupServer, SetupMode } from 'msw/node'

const mode = process.env.RECORD ? SetupMode.Record : SetupMode.Replay
const server = setupServer(...cassetteHandlersFrom('./__fixtures__/payments'))
await server.start({ mode })
```

This gives you a *real* provider shape in your tests without network dependency, and it is the best available substitute for a third-party sandbox. Its costs: PII in the cassette (mask it), staleness (a provider can change without telling you), and the temptation to treat the cassette as a spec (it is not -- it is a snapshot of one moment).

**When to use MSW vs not.**

Use MSW for: any third-party HTTP dependency in a test or a dev environment; frontend component tests that need data; Storybook stories; Storybook-style state; developing UI against a not-yet-existing backend; and contract-shape regression tests for outbound clients.

Do not use MSW for: testing the third party's own behaviour (you cannot; use a real sandbox on a schedule), performance tests (the interceptor adds overhead), or as a substitute for a real database in backend integration tests.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Your outbound adapter's behaviour is now genuinely tested** -- the big win. URL construction, headers, auth, serialisation, timeouts, retries, and error mapping all run for real.
- **It is a fake server, so the provider's real behaviour is still unknown.** MSW proves you speak the protocol you *think* the provider speaks. Contract tests (9.2.7) and periodic sandbox runs close the remaining gap.
- **`onUnhandledRequest: 'error'` is a setting you should make and keep.** It turns "my handler has the wrong URL" into an immediate, obvious failure instead of a mysterious 404 from the wrong place. In development/Storybook, use `'bypass'`.
- **Service Worker mode has gotchas**: it requires a secure context (localhost counts), it can serve stale handlers until unregistered, and in Playwright you must route MSW's worker script or disable SW. In Node, `@mswjs/interceptors` patches `http`/`fetch`/`XHR`, which does not intercept a raw `net.Socket` write.
- **Latency simulation with `delay()` is the fastest way to write a debounce/spinner test** -- and also the fastest way to introduce a real-time flake if you forget it is real. Prefer `advanceTimersByTimeAsync` with fake timers, or keep the delay well under the test timeout.

**Production considerations.**

- Keep handlers in one module, imported by tests, Storybook, and the dev server. Single source of truth for the backend shape is the entire value.
- Generate handlers **from your OpenAPI/GraphQL schema** where you can (`msw-openapi-har` or `openapi-msw`), so a schema change breaks the handler set rather than silently serving stale shapes.
- Mask or synthesise PII in recorded cassettes; a cassette is a file in git.
- Run a nightly "provider smoke" suite against real sandboxes: 3-5 tests that hit the provider's sandbox and assert your adapter still maps the response correctly. This is the only way MSW's central assumption stays true.
- In the browser, `worker.start()` must run *before* the app's first request; put it in `index.html` (dev only) or in the test setup.

**Common mistakes.**

1. `onUnhandledRequest: 'warn'` (the default) -- typos in handler URLs go unnoticed, and your test passes against a *bypassed* real request in dev.
2. Handlers registered inside each test file instead of in the shared server, so the handler set is inconsistent across files.
3. Forgetting `server.resetHandlers()` in `afterEach`, so a per-test override leaks into the next test (this is the #1 MSW flake).
4. Using `HttpResponse.json(body, { status: 400 })` but forgetting the `Content-Type` on a non-JSON error, so your client's `res.json()` throws instead of your error mapper running.
5. Mocking the network *and* your own service wrapper, so neither the wrapper nor the network is really tested.
6. `delay(5000)` in a base handler, turning every test in the suite slow and flaky.
7. Using a hardcoded absolute URL in handlers while your app calls a relative URL (or vice versa), so nothing intercepts.
8. Committing recorded cassettes with real customer data.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is MSW and why is it better than mocking your API client?"* -- It intercepts at the network layer, so your real client code -- URL, headers, auth, serialisation, retry, error mapping -- executes. Mocking your client function removes all of that from the test.
- 🟡 *"How do you test a third-party timeout?"* -- Register a handler that delays (or never resolves), set your client's timeout below it, and assert your timeout path, the error mapping, and that no partial state was written.
- 🔴 *"Why does my MSW test work alone and fail in the suite?"* -- Almost always a missing `server.resetHandlers()` in `afterEach`, so a per-test override (a 500, a delay) leaks into the next test. Second most common: a module-level cache or query client surviving between files.
- 🔴 *"How do you know your mock is right about a real provider?"* -- You cannot, from the mock alone. You close the gap with (1) contract tests derived from the provider's published spec/OpenAPI, (2) a nightly suite against the provider's sandbox, and (3) generated handlers from the provider's schema so shape drift is a type error.
- ⚫ *"When is MSW wrong?"* -- When you are testing the provider's behaviour, in performance tests (interception overhead), or in tests that need a *real* TLS/network failure such as a DNS failure or a connection reset -- MSW cannot produce a real socket-level error; use a local TCP server that destroys connections for that.

---

### 9.2.7 Contract testing with Pact and consumer-driven contracts

**Definition.**

A **consumer-driven contract (CDC)** is an artefact produced by the *consumer* of an API and consumed by the *provider*. It records exactly which requests the consumer makes and what response shapes it relies on. **Pact** is the dominant implementation (Pact-JVM, Pact-JS, Pact-Go, pact-rust, Pact-Python). The mechanism: the consumer's tests run against a **mock provider** generated from the pact, and the resulting pact file is published to a broker; the provider's CI then **verifies** its real API against every consumer pact before deploying.

**Mechanics -- the three phases.**

```
  CONSUMER CI                        BROKER                    PROVIDER CI
  -----------                        ------                    -----------
  1. write consumer test
     (no provider running)
  2. pact-jest / pact-verifier
     generates a mock server        3. publish pact          4. fetch pacts for
     from the pact file                 (canary)                 this provider
  3. consumer test runs
     against the mock              5. notify team          6. pact-verifier replays
                                       (Slack/PR)                each interaction
                                                                   against the REAL API
                                                            7. fail if any interaction fails
```

**The critical insight that makes CDC different from everything else in this phase:** the consumer's tests run **without the provider existing at all**. That is a genuine decoupling win -- teams can build a frontend in parallel with a backend -- and it is also the main risk: a mock that is generated from *your* assumptions is not a contract, it is a shared assumption. Pact's value is that the provider is then *obliged* to verify it.

**Runnable code -- the consumer side (Pact-JS / pact-js).**

```ts
// consumer: order-service/test/pact/order-service.pact.spec.ts
import { pact } from '@pact-foundation/pact'

const provider = new pact.Provider({
  name: 'order-service',                    // this consumer's name
  version: process.env.GIT_COMMIT_SHA!,
  consumer: 'checkout-service',             // the consumer of the provider
  logLevel: 'error',
  // Point at the real provider in provider-verification mode; omit for consumer tests.
  ...(process.env.PACT_BROKER_BASE_URL && { pactBrokerUrl: process.env.PACT_BROKER_BASE_URL }),
})

// The interaction: exactly one, named for the behaviour it guarantees.
provider.addInteraction({
  state: 'an order exists with id ord_1 in status draft',
  uponReceiving: 'a GET request for /v1/orders/ord_1',
  withRequest: { method: 'GET', path: '/v1/orders/ord_1', headers: { Accept: 'application/json' } },
  willRespondWith: {
    status: 200,
    headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
    body: {
      id: 'ord_1',
      status: 'draft',
      currency: 'GBP',
      totalCents: 4200,
      lines: [
        {
          sku: expect.stringMatching(/^SKU-\d+$/),   // generators: consumers are tolerant, providers must satisfy
          qty: expect.any(Number),
          unitPriceCents: expect.any(Number),
        },
      ],
    },
  },
})

// Also assert the *negative* case -- this is where most hand-written mocks are missing.
provider.addInteraction({
  state: 'no order with id ord_missing exists',
  uponReceiving: 'a GET request for /v1/orders/ord_missing',
  withRequest: { method: 'GET', path: '/v1/orders/ord_missing' },
  willRespondWith: {
    status: 404,
    headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
    body: { error: { code: 'NOT_FOUND', message: expect.any(String) } },
  },
})

describe('OrderApiClient (consumer contract)', () => {
  let client: OrderApiClient
  beforeAll(() => { client = new OrderApiClient({ baseUrl: provider.url }) })   // mock provider
  afterAll(() => provider.finalize())

  it('reads a draft order and computes the payable total', async () => {
    const order = await client.getOrder('ord_1')
    expect(order.totalCents).toBe(4200)
    expect(order.lines).toHaveLength(1)
  })

  it('raises NotFound for a missing order', async () => {
    await expect(client.getOrder('ord_missing')).rejects.toThrow(NotFoundError)
  })
})
```

**Runnable code -- the provider side (verification).**

```ts
// provider: test/contract/order-service.pact.spec.ts
import { verifier } from '@pact-foundation/pact'

describe('Order service provider verification', () => {
  beforeAll(() => {
    // Kills the process if any interaction fails; CI gate.
    verifier.addSetup({
      providerBaseUrl: process.env.PROVIDER_BASE_URL!,
      requestFilter: (req, res, next) => {
        res.on('finish', () => {
          metrics.histogram('pact_interaction', { status: res.statusCode }).observe(0)
        })
        next()
      },
    })
  })

  afterAll(() => {
    verifier.execute({
      provider: 'order-service',
      consumerVersionTags: ['main', /^feat\/.+/],
      providerBaseUrl: process.env.PROVIDER_BASE_URL!,
      providerVersion: process.env.GIT_COMMIT_SHA!,
      providerStatesSetupUrl: `${process.env.PROVIDER_BASE_URL!}/__test/state`,  // sets up "an order exists with id ord_1"
      pactBrokerUrl: process.env.PACT_BROKER_BASE_URL!,
      provider: process.env.GIT_COMMIT_SHA!,
      logLevel: 'error',
    }).then(() => process.exit(0))     // non-zero exit fails the provider build
  }, 60_000)
})
```

```ts
// provider: the state setup endpoint. It receives { state, params } and must make it true.
app.post('/__test/state', async (req, res) => {
  const { state, params } = req.body as { state: string; params?: Record<string, string> }
  if (state === 'an order exists with id ord_1 in status draft') {
    await db.order.create({
      data: {
        id: params?.id ?? 'ord_1',
        status: 'draft',
        currency: 'GBP',
        lines: { create: [{ sku: 'SKU-1', qty: 1, unitPriceCents: 4200 }] },
      },
    })
  }
  if (state === 'no order with id ord_missing exists') {
    // no-op: the absence is the state
  }
  res.json({ ok: true })
})
```

**Provider state is where contracts rot.** The state setup endpoint is hand-written, and it is a *test-only code path in production* unless you gate it. Two mitigations: (1) mount the endpoint only when `NODE_ENV === 'test'` or behind an auth token; (2) make states declarative and reset the database before each verification run.

**Matcher strictness is the design decision that matters most in CDC.** Pact matchers can be `type` (any string), `regex`, `equality`, `include`, `number`, `eachLike`, and generators. Choose per field based on what the consumer *actually* needs:

| Field kind | Correct matcher | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Status enum | `equality('draft')` or regex `^(draft\|placed\|shipped)$` | the consumer branches on it; a new enum value is a contract change you want to see |
| Free-text display name | `type('string')` or regex `.{1,120}` | do not pin a value you do not depend on |
| Array of items | `eachLike({...}, { min: 1 })` | the consumer needs the element shape, not the length |
| Nullable optional field | `nullOrType('string')` | absence must be a legal response |
| New field the provider adds | absent from the pact | by design: pacts are additive-friendly, and unlisted fields are ignored -- which is both Pact's main ergonomic win and its main risk |

**When to use Pact / CDC vs not.**

Use it when: you have multiple independently-deployed services or teams; a frontend team needs to develop against a not-yet-built API; a change to a service has historically broken consumers without warning; or you have a governance/microservices org where API change control matters.

Do not use it when: there is one monolithic codebase (an integration test is cheaper and stronger), the API is internal to one team with a shared repo (code review catches it), or your "services" are really modules (you have full visibility into both sides -- use direct tests).

**Trade-offs.**

- **You get decoupling and a real change-warning mechanism; you pay for it in ceremony**: a broker, a state-setup endpoint, per-field matcher decisions, and a provider verification job. For two teams this is a clear win; for twenty services it is an operational platform you must staff.
- **Pacts are *tolerant* by default: unlisted fields are ignored and extra requests cause a failure only if you configure it.** This is right for additive change and wrong for accidental behaviour change. Use `allowUnexpectedRequests: false` if you want strictness, accepting that it will flag benign additions.
- **Canary deployments** solve the "which pact version do I verify?" problem: the provider verifies against the consumer version that will actually be deployed (or soon will be), which is what `consumerVersionSelectors` with `deployedOrRelease` gives you.
- **State setup is real work and a real maintenance burden.** A state that takes 20 lines of seeding is a state nobody will add, and a missing state means a failed verification, which means teams learn to `--skip` verification, which kills the whole mechanism.
- **A generated mock cannot be the source of truth for the provider's own tests.** Pact verifies the provider; it does not improve the provider's test suite.

**Production considerations.**

- Run provider verification in **CI before deploy**, and block the pipeline on failure. A green main branch that has not been verified against canary consumer pacts is a broken gate.
- Tag consumer pacts: `main` (the current stable), `prod` (what is deployed), and feature branches. Then the provider can ask the broker "what am I about to break?"
- Use the **PactFlow** (or PactSafe-style) CI integration so the Slack notification and the PR comment come for free.
- **Break the glass for real-time blocking:** a `/pact-matrix` in the provider repo shows which consumer versions are green and which are pending. This is the highest-value visible artefact for API governance.
- Never put production data in provider states. Use fixed synthetic ids (as above).
- If a provider change *will* break a consumer, the right response is a versioned endpoint or a new optional field, then a deprecation window -- not a pact edit.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Gating CI on verification but allowing `--skip` or `pact.skip()` in a test, so verification silently stops running.
2. Provider states that are incomplete (you state "an order exists" but set up an order with no lines), so verification fails for reasons unrelated to the contract.
3. Using `expect.anything()` everywhere, which produces a pact that verifies almost nothing -- a green contract that constrains nothing.
4. Putting the `/__test/state` endpoint in production without gating it. It is a database-write endpoint with no auth.
5. Not tagging pacts, so the provider verifies against a stale `main` pact and the canary protection is fictional.
6. Treating an unexpected-field failure as a bug to suppress with `allowUnexpectedRequests: true`; it is usually a real signal that you changed a shape a consumer may depend on.
7. Verifying only in a nightly job, so a breaking change is merged and deployed before anyone checks.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is consumer-driven contract testing?"* -- The consumer's tests run against a mock provider generated from a pact, the pact is published, and the provider's CI replays every consumer pact against its real API to prove it has not broken anyone.
- 🟡 *"Why is that better than an integration test between the two services?"* -- It removes the runtime dependency: the consumer can build and test in parallel with no shared environment, and the provider gets told *before* deploy which specific consumer interaction will break, not just "something may be wrong".
- 🔴 *"How do you decide matcher strictness for a field?\"* -- By what the consumer's code does with it. Branching fields (`status`) get equality or a regex enum; free text gets `type`; collections get `eachLike`; optional fields get `nullOrType`. Never pin a value the consumer does not read, and never use `expect.anything()` as a shortcut.
- 🔴 *"Your provider verification started failing for every consumer. What do you do?\"* -- Check the pact diffs in the broker first: a shared field likely changed shape. If a provider state regressed (seeding changed), all pacts fail together, which is a state-setup bug, not a contract break. Only one consumer failing is a real contract break; all failing is almost always your state setup or your base URL.
- ⚫ *"When would you *not* add Pact?\"* -- Single deployable, single team, or a "microservice" that is a library. Also when the API is high-churn and you cannot afford the ceremony: the operational cost of a Pact platform is only justified when the cost of a cross-team break is high and frequent.

---

### 9.2.8 Testing auth flows and authorization boundaries

**Definition.**

Auth testing splits into two distinct disciplines that are constantly confused:

- **Authentication**: proving *who* the caller is. Tests cover: credential verification, token issuance, expiry, refresh, revocation, signature and algorithm handling, and the failure modes (missing, malformed, expired, wrong algorithm, revoked session, clock skew).
- **Authorization**: proving *what* the caller may do. Tests cover: role and permission checks, object-level ownership (can this user read *this* row?), tenant isolation, field-level redaction, rate limits, and the *absence* of privilege escalation.

**The rule that makes auth tests valuable: test the negative path first.** Almost every auth breach is a missing `return`, an `||` where `&&` was meant, a query that filters by `id` but not by `userId`, or a scope check that runs after the data is already returned. Positive-path tests pass on code with no authorisation at all.

**Mechanics -- the five auth boundary patterns and the test for each.**

| Pattern | The bug class | The test |
| --- | --- | --- |
