| **Route guard** (middleware) | missing middleware on a new route | enumerate every route and assert 401 without a token |
| **Scope/role check** | a new action added without a scope | matrix test: every action x every role |
| **Object-level ownership** | `findById(id)` without `userId` filter | create two users' objects; assert 404 (not 403) for the other user's |
| **Tenant isolation** | a query missing `tenantId` | same object ids in two tenants; assert cross-tenant reads fail |
| **Field-level redaction** | serialising the full row to a non-admin | assert the response omits `email`/`costBasis`/`internalNotes` |

```ts
// test/integration/auth/authz.matrix.test.ts
import { describe, expect, it } from 'vitest'
import { getPrisma } from '../helpers/db'
import { inTransaction } from '../helpers/transactional'
import { signToken } from '../../src/auth/tokens'

type Role = 'anon' | 'member' | 'support' | 'admin'
type Action = 'read:own' | 'read:any' | 'write:own' | 'write:any' | 'refund' | 'export'

// ── 1. The route-guard completeness test. Generate the list from the router, not by hand,
//      so a route added without a guard fails the build.
it('every route rejects an unauthenticated request with 401', async () => {
  const routes = listProtectedRoutes(app)   // see helper below
  expect(routes.length).toBeGreaterThan(20)  // guard against the helper silently returning []
  for (const route of routes) {
    const res = await api(route.path).send(route.probe(method(route)))
    expect(res.status, `${method(route)} ${route.path} must require auth`).toBe(401)
  }
})

// ── 2. The authorisation matrix. Generated from the permission table, so it cannot drift.
it('enforces the role x action matrix exactly', async () => {
  const matrix: Record<Role, Action[]> = {
    anon: [],
    member: ['read:own', 'write:own'],
    support: ['read:own', 'read:any'],
    admin: ['read:own', 'read:any', 'write:own', 'write:any', 'refund', 'export'],
  }
  const allActions: Action[] = ['read:own', 'read:any', 'write:own', 'write:any', 'refund', 'export']

  for (const role of Object.keys(matrix) as Role[]) {
    for (const action of allActions) {
      const res = await perform(action, role)
      const shouldBeAllowed = matrix[role].includes(action)
      expect([role, action, res.status]).toEqual(
        [role, action, shouldBeAllowed ? 200 : 403],
      )
    }
  }
})

// ── 3. Object-level ownership. The single highest-value authz test there is.
it('returns 404, not 403, for another user\'s order', async () => {
  await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ api, trx }) => {
    const otherUser = await trx.user.create({ data: { id: 'usr_other', email: 'o@example.test', role: 'member' } })
    const theirOrder = await trx.order.create({
      data: { id: 'ord_theirs', userId: otherUser.id, status: 'draft', currency: 'GBP',
              lines: { create: [{ sku: 'A', qty: 1, unitPriceCents: 999999 }] } },
    })

    const res = await api(`/api/orders/${theirOrder.id}`).get(auth('usr_00000001'))

    // 403 would confirm the order exists. 404 leaks nothing.
    expect(res.status).toBe(404)
    expect(res.body.error.code).toBe('NOT_FOUND')
  })
})

// ── 4. Tenant isolation with identical object ids.
it('never returns another tenant\'s data even when the id matches', async () => {
  await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ trx }) => {
    await trx.report.create({ data: { id: 'rep_1', tenantId: 'ten_A', body: 'A secret' } })
    await trx.report.create({ data: { id: 'rep_2', tenantId: 'ten_B', body: 'B secret' } })

    const repo = new ReportRepository(trx)
    const mine = await repo.findAllForTenant('ten_B')

    expect(mine.map((r) => r.id)).toEqual(['rep_2'])
    expect(mine.some((r) => r.body === 'A secret')).toBe(false)
  })
})

// ── 5. Field-level redaction.
it('never serialises internal fields to a non-admin', async () => {
  await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ api, trx }) => {
    const order = await trx.order.create({
      data: { id: 'ord_1', userId: 'usr_00000001', status: 'draft', currency: 'GBP',
              internalCostBasisCents: 3000, supplierName: 'ACME',
              lines: { create: [{ sku: 'A', qty: 1, unitPriceCents: 4200 }] } },
    })
    const asMember = await api(`/api/orders/${order.id}`).get(auth('usr_00000001', 'member'))
    const asAdmin = await api(`/api/orders/${order.id}`).get(auth('usr_admin', 'admin'))

    expect(Object.keys(asMember.body)).toEqual(expect.not.arrayContaining(['internalCostBasisCents', 'supplierName']))
    expect(asAdmin.body.internalCostBasisCents).toBe(3000)
  })
})
```

**Token and session tests.**

```ts
describe('token lifecycle', () => {
  it('rejects an expired access token with 401 TOKEN_EXPIRED', async () => {
    const expired = signToken({ sub: 'usr_1' }, { secret: SECRET, expiresIn: '-1s' })
    const res = await api('/api/me').get({ Authorization: `Bearer ${expired}` })
    expect(res.status).toBe(401)
    expect(res.body.error.code).toBe('TOKEN_EXPIRED')
  })

  it('rejects a token signed with the wrong algorithm (alg:none / RS256->HS256 confusion)', async () => {
    const forged = await forgeHs256Token({ alg: 'none' })
    const res = await api('/api/me').get({ Authorization: `Bearer ${forged}` })
    expect(res.status).toBe(401)
  })

  it('rejects a token whose audience is a different service', async () => {
    const wrongAud = signToken({ sub: 'usr_1', aud: 'internal-admin' }, { secret: SECRET })
    const res = await api('/api/me').get({ Authorization: `Bearer ${wrongAud}` })
    expect(res.status).toBe(401)
    expect(res.body.error.code).toBe('INVALID_AUDIENCE')
  })

  it('honours revocation: a token whose session was deleted stops working immediately', async () => {
    await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ api, trx }) => {
      await trx.session.create({ data: { id: 'sess_1', userId: 'usr_1', revokedAt: null } })
      const token = signToken({ sub: 'usr_1', sid: 'sess_1' }, { secret: SECRET })

      expect((await api('/api/me').get({ Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` })).status).toBe(200)

      await trz(sessionRevoke('sess_1'))
      expect((await api('/api/me').get({ Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` })).status).toBe(401)
    })
  })

  it('rotates the refresh token and invalidates the old one (reuse detection)', async () => {
    await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ api }) => {
      const first = await api('/api/auth/refresh').post().send({ refreshToken: seedRefreshToken() })
      expect(first.status).toBe(200)
      const second = await api('/api/auth/refresh').post().send({ refreshToken: first.body.refreshToken })
      expect(second.status).toBe(200)
      // Reusing the first refresh token must fail and revoke the family.
      const replay = await api('/api/auth/refresh').post().send({ refreshToken: first.body.refreshToken })
      expect(replay.status).toBe(401)
      expect((await api('/api/auth/refresh').post().send({ refreshToken: second.body.refreshToken })).status).toBe(401)
    })
  })
})

const trz = (p: Promise<unknown>) => p
```

**Generating the route list so the completeness test cannot drift.**

```ts
// test/integration/auth/listRoutes.ts -- derive the matrix from the router itself
import type { Express } from 'express'

/** Walks the Express router and returns every path it can serve, with a probe method. */
export function listProtectedRoutes(app: Express): { path: string; methods: string[] }[] {
  const out: { path: string; methods: string[] }[] = []
  const walk = (stack: any[], prefix = '') => {
    for (const layer of stack) {
      if (layer.route) {
        out.push({ path: prefix + layer.route.path, methods: Object.keys(layer.route.methods) })
      } else if (layer.name === 'router' && layer.handle?.stack) {
        walk(layer.handle.stack, prefix + (layer.regexp.source === '^\\/?(?=\\/|$)' ? '' : ''))
      } else if (layer.handle?.stack) {
        walk(layer.handle.stack, prefix)
      }
    }
  }
  walk((app as any)._router?.stack ?? (app as any).router?.stack ?? [])
  return out.filter((r) => !r.path.startsWith('/health') && !r.path.startsWith('/__'))
}
```

**Cookies and browser-visible auth.**

```ts
it('sets the session cookie with Secure, HttpOnly and SameSite=Lax, and no Domain', async () => {
  const res = await api('/api/auth/login').post().send({ email: 'ada@example.test', password: 'correct horse' })
  const cookies = res.headers['set-cookie'] as unknown as string[]
  const session = cookies.find((c) => c.startsWith('sid='))!
  expect(session).toMatch(/HttpOnly/i)
  expect(session).toMatch(/Secure/i)
  expect(session).toMatch(/SameSite=(Lax|Strict)/i)
  expect(session).not.toMatch(/Domain=/i)
})

it('sets the CSRF cookie without HttpOnly (the JS must read it) and requires the header on mutations', async () => {
  const login = await api('/api/auth/login').post().send(creds())
  const csrf = (login.headers['set-cookie'] as unknown as string[]).find((c) => c.startsWith('csrf='))!
  expect(csrf).not.toMatch(/HttpOnly/i)

  const withoutHeader = await api('/api/orders').post(login.headers['set-cookie'] as any).send(body())
  expect(withoutHeader.status).toBe(403)
  expect(withoutHeader.body.error.code).toBe('CSRF_FAILED')
})
```

**Rate limiting and abuse controls.**

```ts
it('rate limits by user id, not by IP, and returns Retry-After', async () => {
  const results: number[] = []
  let retryAfter: string | undefined
  for (let i = 0; i < 12; i++) {
    const res = await api('/api/search').get(auth('usr_00000001'))
    results.push(res.status)
    if (res.status === 429) retryAfter = res.headers['retry-after']
  }
  expect(results.filter((s) => s === 429).length).toBeGreaterThan(0)
  expect(Number(retryAfter)).toBeGreaterThan(0)
})
```

**When to use auth tests vs not.**

Always, and in the integration tier (or E2E for the browser-visible parts), because the risks are (a) revenue loss, (b) regulatory exposure, and (c) reputation. Specifically:
- **Unit**: the permission/role decision functions, the token *parsing* logic, password hashing verification.
- **Integration**: middleware, route guards, scope checks, object-level queries, tenant filters, redaction, rate limiting, refresh/revocation.
- **E2E**: the login UI flow, session persistence, logout, "remember me", and the SSO redirect flow.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Matrix tests are combinatorial.** 6 roles x 6 actions x 3 resource types is 108 tests. Generate them from a table (as above) and you get free completeness; hand-write them and you get partial coverage and a maintenance bill.
- **Generating the route list from the router** requires that the router is introspectable (Express and Fastify are; Next.js App Router and some frameworks are not). Where it is not, assert against a declared manifest that the app validates at boot -- i.e. make the manifest load-bearing.
- **Testing real cryptography in integration tests is slow** (bcrypt at cost 12 is ~300ms per hash). Use the lowest cost factor in tests via an env var, and test the *algorithm choice* in a unit test that asserts the cost factor is >= a threshold in production config.
- **Faking the clock for token expiry** must not weaken the test: assert with a genuinely expired token (signed with a past `exp`) rather than by faking `Date.now()` in the middleware, so the signature check is genuinely exercised.
- **Never mock the auth middleware in a test you are using to prove authorisation works.** The most dangerous test in a codebase is one that bypasses the very code it claims to verify.

**Production considerations.**

- Add the "every route requires auth" generated test to CI. It has caught more real auth holes than any manual test.
- Test **tenant isolation** with identical object ids across tenants; it is the only way to catch a missing filter.
- Assert **404 not 403** for cross-user access, and treat a 403 in a review as a finding.
- Add a **CSRF** test for every cookie-authenticated mutation route.
- Log an audit event for every authorisation failure, and assert the event was written in the test -- an authz failure with no audit trail is a security incident you cannot investigate.
- Keep a **`/api/__whoami`**-style debug route behind a build flag, and test it; it saves hours of auth debugging.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Testing only the happy path: `expect(status).toBe(200)` for the owner, nothing for anyone else.
2. Mocking the auth middleware with a `x-test-user` header "just for tests", and shipping the bypass.
3. Using a fixed `JWT_SECRET` that is also the production default in a `.env.example`.
4. `bcrypt` cost 12 in tests, so the suite takes minutes.
5. Returning 403 instead of 404 for another user's object, leaking existence.
6. Testing `bcrypt.compare` returns true, but never testing that a *wrong* password returns false and increments a lockout counter.
7. Forgetting the refresh-token rotation and reuse-detection test, which is where session hijacking lives.
8. Asserting `Set-Cookie` contains `HttpOnly` but not checking `Secure`, `SameSite`, or the absence of `Domain`.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you test authorisation?"* -- With a generated role x action matrix, plus object-level tests where user A cannot read user B's object (expecting 404), plus tenant-isolation tests with colliding ids, plus a generated test that every route rejects an unauthenticated request.
- 🟡 *"Why 404 instead of 403 for someone else's record?"* -- 403 confirms the record exists, which is an enumeration oracle. Return the same 404 shape as a genuinely missing record, with the same response time.
- 🔴 *"How do you make sure a newly added route has an auth guard?"* -- Derive the route list from the router (Express `app._router.stack`, Fastify's `printRoutes` or `onRoute` hook) and assert each returns 401 without a token. Where the framework is not introspectable, require routes to declare `auth: true` in a manifest that the app validates at boot, so an undeclared route refuses to register.
- 🔴 *"Where do you test auth: unit, integration, or E2E?"* -- All three, at different layers. Unit: the permission functions and token parsing. Integration: middleware, guards, object-level queries, tenant filters, revocation, rate limits -- with real tokens, not mocks. E2E: the login/logout/session-persistence flow through the browser, because cookie and CSRF behaviour only exists there.
- ⚫ *"How do you test a JWT algorithm-confusion attack?"* -- Forge a token with `alg: none` and one with an HS256 signature using your RS256 public key as the secret; both must be rejected with 401. Assert the *code* of the library's error is checked against an allowlist of accepted algorithms, not that "verification failed".

---

### 9.2.9 Service mocking without lying to yourself

**Definition.**

"Service mocking" means substituting a collaborator in a test. The failure mode called "lying to yourself" is when the substitute's behaviour does not match the real thing, so the test passes while production fails -- or worse, the substitute is *more permissive* than reality, so the test exercises a path that cannot occur.

**The single most important rule: every mock is an unverifiable claim about someone else's behaviour, and the claim must be verified somewhere.** The techniques below are ordered by how verifiable the claim is.

**Mechanics -- the fidelity ladder, with the verification mechanism for each rung.**

| Rung | Substitute | Fidelity | How you verify the claim |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Real object, real in-memory backend (`InMemoryUserRepo`) | high | the same repository interface tests run against both implementations (contract test) |
| 2 | Hand-written fake with real semantics (`FakeClock`, `FakePaymentGateway` that declines specific cards) | high | state assertions + a contract test |
| 3 | MSW at the network boundary | medium-high | your real adapter runs; protocol fidelity is verified by recorded cassettes + nightly sandbox |
| 4 | `vi.mock` of a method on your own class, returning a canned value | low | only the call shape; the behaviour is unverified |
| 5 | `vi.mock` of a whole module (a domain module) | very low | nothing; you are testing your test |
| 6 | A framework-level stub (a mock DB client, a mock queue that "succeeds") | very low | nothing, and it hides schema and semantic errors |

**Anti-patterns, with the specific failure each causes.**

```ts
// 🔴 A mock that is MORE permissive than reality. The test exercises a state that cannot occur.
vi.mock('./userRepo', () => ({ findById: vi.fn().mockResolvedValue({ id: 'u1', role: 'admin' }) }))
// Reality: findById returns null for a deleted user, and role can be null during onboarding.
// Consequence: your authz test never sees the null case, and production 500s on it.

// 🔴 A mock that returns a SHAPE your real code never returns. Your serialiser is never tested.
vi.mock('./stripe', () => ({ charge: vi.fn().mockResolvedValue({ id: 'ch_1' }) }))
// Reality (Stripe): { id, object: 'charge', amount, currency, status: 'succeeded', ... }
// Consequence: `res.status === 'succeeded'` is never exercised; the real response makes your
// code take the "unrecognised status" branch and the payment is never recorded.

// 🔴 A mock that always succeeds. The failure path is the one you most needed to test.
vi.mock('./emailSender', () => ({ send: vi.fn().mockResolvedValue(undefined) }))
// Consequence: queue-full, bounce, and rate-limit behaviour are untested until production.

// 🔴 A mock of your own domain module. Now the interesting code has zero tests.
vi.mock('../domain/calculateRefund', () => ({ calculateRefund: () => 0 }))
// Consequence: a test asserting `refund === 0` passes and means nothing.

// 🔴 A mock whose call assertions encode the CURRENT implementation, not a requirement.
expect(skuRepo.decrement).toHaveBeenCalledWith(sku, 1, { orderId, noRetry: true, source: 'checkout' })
// Consequence: adding a parameter to a helper breaks 60 tests. Refactor tax, zero defect detection.
```

**The four verification mechanisms that keep you honest.**

1. **Contract-test the double.** Write one suite that runs against both the fake and the real implementation.

```ts
// test/contract/user-repo.contract.ts -- runs against BOTH implementations
export function describeUserRepoContract(name: string, make: () => UserRepo) {
  describe(name, () => {
    let repo: UserRepo
    beforeEach(async () => { repo = make(); await repo.reset() })

    it('returns null for an unknown id (never throws, never a stub object)', async () => {
      expect(await repo.findById('usr_missing')).toBeNull()
    })
    it('returns the full record including nullable fields, with explicit nulls', async () => {
      const created = await repo.create({ email: 'a@b.test', displayName: null })
      const found = await repo.findById(created.id)
      expect(found).toEqual({ id: created.id, email: 'a@b.test', displayName: null, role: null })
    })
    it('rejects an update that violates a unique constraint with a typed error', async () => {
      await repo.create({ email: 'dup@b.test', displayName: 'A' })
      await expect(repo.create({ email: 'dup@b.test', displayName: 'B' }))
        .rejects.toThrow(UniqueViolation)
    })
    it('does not return deleted records', async () => {
      const u = await repo.create({ email: 'x@b.test', displayName: 'X' })
      await repo.softDelete(u.id)
      expect(await repo.findById(u.id)).toBeNull()
    })
  })
}

describeUserRepoContract('InMemoryUserRepo', () => new InMemoryUserRepo())
describeUserRepoContract('PrismaUserRepo', () => new PrismaUserRepo(getPrisma()))   // in the integration suite
```

2. **Use recorded fixtures from the real system.** For outbound third-party clients, record a real response once and assert the fake reproduces it.

```ts
// A cassette captured from the provider's sandbox, sanitised.
export const stripeChargeSucceeded = {
  id: 'ch_3Px', object: 'charge', amount: 4200, amount_captured: 4200,
  currency: 'gbp', status: 'succeeded', captured: true, refunded: false,
  payment_method: 'pm_1', outcome: { network_status: 'approved_by_network', type: 'authorized' },
} as const

it('treats a captured charge with outcome.authorized as paid', () => {
  const result = mapChargeToPaymentResult(stripeChargeSucceeded)
  expect(result).toEqual({ state: 'paid', providerRef: 'ch_3Px' })
})
```

3. **Type the double as the real port and forbid divergence.**

```ts
// eslint-disable-next-line @typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any
export class FakePaymentGateway implements PaymentGateway { /* must satisfy the port */ }
// If the port changes, this class fails to compile. That is the whole point.
```

4. **Assert the negative space.** For every mock, assert the paths that the mock would *not* let you reach: timeouts, cancellation, malformed responses, partial responses, HTTP 429, HTTP 5xx, connection reset.

```ts
it('treats a 429 with Retry-After as retryable and does not fail the order', async () => { /* MSW 429 */ })
it('treats a malformed provider body as a permanent failure, not a retry', async () => { /* MSW invalid JSON */ })
it('does not double-charge when the provider times out after capture (idempotency key required)', async () => { /* delay */ })
```

**"Do not mock the thing you own to test the thing you own."** A useful heuristic: *a mock is legitimate when the dependency is expensive, nondeterministic, or outside your control; a mock is a smell when it is cheap, deterministic, and inside your control.*

| Dependency | Inside your control? | Verdict |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Math.random()` | no (nondeterministic) | mock/inject |
| `Date.now()` | no (nondeterministic) | mock/inject |
| Your own pure function | yes, cheap, deterministic | never mock |
| Your own validator | yes, cheap, deterministic | never mock |
| Postgres | no (external engine) | real, in the integration tier |
| Stripe | no (third party) | MSW / cassette |
| Your own repository, in a unit test of a service | yes, cheap-ish | in-memory fake, contract-tested |

**When to use service mocks vs real dependencies.**

Mock (at the network boundary) when: the third party is unavailable, slow, billable, rate-limited, or requires credentials; when you need a specific failure you cannot trigger on demand; and when you are testing your client in isolation.

Use the real thing when: the semantics of the dependency are the risk (SQL, TTL, transaction isolation, message ordering); when you are testing a claim about the provider's behaviour; and whenever the tier allows it.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Mocks are fast and precise; reality is slow and messy.** The cost of a mock is that it is a hypothesis. The cost of reality is a 3-second test, a container, and a schema you have to keep in sync. You pay the second cost exactly once per dependency, in the contract test, and then you get the first cost everywhere else.
- **A contract test is a real, ongoing cost** that is easy to forget. It is, however, dramatically cheaper than the incident it prevents ("we assumed the provider returns 200 on a soft decline; it returns 202 with `status: pending`").
- **A mock-heavy suite is a knowledge transfer problem.** The next engineer must reconstruct the real behaviour from the mocks. Where you can, prefer MSW so that the real adapter code is the thing being described.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Mocking your own pure function, so the logic is never tested.
2. A mock whose return value is narrower than reality, so your real serialisation/deserialisation path is never executed.
3. Asserting on the mock's calls instead of the SUT's output, so the test breaks on every refactor and catches no bugs.
4. `mockResolvedValue(undefined)` for a function whose real return is used, so the SUT crashes on `undefined.length` in the test but not in production (or vice versa).
5. A `Date`-returning mock that returns a `string`, so `toEqual` deep-equality behaves differently than with the real type.
6. Not testing the failure paths a mock makes hard to reach (timeout, abort, 5xx).
7. A fake whose default state is "empty" when the real system usually returns "some data", so the empty path is over-tested and the populated path is not.
8. Never running the same contract suite against the real implementation, so the fake's divergence goes unnoticed for a year.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you know your mock is accurate?"* -- You run a contract test: the same suite against the fake and the real implementation, so any divergence in return shape, null behaviour, and error types fails a test. For third parties, you also record a real response and a nightly sandbox run.
- 🟡 *"When is mocking your own code acceptable?"* -- Almost never, and never for pure or deterministic logic. It is acceptable when the collaborator is expensive to construct (a database client) and you are testing a pure service above it, provided the fake is contract-tested and in-memory.
- 🔴 *"A test passes with a mock and fails in production. What are the three most likely causes?"* -- (1) The mock returns a shape the real one never returns (or omits nullable fields). (2) The mock's success path is the only path you tested. (3) The real dependency has a timing or ordering behaviour the mock has none of (eventual consistency, 202-then-200, rate limits). Fix with a contract test, negative-path tests, and recorded fixtures.
- 🔴 *"Explain a mock that is more dangerous than no mock."* -- A permissive mock. `findById` always returns an object, so the null-handling branch is never executed in tests but runs constantly in production; the tests then certify behaviour that cannot occur. A missing mock at least fails loudly.
- ⚫ *"How do you handle mocking a dependency that changes its API?"* -- Contract-test the double, generate the double's types from the real client when the vendor ships a typed SDK, and pin the vendor version in tests (`^3.4.0` plus a `pnpm.overrides` lock), so a vendor upgrade is a deliberate PR that must fix the double. Record a nightly sandbox run that fails loudly on shape change.

---

### 9.2.10 Error simulation and testing failure paths

**Definition.**

**Error simulation** is the deliberate production of failure conditions in a test so the system's failure *behaviour* is verified: the error contract, the cleanup, the retry, the observability, and the absence of partial state. It is the most neglected area of most test suites, and it is where the most expensive production incidents live, because a failure path is a path that is exercised by nobody until it matters.

**Mechanics -- the eight failure classes you must be able to produce, and how.**

| Failure class | How to produce it | What you must assert |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Downstream HTTP 4xx/5xx** | MSW `HttpResponse.json({...}, { status: 503 })` | your error mapping, your retry policy, no partial state |
| **Downstream timeout** | MSW `delay('infinite')` or a client timeout below the handler delay | 504 to the client, no leaked timers, no hanging request |
| **Connection reset / DNS failure** | a local TCP server that destroys the socket (`net.createServer().on('connection', s => s.destroy())`) | 502/503, not a 500, and the error is classified as *retryable* |
| **Malformed response** | MSW returning invalid JSON or a missing required field | 502 with a generic message; no `JSON.parse` error leaking |
| **Slow dependency** | MSW `delay(3000)` with a client timeout of 1000 | timeout path; also assert the *total* request time is bounded |
| **Database error** | `SET CONSTRAINTS` violation, a killed backend (`SELECT pg_terminate_backend(pid)`), or a trigger that raises | transaction rollback, no orphan rows, 500 with a request id |
| **Resource exhaustion** | a queue at capacity, a pool at max, a rate limit 429 | 429 + `Retry-After`, no unbounded retry, no memory growth |
| **Partial success** | a batch endpoint where item 3 of 5 fails | documented all-or-nothing vs partial semantics, and the response says which |

**Runnable code -- the failure-path suite for an outbound client, done properly.**

```ts
// test/integration/failures/downstream.test.ts
import { describe, expect, it, vi } from 'vitest'
import { http, HttpResponse, delay } from 'msw'
import { createServer } from 'node:net'
import { server } from '../helpers/server'

const PROVIDER = 'https://payments.example.test'

// ── A. 5xx with a retry-after: retried, then surfaced
it('retries a 503 exactly twice with jittered backoff, then returns 502 to the caller', async () => {
  let calls = 0
  server.use(
    http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/charges`, () => {
      calls += 1
      return HttpResponse.json({ error: 'unavailable' }, { status: 503, headers: { 'Retry-After': '0' } })
    }),
  )
  const client = createPaymentClient({ maxAttempts: 3, backoff: () => 1, jitter: () => 0 })
  await expect(client.charge({ amountCents: 100, idempotencyKey: 'k1' })).rejects.toMatchObject({
    kind: 'upstream', retryable: true, upstreamStatus: 503,
  })
  expect(calls).toBe(3)
})

// ── B. 4xx is permanent: no retry, distinct error kind
it('does not retry a 422 and classifies it as a permanent rejection', async () => {
  let calls = 0
  server.use(
    http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/charges`, () => {
      calls += 1
      return HttpResponse.json({ error: { code: 'amount_exceeds_limit' } }, { status: 422 })
    }),
  )
  const client = createPaymentClient({ maxAttempts: 3, backoff: () => 1 })
  await expect(client.charge({ amountCents: 10_000_000, idempotencyKey: 'k1' }))
    .rejects.toMatchObject({ kind: 'rejected', retryable: false, upstreamStatus: 422 })
  expect(calls).toBe(1)
})

// ── C. Timeout: bounded, and the request does not hang
it('times out at 1500ms, aborts the socket, and reports upstream_timeout', async () => {
  server.use(http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/charges`, async () => { await delay('infinite'); return new HttpResponse() }))
  const client = createPaymentClient({ timeoutMs: 1500, maxAttempts: 1 })
  const started = performance.now()
  await expect(client.charge({ amountCents: 100, idempotencyKey: 'k1' }))
    .rejects.toMatchObject({ kind: 'timeout', retryable: true })
  expect(performance.now() - started).toBeLessThan(2500)
})

// ── D. Real socket destruction: MSW cannot produce this, so use a real server
it('classifies ECONNRESET as a retryable transport error, not a 500', async () => {
  const evil = createServer((socket) => socket.destroy())      // reset immediately
  await new Promise<void>((r) => evil.listen(0, '127.0.0.1', r))
  const port = (evil.address() as { port: number }).port
  const client = createPaymentClient({ baseUrl: `http://127.0.0.1:${port}`, maxAttempts: 2, backoff: () => 1 })

  await expect(client.charge({ amountCents: 100, idempotencyKey: 'k1' }))
    .rejects.toMatchObject({ kind: 'transport', retryable: true })
  await new Promise<void>((r) => evil.close(() => r()))
})

// ── E. Malformed body: no JSON.parse leak, no 500
it('returns 502 with a generic message when the provider returns invalid JSON', async () => {
  server.use(http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/charges`, () => new HttpResponse('<html>oops</html>', {
    status: 200, headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
  })))
  const client = createPaymentClient({ maxAttempts: 1 })
  const err = await client.charge({ amountCents: 100, idempotencyKey: 'k1' }).catch((e) => e)
  expect(err).toMatchObject({ kind: 'malformed_response' })
  expect(err.message).not.toMatch(/JSON|Unexpected token/i)
})

// ── F. Failure inside a workflow leaves NO partial state
it('leaves no pending payment record when the provider call fails', async () => {
  server.use(http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/charges`, () => HttpResponse.json({}, { status: 500 })))
  await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ api, trx }) => {
    const orderId = await seedPayableOrder(trx)
    const res = await api('/api/checkout').post(auth()).send({ orderId, cardToken: 'tok_ok' })
    expect(res.status).toBe(502)
    const order = await trx.order.findUniqueOrThrow({ where: { id: orderId } })
    expect(order.status).toBe('pending')                 // not 'paid', not 'payment_failed' from a half-write
    expect(await trx.payment.count({ where: { orderId } })).toBe(0)
  })
})

// ── G. Idempotency: a retry after an ambiguous failure must not double-charge
it('reuses the idempotency key across retries so a timeout cannot double-charge', async () => {
  const seen: string[] = []
  server.use(http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/charges`, async ({ request }) => {
    seen.push(request.headers.get('Idempotency-Key')!)
    if (seen.length === 1) { await delay('infinite'); return new HttpResponse() }
    return HttpResponse.json({ id: 'ch_once', status: 'succeeded' }, { status: 201 })
  }))
  const client = createPaymentClient({ timeoutMs: 200, maxAttempts: 2, backoff: () => 1 })
  const result = await client.charge({ amountCents: 100, idempotencyKey: 'idem_abc' })
  expect(result.status).toBe('succeeded')
  expect(seen).toEqual(['idem_abc', 'idem_abc'])   // same key both times
})

// ── H. The whole request is bounded even when everything is slow
it('bounds the total request time when every dependency times out', async () => {
  server.use(
    http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/charges`, async () => { await delay('infinite') }),
    http.get(`${PROVIDER}/v1/customer`, async () => { await delay('infinite') }),
  )
  const res = await api('/api/checkout').post(auth()).send({ orderId: 'ord_1', cardToken: 'tok_ok' })
  expect(res.status).toBe(504)
})
```

**Database failure simulation.**

```ts
// A unique-constraint violation mid-transaction
it('rolls back the whole transaction when a line insert violates a constraint', async () => {
  await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ trx, api }) => {
    await trx.sku.createMany({ data: [{ code: 'A', name: 'A' }] })
    // 'A' already exists -> the second line insert violates the unique index
    const res = await api('/api/orders').post(auth()).send({
      lines: [{ sku: 'A', qty: 1, unitPriceCents: 100 }, { sku: 'A', qty: 1, unitPriceCents: 100 }],
    })
    expect(res.status).toBe(409)
    expect(res.body.error.code).toBe('DUPLICATE_SKU')
    expect(await trx.order.count()).toBe(0)          // the order itself was not persisted
  })
})

// A killed backend: proves your retry/circuit-breaker works
it('recovers when the database connection is killed mid-request', async () => {
  const prisma = getPrisma()
  const myPid = await prisma.$queryRaw<{ pid: number }[]>`SELECT pg_backend_pid() as pid`
  server.use(...)   // n/a
  const [backendPid] = await prisma.$queryRawUnsafe<number[]>(
    `SELECT pid FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE application_name = 'app_test' AND pid <> ${myPid[0]!.pid} LIMIT 1`,
  )
  await prisma.$executeRawUnsafe(`SELECT pg_terminate_backend(${backendPid})`)
  // The next request should succeed once the pool recycles the dead connection.
  await expect(api('/api/orders').get(auth())).rejects.toThrow()   // or succeeds: assert your policy
})

// A trigger that raises: simulate an application-level invariant violation
it('surfaces a database check-constraint violation as a 422, not a 500', async () => {
  await inTransaction(getPrisma(), async ({ trx, api }) => {
    await trx.$executeRawUnsafe(`
      ALTER TABLE orders ADD CONSTRAINT orders_no_negative_total
      CHECK (total_cents >= 0) NOT VALID
    `)
    const res = await api('/api/orders').post(auth()).send({ lines: [], refundCents: 999_99 })
    expect(res.status).toBe(422)
    expect(res.body.error.code).toBe('INVARIANT_VIOLATION')
  })
})
```

**Observability assertions -- the failure path you almost never test.**

```ts
it('logs the failure with the request id, the upstream status, and no PII or secrets', async () => {
  const logs: Record<string, unknown>[] = []
  const spy = vi.spyOn(logger, 'error').mockImplementation((meta: any) => { logs.push(meta) })
  server.use(http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/charges`, () => HttpResponse.json({}, { status: 500 })))

  const res = await api('/api/checkout').set('X-Request-Id', 'req_abc').post(auth()).send({ orderId: 'ord_1' })

  expect(res.status).toBe(502)
  expect(spy).toHaveBeenCalled()
  const entry = logs[0]!
  expect(entry.requestId).toBe('req_abc')
  expect(entry.upstreamStatus).toBe(500)
  expect(entry.durationMs).toBeGreaterThan(0)
  const serialised = JSON.stringify(entry)
  expect(serialised).not.toMatch(/tok_[a-zA-Z0-9]+/)      // no card tokens
  expect(serialised).not.toMatch(/ada@example\.test/)      // no PII
  expect(serialised).not.toMatch(/BEGIN (RSA )?PRIVATE KEY/)
  spy.mockRestore()
})

it('increments the upstream-failure metric with a label that does not explode in cardinality', async () => {
  const counter = new InMemoryMetrics()
  const client = createPaymentClient({ metrics: counter, maxAttempts: 1 })
  server.use(http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/charges`, () => HttpResponse.json({}, { status: 503 })))
  await client.charge({ amountCents: 1, idempotencyKey: 'k' }).catch(() => {})
  expect(counter.count('payments.upstream.failure', { provider: 'stripe', status: '5xx' })).toBe(1)
})
```

**Testing partial failure in a batch.**

```ts
it('processes a 5-item batch atomically and reports which item failed', async () => {
  server.use(
    http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/bulk-refunds`, () =>
      HttpResponse.json({ error: { code: 'refund_too_old', itemIndex: 2 } }, { status: 422 }),
    ),
  )
  const res = await api('/api/refunds/bulk').post(auth()).send({
    items: Array.from({ length: 5 }, (_, i) => ({ paymentId: `pay_${i}`, amountCents: 100 })),
  })
  expect(res.status).toBe(422)
  expect(res.body.error).toMatchObject({ code: 'REFUND_BATCH_REJECTED', failedIndex: 2 })
  expect(await prisma.refund.count()).toBe(0)      // atomic: nothing was refunded
})
```

**The reliability-pattern tests you should always have** (these are the ones that fire at 3am):

```ts
it('opens the circuit after 5 consecutive failures and short-circuits without calling upstream', async () => {
  let calls = 0
  server.use(http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/charges`, () => { calls += 1; return HttpResponse.json({}, { status: 500 }) }))
  const client = createPaymentClient({ circuit: { failureThreshold: 5, openMs: 1000 }, maxAttempts: 1 })

  for (let i = 0; i < 5; i++) await client.charge({ amountCents: 1, idempotencyKey: `k${i}` }).catch(() => {})
  expect(calls).toBe(5)
  await client.charge({ amountCents: 1, idempotencyKey: 'k6' }).catch(() => {})
  expect(calls).toBe(5)                          // short-circuited
  await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 1100))
  await client.charge({ amountCents: 1, idempotencyKey: 'k7' }).catch(() => {})
  expect(calls).toBe(6)                          // half-open probe
})

it('propagates an AbortSignal to the fetch and stops work on client disconnect', async () => {
  const ac = new AbortController()
  server.use(http.post(`${PROVIDER}/v1/charges`, async () => { await delay('infinite') }))
  const p = client.charge({ amountCents: 1, idempotencyKey: 'k' }, { signal: ac.signal })
  ac.abort()
  await expect(p).rejects.toMatchObject({ kind: 'aborted' })
})

it('degrades gracefully: checkout still succeeds when the recommendation service is down', async () => { /* ... */ })
```

**When to focus on failure paths.**

Focus when: the dependency is critical-path money or safety; the failure mode is ambiguous (timeouts, partial writes); you have an SLO; or you have been paged for this before. **Every production incident you have ever had is a test you did not write.** Build a catalogue of your incidents and make each one a test.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Failure tests are the highest-value and least-maintained tests in most suites.** They tend to be written once during an incident and then rot as the code changes shape. Budget for rewriting them when you refactor the client.
- **Simulating real socket-level failures requires real servers**, which is slower and less portable than MSW. Use MSW for protocol-level failures and a real `net.Server` for transport-level ones.
- **Injecting timeouts via `delay('infinite')` in MSW is real time.** A 1500ms timeout test takes 1.5s. With 20 such tests that is 30 seconds. Consider `delay(10)` with a client timeout of 5ms for speed, accepting that it tests the timeout logic rather than the exact duration, and keep one test with realistic numbers.
- **Killing a database backend is genuinely disruptive** -- it will fail *other* tests running in parallel. Only do it in a dedicated, serialised suite.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Testing only the 4xx paths, because they are easy to trigger with a mock, and never the 5xx/timeout/reset paths.
2. Asserting only the status code on the error path, not the error code, the log, and the metric.
3. No assertion that partial state was *not* written, so a half-completed workflow ships.
4. Not testing retry, so a retry storm against a struggling dependency is discovered in production.
5. Not testing idempotency, so a timeout-after-capture double-charges a customer.
6. `await sleep(2000)` to "let the retry happen", which is both slow and flaky.
7. Leaking the upstream error body to the client (`res.json(e.body)`), which leaks internal structure and sometimes PII.
8. High-cardinality metric labels (a user id, an order id) that melt your metrics backend during exactly the incident you need them for.
9. A test that kills the database while 7 other workers are running, generating seven unrelated failures that get blamed on your change.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you test that your code handles a downstream timeout?"* -- Make the handler hang (MSW `delay('infinite')`), set a client timeout below it, and assert three things: the client receives your mapped error (504, not 500), the total elapsed time is bounded, and no partial state was persisted.
- 🟡 *"Why is a 4xx not retried but a 5xx is?"* -- A 4xx is a statement about the request: retrying the identical request produces the identical 4xx. A 5xx is a statement about the server's state and may be transient. The exception is 429 and 503 with a `Retry-After`, which are explicitly retryable signals from the server.
- 🔴 *"How do you test a database failure?"* -- Induce a real one: violate a unique or check constraint, `pg_terminate_backend` your own connection in a dedicated serialised suite, or add a temporary trigger that raises. Then assert the transaction rolled back, no orphan rows exist, the client got a 500 with a request id, and the error was logged without PII.
- 🔴 *"Your provider returns 202 with a pending status and then 200 with succeeded. Your client only handles 200. How do you catch it?"* -- Record both real responses from the sandbox as fixtures and assert the client's state machine handles every observed status. Add a nightly sandbox test that re-records and diffs against the fixtures, so a new provider status fails CI the day it appears.
- ⚫ *"How do you test a circuit breaker without waiting real time?"* -- Inject the clock (`FakeClock`) and the state persistence, then drive the transition table: closed -> failures -> open -> advance the clock past `openMs` -> half-open -> success -> closed. Assert the upstream call count at each step, which is the assertion that actually proves the breaker works.

---

## 9.3 End-to-End Testing

### 9.3.1 What E2E tests are actually for

**Definition.**

An **end-to-end (E2E) test** drives a real user interface (usually a real browser) against a real running system, performing a realistic user journey and asserting on user-visible outcomes. E2E is the only tier that can verify the parts of your system that no other tier touches: the actual bundle that ships, the service worker, the CSP, the CORS configuration, the reverse proxy, DNS and TLS, the real cookie flags, third-party scripts, browser storage, the auth redirect flow, and the fact that the pieces are wired together at all.

**The correct mental model: E2E is a *sample*, not a proof.** You cannot test every journey. A suite of 300 E2E tests that takes 40 minutes and fails 3% of the time will be ignored, retried into green, and eventually deleted. A suite of 20 E2E tests covering the money paths, the auth path, and the top-5 conversions, that runs in 4 minutes with a 0.1% flake rate, is a genuine safety net.

**The three jobs E2E does that nothing else can.**

1. **Prove the whole thing works together, once.** A smoke test that the deployed artefact boots, authenticates, and completes the primary conversion. This is the "is the release actually shippable" gate.
2. **Verify the boundaries that only exist in the browser and in production configuration.** Login/logout with real cookies, OAuth redirect, file upload, WebSocket, geolocation, payment redirect (3DS), download, print, service worker offline, multi-tab state, back/forward navigation, deep links with server-side routing.
3. **Detect cross-cutting regressions nobody localises.** A migration that succeeds locally and fails against production data volume; a query that is 5ms on an empty table and 5s on a real one; a timezone assumption; a locale-specific format; a mobile viewport overflow; a CSP that blocks a third-party script.

**The three jobs E2E must NOT do.**

1. **Verify business rules.** Discount maths belongs in a unit/property test where it runs 5000 times in 40ms. In E2E you can check *one* example.
2. **Cover UI layout exhaustively.** That is component testing plus visual regression.
3. **Be a substitute for a fast feedback loop.** A developer who waits 6 minutes to learn their typo broke the build has been taught to batch work, and batching work is how defects enter.

**Mechanics -- the anatomy of a good E2E test.**

Four properties, and if you cannot satisfy all four, the test will cost more than it protects:

| Property | Meaning | Test for it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **User-observable only** | asserts on roles, text, URLs, and network, never on internal state | read the test: does it mention a React state variable? |
| **Independent** | creates and destroys its own data; order does not matter | run it alone, then with `--repeat-each 3` |
| **Deterministic** | no reliance on timing, ordering, or a specific random seed | run 20 times locally; must be 20/20 |
| **Fast** | under 30s, ideally under 10s | measure `test.info().duration` |

```ts
// The anatomy, in order: arrange (test API) -> act (user) -> assert (user-visible) -> cleanup (test API).
test('a returning customer can apply a saved card and see the order confirmed', async ({ page, api, signIn }) => {
  // ---- Arrange: create the world through the API, not through the UI.
  const user = await api.createUser({ email: 'ada@example.test', password: 'correct horse' })
  await api.seedSavedCard(user.id, '4242 4242 4242 4242')

  // ---- Act: sign in and check out like a human.
  await signIn(user.email, 'correct horse')
  await page.goto('/shop/widget')
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Add to basket' }).click()
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Go to basket' }).click()
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Checkout' }).click()
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: /pay with saved card/i }).click()
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Confirm order' }).click()

  // ---- Assert: what the user sees, and what was persisted.
  await expect(page.getByRole('heading', { name: /thank you for your order/i })).toBeVisible()
  await expect(page.getByTestId('order-total')).toHaveText('£42.00')
  await expect(page).toHaveURL(/\/orders\/ord_[a-z0-9]+$/)
  expect(await api.getOrderCountFor(user.id)).toBe(1)
})
```

**The coverage question E2E must answer honestly.** Ask: "if this test disappeared, what class of defect would reach production?" If the answer is "none that our other tiers would catch," the test is cost without benefit and should be deleted. A healthy E2E suite is small, and *shrinking* it is a sign of a maturing test strategy, not a sign of neglect.

**The E2E budget, in numbers.** A useful rule of thumb for a mid-size product: **10-30 E2E tests per major user journey**, running in under 10 minutes wall clock with 4-8 parallel workers, with a flake rate below 0.5%. Everything else is component, integration, or visual.

**When to use E2E vs not.**

Use E2E for: the primary conversion path per platform, authentication and session lifecycle, authorisation-visible behaviour (a user cannot see another's data *through the UI*), payment flows including 3DS/redirect, file upload/download, and the "does the release work at all" smoke test after deploy.

Do not use E2E for: rule verification, every CRUD screen, every validation message, edge-case data permutations, and anything you can prove faster and more precisely one tier down.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Confidence is the highest available.** Nothing else can tell you that the login *works on Safari on an iPhone*.
- **Cost is the highest available.** Minutes per test, browser startup, environment provisioning, and a flake rate that erodes trust.
- **Debugging is the worst.** A failure says "the checkout broke"; it does not say which of 12 services, which of 300 network calls, or which line.
- **The tests are coupled to the UI.** Every copy change breaks selectors, and every refactor costs a test migration. This is the strongest argument for keeping the suite small and for putting the assertions in Page Objects (9.3.5) so a copy change is a one-line fix.
- **Environment parity is a real risk.** A suite green against a mock API and a fake payment gateway proves nothing about production. Keep the *last mile* real: a real-ish auth, a real database, a sandbox payment provider.

**Production considerations.**

- **Run E2E against a production-like environment**, not against `localhost:3000` with a mocked API. That means: real database, real queue, real cache, real session store, MSW *only* for genuinely external third parties you cannot own a sandbox for.
- **Smoke E2E after every deploy** (2-3 tests, 60 seconds) as a release gate; **full E2E on main and pre-release**; **full E2E on PRs for user-facing changes only** (path-filtered by changed area).
- **Quarantine, do not delete.** A known-flaky test is moved to a `test.describe.skip` with a linked ticket and a named owner, and a CI check fails if the quarantine grows. Deleting hides the problem; quarantining makes it visible and bounded.
- **Never blanket-retry a failing E2E in CI without a threshold.** `retries: 1` is acceptable; `retries: 5` means the suite is decoration. Alert on the flake rate and on the "retried test count" metric.
- **Record artifacts on every failure**: trace (Playwright's `trace.zip`), screenshot, video (short, 5s rolling), network log, and the test's data seed. This is the difference between a 2-minute and a 2-hour triage.

**Common mistakes.**

1. E2E tests that create data through the UI, so setup takes 40s and fails on a validation message change.
2. Assertions on CSS classes, `data-testid`s of internal components, and network response bodies instead of user-visible text.
3. Test interdependence: test 2 depends on test 1's order existing.
4. `waitForTimeout(2000)` instead of waiting for a locator or a network response.
5. Running the full E2E suite on every PR regardless of what changed.
6. No environment-specific data seeding, so tests interfere with each other and with real data.
7. A "flake budget" of unlimited retries, which trains the team to ignore red.
8. Asserting only the happy path, because the E2E suite is too slow to add the failure paths -- so the failure paths ship untested everywhere.
9. Using `page.click()` on coordinates or CSS selectors rather than roles, so every markup change is a broken test.
10. Never running the suite in the same browsers/engines as production, so an engine-specific bug reaches users.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is an end-to-end test for?"* -- Verifying that the real, assembled, deployed system works for a real user journey: the actual bundle, real auth, real infrastructure, and the browser behaviours no other tier can see. It is a sample of journeys, not exhaustive coverage.
- 🟡 *"How many E2E tests should a project have?"* -- Ten to thirty per major journey, under ten minutes wall clock, with a sub-0.5% flake rate. If you have 300, you are using E2E to do work that belongs in component and integration tests, and paying for it in flake.
- 🔴 *"How do you debug an E2E failure?"* -- Read the trace, which gives you the DOM snapshot, screenshots at each action, the network log, and console output at the moment of failure. Then bisect: reproduce against a known-good build, narrow to a single journey step, and check whether the failure is data (bad seed), environment, timing, or product. If it is not reproducible, it is flake and belongs in the flake catalogue.
- 🔴 *"When is E2E the wrong choice?"* -- To verify business rules (use property tests), to verify layout across viewports (use visual regression), to verify every validation message (use component tests), or as the only feedback loop for a developer. It is the wrong choice whenever the same property can be proven faster and more precisely one tier down.
- ⚫ *"E2E suite is green but users report the checkout fails. How is that possible, and what do you change?"* -- Green E2E means the paths you wrote pass. You probably have no test for the actual failing condition: an expired card, a 3DS challenge, an address that fails validation, a session that timed out mid-flow, a currency that rounds oddly, a slow third-party script. Add those journeys, and add observability (trace IDs propagated to logs) so the next report is diagnosable in minutes. Also check whether your E2E environment's MSW handlers are more permissive than production.

---

### 9.3.2 Playwright: locators, auto-waiting, and isolation

**Definition.**

**Playwright** (Microsoft, 2020, Apache-2.0) is a browser automation framework that speaks CDP (Chromium), Juggler (Firefox) and WebKit (WebKit -- the engine Safari uses, on Linux). Its three defining properties are: **auto-waiting** locators, **browser-context isolation** (a fresh context, with its own cookies, storage and permissions, in milliseconds), and **tracing** (a full record of the run: DOM snapshots, screenshots, network, console, timing). Its test runner is a first-class test runner with projects, fixtures, retries, sharding, reporters, and parallelism across browsers.

**Mechanics -- the locator model, which is the whole philosophy.**

A **locator** is a lazy, auto-retrying query. Nothing happens until you act or assert. Each action polls the locator until it is actionable (visible, stable, enabled, receives events) or the timeout expires. This is why a Playwright test with no explicit waits is more reliable than a Selenium test with `sleep`s.

```ts
// The priority order, strongest first.
const byRole       = page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Place order' })        // accessible role + name
const byLabel      = page.getByLabel('Email address')                          // form control label
const byPlaceholder = page.getByPlaceholder('Search products')                  // weakest text-ish
const byText       = page.getByText('Order confirmed')                          // static text
const byAlt        = page.getByAltText('Company logo')                          // image
const byTestId     = page.getByTestId('order-total')                           // last resort
const byCss        = page.locator('.btn-primary')                               // almost always wrong
const byXpath      = page.locator('xpath=//div[contains(@class,"btn")]')        // almost always wrong

// Chaining, scoping, and filtering -- the power features.
await page.getByRole('row').filter({ hasText: 'Ada Lovelace' }).getByRole('button', { name: 'Edit' }).click()
await page.locator('article').filter({ has: page.getByRole('img', { name: 'broken' }) }).count()
await page.getByRole('listitem').nth(2).getByText('Delete').click()
await page.getByTestId('order').locator('css=dt:has-text("Total") + dd').innerText()

// Strictness: locators that match multiple elements FAIL, which is the point.
await expect(page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Save' })).toHaveCount(1)   // explicit
// page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Save' }).click()  ->  strict mode violation if 2 match
```

**Auto-waiting, and the two things that defeat it.**

```ts
// ✅ Auto-waits for clickability, visibility, stability (not animating), and enabled state.
await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Submit' }).click()

// ✅ Auto-waits for the text to appear and to be stable.
await expect(page.getByText('Order confirmed')).toBeVisible({ timeout: 10_000 })

// ❌ Defeats auto-waiting: waiting for a state the app never sets.
await expect(page.locator('#app')).toHaveClass('loaded')      // implementation detail

// ❌ Defeats auto-waiting: a JS condition unrelated to the DOM.
await page.waitForFunction(() => (window as any).__ready === true)   // you have coupled to internals

// ❌ The forbidden one: an unconditional sleep.
await page.waitForTimeout(3000)     // always a defect, always a flake source
```

**Web-first assertions** are the reason Playwright tests are short and reliable:

```ts
await expect(page).toHaveURL(/\/orders\/ord_/i)
await expect(page).toHaveTitle(/Checkout/)
await expect(locator).toBeVisible() / toBeHidden() / toBeEnabled() / toBeDisabled()
await expect(locator).toHaveText('£42.00')                  // full text (whitespace-normalised)
await expect(locator).toContainText('42.00')                // substring
await expect(locator).toHaveValue('ada@example.test')       // inputs
await expect(locator).toHaveAttribute('aria-expanded', 'true')
await expect(locator).toHaveClass(/is-invalid/)
await expect(locator).toHaveCount(2)
await expect(locator).toBeChecked()                          // checkboxes/radios
await expect(locator).toHaveJSProperty('scrollWidth', 300)
await expect(locator).toBeInViewport()
await expect(page).toHaveScreenshot('checkout.png', { maxDiffPixelRatio: 0.01 })
```

**Browser contexts: the isolation unit.** A `BrowserContext` is an incognito-like profile: its own cookies, `localStorage`, `sessionStorage`, IndexedDB, permissions, geolocation, locale, timezone, and colour scheme. Creating one takes ~50ms versus ~1s for a new browser. This is what makes parallel tests cheap and isolated.

```ts
const context = await browser.newContext({
  locale: 'en-GB',
  timezoneId: 'Europe/London',
  colorScheme: 'dark',
  viewport: { width: 1280, height: 800 },
  storageState: await saveStorageState('admin'),   // reuse an authenticated profile
  permissions: ['clipboard-read', 'geolocation'],
  geolocation: { latitude: 51.5, longitude: -0.12 },
  extraHTTPHeaders: { 'X-Test-Run': process.env.RUN_ID },
  recordVideo: { dir: 'videos/', size: { width: 1280, height: 800 } },
  trace: 'retain-on-failure',
  screenshot: 'only-on-failure',
})
const page = await context.newPage()
```

**Fixtures: the mechanism for arrange and cleanup.**

```ts
// playwright/fixtures.ts
import { test as base, type Page } from '@playwright/test'

export type Fixtures = {
  api: ApiClient
  signIn: (email?: string, password?: string) => Promise<void>
  adminPage: Page
  seed: Seeder
}

export const test = base.extend<Fixtures>({
  // API client bound to this test's own namespace -- full isolation, parallel-safe.
  api: async ({}, use, testInfo) => {
    const ns = `${process.env.RUN_ID ?? 'local'}-${testInfo.workerIndex}-${testInfo.repeatEachIndex}-${testInfo.title.hashCode()}`
    const api = new ApiClient({ baseURL: process.env.API_URL!, namespace: ns, auth: { token: process.env.E2E_TOKEN! } })
    await use(api)
    await api.cleanupNamespace()      // runs even on failure: this is the isolation guarantee
  },

  signIn: async ({ page, api }, use) => {
    await use(async (email = 'ada@example.test', password = 'correct horse') => {
      await api.createUser({ email, password })
      const user = await api.login({ email, password })
      // Set the session the same way the app does, so the test uses the real auth path.
      await page.goto('/api/dev/session')                 // a test-only endpoint, gated by E2E_TOKEN
      await page.evaluate((token) => {
        ;(window as any).__e2eToken = token
      }, user.token)
      await page.goto('/')
    })
  },

  adminPage: async ({ browser }, use) => {
    const context = await browser.newContext({ storageState: 'test/.auth/admin.json' })
    const page = await context.newPage()
    await use(page)
    await context.close()
  },

  seed: async ({ api }, use) => { await use(new Seeder(api)) },
})

export { expect } from '@playwright/test'
```

**The config that makes a suite fast and useful.**

```ts
// playwright.config.ts
import { defineConfig, devices } from '@playwright/test'

const isCI = !!process.env.CI

export default defineConfig({
  testDir: './e2e',
  outputDir: './e2e/.artifacts',           // per-test artifacts (traces, videos, screenshots)
  fullyParallel: true,                    // tests in a file may run in parallel across workers
  forbidOnly: isCI,
  retries: isCI ? 1 : 0,                  // exactly 1; anything more is hiding flake
  workers: isCI ? process.env.CI_WORKERS ?? 4 : undefined,
  timeout: 45_000,                        // per test
  expect: { timeout: 7_500 },             // per assertion -- do not set this high
  globalTimeout: 20 * 60_000,             // the whole run; catches a hung test
  reporter: isCI
    ? [['html', { open: 'never' }], ['github'], ['junit', { outputFile: 'e2e/junit.xml' }], ['blob']]
    : [['html', { open: 'never' }], ['list']],

  use: {
    baseURL: process.env.E2E_BASE_URL ?? 'http://127.0.0.1:3100',
    trace: 'retain-on-failure',           // the single most valuable setting
    screenshot: 'only-on-failure',
    video: 'retain-on-failure',
    actionTimeout: 10_000,
    navigationTimeout: 20_000,
    testIdAttribute: 'data-testid',
    ignoreHTTPSErrors: false,
    extraHTTPHeaders: isCI ? { 'X-Playwright-Run': process.env.RUN_ID ?? 'local' } : {},
  },

  expect: {
    toHaveScreenshot: { maxDiffPixelRatio: 0.01, animations: 'disabled', scale: 'css' },
  },

  // Projects = the browser x device matrix, and a stable set for visual baselines.
  projects: [
    { name: 'setup', testMatch: /global\.setup\.ts/ },
    { name: 'chromium', use: { ...devices['Desktop Chrome'] } },
    { name: 'firefox', use: { ...devices['Desktop Firefox'] } },
    { name: 'webkit', use: { ...devices['Desktop Safari'] } },
    { name: 'mobile-chrome', use: { ...devices['Pixel 7'] } },
  ],

  webServer: [
    {
      command: 'pnpm build && pnpm start:preview',
      url: 'http://127.0.0.1:3100/healthz',
      reuseExistingServer: !isCI,
      timeout: 180_000,
      env: { NODE_ENV: 'test' },
    },
  ],
})
```

**Network control, which is the escape hatch you must have.**

```ts
// Block third-party noise so failures and timings are yours, not Facebook's.
await context.route('**/*', (route) => {
  const url = route.request().url()
  if (/google-analytics|doubleclick|hotjar|segment\.io/.test(url)) return route.abort()
  return route.continue()
})

// Stub a specific API to test a degraded state.
await page.route('**/api/recommendations', (route) =>
  route.fulfill({ status: 200, contentType: 'application/json', body: JSON.stringify({ items: [] }) }),
)

// Simulate a slow dependency precisely.
await page.route('**/api/slow', async (route) => { await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 8000)); await route.fulfill({ json: {} }) })

// Abort to simulate an offline failure of a specific call.
await page.route('**/api/payment-intent', (route) => route.fulfill({ status: 502, body: 'upstream down' }))

// NEVER route your own app's API in the happy path: it turns E2E into an integration test with extra steps.
```

**Tracing and debugging.**

```ts
test('checkout', async ({ page }, testInfo) => {
  // Explicit steps show in the trace's timeline, which makes triage fast.
  await test.step('add to basket', async () => { /* ... */ })
  await test.step('pay with card', async () => { /* ... */ })
  // Later, inspect:
  //   npx playwright show-trace e2e/.artifacts/checkout-abc123/trace.zip
})
```

```bash
# Replay a failed test with the trace, headed, no retries, and the trace viewer attached.
npx playwright test e2e/checkout.spec.ts --project=chromium --headed --retries=0 \
  --trace=on --reporter=list

# Re-run only the failed tests from the last run (blob reporter).
npx playwright merge-reports ./blob-report
npx playwright test --last-failed

# Codegen: record a session and generate a starting spec. The fastest way to bootstrap.
npx playwright codegen --save-storage=auth.json https://e2e.example.test

# Install only the browsers you need (a real CI time saver).
npx playwright install --with-deps chromium
```

**When to use Playwright vs not.**

Use it when: you need real-browser verification of journeys, you need all three engines, you want first-class tracing, you want fast parallel execution with cheap isolation, or you are starting a new E2E suite (its ergonomics and debugging are the best available).

Consider Cypress when: your team already knows it, or your testing is heavily component/DOM-oriented and you value its time-travel debugging. Consider Selenium (9.3.4) only for a legacy suite, an unusual device/grid requirement, or a WebDriver-only environment.

**Trade-offs.**

- **WebKit on Linux is not Safari.** It is close (same engine lineage) but not identical: font rendering, media codecs, and some platform APIs differ. Visual baselines must be generated per platform, and you should keep one real-Mac or BrowserStack job for Safari if Safari matters.
- **`fullyParallel: true` inside a file** is a step change for suite size, and it *forces* per-test data isolation. Teams that enable it must adopt API-based setup and namespaced cleanup; otherwise they trade slowness for flake.
- **Retry = 1 is a policy, not a setting.** A suite with 3% flake and `retries: 2` will be green and worthless. Track the flake rate (`flaky-tests` reporter) and treat it as a P1.
- **Auto-waiting hides real UX problems** in the sense that it waits for *stability*; a spinner that never resolves simply times out. That is a feature. But auto-waiting cannot wait for a condition your app never expresses in the DOM, so a test that needs `waitForFunction` is telling you the app lacks an observable state.
- **Trace files are large** (5-30MB). Budget artifact storage, upload on failure only, and set a retention policy.

**Production considerations.**

- `globalSetup`: apply migrations, seed reference data, and create an admin `storageState` file (`test/.auth/admin.json`) once per run rather than logging in per test.
- Pass a per-run namespace header (`X-Playwright-Run`) so DB cleanup can delete exactly this run's rows, and set a scheduled janitor to delete rows older than 24h.
- Use `test.info().project.name` in data ids so a test cannot collide across browser projects.
- On failure, upload `trace.zip`, `screenshot`, `video`, and the JUnit XML to CI artifacts with a 7-day retention.
- Set `workers` to the number of available cores, not more, and reduce it for video-enabled runs.
- Pin the Playwright version and the browser build together (`@playwright/test` 1.5x ships pinned browsers); upgrade the browsers on a schedule with visual-baseline regeneration in the same PR.

**Common mistakes.**

1. `page.waitForTimeout(3000)` everywhere -- the single largest source of flake and slowness in E2E suites.
2. `page.locator('.css-class')` or XPath instead of roles, so every markup refactor is a test migration.
3. A single `storageState` for all tests, so state leaks (a cart left in the basket) between tests.
4. Creating a new browser per test rather than a new context.
5. `retries: 3` as a flake remedy.
6. Relying on test execution order, with `fullyParallel` off and a `test.describe.serial`.
7. `page.route` stubbing your own API in the happy-path tests, which makes the E2E suite prove nothing.
8. Generating visual baselines on macOS but comparing in Linux containers -- every test fails on fonts.
9. No `trace: 'retain-on-failure'`, so triage is guesswork from the failure message.
10. Sharing a database with production-like data, so a test run can corrupt data other runs depend on.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"Explain Playwright's auto-waiting."* -- Locators are lazy and retry: each action polls until the element is visible, stable (not animating), enabled, and hit-testable; each assertion polls until it passes. So you write no explicit waits and still get deterministic behaviour.
- 🟡 *"Why create a new BrowserContext per test rather than a new browser?"* -- A context is an isolated incognito-like profile (cookies, storage, permissions, locale, timezone) that takes milliseconds, versus a second or more for a new browser process. That is what makes per-test isolation affordable.
- 🔴 *"Your E2E suite is 3% flaky. Walk me through the fix."* -- (1) Turn on tracing for every run and read ten traces; (2) classify: data, environment, timing, or product; (3) kill every `waitForTimeout` and replace with a locator assertion; (4) enable `fullyParallel` and per-test namespaced data; (5) check for shared state (storageState, DB, ports); (6) set `retries: 1` and start tracking the flake rate; (7) add a CI check that fails when quarantine grows.
- 🔴 *"When is a `BrowserContext` not enough isolation?"* -- When the resource lives outside the browser: a database, a Redis key, a file on disk, a queue, a third-party sandbox account. The context isolates cookies and storage only. That is why the API-level namespace in the fixture is mandatory.
- ⚫ *"WebKit vs Safari?"* -- WebKit is the same engine, but not the same browser: font rendering, media codecs, and some platform integrations differ, and it runs on Linux. Use WebKit for the majority of coverage and keep one real-Safari job (Mac runner or a cloud device farm) for the Safari-specific residue.

---

### 9.3.3 Cypress: its model and where it differs

**Definition.**

**Cypress** (Cypress.io) is a browser-based end-to-end and component testing tool with a distinctive execution model. It runs *inside* the browser, in the same JS runtime as your application, which gives it two superpowers and one constraint.

**The superpowers.**
1. **Time travel.** Every test command is recorded with a snapshot of the DOM, the JavaScript state, network activity, and console output at that moment. When a test fails, you can hover backwards and inspect the application exactly as it was.
2. **Debuggability by construction.** `cy.pause()` opens a real debugger at that point in the test, and failures open the DevTools automatically.
3. **Deterministic command queue.** Commands are enqueued and executed in order, with retries on assertions (`should('be.visible')` retries for 4s by default), so you do not write waits.

**The constraint.** Because Cypress runs in the browser, it executes in the *same* page as your application. That means:
- It cannot run **multiple tabs** natively.
- It cannot visit **two superdomains in one test** (cross-origin navigation is restricted; `cy.visit` to a different origin is not supported in the same test).
- It is **not a real WebDriver**, so testing native browser features (the file picker, some OS dialogs, WebAuthn, real downloads in some modes) is harder.
- It requires a **proxy** in front of your app, which is a real deployment-shape difference (and occasionally a real source of CORS/cookie bugs that do not exist in production).

**Mechanics -- the vocabulary, which is the main learning curve.**

```ts
// Cypress
describe('checkout', () => {
  beforeEach(() => {
    cy.intercept('POST', '/api/orders').as('createOrder')     // "intercept" + "alias"
    cy.visit('/shop')
  })

  it('completes a purchase', () => {
    cy.get('[data-cy=add-to-basket]').click()                  // data-cy is the Cypress convention
    cy.contains('button', 'Checkout').click()                 // contains() = text + selector
    cy.get('#card').type('4242424242424242')                  // .type() is the user-like input
    cy.get('[data-cy=submit]').click()

    cy.wait('@createOrder').its('response.statusCode').should('eq', 201)   // alias wait
    cy.contains('Thank you for your order').should('be.visible')           // auto-retrying
  })

  it('fails fast on an API error', () => {
    cy.intercept('POST', '/api/orders', { statusCode: 500, body: { error: 'boom' } })
    cy.get('[data-cy=submit]').click()
    cy.contains("We couldn't complete your order").should('be.visible')
  })
})
```

**Where Cypress differs from Playwright, concretely.**

| Dimension | Cypress | Playwright |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Execution | in-browser, in the app's page/context | out-of-process, via CDP/Juggler/WebDriver-class protocols |
| Commands | queued, imperative chain | async/await, promise-like |
| Retrying assertions | `should()` retries by default | `expect().toX()` retries by default |
| Multi-tab | not supported | native |
| Cross-origin in one test | not supported | supported |
| iframes | `cy.iframe()` plugin-ish; `cy.origin()` for cross-origin | first-class `frameLocator()` |
| Shadow DOM | limited | first-class piercing locators |
| Component testing | **first-class and a major strength** | available via Vitest Browser Mode / CT |
| Parallelism | paid Cloud feature (free split via sharding is limited) | free, first-class, any CI |
| Trace viewer | time travel + DevTools, no separate trace artefact | `trace.zip` viewer |
| Browser support | Chromium-family, Firefox, Edge, WebKit (Electron-based runner) | Chromium, Firefox, WebKit |
| Best fit | component/DOM-heavy testing, single-tab journeys, rich debugging | multi-browser, multi-tab, parallel, E2E at scale |
| Bundle cost | large, must be in the dev build only | separate package, not shipped |

**Code that shows the model difference most clearly: waiting for something that is not an element.**

```ts
// Cypress: retrying assertion on a non-DOM value, via `should(callback)`
cy.get('[data-cy=spin]').should('not.exist')
cy.wrap(sessionStore.get('cartId')).should('equal', 123)   // no DOM required
cy.get('[data-cy=item]').should(($els) => {
  expect($els).to.have.length(3)                            // custom retryable assertion
})

// Playwright: same idea
await expect(page.locator('[data-cy=item]')).toHaveCount(3)
await expect.poll(() => sessionStore.get('cartId')).toBe(123)   // expect.poll for non-DOM state
```

**Mechanics -- `cy.intercept`, which is Cypress's MSW and its most useful feature.**

```ts
// Static stub
cy.intercept('GET', '/api/user', { statusCode: 200, body: { id: 'usr_1', name: 'Ada' } })

// Dynamic stub with a request handler
cy.intercept('GET', '/api/orders/*', (req) => {
  req.reply((res) => {
    res.send({ ...res.body, lines: [] })
  })
})

// Delay, for loading-state tests
cy.intercept('GET', '/api/slow', (req) => {
  req.on('response', (res) => res.setDelay(3000))     // delay the response
  req.continue()
})

// Fail the request entirely (network error)
cy.intercept('GET', '/api/fragile', { forceNetworkError: true })

// Alias for waiting and asserting
cy.intercept('POST', '/api/orders').as('createOrder')
cy.wait('@createOrder').its('request.body').should('deep.equal', expected)

// Add a header to every request
cy.intercept({ url: '**/api/**' }, (req) => { req.headers['x-e2e'] = '1'; req.continue() })
```

**Component testing in Cypress is its real differentiator.**

```tsx
// cypress/component/OrderSummary.cy.tsx -- mounts a component with real providers
import { mount } from 'cypress/react18'
import { MemoryRouter } from 'react-router-dom'

describe('<OrderSummary />', () => {
  it('renders the total', () => {
    mount(
      <MemoryRouter>
        <OrderSummary orderId="ord_1" />
      </MemoryRouter>,
    )
    cy.contains('Total: £42.00').should('be.visible')
  })

  it('shows an error state', () => {
    cy.intercept('GET', '/api/orders/ord_1', { statusCode: 503, body: {} })
    mount(<MemoryRouter><OrderSummary orderId="ord_1" /></MemoryRouter>)
    cy.contains('We could not load this order').should('be.visible')
  })
})
```

```ts
// cypress.config.ts
import { defineConfig } from 'cypress'
export default defineConfig({
  e2e: {
    baseUrl: 'http://127.0.0.1:3100',
    specPattern: 'cypress/e2e/**/*.cy.ts',
    supportFile: 'cypress/support/e2e.ts',
    viewportWidth: 1280,
    viewportHeight: 800,
    defaultCommandTimeout: 8000,
    retries: { runMode: 1, openMode: 0 },
    video: false,
    screenshotOnRunFailure: true,
    setupNodeEvents(on) {
      on('before:browser:launch', (browser, launchOptions) => {
        if (browser.family === 'chromium') launchOptions.args.push('--disable-dev-shm-usage')
        return launchOptions
      })
    },
  },
  component: {
    devServer: { framework: 'react', bundler: 'vite' },
    specPattern: 'cypress/component/**/*.cy.tsx',
    supportFile: 'cypress/support/component.tsx',
  },
})
```

**When to use Cypress vs Playwright.**

Cypress when: your team knows it, your testing is heavily component/DOM-oriented (its component testing with real providers and `cy.intercept` is excellent), your journeys are single-tab, and the debugging affordances are worth more to your team than multi-tab support and free parallelism.

Playwright when: you need multi-tab, cross-origin flows, real parallel execution at scale, or you are starting fresh and want the more capable default.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Cypress's in-browser model is a genuine architectural constraint**, not a preference: multi-tab, cross-origin, and some native browser features are simply out of reach. Migrating later is expensive.
- **Parallelism cost.** Free, first-class parallelism is Playwright's structural advantage. If you are on Cypress and need it, the honest options are Cypress Cloud (paid) or splitting specs across CI jobs (workable, more config).
- **Cypress bundles a browser and a large runtime into your app's dev build.** It is never shipped to production, but it does affect your dev bundle and must be conditionally imported.
- **Time travel is uniquely good for UI failures** and has no Playwright equivalent -- though Playwright's trace covers much of the same ground in a form that works in CI.
- **`cy.then()` and queue semantics** trip up people coming from Playwright, and mixing promises into the chain causes "returned a promise from a command" errors.

**Common mistakes.**

1. `cy.wait(1000)` -- Cypress's `cy.wait` with a number is discouraged in favour of alias waits; it is the same anti-pattern as `waitForTimeout`.
2. Using `data-cy` everywhere instead of accessible queries, which locks you to the test attribute.
3. `cy.get()` on an element inside an iframe without `cy.origin()` for cross-origin iframes.
4. Testing multi-tab behaviour and discovering Cypress cannot do it, after committing to it.
5. Adding a fixed `baseUrl` that differs from production's path prefix, so routing tests pass and production fails.
6. `cy.intercept` stubbing your own API in every test, making the suite prove only that your React app renders.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is Cypress's distinctive execution model, and what does it buy you?"* -- It runs inside the browser, in the same context as the app, which gives time travel (DOM/JS/network snapshots at every step) and first-class debugging, plus deterministic command queuing with auto-retrying assertions. The cost is no multi-tab, no cross-origin navigation in one test, and a proxy-based dev topology.
- 🟡 *"When would you choose Cypress over Playwright?"* -- When component/DOM testing is a large part of your suite and you value the debugging affordances, your journeys are single-tab, and the team is already productive in it. Not when you need multi-tab, heavy parallelism, or cross-origin flows.
