- 🔴 *"How do you test a multi-tab flow in Cypress, given its model?"* -- You cannot, natively. Options: (1) drive the second tab with direct Playwright/CDP from a separate process and coordinate through the API; (2) refactor the feature so cross-tab communication is mediated by the server or `BroadcastChannel` and test the *contract* rather than the second tab; (3) choose Playwright. Asserting on `localStorage` `storage` events is a proxy, not a real test.
- 🔴 *"`cy.wait(2000)` vs `cy.wait('@alias')`?"* -- The numeric wait is a fixed sleep that is slow when unnecessary and flaky when insufficient. Alias waits block until the named request completes, then let you assert on its request and response, which is both faster and a real assertion.
- ⚫ *"What is `cy.origin` for?"* -- Cypress's escape hatch for interacting with a cross-origin iframe: you must re-declare your commands inside the callback because Cypress cannot inject its script into another origin. It works, but it is a signal that the architecture crosses origins in a way that deserves a second look.

---

### 9.3.4 Selenium and the WebDriver protocol, for awareness

**Definition.**

**Selenium** is the long-standing, cross-browser automation standard. **Selenium 4** is a W3C-compliant implementation of the **WebDriver** protocol: the test process talks to a `WebDriver` server (e.g. `chromedriver`, `geckodriver`, `safaridriver`, or a Selenium Grid), which drives the browser. The protocol is an HTTP/JSON or W3C-Remote-Endpoint API, which is why Selenium can drive browsers on other machines, in other languages, and in a device grid.

**Why you still need to know it (2026).**
- It is the **only** option for some environments: real Safari on real macOS via `safaridriver` (managed by Apple), legacy devices, and some enterprise grids.
- It is the substrate of **Appium**, which drives native and hybrid mobile apps with the same WebDriver model. Any mobile native-testing strategy you meet will use it.
- It is the **interop reference** for any automation tool: WebDriver is the standard the other tools are measured against.
- Many organisations have an existing Selenium suite or grid, and migrating is a project, not a decision.
- Understanding the protocol makes you understand what the modern tools abstract away (the session, the element lookup, the shadow root, the action sequence, the document readiness).

**Mechanics -- the W3C WebDriver session lifecycle.**

```
POST /session  { capabilities: { alwaysMatch: { browserName: "firefox", ... } } }   -> { value: { sessionId, capabilities } }
POST /session/{id}/url         { url: "https://..." }
POST /session/{id}/element    { using: "css selector", value: "#submit" }             -> { value: { element-6066-11e4-a52e-4f735466cecf: "<node id>" } }
POST /session/{id}/element/{eid}/click
GET  /session/{id}/element/{eid}/text
POST /session/{id}/execute/sync   { script: "return document.title", args: [] }
DELETE /session/{id}
```

**Runnable code -- the modern Selenium 4 API, in TypeScript, and what has changed.**

```ts
// e2e/selenium/login.test.ts  (run with mocha/vitest + WebdriverIO, or the official selenium-webdriver client)
import { Builder, By, until, Key } from 'selenium-webdriver'
import chrome from 'selenium-webdriver/chrome.js'

describe('login (Selenium 4 / W3C WebDriver)', () => {
  let driver: Awaited<ReturnType<Builder['build']>>

  before(async () => {
    const options = new chrome.Options()
    // Selenium 4: BiDi. This is the modern event API, the successor to the old JSONWire/CDP dance.
    options.set('webSocketUrl', true)
    // Selenium Manager (bundled since 4.6) auto-resolves and starts the matching driver:
    // no more "please download chromedriver 114" tickets.
    driver = await new Builder().forBrowser('chrome').setChromeOptions(options).build()

    // Selenium 4.10+: attach the BiDi log subscriber for network events.
    const bidi = await driver.getBidi()
    await bidi.network.enable()
    await bidi.network.addEventListener('network.responseReceived', (e) => {
      if (e.response.status >= 400) console.warn('[bidi]', e.response.url, e.response.status)
    })

    await driver.manage().window().setRect({ width: 1280, height: 800 })
    await driver.manage().timeouts().pageLoadTimeout(30_000)
  })

  after(async () => { await driver?.quit() })

  it('signs a user in', async () => {
    await driver.get('https://e2e.example.test/login')

    // Explicit wait: the W3C way, and the one thing you must never do is sleep.
    const email = await driver.wait(until.elementLocated(By.css('input[name=email]')), 10_000)
    await email.sendKeys('ada@example.test')
    await driver.findElement(By.css('input[name=password]')).sendKeys('correct horse', Key.ENTER)

    await driver.wait(until.elementIsVisible(By.css('[data-cy=welcome]')), 10_000)
    const text = await driver.findElement(By.css('[data-cy=welcome]')).getText()
    expect(text).toContain('Ada')
  })

  it('can screenshot and page source on failure', async () => {
    try {
      await driver.get('https://e2e.example.test/checkout')
    } catch {
      const png = await driver.takeScreenshot()
      await fs.writeFile('artifacts/selenium-failure.png', png, 'base64')
      const html = await driver.getPageSource()
      await fs.writeFile('artifacts/selenium-failure.html', html)
      throw new Error('see artifacts/')
    }
  })
})
```

**The four hard parts of Selenium, and how the modern tools solve them.**

1. **Waiting.** WebDriver is unopinionated: an element may be located but not yet clickable, and `click()` throws `ElementNotInteractableException`. The answer is `driver.wait(until.elementIsVisible(...))` or explicit `ExpectedConditions`. Every Selenium suite is 30% waiting code. Playwright and Cypress build retrying locators into the API, so this code does not exist.
2. **Driver/browsers version matching.** Historically the biggest tax in Selenium. **Selenium Manager** (4.6+) bundles a binary that resolves and caches the right driver for the installed browser. This single change removed a whole category of support tickets.
3. **Grid and distribution.** Selenium Grid 4 provides a hub and node architecture, W3C-compliant, with Docker/Kubernetes deployment and session routing policies (strict, dynamic, fixed). It is still the answer for "we must run on 40 real devices/OS versions in one place".
4. **No first-class tracing.** You build artifact capture yourself (screenshots, `getPageSource`, network logs via BiDi). Modern tools do this for you.

**When to actually use Selenium (2026).**

Use it when: you need **real Safari on real macOS** and have no cloud device farm; you are testing **native or hybrid mobile apps** via Appium; you have an existing Grid to consolidate device coverage onto; or you need a browser/environment the modern tools do not support.

Do not use it for: a new web E2E suite. You will spend weeks building the harness, the fixture system, tracing, and the waiting layer that Playwright or Cypress give you on day one.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Maximum reach (Grid, real devices, any language) versus maximum ergonomics.** Selenium's protocol is a genuine standard, and the abstraction cost is real.
- **A language-agnostic protocol** is a genuine advantage for organisations with polyglot test suites.
- **Cost of a greenfield suite is high**: driver management, implicit/explicit wait policy, page-object discipline, artifact capture, parallelisation via Grid, all hand-built.
- **The BiDi direction changes the calculus.** Selenium 4's BiDi (bidirectional) API adds standard network, log, script, and input events across browsers, which is the direction that makes Selenium more competitive against the CDP-based tools. It is not yet a replacement for Playwright's ergonomics.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Mixing implicit and explicit waits, producing unpredictable double-waiting and flakiness. Pick explicit waits.
2. Using `Thread.sleep` in Java, or `await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 2000))` in JS.
3. Hand-managing `chromedriver` binaries instead of using Selenium Manager.
4. Running the whole grid in a CI job without containerisation, so browsers leak processes and memory.
5. Not capturing artifacts on failure, so a Selenium failure is unreproducible.
6. Assuming a Linux grid can validate real Safari.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is WebDriver and why does it still matter?"* -- It is the W3C standard browser-automation protocol. Selenium 4 implements it, Appium uses it for native mobile, and it is the only practical route to real Safari on real macOS and to device grids.
- 🟡 *"What are Selenium's main practical drawbacks in 2026?"* -- No built-in auto-waiting (you write `ExpectedConditions` everywhere), no first-class tracing, version matching between browser and driver (mitigated by Selenium Manager), and a Grid you have to operate yourself. The ergonomics gap versus Playwright is the reason new suites pick Playwright.
- 🔴 *"Explain BiDi and why it matters."* -- BiDi is the W3C bidirectional protocol Selenium 4 uses: a persistent WebSocket where the client subscribes to standard events (network, log, script, input) across all supported browsers, not just Chromium. It is Selenium's route to the debugging and network-inspection capabilities that CDP gives the Chromium-only tools.
- ⚫ *"You must validate Safari on real macOS with no budget for a device farm. What do you do?"* -- Selenium plus `safaridriver` (which requires `Allow Remote Automation` enabled and a real macOS runner, ideally a small self-hosted Mac mini or a managed macOS CI runner), with a minimal suite of the journeys that are Safari-specific, and a documented known-gap list. That is the honest answer: real Safari validation costs macOS.

---

### 9.3.5 Page object model: when it helps and when it hides the test

**Definition.**

The **Page Object Model (POM)** is a design pattern for test automation in which each page (or, more usefully, each *capability*) is represented by a class that owns the locators and the interactions with that region of the UI, exposing domain-named methods (`addToBasket()`, `submitPayment()`) rather than element handles. The original intent, from the 2006 Selenium/Java literature, was to eliminate duplicated selectors and to make a UI change require an edit in exactly one place.

**The modern, more useful framing: prefer *capability objects* over *page objects*.** A page object models a screen. A capability object models an *intentional* set of actions that may cross screens: `const checkout = new CheckoutJourney(page); await checkout.completeWith(card)`. Capability objects are the unit that reads like a user story, they survive navigation changes, and they are the thing a test should call exactly once.

**Mechanics -- the two implementations, and why the second is better.**

```ts
// ❌ Screen-scoped page object: locators leak into the test, and the test is still 20 lines long.
class ProductPage {
  constructor(private page: Page) {}
  readonly addButton = this.page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Add to basket' })
  readonly qtyInput = this.page.getByLabel('Quantity')
}
class BasketPage {
  constructor(private page: Page) {}
  readonly checkoutButton = this.page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Checkout' })
}

// The test: this is a script, not a scenario. It will break on every copy change
// and it does not express intent.
it('buys a thing', async ({ page }) => {
  const product = new ProductPage(page)
  await product.addButton.click()
  await product.qtyInput.fill('2')
  const basket = new BasketPage(page)
  await basket.checkoutButton.click()
  await page.getByLabel('Card number').fill('4242424242424242')
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Confirm' }).click()
  expect(await page.textContent('h1')).toBe('Thank you')
})

// ✅ Capability object: the test reads as the user journey, and all selectors live in one place.
export class CheckoutJourney {
  constructor(private readonly page: Page) {}

  async addToBasket(productName: string): Promise<this> {
    await this.page.getByRole('article', { name: productName }).getByRole('button', { name: 'Add to basket' }).click()
    return this
  }

  async setQuantity(n: number): Promise<this> {
    await this.page.getByLabel('Quantity').fill(String(n))
    return this
  }

  async openBasketAndCheckout(): Promise<this> {
    await this.page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Basket' }).click()
    await this.page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Checkout' }).click()
    return this
  }

  async payWithCard(card: { number: string; expiry: string; cvc: string; name: string }): Promise<this> {
    await this.page.getByLabel('Card number').fill(card.number)
    await this.page.getByLabel('Expiry').fill(card.expiry)
    await this.page.getByLabel('CVC').fill(card.cvc)
    await this.page.getByLabel('Name on card').fill(card.name)
    return this
  }

  async confirm(): Promise<this> {
    await this.page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Confirm order' }).click()
    return this
  }

  // Assertions belong with the capability too, when they are about *that* capability.
  async expectOrderConfirmed(total: string): Promise<void> {
    await expect(this.page.getByRole('heading', { name: /thank you for your order/i })).toBeVisible()
    await expect(this.page.getByTestId('order-total')).toHaveText(total)
  }
}

// The test is now three lines and one sentence of intent.
it('a customer can buy two widgets with a saved card', async ({ page, api, signIn }) => {
  await signIn()
  const checkout = new CheckoutJourney(page)
  await checkout.addToBasket('Widget').setQuantity(2).openBasketAndCheckout()
  await checkout.payWithCard(VISA).confirm()
  await checkout.expectOrderConfirmed('£84.00')
})
```

**The POM that hides the test -- the failure mode, named.** Symptoms:

1. **The page object returns data and the test asserts everything.** Then the page object is a "getter bag" and the assertions are in the test; you have the worst of both.
2. **The page object contains business logic.** `await cart.applyCouponIfOrderOver(50)` now duplicates a pricing rule that a unit test owns, and it will drift.
3. **The page object hides failures.** A method that internally retries, catches, and falls back will make a broken app look like a passing test.
4. **The page object is a one-line wrapper.** `get addButton() { return this.page.locator('#add') }` adds indirection and no value.
5. **Selectors are stored as fields at construction time.** `private readonly qty = page.locator('#qty')` binds the locator before navigation; in Playwright a locator is lazy so this is usually fine, but with `page.$` (a promise) it captures the wrong element. Always store the `Page`, not resolved elements.
6. **One page object per screen means N objects per test.** The combinatorial cost is what pushes teams to abandon the pattern -- and then the selectors leak back into the tests, and every copy change breaks 40 tests.

**The decision rule, and what to do instead.**

| Situation | Use |
| --- | --- |
| The same flow is used by 3+ tests | Capability object -- extract it |
| A selector is used by 3+ tests | Extract a locator function or a component query, not a whole page class |
| A test is 3-6 steps of straight-line user actions | **No abstraction.** Write it inline; it is already short and readable |
| A test needs a complex data table / drag-drop / canvas | Component-level helper for the widget |
| A flow crosses 5+ screens | Capability object, and split into `given` steps that each end on a stable anchor |

**Mechanics -- the supporting pattern: page-specific test fixtures and component queries.**

```ts
// e2e/pages.ts -- locators as exported functions, not fields.
export const basket = {
  root: (page: Page) => page.getByRole('region', { name: 'Basket' }),
  line: (page: Page, sku: string) => basket.root(page).getByRole('row').filter({ hasText: sku }),
  removeButton: (page: Page, sku: string) => basket.line(page, sku).getByRole('button', { name: 'Remove' }),
  total: (page: Page) => page.getByTestId('basket-total'),
}

// e2e/fixtures.ts -- register the capability objects as fixtures, typed, once.
type MyFixtures = { checkout: CheckoutJourney; basket: typeof basket }
export const test = base.extend<MyFixtures>({
  checkout: async ({ page }, use) => { await use(new CheckoutJourney(page)) },
  basket: async ({}, use) => { await use(basket) },
})
```

**Trade-offs.**

- **POM pros:** one place to change when the UI changes; readable tests; reusable flows; a natural home for waits and for i18n; keeps test files focused on intent.
- **POM cons:** indirection makes failures harder to localise (a stack trace points into the page object, not the test); over-abstraction makes tests read like a DSL nobody but the author can use; and a page object per screen multiplies the files you must maintain.
- **Capability objects beat page objects** on intent-readability and navigation-resilience, at the cost of being less "reusable" for flows that only one test uses. Given that E2E suites should be small and intent-focused, this is the right trade.
- **Never put assertions in a page object that are about the *test's* data** (e.g. "this order is in the database"); keep those in the test. Assertions about the *screen's* state belong with the screen's capability.

**Common mistakes.**

1. A `BasePage` class with 40 generic helpers (`click`, `type`, `waitForText`) that every page object inherits -- this is a mini-framework, and it is worse than no abstraction.
2. Page objects that do assertions, so the test body is empty and a failure message says nothing about the journey.
3. Storing `ElementHandle`s (Selenium/Cypress) instead of locators, which dangles after navigation.
4. A separate page object for every screen of a 6-screen flow, so the test constructs 6 objects and reads like a script.
5. Page objects that import test expectations (`expect`), coupling reusable code to a test framework.
6. `page.waitForTimeout` inside page-object methods to "fix" timing, which converts a design problem into permanent slowness.
7. Never updating the page object after a UI change, so the whole suite breaks at once instead of one test.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is a page object?"* -- A class that encapsulates the locators and interactions for a screen, so selectors live in one place and tests express intent rather than mechanics.
- 🟡 *"When is a page object the wrong abstraction?"* -- When the test is already three or four straight-line actions: a page object adds indirection without reuse, and the failure message points away from the test. Extract only when a flow is used by three or more tests, and prefer a *capability* object for the flow over a *page* object for the screen.
- 🔴 *"How do you know a page object is hiding the test?"* -- When the test body is a sequence of one-line calls that require reading the object to understand, when the object contains business logic that a unit test also owns, when it contains retries or try/catch, or when the assertions moved into it and the failure message no longer names the journey.
- 🔴 *"A UI copy change broke 40 E2E tests. What's the fix?"* -- Two layers. First, the immediate one: locators should use roles and names sourced from a single constants module so a copy change is one edit. Second, the structural one: E2E should not assert on copy at all where a stable identifier exists -- assert on roles plus a `data-testid` for the specific element, and verify the *copy* in component tests. E2E should verify the journey works, not that the string is spelled a particular way.
- ⚫ *"Page objects or fixtures?"* -- Both, at different levels: page/capability objects for the domain actions, Playwright fixtures for lifecycle and setup (API client, auth state, artifacts). Fixtures are dependency injection for the test; page objects are domain knowledge about the UI. Mixing them produces classes that do both jobs badly.

---

### 9.3.6 Test environments: data, seeding, parallelism, and cost

**Definition.**

An E2E **test environment** is the deployed system the suite runs against: the application build, the database, the queue, the cache, the third-party sandboxes, and the configuration. The environment is where most E2E cost and most E2E flake live. "Works on my machine" at the E2E tier means "works against *my* local stack", which is usually a different database engine, a different clock, and a different set of data.

**Mechanics -- the four environment archetypes and their trade-offs.**

| Archetype | Fidelity | Cost | Flake | Use when |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Local docker-compose** | low-medium (your machine, your versions) | low | medium (no isolation from your dev work) | developing tests, debugging a single failure |
| **Ephemeral per-PR environment** (preview deploy) | high (production image, real migrations, real data shape) | medium-high (build + deploy time) | low | PR gating for user-facing changes |
| **Shared long-lived environment** (staging) | high | low (one deploy) | high (concurrent runs share state) | smoke tests, manual QA, nightly full suites |
| **Cloud device farm / per-run sandbox** | high + real devices | high (per-minute billing) | low | Safari/real-device validation, visual regression across platforms |

**The three things that make an environment test-grade, and that everyone gets wrong initially.**

1. **Data isolation per run, per worker, and per test.** This is the single biggest flake source in a shared environment.
2. **Real migrations against a real database at a realistic scale.** A fresh empty database hides exactly the production bugs you want E2E to find.
3. **A bounded lifetime.** Every environment must be destroyable, and its data must be garbage-collectable, or you will accumulate a data lake that slows every run.

**Data isolation -- the four levels, and which to use.**

```ts
// ── Level 1: namespace per run (cheapest, needs app support)
const RUN_ID = `${process.env.RUN_ID ?? 'local'}-${Date.now().toString(36)}`
// every created record gets a run-scoped external id, and the app's API filters by it
await api.createUser({ email: `${RUN_ID}-ada@example.test` })
// cleanup: DELETE FROM users WHERE email LIKE '${RUN_ID}-%'
// Requires: an app-level `X-Run-Id` column or a soft-delete filter. This is a real design decision.

// ── Level 2: schema/database per worker (best general-purpose isolation)
const WORKER = process.env.VITEST_WORKER_ID ?? process.env.PW_WORKER_INDEX ?? '0'
const DB = `e2e_${RUN_ID}_${WORKER}`
// one database per worker; truncate between tests inside a worker; drop all at the end of the run

// ── Level 3: unique data per test (works even with a shared DB, if the app has no hard uniqueness)
const TEST_TAG = crypto.randomUUID().slice(0, 8)   // adaxe@example.test
// assertions always scoped by the tag; cleanup by tag
// Weakness: any UNIQUE constraint on a natural key (email, slug) still collides across parallel runs
// unless the natural key is derived from the tag (which is why `${RUN_ID}-` prefixed emails matter)

// ── Level 4: transaction per test (see 9.2.4) -- only for DB state, never for HTTP-driven flows
```

**Seeding that survives parallelism.** The rules that matter:

```ts
// e2e/fixtures/api.ts -- an API client that is namespace-aware and self-cleaning.
export class ApiClient {
  private readonly ns: string
  constructor(private readonly cfg: { baseURL: string; token: string; ns: string }) { this.ns = cfg.ns }

  private scoped<T extends { email?: string; slug?: string }>(payload: T): T {
    // Every natural key is namespaced, so parallel runs cannot collide.
    return {
      ...payload,
      ...(payload.email ? { email: `${this.ns}-${payload.email}` } : {}),
      ...(payload.slug ? { slug: `${this.ns}-${payload.slug}` } : {}),
    }
  }

  async createUser(p: { email: string; password?: string; role?: string }) {
    const r = await this.request('POST', '/api/test/users', this.scoped(p))
    return r.json()
  }

  async cleanupNamespace(): Promise<void> {
    // Server-side bulk delete, not per-record: one request, one round trip.
    await this.request('DELETE', `/api/test/namespaces/${this.ns}`)
  }
}
```

```ts
// The cleanup must run even when the test failed -- the fixture guarantees that.
api: async ({}, use, testInfo) => {
  const ns = runNamespace(testInfo)                  // stable per test, unique per run
  const api = new ApiClient({ baseURL: BASE_URL, token: E2E_TOKEN, ns })
  await use(api)
  await api.cleanupNamespace().catch((e) => {
    // Never mask the original failure.
    console.error(`[e2e] namespace cleanup failed for ${ns}:`, e.message)
  })
}
```

**The test-only API surface.** Almost every E2E suite needs a way to arrange state quickly. Provide it deliberately, with hard controls, rather than letting each test create data through the UI or by hitting the database directly.

```ts
// apps/api/src/http/routes/test-support.ts
// Mounted ONLY when E2E_TENANT_KEY is present AND the request carries the matching header.
import { timingSafeEqual } from 'node:crypto'

const EXPECTED = Buffer.from(process.env.E2E_TENANT_KEY ?? '')

export const testSupportGuard = (req: Request, res: Response, next: NextFunction) => {
  if (process.env.NODE_ENV === 'production' && process.env.ALLOW_TEST_ROUTES !== '1') {
    return res.status(404).json({ error: { code: 'NOT_FOUND' } })   // invisible in production
  }
  const provided = Buffer.from(String(req.header('x-e2e-key') ?? ''))
  if (provided.length !== EXPECTED.length || !timingSafeEqual(provided, EXPECTED)) {
    return res.status(403).json({ error: { code: 'FORBIDDEN' } })
  }
  next()
}

// Routes:
//   POST   /api/test/users                 -> create a user
//   POST   /api/test/namespaces/:ns/purge   -> delete everything in a namespace
//   POST   /api/test/reset-cache            -> flush redis
//   POST   /api/test/reseed-reference       -> re-seed countries, currencies, plans
//   POST   /api/test/simulate               -> queue a failure injection (see 9.2.10)
```

**Reference data versus test data.** Reference data (countries, currency codes, tax rates, feature flag defaults) is shared and immutable per environment: seed it in the environment's deployment step, never per test. Test data is per-run: create and purge it in the fixtures.

**Realistic scale matters, and it is the thing teams skip.** A checkout that works against 3 orders and fails against 30 000 because of an N+1 query or a missing index will not be found by E2E against a fresh database. So:

```ts
// Seed a realistic data volume ONCE per environment, not per test.
api: async ({}, use, testInfo) => {
  // (Idempotent, keyed on the run id, so a retry does not double-seed.)
  await api.ensureBulkCatalog({ products: 50_000, users: 2_000, ordersPerUser: 12 })
  // ...
}
```

And assert on **performance** as part of E2E, once:

```ts
test('the home page is usable within the performance budget on seeded data', async ({ page }) => {
  const t0 = Date.now()
  await page.goto('/')
  await expect(page.getByRole('main')).toBeVisible()
  const ttfb = await page.evaluate(() =>
    performance.getEntriesByType('navigation')[0]!.responseStart - performance.getEntriesByType('navigation')[0]!.startTime,
  )
  expect(ttfb).toBeLessThan(800)
  expect(Date.now() - t0).toBeLessThan(3_000)
})
```

**Parallelism.**

```ts
// playwright.config.ts
export default defineConfig({
  fullyParallel: true,
  workers: process.env.CI ? 6 : 4,
  // Sharding for CI, so 6 shards of ~equal duration run in parallel.
  // npx playwright test --shard=1/6
  reporter: [['html'], ['junit', { outputFile: 'e2e/results.xml' }]],
})
```

```yaml
# CI: fan out shards, then a mandatory smoke job against the deployed preview.
jobs:
  e2e:
    strategy:
      fail-fast: false            # do not cancel siblings: their artifacts are still useful
      matrix:
        shard: [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - run: pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
      - run: pnpm exec playwright install --with-deps chromium
      - run: pnpm exec playwright test --shard=${{ matrix.shard }}/6
      - if: failure()
        uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        with: { name: trace-shard-${{ matrix.shard }}, path: e2e/.artifacts }
```

**Cost -- where the money actually goes, and how to cut it.**

| Cost | Typical magnitude | Lever |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Environment build + deploy | 2-10 min per PR | reuse a preview environment per branch; deploy once, test many times |
| Browser startup | ~1-2s per worker, amortised | contexts over browsers; one worker per core |
| Bulk seeding | 10-60s per run | seed once per environment, not per test or per shard |
| Per-test data create/cleanup | 0.3-2s per test | one bulk cleanup endpoint, not per-record deletes |
| Device farm minutes | £/$ per device-minute | only for Safari/real devices; run the smallest meaningful matrix |
| Artifact storage (traces/videos) | GBs per run | retain on failure, 7 days, compress traces |
| Flake investigation | engineer-hours, the biggest cost | track flake rate; a 3% flake suite consumes more time than it saves |
| Human triage | the real cost | good traces, good failure messages, `--last-failed` |

**The economics rule: an E2E suite is worth its cost when the sum of (probability of a bug it catches) x (cost of that bug) exceeds (cost per run x runs per day x engineer-minutes spent triaging).** In practice: keep the suite under 10 minutes and under 0.5% flake, and you are in a healthy regime. At 40 minutes and 3% flake you are net negative and the team will route around it.

**When to use which environment archetype.**

- **PR gating for user-facing changes:** ephemeral preview deploy from the PR's build. Highest fidelity, no shared state.
- **Smoke after deploy:** the shared staging environment, 2-3 tests, plus a synthetic monitoring check. This is the "is the release healthy" gate.
- **Nightly full suite:** shared environment with a namespace, all browsers, plus visual regression across platforms.
- **Local development:** docker-compose with the production images and the real migrations; never a hand-built local stack that differs from production.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Ephemeral per-PR environments are the highest fidelity and the most expensive.** They also give the best developer experience: "the test passed against the exact artefact that will ship".
- **A shared environment is cheap and eventually unsound.** Cross-run interference is not a bug you fix; it is a property of the design. Namespacing plus a nightly reset is the mitigation, and it is a permanent tax.
- **Bulk seeding costs time and hides real slowness.** A perfectly tuned 3-row query is a liability; a query on 50 000 rows is the truth. Seed for realism, and pay the seeding cost once per environment.
- **A test-support API is a production code path.** It must be gated, audited, and ideally compiled out of the production bundle. Treat its endpoints as privileged: an unauthenticated `purge` in production is a catastrophic vulnerability.
- **Parallelism increases DB load linearly.** 8 workers against a 2-core test Postgres will show you lock contention that looks like application flake. Size the database, or shard across environments.

**Production considerations.**

- Reuse a preview environment across a branch's pushes; redeploy only when the build changes, and always when migrations change (a migration is the one thing that invalidates an environment).
- Set a **data TTL janitor**: a scheduled job that purges namespaces older than 24 hours and drops databases older than 3 days. Without it, the test database becomes the slowest thing in the system.
- Never run E2E against production. Ever. Even "read-only" smoke tests against prod are a bad idea, because a test that mutates data is one bug away from an incident, and a "harmless" smoke test is how that happens.
- Version the E2E contract: record the API version the suite expects and fail fast with a clear message if the environment reports a different one, instead of 40 confusing failures.
- Keep a `SMOKE` tag on the 3-5 absolutely critical tests; run those after every deploy regardless of the full suite.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Running the full E2E suite against a shared staging environment in parallel, and then "fixing" flake with retries.
2. Creating data through the UI in setup, so each test takes 30-60s and fails on a copy change.
3. An unguarded `/api/test/...` router in production, or one guarded by `if (NODE_ENV === 'test')` which is trivially bypassed by setting the variable.
4. Seeding a tiny dataset, so E2E is green on a system that would time out in production.
5. Re-seeding the reference data on every test.
6. No cleanup, so the suite is only green on the first run of the day.
7. Sharding without `fail-fast: false`, so one shard's failure cancels the others and you lose the traces.
8. No `RUN_ID`/namespace at all, and a "it was green yesterday" culture.
9. Deploying a new environment per *test* (not per run) -- you have built a very slow integration test suite.
10. Video recording enabled for the whole suite, which doubles the runtime and the storage.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you run E2E tests in CI reliably?"* -- Deploy an ephemeral, production-like preview environment from the PR build, seed reference data once, create per-test namespaced data through a gated test API, run Playwright with full parallelism and sharding, upload traces on failure, and purge all test data at the end of the run.
- 🟡 *"How do you avoid tests interfering with each other?"* -- Namespacing: every natural key (email, slug, external id) is prefixed with a run-and-test-unique token, every query the test makes is scoped to it, and a bulk purge endpoint removes it in teardown. Parallel-safe because the namespaces cannot collide.
- 🔴 *"Your E2E suite is green on a fresh database and red on staging. What are the likely causes?"* -- (1) data volume (an N+1 or a missing index only appears at scale), (2) a seed difference between environments, (3) a config or feature flag difference, (4) real PII-adjacent data shapes that break a parser, (5) timezone/locale differences, (6) a third-party sandbox with different behaviour. Fix by making the environment parity explicit and adding a data-volume seed.
- 🔴 *"How do you keep test environments from becoming the slowest thing you own?"* -- Reuse environments per branch rather than per test, seed bulk data once per environment with an idempotent endpoint, purge per test with a single bulk call, and run a scheduled TTL janitor for orphaned namespaces and databases.
- ⚫ *"How would you handle a migration in an E2E environment?"* -- Recreate the environment rather than migrating in place. A migration applied to a database with existing test data is a different test from a migration applied to a fresh one, and you need the fresh one to match production. So: new build plus new environment, and a separate targeted test that exercises the migration path against a snapshot of production-shaped data.

---

### 9.3.7 Visual regression testing

**Definition.**

**Visual regression testing** (also called screenshot testing, VRT, or screenshot diffing) captures a rendered screenshot of a UI in a known state and compares it, pixel by pixel, against a committed baseline. It is the only automated way to catch a class of regression that no functional test can see: a broken layout, a misaligned baseline, a missing font, a clipped text, a wrong colour, a broken responsive breakpoint, an icon that fails to load, a dark-mode regression, or a hydration mismatch that leaves the page half-styled.

**Mechanics -- the four problems, and the solutions that work.**

1. **Nondeterminism.** Animations, transitions, carousels, clocks, random data, cursor position, and scrollbars produce different pixels on every run.
2. **Font rendering differences** between OSes and even between machines with different font versions.
3. **Sub-pixel and anti-aliasing noise** from fractional layout.
4. **Dynamic content** (dates, names, "3 minutes ago", randomised ids).

Solutions, in order of importance:

```ts
// 1. Freeze time, seed randomness, and disable animation in the *app* for tests.
await page.clock.install({ time: new Date('2026-03-01T09:00:00Z') })     // Playwright 1.45+
await page.addInitScript(() => {
  // Deterministic randomness
  let seed = 42
  Math.random = () => { seed = (seed * 1664525 + 1013904223) >>> 0; return seed / 2 ** 32 }
  // No transitions/animations
  const style = document.createElement('style')
  style.textContent = `*, *::before, *::after { animation: none !important; transition: none !important;
                    caret-color: transparent !important; scroll-behavior: auto !important; }`
  document.head.appendChild(style)
})
// Web fonts: wait for them, so you never screenshot a fallback font.
await page.evaluate(() => document.fonts.ready)

// 2. The config: masks, tolerances, and a stable rendering environment.
expect: {
  toHaveScreenshot: {
    maxDiffPixelRatio: 0.01,      // 1% of pixels may differ; raise for text-heavy pages
    threshold: 0.2,               // per-pixel colour tolerance 0-1
    animations: 'disabled',       // wait for CSS animations to finish
    caret: 'hide',                // hide the text cursor
    scale: 'css',                 // screenshot in CSS pixels, not device pixels
    stylePath: path.join(__dirname, 'visual.css'),   // per-test CSS overrides
  },
}

// 3. Mask the parts that can never be stable.
await expect(page).toHaveScreenshot('checkout.png', {
  mask: [page.getByTestId('order-id'), page.getByTestId('relative-time')],
  maskColor: '#FF00FF',          // a colour that never appears in the design system
  fullPage: true,
})
```

**Where visual baselines live, and how they are reviewed.** Baselines are binary PNGs in git. This creates a real workflow problem, and the honest workflow is:

```bash
# Locally: run the visual tests, get diffs, inspect them in the HTML report
npx playwright test --update-snapshots      # regenerate
npx playwright show-report                  # open the report with side-by-side diffs

# In PR: a human must look at the diff images. The report is a CI artefact.
```

Practical rules that keep this workable:
- **Keep the baseline count low.** 20-40 screenshots per platform for critical screens; not every page. Visual coverage of stable, high-value screens only.
- **Group shots into few files.** One screenshot per "state" (empty, loading, error, populated, mobile, desktop) rather than one per component.
- **Freeze a platform for baselines.** Generate them in the same container image you compare in, so fonts and rendering are identical. In CI, use the official Playwright Docker image.
- **Threshold is not a licence.** A 5% diff ratio on a text-heavy page will accept a completely different paragraph. Prefer masking text to loosening thresholds.

**Playwright's visual comparison is the practical default.**

```ts
import { test, expect } from '@playwright/test'

// A single, table-driven visual spec keeps the baseline count and the maintenance cost low.
const STATES = [
  { name: 'empty', setup: async (page: Page) => { await api.setCart([]) } },
  { name: 'single-item', setup: async (page: Page) => { await api.setCart([{ sku: 'WIDGET', qty: 1 }]) } },
  { name: 'many-items', setup: async (page: Page) => { await api.setCart(bulkCart(40)) } },
  { name: 'price-error', setup: async (page: Page) => { await api.setPriceError('SKU', 'unavailable') } },
]

for (const state of STATES) {
  test(`basket -- ${state.name}`, async ({ page, api }) => {
    await api.setViewportProfile('member')
    await state.setup(page)
    await page.goto('/basket')
    await expect(page.getByRole('region', { name: 'Basket' })).toBeVisible()
    await expect(page.getByTestId('basket-settled')).toBeAttached()   // explicit app-level "ready" signal
    await expect(page).toHaveScreenshot(`basket-${state.name}.png`, { fullPage: true })
  })
}

test('basket -- dark mode', async ({ page, api }) => {
  await page.emulateMedia({ colorScheme: 'dark', reducedMotion: 'reduce' })
  await api.setCart([{ sku: 'WIDGET', qty: 1 }])
  await page.goto('/basket')
  await expect(page).toHaveScreenshot('basket-dark.png')
})

test('basket -- long content does not break layout', async ({ page, api }) => {
  await api.setCart([{ sku: 'A-VERY-LONG-SKU-THAT-SHOULD-NOT-OVERFLOW-OR-WRAP-THE-LAYOUT', qty: 999_999 }])
  await page.goto('/basket')
  await expect(page).toHaveScreenshot('basket-long-content.png')
})
```

**The explicit ready signal is important and often missing.** `toHaveScreenshot` waits for fonts and two animation frames, but your data may still be loading. Add a `data-settled` attribute set by the app when the view is in its final state:

```tsx
// In the app, under a test-friendly flag.
export function BasketView({ state }: { state: BasketState }) {
  return (
    <section
      aria-label="Basket"
      data-testid="basket-settled"
      data-settled={state.status === 'ready'}
      hidden={state.status !== 'ready'}
    >
      {/* ... */}
    </section>
  )
}
// Then: await expect(page.getByTestId('basket-settled')).toHaveAttribute('data-settled', 'true')
```

**Other visual regression tools, briefly, for awareness.**

- **Percy** (BrowserStack): hosted, cross-browser baselines, good review workflow, paid.
- **Chromatic** (Storybook): component-level visual testing, excellent for design systems, integrates with Storybook, paid with a free tier.
- **BackstopJS** / **Loki**: config-as-code, Docker, self-hosted, good for teams that want their own pipeline.
- **Argos / Happo**: similar hosted options.
- **jest-image-snapshot / @vitest/image-snapshot**: component-level image comparison inside your unit/component suite -- useful for canvas and chart output, not for full pages.

**When to use visual regression vs not.**

Use it for: layout and responsive-breakpoint regressions, design system components, dark/high-contrast/print modes, error and empty states that must look specific, and any screen where a visual change is a product change (marketing pages, onboarding, checkout).

Do not use it for: verifying that a button works (functional test), for every page of a large app (the maintenance and review cost explodes), or for screens whose content is inherently user-specific with no masking possible (a dashboard of arbitrary customer data -- use masked component shots instead).

**Trade-offs.**

- **It catches what nothing else can.** A one-pixel misalignment, a font that failed to load, a flex container that collapsed on a 320px viewport, a z-index regression hiding a button. No functional test sees these.
- **It is noisy, and noise is fatal.** A suite where 5% of runs differ without a change gets ignored, and then it catches nothing. The discipline is: freeze time, freeze randomness, mask dynamic regions, pin the rendering environment, and keep the count low.
- **Baseline review is a human process.** A PR with 15 changed screenshots requires a designer or a careful engineer to look at each one. Automating the review is not possible; automating the *generation* is.
- **Font licensing and availability** are a real operational problem. If the app uses a licensed webfont, your CI container needs it, and your dev machines need it, or every baseline is wrong.
- **It does not tell you *why*.** A diff image shows that something changed; finding the cause is still manual.
- **Platform-specific baselines multiply the work.** Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit baselines are three sets. Generate all three but review them as one logical group, and accept that only the Chromium set needs to be pristine.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Animations and carousels not frozen, so every run differs.
2. Screenshots taken before data has loaded, so the baseline is a spinner.
3. Unstable content (relative times, order ids, avatars from a CDN) not masked, so the suite is 100% flake.
4. Baselines generated on a developer Mac and compared in a Linux container -- font rendering guarantees failure.
5. A `maxDiffPixelRatio: 0.2` "fix" that accepts any layout change.
6. Snapshotting full pages of dynamic dashboards, so the baseline is meaningless.
7. Auto-updating baselines in CI, which silently accepts every visual regression.
8. Reviewing the diff images not at all, so visual tests exist but protect nothing.
9. Testing only the default viewport; the regressions happen at 320px and at 1920px.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is visual regression testing and what does it catch that functional tests cannot?"* -- Comparing a rendered screenshot against a committed baseline, which catches layout, styling, font-loading, breakpoint, colour, and z-index regressions that no DOM assertion can see.
- 🟡 *"How do you stop a visual suite being flaky?"* -- Freeze the clock, seed randomness, disable animations and transitions, wait for web fonts, wait for an explicit app-level "settled" signal, mask every dynamic region, generate and compare baselines in the same pinned container image, and use a per-pixel threshold rather than a large ratio.
- 🔴 *"Your visual suite is 20% flaky. Diagnose."* -- Look at the failing diffs and classify: animation (not frozen), timing (screenshot before settled), data (unmasked dynamic content), environment (fonts or GPU), or genuine nondeterminism (a random layout-affecting value). In that order; animation and timing account for most cases.
- 🔴 *"Should a designer be in the visual review loop?"* -- Yes, for the same reason a designer is in the design review loop: visual changes are product changes, and the diff image is the cheapest possible design review artefact. If there is no designer available, at minimum require an explicit human approval step on any PR with changed baselines, so a regression cannot be merged by accident.
- ⚫ *"How do you test a page with a live chat widget and a rotating ad?"* -- Mask both regions in the assertion (`mask: [chatWidget, adSlot]`), stub the ad and chat requests at the network layer so they render a fixed placeholder, and disable their animations. If a region cannot be both stubbed and masked (e.g. it is above the fold and its absence changes layout), exclude that state from visual testing and cover it functionally.

---

### 9.3.8 Network conditions, throttling, and offline

**Definition.**

**Network condition testing** verifies behaviour under degraded connectivity: slow throughput, high latency, packet loss, intermittent failure, and complete offline. It is a distinct discipline from error simulation (9.2.10), because the question is not "what does the 500 do" but "what does the *user* experience when the network is bad, and does the app degrade gracefully rather than lose data".

**The behaviours worth testing (the canonical list).**

| Condition | What the user should see | What must not happen |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Slow 3G (400kbps, 400ms RTT) | a loading state, then content | an infinite spinner, a frozen UI, an unsaved form |
| Request timeout | a clear "taking too long" message with retry | a silent failure, a stuck disabled button |
| Intermittent failure (some requests fail) | partial success handled, retry offered | a crash, a corrupted state, a duplicated write |
| Full offline (after load) | an offline banner, cached content, queued writes | a lost form submission, a "something went wrong" with no path forward |
| Reconnect | automatic resync, clear indication | silent data loss, duplicate submissions |
| Captive portal / 401 on API | a re-auth prompt | an infinite redirect loop |

**Mechanics -- how to produce each condition, and the important limitation.**

```ts
// ── Playwright: network emulation via CDP (Chromium only for the *devtools* variants,
//    but these two work everywhere via the protocol surface Playwright exposes).
test('renders progressively on a slow connection', async ({ page, context }) => {
  // CDP: latency + throughput + offline. Chromium-family only.
  const cdp = await context.newCDPSession(page)
  await cdp.send('Network.emulateNetworkConditions', {
    offline: false,
    latency: 400,                    // ms
    downloadThroughput: 400 * 1024 / 8,   // 400 kbps
    uploadThroughput: 400 * 1024 / 8,
  })
  await page.goto('/catalogue')
  // Assert the *loading* state appears, then the content, and that it eventually settles.
  await expect(page.getByRole('status', { name: /loading/i })).toBeVisible()
  await expect(page.getByRole('region', { name: 'Products' })).toBeVisible({ timeout: 30_000 })
})

// ── A route-level delay is portable and often more precise than CDP emulation.
test('shows a timeout message when a request exceeds 8s', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.route('**/api/recommendations', async (route) => {
    await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 10_000))
    await route.abort('timedout')
  })
  await page.goto('/')
  await expect(page.getByRole('status', { name: /this is taking longer than usual/i })).toBeVisible()
  await expect(page.getByRole('button', { name: /retry/i })).toBeEnabled()
})

// ── Offline: context.setOffline
test('shows an offline banner and allows queuing a draft', async ({ page, context }) => {
  await page.goto('/')
  await context.setOffline(true)
  await page.evaluate(() => window.dispatchEvent(new Event('offline')))
  await expect(page.getByRole('status', { name: /you are offline/i })).toBeVisible()

  await page.getByRole('textbox', { name: /note/i }).fill('Written while offline')
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: /save draft/i }).click()
  // The draft is queued locally, not lost.
  expect(await page.evaluate(() => JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem('drafts')!)[0].note))
    .toBe('Written while offline')
})

// ── Intermittent failure, deterministic rather than random
test('survives 3 of 5 requests failing', async ({ page }) => {
  const urls = ['/a', '/b', '/c', '/d', '/e']
  let n = 0
  await page.route('**/api/widgets/**', (route) => {
    n += 1
    // Deterministic: fail specific requests, not a random 40%.
    return n % 2 === 1 ? route.abort('failed') : route.continue()
  })
  await page.goto('/widgets')
  await expect(page.getByRole('region', { name: 'Widgets' })).toBeVisible()
  await expect(page.getByRole('status', { name: /some widgets could not be loaded/i })).toBeVisible()
  expect(urls).toHaveLength(5)
})
```

**The limitation you must know and state: `route.fulfill` and `route.abort` do not produce real network failures, and CDP emulation is Chromium-only.** For genuine transport-level behaviour (a connection reset mid-body, a truncated response, a proxy that returns 502 for WebSockets), use a real local proxy or a real server. The highest-value technique here is a **controllable proxy** in front of the app in your test environment:

```ts
// test/tools/chaos-proxy.ts -- a tiny HTTP proxy with programmatic fault injection.
import http from 'node:http'
import net from 'node:net'

export type Fault = 'none' | 'slow' | 'reset' | 'truncate' | 'status503' | 'hang'

export function createChaosProxy(targetPort: number) {
  const state: { fault: Fault; delayMs: number; failPaths: string[] } = {
    fault: 'none', delayMs: 0, failPaths: [],
  }
  const server = http.createServer((req, res) => {
    const apply = state.fault !== 'none' &&
      (state.failPaths.length === 0 || state.failPaths.some((p) => req.url!.startsWith(p)))
    if (apply) {
      if (state.fault === 'status503') { res.writeHead(503); return res.end('upstream down') }
      if (state.fault === 'hang') return                                     // never respond
      if (state.fault === 'slow') {
        setTimeout(() => proxyThrough(req, res, targetPort), state.delayMs)
        return
      }
      if (state.fault === 'reset') { res.socket?.destroy(); return }          // real ECONNRESET
      if (state.fault === 'truncate') {                                     // real truncated body
        res.writeHead(200, { 'Content-Type': 'application/json', 'Content-Length': '1000' })
        res.write('{"partial":')
        return setTimeout(() => res.socket?.destroy(), 50)
      }
    }
    proxyThrough(req, res, targetPort)
  })
  return { server, state, listen: (p: number) => new Promise<void>((r) => server.listen(p, r)) }
}
```

With such a proxy, a single environment can serve the happy-path E2E suite *and* the degraded-network suite, and the failures are real.

**Testing the offline write path -- the highest-value test in this section.** If the app claims to work offline, the interesting bugs are: queued writes lost on refresh, duplicate submissions on reconnect (no idempotency key on the queued request), a queue that grows unbounded, conflicting writes resolved silently, and a UI that says "saved" when it is only queued.

```ts
test('a queued comment is sent exactly once after reconnecting', async ({ page, context, api }) => {
  await page.goto('/thread/1')
  await context.setOffline(true)
  await page.getByRole('textbox', { name: /comment/i }).fill('Written offline')
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: /post/i }).click()
  await expect(page.getByText('Queued — will send when you reconnect')).toBeVisible()

  await page.reload()
  await expect(page.getByText('Written offline')).toBeVisible()          // survived a refresh

  await context.setOffline(false)
  await page.evaluate(() => window.dispatchEvent(new Event('online')))
  await expect(page.getByText('Queued — will send when you reconnect')).toBeHidden({ timeout: 15_000 })

  // Exactly one comment, not two: the queued request carried an idempotency key.
  expect(await api.countComments(1)).toBe(1)
  const comments = await api.listComments(1)
  expect(comments.filter((c: { body: string }) => c.body === 'Written offline')).toHaveLength(1)
})
```

**When to test network conditions.**

Always, if your product is mobile-first, used on poor connections, or claims offline/PWA capability. The specific journeys: first load, form submission with a slow network, media loading, a large data list, and (if claimed) offline write and reconnect.

Do not build a full throttling matrix. Pick the three conditions that produce the three distinct code paths in your app: slow, failed, offline.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Throttling makes tests slow** -- a 3G test can take 30s. Keep it to one or two tests per critical journey, and use `page.route` delays (portable) rather than CDP throughput emulation (accurate but Chromium-only and slower).
- **CDP network emulation is Chromium-only**, so a throttling test in the WebKit/Firefox projects will silently do nothing if you write it with `newCDPSession`. Guard it with `test.skip(browserName !== 'chromium')` or use route-based delays.
- **Service workers complicate everything.** A service worker may serve a cached response and never hit the network, so your throttle test measures nothing. Test service-worker behaviour explicitly (a dedicated context with `serviceWorkers: 'block'`, and another with them enabled).
- **Realistic offline testing requires a real offline story.** If the app has no offline capability, "offline" is just "an error page", and that is a product finding, not a test.
- **Randomised fault injection produces flaky tests.** Make faults deterministic (specific paths, specific counts, specific order) so a failure is reproducible.

**Common mistakes.**

1. `setOffline` on the context but the app uses a service worker that serves from cache, so nothing changes.
2. Testing throttling in the WebKit project with a CDP-only call.
3. Asserting a fixed time (`expect(elapsed).toBeGreaterThan(3000)`), which is a load test not an E2E test.
4. Not testing the *reconnect* path, which is where duplicate writes and data loss live.
5. Assuming the app is offline-capable because it has a service worker, without testing the write path.
6. Random delays instead of deterministic ones, so failures cannot be reproduced.
7. Skipping the slow-network test on the critical form journey because "users are on 5G now" -- and then shipping a form that times out on 3G.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you test a slow network in E2E?"* -- CDP `Network.emulateNetworkConditions` (Chromium) or a `page.route` delay (portable). Assert that a loading state appears, that the content eventually renders, that the UI remains interactive, and that the total time is bounded.
- 🟡 *"What does `context.setOffline(true)` actually do, and what does it not do?"* -- It makes the browser's network stack report offline: fetches fail immediately and the `offline` event fires. It does not affect a service worker's cache, and it is not the same as the device being in aeroplane mode with a captive portal.
- 🔴 *"Your app claims to work offline. What is the single most important test?"* -- Write a record while offline, reload the page, confirm the record survived, reconnect, and assert it was sent **exactly once**. The duplicate-on-reconnect bug is the classic failure, and it is invisible until you test the whole cycle.
- 🔴 *"How do you produce a real connection reset, which MSW and route.abort cannot?"* -- Run a controllable proxy or a local `net` server between the browser and the app and destroy the socket mid-response. That is the only way to test behaviour on a truncated body, a half-open connection, or a socket that closes during a streaming response.
- ⚫ *"Why is a throttling test with `expect(elapsed).toBeGreaterThan(3000)` a bad test?"* -- It asserts on timing, which is a property of the machine, not of the app. It passes on a slow CI box for the wrong reason and fails on a fast one for no reason. Assert on the *state transitions* (loading shown, then content shown, then spinner gone) and let the timing fall where it may.

---

### 9.3.9 CI integration and sharding

**Definition.**

**CI integration** for testing means the pipeline design that decides which tests run when, on what, in what order, with what parallelism, and what blocks a merge. **Sharding** is splitting the test set into N roughly equal-duration groups that run in parallel, so wall-clock time falls as 1/N. Both are quality mechanisms: a test that does not run on every change is not a test.

**Mechanics -- the pipeline shape that works, and why each stage exists.**

```yaml
# .github/workflows/ci.yml
name: CI
on:
  pull_request:
  push:
    branches: [main]

concurrency:
  group: ci-${{ github.ref }}
  cancel-in-progress: true                 # never waste CI on a superseded commit

jobs:
  # ── Stage 1: the fast gate. 20 seconds, hard required. Nobody waits for this.
  verify:
    name: Lint, types, unit, property
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 10
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: pnpm/action-setup@v4
      - run: pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
      - run: pnpm lint
      - run: pnpm typecheck                     # tsc --noEmit, separate from tests on purpose
      - run: pnpm test:unit -- --coverage
      - run: pnpm test:property
      - run: pnpm test:mutation --changed        # Stryker on changed files only
      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        if: always()
        with: { name: coverage, path: coverage/ }

  # ── Stage 2: integration. Parallel with stage 1 where the dependency graph allows.
  integration:
    name: Integration (Postgres, Redis)
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 20
    services:
      postgres:
        image: postgres:16.4-alpine
        env:
          POSTGRES_USER: test
          POSTGRES_PASSWORD: test
          POSTGRES_DB: app_test
        ports: ['5432:5432']
        options: >-
          --health-cmd "pg_isready -U test -d app_test"
          --health-interval 5s --health-timeout 5s --health-retries 10
      redis:
        image: redis:7.4-alpine
        ports: ['6379:6379']
        options: >-
          --health-cmd "redis-cli ping" --health-interval 5s --health-timeout 5s --health-retries 10
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - run: pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
      - run: pnpm test:integration
        env:
          TEST_DATABASE_URL: postgres://test:test@localhost:5432/app_test
          TEST_REDIS_URL: redis://localhost:6379
      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        if: failure()
        with: { name: integration-diagnostics, path: test-artifacts/ }

  # ── Stage 3: build once, reuse for E2E and preview deploy.
  build:
    needs: [verify]
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    outputs:
      image: ${{ steps.meta.outputs.image }}
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - run: pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
      - run: pnpm build
      - id: meta
        run: echo "image=ghcr.io/org/app:${{ github.sha }}" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"

  # ── Stage 4: E2E against a preview environment built from the PR artefact.
  e2e:
    needs: [build]
    if: contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'e2e') || github.event_name == 'push'
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 25
    strategy:
      fail-fast: false                        # keep siblings running: their traces are the evidence
      matrix:
        shard: [1, 2, 3, 4]
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - run: pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
      - run: pnpm exec playwright install --with-deps chromium
      - run: pnpm exec playwright test --shard=${{ matrix.shard }}/4
        env:
          E2E_BASE_URL: ${{ needs.deploy-preview.outputs.url }}
          RUN_ID: ${{ github.run_id }}-${{ github.run_attempt }}
      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        if: failure()
        with: { name: trace-${{ matrix.shard }}, path: e2e/.artifacts }

  # ── Stage 5: what blocks the merge.
  required:
    needs: [verify, integration, e2e]
    if: always()
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - run: |
          echo "verify=${{ needs.verify.result }}" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
          echo "integration=${{ needs.integration.result }}" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
          echo "e2e=${{ needs.e2e.result }}" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
      - run: |
          test "${{ needs.verify.result }}" = "success"
          test "${{ needs.integration.result }}" = "success"
          if [ "${{ github.event_name }}" = "push" ]; then
            test "${{ needs.e2e.result }}" = "success"
          else
            echo "::notice::E2E is advisory on PRs; required on main."
          fi

  # ── Stage 6: nightly. The expensive matrix that must not gate every PR.
  nightly:
    if: github.event_name == 'schedule'
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 90
    steps:
      - run: pnpm exec playwright test --project=chromium --project=firefox --project=webkit --project=mobile-chrome
      - run: pnpm test:mutation
      - run: pnpm audit --audit-level=high
```

**Sharding mechanics and the mistake everyone makes.**

```bash
# Playwright shards by *file*, and --shard=n/total splits the file list.
# It does NOT balance by duration. A suite where one spec file takes 6 minutes
# and 40 files take 5 seconds each will have one shard that takes 6 minutes.
#
# Measure and rebalance:
npx playwright test --list                      # list all tests
npx playwright test --reporter=json > run.json   # per-test durations from the last run
node -e "                                        # greedy bin-packing by duration
  const r = require('./run.json');
  const shards = 4, bins = Array.from({length: shards}, () => ({d:0, files:new Set()}));
  const byFile = {};
  for (const s of r.suites.flatMap(s => s.specs)) {
    const f = s.file; (byFile[f] ||= []).push(s.tests[0]?.results?.[0]?.duration ?? 0);
  }
  for (const [f, ds] of Object.entries(byFile).sort((a,b) => b[1][0]-a[1][0])) {
    bins.sort((a,b) => a.d-b.d)[0].files.add(f); bins[0].d += ds.reduce((a,b)=>a+b,0);
  }
  console.log(bins.map((b,i) => ({shard: i+1, files: b.files.size, seconds: Math.round(b.d/1000)})));
"
```

Other sharders and their behaviour:
- **Vitest/Jest**: `--shard=1/4` (Vitest) and `--shard=1/4` with `--sequence.shuffle` off; both split by file. Vitest also supports `--reporter=json` for the same duration analysis.
- **Cypress**: `--shard=1/4` (Cypress 10.7+), splitting by spec file; free.
- **K6**: no sharding; scale VUs per stage and run parallel processes with different `startTime`, or use k6 Cloud.

**Conditional execution: which tests run for which change.** The highest-leverage optimisation is not running the E2E suite for a documentation change.

```ts
// playwright.config.ts -- tag by area, and select by area in CI.
test('checkout', { tag: ['@checkout', '@money'] }, async ({ page }) => { /* ... */ })
test('about page', { tag: ['@content'] }, async ({ page }) => { /* ... */ })
```

```bash
# PR: run only the areas the diff touches. Labels come from a path->tag map in CI.
npx playwright test --grep-invert @content
# @smoke always runs, on every PR, in under 60 seconds.
npx playwright test --grep @smoke
```

**Test ordering by dependency and value.** Order your pipeline by `(value per second)`:

1. Lint + typecheck (seconds, catches 40% of mistakes)
2. Unit + property (seconds to a minute)
3. Integration (1-3 min)
4. Build (1-3 min, and every E2E test needs it)
5. E2E smoke (60s, on a cached environment)
6. Full E2E (5-10 min, parallel shards)
7. Nightly matrix (30-90 min, all browsers + mutation + audits)

Run 5 in parallel with 6 when possible. And **make the merge gate the union of the fast stages**, with full E2E required on `main` and advisory on PRs unless the change is user-facing.

**Caching that actually helps.**

```yaml
- uses: actions/cache@v4
  with:
    path: |
      node_modules/.pnpm-store
      ~/.cache/ms-playwright
      ~/.cache/pnpm
    key: pnpm-${{ hashFiles('pnpm-lock.yaml') }}-playwright-${{ hashFiles('playwright.config.ts') }}
    restore-keys: pnpm-${{ hashFiles('pnpm-lock.yaml') }}-
```

Cache: the pnpm store, the Playwright browser binaries, the Vite/Vitest transform cache, and the Stryker report directory. Do **not** cache `node_modules` with a native-addon-heavy tree, and never cache the test database.

**Trend and enforce.**

```yaml
- name: Fail if the flake rate exceeds the budget
  run: |
    npx playwright test --last-failed --reporter=json > retry.json || true
    node -e "
      const flaky = require('./retry.json').suites.flatMap(s=>s.specs).filter(s => s.tests.some(t => t.results.length > 1));
      const total  = require('./retry.json').suites.flatMap(s=>s.specs).length;
      const rate = (flaky.length / total) * 100;
      console.log('flake rate: ' + rate.toFixed(2) + '%');
      if (rate > 0.5) { console.error('::error::Flake budget exceeded'); process.exit(1); }
    "
```

**Trade-offs.**

- **More stages = better feedback latency but more pipeline complexity** and more places for a status badge to lie. Four or five well-defined stages beat ten.
- **A preview environment per PR is the best fidelity and the slowest.** Cache aggressively; reuse environments per branch; deploy only when the build or migrations change.
- **Advisory vs required E2E on PRs** is a genuine political decision. Required gives confidence and costs developer time; advisory gives speed and lets regressions reach main. The pragmatic answer: required for user-facing changes (label-driven), advisory otherwise, always required on main.
- **Sharding by file is a blunt instrument.** Unbalanced suites need either rebalancing by file or splitting the long specs.
- **Fail-fast cancels useful work.** Always `fail-fast: false` in a test matrix.
- **A "required" aggregator job** is often worth it, because branch protection cannot express "these 3 of 5 jobs are required, and E2E only on main" cleanly.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Running everything on every PR, so the pipeline takes 40 minutes and developers batch their work.
2. `fail-fast: true` on a test matrix, cancelling the shards that were about to fail and whose traces you needed.
3. Sharding 4 ways when the suite is 90 seconds; the coordination overhead exceeds the gain.
4. Uploading traces only on the aggregated result, losing the per-shard evidence.
5. Reinstalling Playwright browsers on every job instead of caching them.
6. No `concurrency` group, so five pushes to a branch queue five 30-minute pipelines.
7. Marking a test "pending" or "skip" in the PR to make CI green, with no follow-up.
8. Treating a green `main` E2E run as optional because "the deploy smoke test passed" -- the deploy smoke test only covers 3 journeys.
9. Not measuring which tests are the slowest, so the shard imbalance persists for years.
10. No flake-rate metric, so the suite silently decays from 0.2% to 8% and nobody notices until a release is blocked.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you structure a CI testing pipeline?"* -- Ordered by value per second: lint/types, unit/property, integration with real services, build, E2E smoke, full E2E sharded, and a nightly matrix. Only the fast stages gate the merge; the expensive ones gate main and releases.
- 🟡 *"What is test sharding and what is the mistake people make?"* -- Splitting the suite into N groups to run in parallel. The mistake is assuming it balances: most sharders split by file count, not by duration, so one slow spec makes one shard five times longer. Measure per-test durations and rebalance.
- 🔴 *"CI is 45 minutes. Walk me through reducing it."* -- (1) Measure where the time goes per stage. (2) Cut what does not need to gate: docs/lint-only changes skip E2E. (3) Parallelise the unit and integration stages. (4) Shard E2E 4-6 ways with `fail-fast: false`. (5) Cache the pnpm store and Playwright browsers. (6) Reuse the preview environment per branch instead of deploying per run. (7) Make full E2E advisory on PRs, required on main. (8) Split the slowest spec files.
- 🔴 *"How do you know your test suite is healthy?"* -- Five numbers, tracked over time: unit suite duration, integration duration, E2E wall clock, flake rate (tests that failed then passed on retry), and quarantine size. Plus the ratio of E2E to total tests, and the defect-escape rate by tier (which bugs got past which tier). If flake rate trends up, quality is down even if everything is green.
- ⚫ *"How would you require E2E on PRs that touch the checkout but not on a README change?"* -- Path or label based selection. Either a label (`e2e`) applied by a bot from the diff paths, or a `tags`/`grep` selector computed in CI from the changed-file list, with the `required` aggregator job reading the job result conditionally. Either way, the rule must be visible in the PR so nobody is surprised by a 10-minute pipeline.

---

### 9.3.10 Flaky test causes and fixes: the complete catalogue

**Definition.**

A **flaky test** is one that passes and fails for the same code, with no change in between. Flake is not a nuisance; it is the single largest destroyer of test-suite value. A suite with 5% flake has an effective gate strength near zero: developers learn to re-run, reviewers stop reading, and real regressions hide in the noise. Eliminating flake is almost always higher-value than writing new tests.

**The taxonomy. Every flaky test fits one of these eleven classes.**

**1. Race condition / missing await.** The most common class by a wide margin.
- *Symptoms:* "expected undefined", intermittent 0/1 counts, tests that pass in isolation and fail in a suite.
- *Causes:* a promise not awaited; `setTimeout` instead of awaiting an event; a test that finishes before an async effect completes; `fireEvent` instead of `userEvent`; an assertion immediately after an action.
- *Fixes:* `await` the promise the function returns; assert with `findBy*`/`waitFor`; use `userEvent`; never assert in the same tick as an action; `expect.assertions(n)`.
- *Detection:* run the file 1000 times (`--repeat-each 1000`) and look at the failure distribution; enable `--sequence.shuffle`.

**2. Shared state / test interdependence.**
- *Symptoms:* passes alone, fails in the suite; order-dependent; "works on a clean checkout".
- *Causes:* a module-level mutable object; a shared file, port, or database; a shared `storageState`; a `beforeAll` that seeds without cleanup; a global cache in the app under test; a singleton Prisma/HTTP client with per-test config.
- *Fixes:* one fresh state per test (`beforeEach`); namespaced data; per-worker databases; `fullyParallel` (which forces the issue); `vi.resetModules()`; `clearAllMocks` in `afterEach`; never mutate module-scope fixtures.
- *Detection:* `--sequence.shuffle`; run with `--maxWorkers=1` (if it passes serial, it is shared state).

**3. Real time and timers.**
- *Symptoms:* fails ~1% in CI, always on loaded machines; "fails under load".
- *Causes:* a real `setTimeout`, a real debounce, a real network timeout, `Date.now()` in a test, an assertion on elapsed time.
- *Fixes:* fake timers (`vi.useFakeTimers`) or an injected `FakeClock`; wait for a condition with a deadline rather than a fixed sleep; bound assertions by *state*, not duration.
- *Detection:* run with the machine loaded (`stress-ng`, or 20 parallel builds); check for `setTimeout`/`sleep` in the test or the code under test.

**4. Ordering-dependent randomness.**
- *Symptoms:* fails on ~1 in 50 runs with no pattern.
- *Causes:* `Math.random()`, `Date.now()`, `uuid`, `Object.keys()` iteration order on a numeric-ish key, `Set`/`Map` ordering, an unseeded property test, a random port, a random sort comparator that is not total.
- *Fixes:* inject `Random`; seed fast-check (`{ seed: 0 }`) and record the failing seed; sort before comparing; use a total ordering comparator; use deterministic ids in fixtures.
- *Detection:* run with `--repeat-each 200`; look for any `Math.random`/`Date.now`/uuid in the diff of the failing test's dependencies.

**5. Resource exhaustion and port/socket collisions.**
- *Symptoms:* `EADDRINUSE`, "too many open files", connection pool timeouts, failures only with high parallelism.
- *Causes:* hardcoded ports; one server per test; connection pools not closed; too many DB connections; file descriptor leaks; a container started per file.
- *Fixes:* port 0 (ephemeral); one server per suite; `afterAll` closing every client; size the pool to the worker count; `globalSetup` for containers; `--maxWorkers` tuned to the pool.
- *Detection:* `lsof -i` during a run; look at pool metrics; the failure correlates with worker index.

**6. Environment / configuration drift.**
- *Symptoms:* fails in CI, passes locally; fails on one OS; fails after an unrelated change.
- *Causes:* a `.env` difference; `TZ` or `LANG` unset in CI; a case-sensitive filesystem (macOS) hiding a case bug; a missing locale; a different Node version; a feature flag defaulting differently; a certificate/proxy difference.
- *Fixes:* one env loader validated by a schema (`zod`) that fails fast; pin `TZ=UTC` in CI and set it locally too; run the suite in the same container locally (a `make test` target with Docker); assert on configuration in a startup test.
- *Detection:* run the exact CI command locally in the CI container; diff the env.

**7. Unseeded / unstable external dependencies.**
- *Symptoms:* fails when a third party is slow, rate-limits, or changes.
- *Causes:* a real API call in a test; a shared sandbox account; a random upstream response; the clock in a sandbox token.
- *Fixes:* MSW for all third parties; recorded cassettes; per-run sandbox accounts; never assert on third-party data you did not stub.
- *Detection:* grep the test and its imports for real hostnames.

**8. Test data that depends on prior runs.**
- *Symptoms:* first run green, subsequent runs red; "it passed yesterday".
- *Causes:* data left behind; a unique constraint hit; a counter that started at 0; a sequence not reset.
- *Fixes:* namespacing and bulk purge; `TRUNCATE ... RESTART IDENTITY`; deterministic ids; an explicit `reset` endpoint; never assert on absolute counts in a shared database.
- *Detection:* run the suite twice in a row; grep for `createMany` without cleanup.

**9. Over-broad selectors and DOM timing in the UI.**
- *Symptoms:* `found multiple elements`, `element is not attached to the DOM`, `element is not visible`.
- *Causes:* a selector matching both a mobile and a desktop element; a re-render replacing the node between query and click; an animation; a hidden duplicate for a11y; a list that re-sorts.
- *Fixes:* scope and filter; use `getByRole` with `name`; use `filter({ has, hasText })`; wait for the specific state; use `data-cy` for deliberately duplicated elements; never use nth-child.
- *Detection:* run with `--repeat-each 20 --workers=4`; inspect the trace.

**10. Concurrency and parallelism bugs.**
- *Symptoms:* fails only at high worker counts; failures that move between tests.
- *Causes:* a shared file or database without namespacing; a non-atomic write; a `Math.random` id collision; a lock contention; a `fullyParallel` suite with a shared fixture.
- *Fixes:* namespace everything; atomic writes (`writeFile` to temp + rename); per-worker resources; `test.describe.configure({ mode: 'serial' })` for the genuinely sequential tests (and document why).
- *Detection:* compare failure rate at `--workers=1` vs `--workers=16`.

**11. Framework and tooling bugs.**
- *Symptoms:* a test that is impossible to make stable; version-specific behaviour.
- *Causes:* a known runner bug; a library with a race in its own test hooks; jsdom missing an API; a fake-timer default that differs.
- *Fixes:* upgrade (often fixed); pin versions; add a polyfill in setup; isolate the library behind your own adapter and test that; a documented, minimal, time-boxed workaround with a linked issue.
- *Detection:* reduce to a minimal reproduction with no application code; check the library's issue tracker.

**The diagnostic protocol. Follow it in order; do not skip to the fix.**

```bash
# 1. Is it flaky, or is it a real failure? Reproduce in isolation, many times.
npx vitest run path/to/file.spec.ts --repeat.each 50 --no-file-parallelism
npx playwright test e2e/checkout.spec.ts --repeat-each 20 --workers=1 --retries=0

# 2. Is it order dependence? Shuffle.
npx vitest run --sequence.shuffle
npx playwright test --workers=1     # if it passes serial but fails parallel: shared state or parallelism

# 3. Is it load sensitivity? Run under load.
npx playwright test --repeat-each 10 --workers=8   # if the failure rate rises: timing

# 4. Is it data dependence? Run the suite twice back to back without cleanup.
npx vitest run && npx vitest run

# 5. Get the evidence. ALWAYS: trace on, video on, console on, network on.
npx playwright test --trace=on --retries=0

# 6. Bisect by bisection, not by guessing.
#    Narrow the test to the smallest failing case; then remove the app under test piece by piece.
```

```ts
// The instrumentation that turns a mystery into a fact.
beforeEach(() => {
  const t0 = performance.now()
  afterEach(() => { metrics.timing('test', { file: currentFile(), ms: performance.now() - t0 }) })
})

// Playwright: log every request and console message, into the report.
test.beforeEach(async ({ page }) => {
  page.on('console', (m) => { if (m.type() === 'error') console.log('[console.error]', m.text()) })
  page.on('requestfailed', (r) => console.log('[requestfailed]', r.url(), r.failure()?.errorText))
  page.on('response', (r) => { if (r.status() >= 400) console.log('[http]', r.status(), r.url()) })
})
```

**The flake-handling policy (this is the governance that actually works).**

1. **Never merge a test with a known flake.** Quarantine it (`test.skip` with a `@flaky` tag, a linked ticket, and a named owner) so it is visible and counted.
2. **Fail CI if the quarantine list grows.** A ratchet: the count may only go down in a given period.
3. **Never blanket-retry more than once.** `retries: 1` is the maximum. Track the "retried tests" count as a metric.
