4. **Every flake gets a 2-hour time-boxed investigation** in the week it is discovered. Flake causes cluster (usually one root cause explains five flakes).
5. **A flake older than two weeks is a bug with a deadline**, not a to-do.
6. **A flake that is genuinely a product bug** (the app really does have a race) is fixed in the app, and the test stays.

```ts
// Quarantine registry: a checked-in list, reviewed like code.
export const QUARANTINE = new Set([
  'e2e/checkout-3ds.spec.ts:47',   // TICKET-4821: 3DS challenge timing; owner: @sam; due 2026-04-01
  'src/legacy/parser.spec.ts:12',  // TICKET-4802: depends on host TZ; owner: @ada; due 2026-03-20
])
```

**Trade-offs.**

- **Time-boxed investigation costs engineering time upfront** and saves multiples of it: a 1% flake suite in a 500-test suite means 5 failures per run, each costing ~10 minutes of triage -- 50 minutes burned per pipeline, every day.
- **Quarantine is honest; deletion is not.** A deleted flaky test is a hidden coverage hole; a quarantined one is a tracked debt item. Teams resist quarantine because it is visible, and that is exactly why it works.
- **`retries: 1` produces false greens.** It is standard practice because a small residual flake is unavoidable, and the flake-rate metric is what makes it honest. Anything more is malpractice.
- **Some flakes are unfixable in the short term** (a third-party library bug). The correct response is to isolate the library behind an adapter, test the adapter, and keep the flaky integration test quarantined with a date.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Retrying until green in CI without a flake metric, which trains everyone to ignore red.
2. Adding `waitForTimeout` to "fix" a flake, which converts flakiness into slowness and hides the cause.
3. Running the failing test 5 more times locally, concluding "must be flaky", and moving on with no diagnosis.
4. Deleting a flaky test without a replacement, quietly losing coverage.
5. Not using `--trace=on --retries=0` when investigating, so the evidence is a one-line error message.
6. Fixing the symptom (more retries, longer timeouts) rather than the cause (shared state, missing await).
7. Running the suite in an order that hides the problem (no shuffle, serial only), so it looks clean in CI and fails on a colleague's machine.
8. Not checking the obvious: did someone commit a `.only`? Did someone raise a timeout "temporarily" three months ago?

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"A test fails intermittently. How do you diagnose it?"* -- Reproduce with repetition (`--repeat-each 50`), then determine the class: order dependence (shuffle/serial), load sensitivity (repeat under load), data dependence (run twice), concurrency (vary workers), or environment. Then get evidence (trace, video, console, network) *before* changing anything.
- 🟡 *"What are the most common causes of flaky tests?"* -- Missing awaits, shared mutable state, real time and timers, unseeded randomness, and test data left from a previous run. Those five account for the large majority; the rest are environment drift, external dependencies, and framework bugs.
- 🔴 *"Your unit suite has 1.5% flake. What do you do, in order?"* -- (1) Get the failing list from CI across many runs (flake is statistical). (2) Reproduce each with `--repeat-each 200 --sequence.shuffle`. (3) Fix by class: add the missing `await`, add `beforeEach` state, replace real timers with a `FakeClock`, seed the RNG. (4) Add the guards that prevent recurrence: `expect.assertions`, `vi.getTimerCount() === 0`, unhandled-rejection traps. (5) Quarantine anything unresolved with a ticket and an owner, and ratchet the quarantine count.
- 🔴 *"A Playwright test flakes 1 in 10. Trace found the button visible, then the click failed. What is the likely cause and the fix?"* -- The node was re-rendered or replaced between the actionability check and the click (a list re-sorted, a state update replaced the element, or a parent re-rendered). Fixes: assert a stable state before acting, use a locator that survives re-render, avoid `nth()` on a re-sorting list, and use `data-cy`/role+name over structural selectors. Also check for a double-render caused by a state update in an effect.
- ⚫ *"Someone proposes `retries: 5` in Playwright to fix a flaky suite. Your response?"* -- No. `retries: 1` is the maximum, and the flake rate must be measured and published. Retries hide the signal that the suite is decaying; the failure mode of a high-retry suite is that a real regression passes on the third attempt and nobody investigates. Fix the causes, quarantine the unfixable with a date.

---

## 9.4 Quality Beyond Tests

### 9.4.1 Performance and load testing with k6

**Definition.**

**Performance testing** measures whether a system meets a performance *requirement* under a specified *workload*. There are four kinds, and confusing them is the most common error in this area:

| Kind | Question | Workload | k6 is for this? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Load test** | Does it meet its SLO at expected peak load? | realistic mix, steady | ✅ primary use |
| **Stress test** | Where is the breaking point, and how does it fail? | ramp beyond peak until failure | ✅ yes |
| **Soak/endurance** | Does it degrade over hours (leaks, degradation)? | expected load, very long | ✅ yes (with care) |
| **Spike** | Does it survive a sudden 10x jump? | sudden step | ✅ yes |

k6 (Grafana) is a JavaScript-based load generator: you write a test script, k6 runs it with N virtual users (VUs) for a duration, and it reports latency percentiles, throughput, error rate, and custom checks. It runs from a single binary, so it is easy to run in CI, in a container, or from a laptop.

**Mechanics -- the metrics that matter, and the thresholds that matter.**

```
http_req_duration              latency of each request          p(95), p(99) — the mean is a lie
http_req_failed                rate of failed requests          the SLO's error budget
http_reqs                      throughput (req/s)                capacity
http_req_waiting (TTFB)        time to first byte               backend vs network
http_req_connect               connection setup                 connection pool sizing
http_req_blocked               time waiting for a VU slot       test is CPU-bound, not app-bound
vus                            concurrency in effect            did the test have enough VUs?
iterations                     transactions completed           throughput of business transactions
checks                         custom assertion pass rate       your business invariants
data_received                  bytes                            payload size regressions
```

**The rule that makes load tests useful: assert the SLO as a threshold, so the test fails.** A load test that only prints a report is a dashboard, not a test.

**Runnable code -- a production-shaped k6 scenario for a checkout API.**

```js
// perf/checkout.js
import http from 'k6/http'
import { check, group, sleep, fail } from 'k6'
import { Trend, Rate, Counter } from 'k6/metrics'
import { checkTrend } from 'https://jslib.k6.io/k6-utils/1.4.0/index.js'

// ── Custom metrics: business outcomes, not just HTTP.
const orderCreated = new Trend('order_created_duration', true)
const checkoutErrors = new Rate('checkout_error_rate')
const declinedCorrectly = new Counter('declines_handled_correctly')
const underpayment = new Counter('underpayment_detected')          // a correctness guard, under load

const BASE = __ENV.BASE_URL || 'http://127.0.0.1:3100'
const THINK = Number(__ENV.THINK_TIME || 1)                         // seconds between user actions

// ── A realistic arrival model: stages with ramping VUs and a spike.
export const options = {
  scenarios: {
    // Steady load at expected peak.
    peak: {
      executor: 'ramping-vus',
      startVUs: 0, stages: [
        { duration: '1m', target: 20 },     // warm-up: JIT, caches, connection pools
        { duration: '5m', target: 50 },     // ramp to peak
        { duration: '10m', target: 50 },    // hold: this is where steady-state metrics are valid
        { duration: '2m', target: 0 },      // drain
      ],
      exec: 'checkoutFlow',
      tags: { scenario: 'peak' },
    },
    // A sudden spike, e.g. a marketing email lands.
    spike: {
      executor: 'ramping-vus',
      startVUs: 50, stages: [{ duration: '10s', target: 300 }, { duration: '2m', target: 300 }, { duration: '1m', target: 50 }],
      exec: 'checkoutFlow',
      startTime: '12m',
      tags: { scenario: 'spike' },
    },
  },
  thresholds: {
    // ── Latency SLO. p95 and p99, not the mean.
    'http_req_duration{scenario:peak}': ['p(95)<400', 'p(99)<1200'],
    'http_req_failed{scenario:peak}':   ['rate<0.01'],                  // 1% error budget
    'http_req_waiting{scenario:peak}':  ['p(95)<250'],
    'http_req_connect{scenario:peak}':   ['p(95)<50'],

    // ── Business SLO: the whole transaction, end to end.
    'order_created_duration':            ['p(95)<800', 'p(99)<2000'],
    'checkout_error_rate':               ['rate<0.005'],
    'checks':                            ['rate>0.995'],

    // ── Guard rails: the test itself must be healthy, or the numbers are meaningless.
    'vus':                              ['max>=50'],                    // enough VUs were available
    'http_req_blocked':                 ['p(95)<50'],                   // the generator is not the bottleneck
    'dropped_iterations':               ['count==0'],                   // no VU starvation

    // Abort the test if the system is clearly broken; do not collect 30 minutes of garbage.
    'http_req_failed{scenario:peak}':    [{ threshold: 'rate>0.10', abortOnFail: true, delayAbortEval: '30s' }],
  },
  noConnectionReuse: false,          // realistic: keep-alive on, so you measure your pool, not TCP setup
  httpDebug: false,
  insecureSkipTLSVerify: __ENV.TLS_SELF_SIGNED === 'true',
  summaryTrendStats: ['avg', 'min', 'med', 'p(90)', 'p(95)', 'p(99)', 'max'],
}

// ── Setup: create data once, reuse for every VU. Never create inside the VU loop.
export function setup() {
  const health = http.get(`${BASE}/healthz`, { tags: { phase: 'setup' } })
  if (health.status !== 200) fail(`service is not healthy: ${health.status} ${health.body}`)

  const users = []
  for (let i = 0; i < 200; i++) {
    const r = http.post(`${BASE}/api/test/load/users`, JSON.stringify({ index: i }), {
      headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json', 'X-E2E-Key': __ENV.E2E_KEY },
    })
    users.push(r.json('id'))
  }
  return { users, startTime: Date.now() }
}

// ── A full user journey, not a single endpoint. Load-testing one GET is not a load test.
export function checkoutFlow(data) {
  const userId = data.users[__VU % data.users.length]
  const headers = {
    'Content-Type': 'application/json',
    Authorization: `Bearer ${__ENV.TOKEN}`,
    'X-Load-Test': 'true',
  }

  group('browse', () => {
    const res = http.get(`${BASE}/api/products?limit=24`, { headers, tags: { step: 'browse' } })
    check(res, { 'browse 200': (r) => r.status === 200, 'browse has items': (r) => (r.json('items') || []).length > 0 })
  })

  group('checkout', () => {
    // 5% of attempts use a card that declines, so the decline path is exercised under load too.
    const willDecline = (__ITER + __VU) % 20 === 0
    const payload = JSON.stringify({
      userId,
      lines: [{ sku: `SKU-${(__VU % 50) + 1}`, qty: 1 }],
      paymentToken: willDecline ? 'tok_decline' : 'tok_visa',
    })

    const res = http.post(`${BASE}/api/orders`, payload, { headers, tags: { step: 'checkout' } })
    const ok = check(res, {
      'checkout 201 or 402': (r) => r.status === 201 || r.status === 402,
      'has order id on 201': (r) => r.status !== 201 || !!r.json('id'),
    })
    if (res.status === 402) declinedCorrectly.add(1)

    // Correctness under load: the total the server computed must be right.
    if (res.status === 201) {
      const total = res.json('totalCents')
      if (typeof total !== 'number' || total <= 0) underpayment.add(1)
      orderCreated.add(res.timings.duration)
    }
    checkoutErrors.add(!ok)
  })

  // Think time makes it a *realistic* load, not a hammer. Omit it for a capacity test.
  sleep(Math.random() * THINK)
}

// ── Teardown: report the invariants the HTTP thresholds cannot see.
export function teardown(data) {
  console.log(`load test ran for ${Math.round((Date.now() - data.startTime) / 1000)}s`)
  console.log(`declines handled correctly: ${declinedCorrectly.count}`)
  console.log(`underpayment/undefined totals detected: ${underpayment.count}  (must be 0)`)
}
```

**Threshold files and environment-driven configuration**, so the same script serves dev, staging, and CI:

```js
// perf/thresholds.js
export const env = {
  dev:     { 'http_req_duration': ['p(95)<1500'], 'http_req_failed': ['rate<0.05'] },
  staging: { 'http_req_duration': ['p(95)<600',  'p(99)<1500'], 'http_req_failed': ['rate<0.02'] },
  prod:    { 'http_req_duration': ['p(95)<400',  'p(99)<1200'], 'http_req_failed': ['rate<0.01'] },
}
```

```bash
# Local smoke: 20 VUs, 30s, against a local stack.
k6 run -e BASE_URL=http://127.0.0.1:3100 -e THINK_TIME=0.2 \
       --vus 20 --duration 30s --out json=results-local.json perf/checkout.js

# Staging: the real profile.
k6 run -e BASE_URL=https://staging.example.test -e THINK_TIME=1 perf/checkout.js

# CI regression gate (a light profile, every PR against a preview environment).
k6 run -e BASE_URL=$PREVIEW_URL --vus 25 --duration 45s perf/smoke.js

# Distributed load, when one machine is not enough.
#   Same script, each instance with a distinct VU offset and the results merged.
k6 run --execution-segment 0/3 -e BASE_URL=$URL perf/checkout.js
k6 run --execution-segment 1/3 -e BASE_URL=$URL perf/checkout.js
k6 run --execution-segment 2/3 -e BASE_URL=$URL perf/checkout.js
```

**When to use k6 vs alternatives.**

Use k6 when: you need HTTP/gRPC load testing with a JS script, in CI, without a large vendor contract; you want business-level checks alongside HTTP metrics; and you can generate realistic traffic from a single machine or a few containers.

Use JMeter/Gatling when: you need a GUI-driven, non-programmer-friendly load definition, a very wide protocol range, or an existing Gatling/JMeter suite. Use Locust when your load pattern is Python-flavoured and your team prefers it. Use k6 Cloud when you need multi-region generation and device-level realism.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Load tests measure the system you point them at, including the test environment's noise.** A 2-core CI runner cannot generate meaningful load for a 32-core service; you will measure k6's own CPU. Check `http_req_blocked` and `vus` in the result, and generate from a machine at least as powerful as the target's load generator needs.
- **k6 is not a browser.** It does not execute JavaScript, so it tests your API, not your frontend. For front-end performance, use Lighthouse/CrUX/web-vitals RUM data and Playwright for a synthetic check.
- **Thresholds are SLOs, not aspirations.** A threshold you set to today's number guarantees the test never fails and therefore never helps. Set it to the number your users agreed to, and let it fail when you regress.
- **Load tests are not idempotent under mutation.** A load test that creates 300 000 orders needs cleanup and a database big enough to hold them, or it measures the vacuum, not your service. Prefer a load-test mode that uses a read-heavy mix with a small write fraction, or a shadow/read replica, or reset the environment afterwards.
- **A load test that only does GETs will never find the write-concurrency bug.** Include a write phase, and include a burst phase.
- **The p99 is where the pain is and where the tests are least stable.** Gate on p95 in every-PR runs and p99 in nightly runs.

**Production considerations.**

- Run a **nightly** performance regression run against staging, and gate p95 and the error rate. Publish the result as a trend, not a pass/fail, so a gradual drift is visible.
- Run a **stress test** on a schedule to find the breaking point and, more importantly, *how it fails*: does it degrade gracefully (errors, then recovery) or collapse (connection refused, cascading timeouts)? That answer is a resilience finding, not a performance finding.
- Run a **soak test** (4-8 hours at 70% of peak) before any release that changes infrastructure, connection pooling, or anything that might leak. It catches the leaks that a 5-minute test cannot.
- Keep load-test traffic out of production analytics: mark every request with a header, exclude it in the analytics pipeline, and never let a load test trigger real emails, real charges, or real webhooks. Sandbox everything, and assert that in the test setup.
- Put a hard ceiling on the load: a "max VUs" and an `abortOnFail` so a test cannot turn into an accidental denial of service against a shared environment.
- **Correlate with real user data**: the RUM p75 for real users is the ground truth; the load test tells you what happens as you approach the limit. If the load test says 400ms at 50 VUs and real users see 200ms at 5% CPU, your model is wrong somewhere and the RUM data wins.

**Common mistakes.**

1. No thresholds -- the test prints a report and nobody decides anything.
2. Thresholds set to current behaviour, so the test can never fail.
3. `sleep(0)` with no think time, which is a saturation test mislabelled as a load test.
4. No `checks`, so the test reports a 200-OK rate on a system that returned 200 with an empty order.
5. Creating data inside the VU loop, so the test measures the test's own inserts.
6. Running the load generator on a machine smaller than the target, so you measure k6.
7. No correctness guard under load, so a load test "passes" while every order is created with total 0.
8. Running against production, or against staging with production webhooks enabled.
9. Only testing steady state, so the spike and the recovery are unknown.
10. Asserting on the mean latency, which hides the tail entirely.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"How do you run a load test with k6?"* -- Write a scenario that models a real user journey, configure ramping VUs and a duration, add thresholds for p95 latency and error rate plus business checks, and run it against a staging environment. The thresholds make it a test rather than a report.
- 🟡 *"Why p95 and not the mean?"* -- Latency distributions are long-tailed. A mean of 120ms can coexist with 1% of requests taking 4 seconds, which is exactly the population that complains. Percentiles describe the experience; the mean hides it.
- 🔴 *"How do you load test a checkout flow without destroying the staging database?"* -- Seed a realistic but bounded dataset in `setup()`, use a read-heavy mix with a small write fraction, run against a database snapshot or a read replica where possible, run inside a window with a hard VU ceiling, mark all traffic with a header so analytics can exclude it, and purge the created records in `teardown()`. Add `abortOnFail` so a broken service stops the test early.
- 🔴 *"Your p95 is 800ms in the load test but 120ms in production. What are the likely causes?"* -- (1) The load generator and the service share a machine, so you are measuring contention. (2) No think time: your scenario is a saturation test. (3) The staging data set is far smaller, so cache hit rates are unrealistically high. (4) Staging has no CDN, no edge cache, and no warm connection pool. (5) The load test is hitting a different code path (no cache, no replica).
- ⚫ *"What is a soak test and why does it matter?"* -- Running at a sustained fraction of peak for hours. It catches connection-pool exhaustion, memory leaks, log/index growth, cache eviction cascades, and background-job pile-ups that a 5-minute test never reaches. Run it before any infrastructure or pooling change.

---

### 9.4.2 Accessibility testing: automated, manual, and assistive-technology testing

**Definition.**

**Accessibility (a11y)** testing verifies that the product is usable by people with disabilities -- visual, auditory, motor, cognitive, and situational. WCAG 2.2 is the standard, at three conformance levels (A, AA, AAA; most regulation targets AA). Testing has three layers, and only the first is automatable:

1. **Automated scanning** (axe-core, Lighthouse, Pa11y, eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y): catches roughly 20-40% of issues -- missing labels, contrast, missing alt, invalid ARIA, heading order, duplicate ids, focus order in simple cases.
2. **Manual expert testing** (keyboard, screen reader, zoom, forced colors, motion): catches the rest -- the ones a machine cannot judge (alt text *quality*, focus *visibility*, reading order *sense*, error message *clarity*, timeout sufficiency).
3. **User testing with disabled people**: catches what neither of the first two can -- whether the experience is actually usable and non-embarrassing.

**The legal and commercial frame you need to know for interviews:** the **European Accessibility Act (EAA)** applies from 28 June 2025 to a broad range of consumer-facing digital products and services in the EU; the **UK Equalities Act 2010** (public sector) and the **US ADA / Section 508** and **WCAG** in procurement contexts are also live. Accessibility is a legal obligation in many markets, not a nice-to-have.

**Mechanics -- automated a11y in each test tier, which is the correct distribution.**

```ts
// ── 1. Lint: catches the cheap, static errors at authoring time.
import jsxA11y from 'eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y'

// eslint.config.js
export default [
  jsxA11y.flatConfigs.recommended,
  {
    rules: {
      'jsx-a11y/alt-text': 'error',
      'jsx-a11y/label-has-associated-control': ['error', { assert: 'either' }],
      'jsx-a11y/anchor-is-valid': ['error', { components: ['Link'], specialLink: ['to'] }],
      'jsx-a11y/aria-role': ['error', { ignoreNonDOM: true }],
      'jsx-a11y/no-redundant-roles': 'error',
      'jsx-a11y/autocomplete-valid': 'error',
      'jsx-a11y/prefer-native-elements': 'error',
    },
  },
]
```

```ts
// ── 2. Component tests: assert a11y on every component's key states. This is where it pays.
import { axe, toHaveNoViolations } from 'jest-axe'
import { expect, it, describe } from 'vitest'
import { render, screen } from '@testing-library/react'
import userEvent from '@testing-library/user-event'
import axe from 'vitest-axe'          // or: import { axe } from 'vitest-axe'
expect.extend(toHaveNoViolations)

const mount = (ui: React.ReactElement) => render(<Providers>{ui}</Providers>)

describe('<CheckoutForm /> accessibility', () => {
  it('has no detectable violations in its initial state', async () => {
    const { container } = mount(<CheckoutForm />)
    expect(await axe(container)).toHaveNoViolations()
  })

  it('has no detectable violations with validation errors displayed', async () => {
    const user = userEvent.setup()
    mount(<CheckoutForm />)
    await user.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: /confirm/i }))    // trigger the error state
    expect(screen.getAllByRole('alert')).toHaveLength(2)
    expect(await axe(document.body)).toHaveNoViolations()
  })

  it('associates each error with its field and describes it in an accessible way', async () => {
    const user = userEvent.setup()
    mount(<CheckoutForm />)
    await user.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: /confirm/i }))

    const email = screen.getByRole('textbox', { name: /email address/i })
    // Programmatically associated via aria-describedby -> the description is announced with the field.
    expect(email).toHaveAccessibleDescription(/enter a valid email address/i)
    expect(email).toHaveAttribute('aria-invalid', 'true')
  })

  it('moves focus to the first invalid field on submit, so keyboard users are not stranded', async () => {
    const user = userEvent.setup()
    mount(<CheckoutForm />)
    await user.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: /confirm/i }))
    expect(screen.getByRole('textbox', { name: /email address/i })).toHaveFocus()
  })

  it('announces a status change with a live region', async () => {
    const user = userEvent.setup()
    mount(<CheckoutForm />)
    expect(screen.getByRole('status')).toBeInTheDocument()
    await user.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: /confirm/i }))
    expect(screen.getByRole('status')).toHaveTextContent(/2 fields need your attention/i)
  })
})
```

```ts
// ── 3. E2E: axe on the assembled page, for the pages that matter. Run on every build.
import AxeBuilder from '@axe-core/playwright'
import { test, expect } from '@playwright/test'

const PAGES = ['/', '/search', '/product/1', '/basket', '/checkout', '/account/orders', '/help']

for (const path of PAGES) {
  test(`a11y: ${path}`, async ({ page }) => {
    await page.goto(path)
    await expect(page.getByRole('main')).toBeVisible()
    const results = await new AxeBuilder({ page })
      .withTags(['wcag2a', 'wcag2aa', 'wcag21a', 'wcag21aa', 'wcag22aa'])
      .analyze()
    expect(results.violations).toEqual([])     // a hard gate on the assembled page
  })
}

// Scan the interactive states too: modals, menus, and errors hide most a11y bugs.
test('a11y: the basket empty state and the error state', async ({ page, api }) => {
  await api.setCart([])
  await page.goto('/basket')
  expect((await new AxeBuilder({ page }).analyze()).violations).toEqual([])

  await api.setError('basket_unavailable')
  await page.goto('/basket')
  expect((await new AxeBuilder({ page }).analyze()).violations).toEqual([])
})
```

```bash
# 4. A page-level CLI gate for non-React pages or a quick audit.
npx pa11y-ci --config .pa11yci.json
npx lighthouse http://localhost:3100 --only-categories=accessibility --budget-path=./lighthouse-budget.json --chrome-flags="--headless"
```

**Mechanics -- the manual checklist, which is where the remaining 60-80% lives.** Run this on every major release for the critical journeys. Budget 2-4 hours per journey; it is the highest-value accessibility work you can do.

1. **Keyboard only.** Unplug the mouse. `Tab`, `Shift+Tab`, `Enter`, `Space`, `Escape`, arrow keys. Every interactive element reachable, in a sensible order, with a visible focus indicator at every stop. No keyboard traps. Skip links work.
2. **Screen reader (NVDA on Windows, VoiceOver on macOS/iOS, Orca on Linux).** Learn the basics of one properly. Test: every image's alt text is *meaningful* (not "image1.jpg"); every form field has a label; errors are announced and associated; dynamic content is announced via a live region; the page has one `h1` and a sane heading structure; tables have headers; the document language is set; landmarks (`header`, `nav`, `main`, `footer`) exist.
3. **Zoom and reflow.** 200% browser zoom, and 400% on a 1280px viewport (WCAG 1.4.10 Reflow). No horizontal scrolling, no clipped content, no overlapping text.
4. **Colour and contrast.** Text contrast >= 4.5:1 (normal) and 3:1 (large, >= 24px or 19px bold). Non-text contrast >= 3:1 for UI components and focus indicators. Never colour alone to convey meaning. Check with a simulator for deuteranopia/protanopia/tritanopia and for achromatopsia.
5. **Forced colours / high contrast mode** (Windows High Contrast, `prefers-contrast: more`). Your custom colours often disappear.
6. **Reduced motion.** `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` honoured: no parallax, no auto-playing carousels, no large transitions. `prefers-reduced-transparency`, `prefers-contrast`, `forced-colors`.
7. **Text spacing** (WCAG 1.4.12): with the bookmarklet applying 1.5x line-height, 1.5x letter-spacing, 4px word-spacing, 1.2x paragraph-spacing, nothing clips.
8. **Target size** (WCAG 2.2, 2.5.8, AA): interactive targets >= 24x24 CSS pixels (or have sufficient spacing).
9. **Timing and session.** `WCAG 2.2.1`: no time limits, or a way to extend them. Session time-outs announce and allow extension (2.2.6).
10. **Cognitive / content.** Alt text quality; instructions before a form, not only in error messages; error messages say *how to fix*; consistent navigation; no "click here" link text; plain language.
11. **Status messages** (4.1.3): success and error messages appear without a focus change and are announced.
12. **Drag-and-drop alternatives** (2.5.7, dragging movements, AA in 2.2): every drag has a click-based alternative.

**Mechanics -- the design-system-level fixes that eliminate whole classes of a11y bugs.**

```tsx
// ── A field component that cannot be rendered without a label, by construction.
type FieldProps = {
  label: string
  hint?: string
  error?: string
  children: (props: {
    id: string
    'aria-describedby': string | undefined
    'aria-invalid': boolean | undefined
  }) => ReactNode
}

export function Field({ label, hint, error, children }: FieldProps) {
  const id = useId()
  const hintId = hint ? `${id}-hint` : undefined
  const errId = error ? `${id}-err` : undefined
  const describedBy = [errId, hintId].filter(Boolean).join(' ') || undefined

  return (
    <div>
      <label htmlFor={id}>{label}</label>
      {hint && <p id={hintId}>{hint}</p>}
      {children({ id, 'aria-describedby': describedBy, 'aria-invalid': error ? true : undefined })}
      {error && <p id={errId} role="alert">{error}</p>}
    </div>
  )
}

// Usage -- impossible to forget the label or the association.
<Field label="Email address" hint="We only use this for receipts" error={errors.email}>
  {(p) => <input type="email" autoComplete="email" {...p} />}
</Field>
```

```tsx
// ── A dialog that manages focus, escape, and the accessible name. Do not roll this by hand;
// ── use a well-tested primitive (Radix, Headless UI, React Aria, Ark UI).
import { Dialog } from '@radix-ui/react-dialog'

<Dialog.Root open={open} onOpenChange={setOpen}>
  <Dialog.Trigger asChild><button>Edit address</button></Dialog.Trigger>
  <Dialog.Content aria-describedby="addr-desc">
    <Dialog.Title>Edit delivery address</Dialog.Title>
    <Dialog.Description id="addr-desc">Changes apply to future orders only.</Dialog.Description>
    <AddressForm />
  </Dialog.Content>
</Dialog.Root>
```

**When to use which layer.**

- **Automated, in every PR:** lint rules, component tests on key states, E2E axe scans of the critical pages. Cheap, catches the objective errors.
- **Manual, every release:** the keyboard and screen-reader pass on the 5-10 critical journeys. Non-negotiable, and it is where the real bugs are.
- **User testing with disabled people:** at least annually, and for any redesign of a critical flow. Nothing else tells you whether the flow is *usable*.
- **Automated is not compliance.** A clean axe report is necessary and not sufficient; an accessibility *statement*, a documented audit, and remediation tracking are what auditors and the EAA require.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Automated tools have a false-positive rate** and a large false-negative rate. Expect ~5-15% false positives (mostly contrast on gradients and complex backgrounds) and ~60-80% false negatives. Budget for triage, and configure a documented allowlist with justifications (each with a re-review date).
- **Screen-reader testing has a learning curve** and depends on the tester. A team with one trained person is better than a team with none; two is better than one. Invest in an hour of training per engineer, and record the sessions.
- **Component-level a11y tests are cheap and high-yield** but can give false confidence: a component that passes axe in isolation may be labelled incorrectly in its real context. Keep a smaller set of E2E page scans for that reason.
- **Design-system work is a big upfront investment** and eliminates a large fraction of issues permanently. It is the highest-ROI accessibility decision a team makes.
- **WCAG 2.2's new criteria** (2.4.11 focus not obscured, 2.5.7 dragging movements, 2.5.8 target size, 3.2.6 consistent help, 3.3.7 redundant entry, 3.3.8 accessible authentication) catch issues that older tooling never flags, and they are AA, so they are in scope for the EAA.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Treating a clean axe report as "accessible".
2. Running axe only on the initial page state, so modals, menus, errors, and empty states are unscanned.
3. Placeholder text used as the only label, which disappears on input and is not reliably announced.
4. `outline: none` on focusable elements without a replacement focus style, making the site unusable by keyboard.
5. Custom controls (a `div` styled as a button) with no `role`, no `tabindex`, and no keyboard handler.
6. Modals that do not trap focus, do not restore it on close, or have no accessible name.
7. Colour-only status indicators (a red dot with no text or `aria-label`).
8. Images with `alt=""` on informative content, or `alt="photo of a chart"` on a decorative one.
9. Testing only with a mouse and only in Chrome.
10. An accessibility "fix" that adds `aria-label` to a `<div>` instead of using a semantic element, so nothing actually works.

**Common mistakes worth a second mention:** an accessibility statement that claims conformance with a level you have not audited; automated checks run only pre-release; no issue tracking or SLA for a11y bugs; and treating WCAG as a checklist rather than as an outcome measured with disabled users.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is automated accessibility testing good at, and what is it not?"* -- Good at objective, machine-checkable errors: missing labels, contrast, invalid ARIA, missing alt, heading order, duplicate ids. Not good at quality and meaning: whether alt text is useful, whether focus order is sensible, whether an error message tells a user how to fix it, whether the experience is usable. That is 60-80% of real issues, so automation is a necessary floor, not a strategy.
- 🟡 *"Where should axe run in your pipeline?"* -- Three places: ESLint for authoring-time errors, component tests on key states (initial, error, empty, modal open), and E2E on the critical assembled pages. Running it only in E2E is too late to be useful; running it only in component tests misses context.
- 🔴 *"A screen-reader user says your checkout is unusable. Walk me through the investigation."* -- Reproduce with a real screen reader first (NVDA or VoiceOver), on the exact journey they described. Then work the checklist: is the progress step announced; is the error summary a live region; does focus move to the first invalid field; is the card number field labelled and grouped; is the order total announced when it updates; can they get back to the previous step. Fix by fixing, add a component test asserting the accessible relationship (role, name, `aria-describedby`, focus target), and re-test with the user.
- 🔴 *"How do you make a11y a continuous practice rather than an audit?"* -- Design-system primitives that cannot be built inaccessible, axe in three test tiers, keyboard-only and screen-reader passes on critical journeys each release, a11y bugs in the normal tracker with an SLA, and user testing with disabled people on any redesign. The audit is the consequence of the practice, not the practice.
- ⚫ *"Name a WCAG 2.2 criterion most teams miss."* -- 2.5.8 Target Size (Minimum): interactive targets must be at least 24x24 CSS pixels or have equivalent spacing. Dense toolbars, icon buttons, and table row actions fail it constantly. Also common: 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured (Minimum) when a sticky header covers the focused element on scroll, and 3.3.8 Accessible Authentication (Minimum), which bans CAPTCHA puzzles and cognitive function tests in login.

---

### 9.4.3 Security testing: SAST, DAST, SCA, and secret scanning in the pipeline

**Definition.**

**Application security testing** has four complementary techniques, and each finds a class of defect the others cannot:

| Technique | What it does | Finds | When it runs | Tool examples |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **SAST** (Static Application Security Testing) | analyses source/bytecode | injection patterns, insecure crypto, missing authz checks, dangerous sinks, hardcoded secrets | every commit, no running app needed | Semgrep, CodeQL, SonarQube, Snyk Code, ESLint security rules |
| **DAST** (Dynamic) | attacks a running app over HTTP | runtime vulns, misconfiguration, authn/authz, injection through real paths, security headers | nightly, against staging | OWASP ZAP (baseline/full), Burp Suite Enterprise, Invicti |
| **SCA** (Software Composition Analysis) | analyses dependencies | known CVEs in packages, licence issues, abandoned packages | every commit (lockfile diff) | Dependabot, Renovate, Snyk, `pnpm audit`, OSV-Scanner |
| **Secret scanning** | scans content and history for credentials | committed keys, tokens, passwords, connection strings | pre-commit and every commit (including history) | Gitleaks, TruffleHog, GitHub secret scanning, `detect-secrets` |

Two more that belong in a mature pipeline: **container and IaC scanning** (Trivy, Grype for images; Checkov, tfsec, KICS for Terraform/K8s) and **API security testing** (schema-aware, which is a specialised DAST).

**Mechanics -- the pipeline, with real configuration.**

```yaml
# .github/workflows/security.yml
name: Security
on:
  pull_request:
  push: { branches: [main] }
  schedule: [{ cron: '17 3 * * *' }]        # nightly: the full set, including DAST

permissions:
  contents: read
  security-events: write                     # for SARIF upload / code scanning

jobs:
  # ── 1. SAST: fast, every PR, diff-aware.
  sast:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with: { fetch-depth: 0 }             # full history for some tools
      - run: pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
      - name: Semgrep (rules: p/default, p/security-audit, p/typescript, p/secrets)
        uses: returntocorp/semgrep-action@v1
        with:
          config: p/default
          generateSarif: true
        env: { SEMGREP_APP_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.SEMGREP_APP_TOKEN }} }
      - name: ESLint security rules (free, zero-config, catches the obvious)
        run: pnpm lint:security
      - name: CodeQL
        uses: github/codeql-action/init@v3
        with:
          languages: typescript,javascript
          queries: security-extended
      - uses: github/codeql-action/analyze@v3

  # ── 2. SCA: every commit, and a hard policy on the diff.
  sca:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - run: pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
      - name: OSV-Scanner (better advisory coverage than npm audit)
        run: |
          curl -sSfL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/google/osv-scanner/main/install.sh | sh
          osv-scanner scan source -r . --format json --output osv.json || true
          node scripts/osv-policy.mjs osv.json     # fail only on high/critical in *direct* deps
      - name: Licence check
        run: pnpm licenses list --json | npx license-checker --onlyAllow 'MIT;Apache-2.0;BSD-3-Clause;ISC;BSD-2-Clause' --production

  # ── 3. Secret scanning: pre-commit AND full history.
  secrets:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with: { fetch-depth: 0 }
      - name: Gitleaks (current tree + full history)
        run: |
          docker run -v "$PWD:/repo" zricethezav/gitleaks:latest detect \
            --source=/repo --report-format=sarif --report-path=gitleaks.sarif --redact
      - uses: github/codeql-action/upload-sarif@v3
        if: always()
        with: { sarif_file: gitleaks.sarif }

  # ── 4. Container + IaC scanning on every build.
  image-and-iac:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - run: docker build -t $IMAGE:$SHA .
      - run: trivy image --severity HIGH,CRITICAL --exit-code 1 --ignore-unfixed $IMAGE:$SHA
      - run: trivy fs --scanners misconfig,secret --severity HIGH,CRITICAL --exit-code 1 .

  # ── 5. DAST: nightly, against a staging deploy. Open-source and free.
  dast:
    if: github.event_name == 'schedule'
    needs: [deploy-staging]
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - name: Start ZAP baseline scan (passive: safe against a live app)
        run: |
          docker run --rm -v "$PWD:/zap/wrk" -t ghcr.io/zaproxy/zaproxy:stable \
            zap-baseline.py -t https://staging.example.test \
              -c zap-baseline.conf -r zap-report.html -J zap-report.json -I || true
      - name: Start ZAP full scan (active: attacks; run against an isolated environment only)
        run: |
          docker run --rm -v "$PWD:/zap/wrk" -t ghcr.io/zaproxy/zaproxy:stable \
            zap-full-scan.py -t https://staging.example.test -r zap-full.html || true
      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        with: { name: zap-report, path: zap-*.html }
```

```js
// eslint.config.js -- the free, instant, high-yield layer
import js from '@eslint/js'

export default [
  {
    rules: {
      'no-eval': 'error',
      'no-implied-eval': 'error',
      'no-new-func': 'error',
      'no-global-eval': 'error',
      'no-script-url': 'error',
    },
  },
  {
    // React-specific XSS footguns
    files: ['**/*.tsx'],
    rules: {
      'react/no-danger': 'error',
      'react/jsx-no-target-blank': 'error',
    },
  },
]
```

```yaml
# semgrep.yml -- project rules that encode *your* architecture's invariants
rules:
  - id: no-raw-sql-interpolation
    patterns:
      - pattern-either:
          - pattern: $DB.query(`...${...}...`)
          - pattern: $DB.raw(`...${...}...`)
    message: Raw SQL with interpolation. Use parameterised queries: $DB.query('... $1', [value]).
    severity: ERROR
    languages: [typescript, javascript]
  - id: no-authorization-without-middleware
    pattern: $APP.$METHOD($PATH, $HANDLER)
    pattern-not: $APP.$METHOD($PATH, auth, $HANDLER)
    message: A route is registered without the auth middleware. Add it or add an explicit exemption comment.
    severity: WARNING
    paths: { include: ['src/http/routes/**'] }
  - id: no-pii-in-logs
    pattern: console.$METHOD(..., $OBJ.email, ...)
    message: PII in a log statement. Use the structured logger with an allowlist of fields.
    severity: ERROR
    languages: [typescript, javascript]
```

**The policy layer, which is where tools become a program.** A scanner that reports 200 findings nobody fixes is worse than no scanner, because it trains people to ignore the tool.

```js
// scripts/security-policy.mjs
import { readFileSync } from 'node:fs'

const SARIF = JSON.parse(readFileSync(process.argv[2], 'utf8'))
const findings = SARIF.runs.flatMap((r) =>
  r.results.map((res) => ({
    rule: res.ruleId,
    severity: res.level,
    file: res.locations?.[0]?.physicalLocation?.artifactLocation?.uri,
    line: res.locations?.[0]?.physicalLocation?.region?.startLine,
  })),
)

// 1. Fail on anything CRITICAL/HIGH unless it is baselined.
const baseline = new Set(JSON.parse(readFileSync('.security-baseline.json', 'utf8')).accepted)  // [{rule, file, line?}]
const NEW = findings.filter(
  (f) => ['critical', 'error', 'high'].includes(f.severity) &&
         !baseline.some((b) => b.rule === f.rule && b.file === f.file),
)

console.log(`scanned: ${findings.length} findings; new high/critical: ${NEW.length}; baselined: ${baseline.size}`)
if (process.argv.includes('--max-new') && NEW.length > Number(process.env.MAX_NEW_FINDINGS ?? 0)) {
  for (const f of NEW) console.error(`  ${f.severity} ${f.rule} ${f.file}:${f.line}`)
  process.exit(1)
}

// 2. Fail if the baseline has shrunk: a fixed finding must have its entry removed.
if (process.argv.includes('--ratchet')) {
  const stillPresent = new Set(findings.map((f) => `${f.rule}|${f.file}`))
  const stale = [...baseline].filter((b) => !stillPresent.has(`${b.rule}|${b.file}`))
  if (stale.length > 0) {
    console.error(`::error::${stale.length} baseline entries no longer match a finding. Remove them from .security-baseline.json.`)
    process.exit(1)
  }
}
```

**Pre-commit secret scanning, so secrets never reach the repository.**

```yaml
# .pre-commit-config.yaml
repos:
  - repo: https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks
    rev: v8.18.4
    hooks:
      - id: gitleaks
  - repo: https://github.com/pre-commit/pre-commit-hooks
    rev: v4.6.0
    hooks:
      - id: detect-private-key
      - id: check-added-large-files
        args: ['--maxkb=1024']
      - id: detect-aws-credentials
      - id: forbid-submodules
```

**When to use which.**

- **SAST:** always, on every commit, diff-aware so it is fast. Use a tuned ruleset (your framework's rules plus `p/secrets` and `p/security-audit`), not the entire Semgrep registry.
- **SCA:** always, on the lockfile, with a policy that distinguishes direct from transitive and high from critical, and an SLA (e.g. critical within 48h).
- **Secret scanning:** always, pre-commit and full history, plus platform-level (GitHub secret scanning / push protection) so leaks are blocked at the source.
- **DAST:** nightly against staging. The **passive/baseline** scan is safe against a live environment and should also run after each deploy as a smoke check. The **active/full** scan only against an isolated, disposable environment.
- **Container/IaC scanning:** on every image build and every IaC change.

**Trade-offs.**

- **False positives are the primary failure mode** of every one of these tools. Without a baselined, ratcheting policy, the tools get muted. With one, they are effective. Budget for the policy work.
- **SAST cannot see authorisation logic.** Its highest-value custom rules are the ones *you* write: "every route has the auth middleware", "no raw SQL with interpolation", "no PII in logs", "no `process.env` in client bundles". A generic ruleset will never find your business-logic authz holes; only review and the tests in 9.2.8 will.
- **DAST finds runtime issues SAST cannot (real injection through a real path, header misconfiguration, CORS, cookie flags) but has low coverage of authenticated and multi-step flows.** Authenticated DAST needs a recorded authentication script, and that is fragile. DAST's best use is headers, cookies, TLS, CORS, and information disclosure.
- **SCA alert volume is enormous** and mostly transitive. A policy of "critical/high in direct dependencies blocks; transitive is tracked weekly" is the only version of this that gets acted on.
- **Security gates slow merges.** A gate that blocks on a new HIGH finding will frustrate people if the false-positive rate is above ~20%. Tune the ruleset until it is below that, then gate.
- **Compliance is not security.** Getting a report does not make you secure; the findings' remediation state is what matters.

**Production considerations.**

- **Enable GitHub Advanced Security / code scanning** so findings appear on the PR as annotations, right next to the code. A SARIF file in an artefact that nobody opens is a waste.
- **A security fix is a normal, fast PR.** Have a documented fast-track: a critical finding gets a same-day hotfix, not a sprint item. Pre-authorise the bypass so nobody is tempted to disable the scanner.
- **Rotate any secret that ever reached the repository.** Gitleaks finding a key is the start of an incident response, not the end of a ticket: revoke, rotate, check the git history and any forks, and audit the provider's access logs.
- **Test your security controls.** Have a test (integration tier) asserting that the authz matrix holds, that security headers are present, that cookies are flagged correctly, and that a user cannot read another user's data. A security control with no test regresses silently.
- **Run the dependency update bot on a weekly schedule with grouped, auto-merged patch PRs** (tests are the gate), and hold major updates for a human.
- Re-run the full DAST set monthly and after any significant architecture change, and re-baseline.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Enabling a scanner with no policy, so the first week of noise gets it muted forever.
2. Only running SCA, and assuming it covers your own code (it does not).
3. Secret scanning only on new commits, so a key committed a year ago is never found.
4. A `jest.mock`-heavy suite with no authz tests, so SAST's blind spot (business authorisation) is also your blind spot.
5. Running the active ZAP scan against a shared environment and taking it down.
6. Ignoring transitive CVEs entirely, and then having a runtime incident from a package three levels down.
7. Storing the security policy file outside the repository, so it is not reviewed and not versioned.
8. Treating a high score from a scanner as a security posture. It is a coverage indicator, nothing more.
9. No test for the security headers, so a proxy config change silently removes `Strict-Transport-Security`.
10. Assuming WAF rules and SAST substitute for a threat model. Neither of them is one.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What are the four types of application security testing, and what does each find?"* -- SAST analyses source for injection, insecure crypto, and dangerous patterns; DAST attacks a running app and finds runtime and configuration issues like headers, cookies, CORS, and real injection paths; SCA finds known CVEs in dependencies; secret scanning finds committed credentials. Each finds a class the others miss.
- 🟡 *"Why is secret scanning not enough for security?"* -- It only finds secrets that are *in the repository*. It says nothing about the ones in a CI variable, a Slack message, a support ticket, a local `.env` that was never committed, or an IAM role that is over-permissioned. And a found secret is only the start: you must revoke and rotate, and audit the provider's logs.
- 🔴 *"How do you stop 400 SCA findings from making the tool useless?"* -- Policy, not suppression. Triage once into a baselined `.security-baseline.json` with justifications; gate only on new HIGH/CRITICAL in direct dependencies; set an SLA for transitive fixes; auto-merge patch-level bumps that pass tests; and ratchet so the baseline can only shrink. Track the count weekly; a rising count is a signal to act.
- 🔴 *"What can SAST not find, and what do you do about it?"* -- Authorisation and business logic: missing permission checks, IDOR, tenant isolation, state-machine abuse, race conditions. SAFT is pattern-matching, so it cannot know that `findById` should also filter by `tenantId`. Cover that with the generated role x action matrix and object-level tests (9.2.8), a threat model, and code review with a security checklist.
- ⚫ *"Explain a real secret-leak response."* -- Gitleaks flags a key. Then: (1) treat the credential as compromised; (2) revoke/rotate it at the provider immediately; (3) check whether it was ever in a pushed branch, a fork, an artefact, or a log; (4) audit the provider's access logs for use; (5) add a pre-commit hook and platform push protection so it cannot recur; (6) document it in the incident record. The scan is the detection, not the response.

---

### 9.4.5 Static analysis: ESLint, TypeScript strictness, and lint rules that matter

**Definition.**

**Static analysis** inspects code without executing it. Three layers, in increasing power and decreasing adoption:

1. **Linting** (ESLint + plugins): style, correctness patterns, React hooks rules, import hygiene, and framework-specific correctness (a11y, security, promise misuse, testing-library rules).
2. **Type checking** (TypeScript): the single highest-value static analysis you can adopt, because it verifies contracts across module boundaries that no test asserts.
3. **Program analysis** (TypeScript compiler API rules, ts-morph, dependency-cruiser, Madge, boundaries, Nx module boundaries): architectural constraints -- which modules may import which, no circular dependencies, no test-only code in production bundles.

**Mechanics -- the ESLint configuration that is worth having in 2026.** ESLint 9's flat config, with the plugins that actually catch bugs rather than formatting.

```js
// eslint.config.js
import js from '@eslint/js'
import tseslint from 'typescript-eslint'
import react from 'eslint-plugin-react'
import reactHooks from 'eslint-plugin-react-hooks'
import jsxA11y from 'eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y'
import importPlugin from 'eslint-plugin-import'
import promise from 'eslint-plugin-promise'
import security from 'eslint-plugin-security'
import vitest from '@vitest/eslint-plugin'
import node from 'eslint-plugin-n'

export default tseslint.config(
  { ignores: ['dist/**', 'coverage/**', 'node_modules/**', '**/*.snap'] },

  js.configs.recommended,
  ...tseslint.configs.strictTypeChecked,        // type-aware rules: the valuable ones
  ...tseslint.configs.stylisticTypeChecked,

  {
    files: ['**/*.{ts,tsx}'],
    languageOptions: {
      parserOptions: {
        projectService: true,                 // type-aware linting with correct per-file configs
        tsconfigRootDir: import.meta.dirname,
      },
    },
    plugins: {
      react, 'react-hooks': reactHooks, 'jsx-a11y': jsxA11y,
      import: importPlugin, promise, security, vitest, node,
    },
    settings: { react: { version: 'detect' } },
    rules: {
      // ── TypeScript: the ones that catch real bugs
      '@typescript-eslint/no-floating-promises': 'error',        // 🔴 the highest-value rule in the set
      '@typescript-eslint/no-misused-promises': 'error',
      '@typescript-eslint/await-thenable': 'error',
      '@typescript-eslint/require-await': 'error',
      '@typescript-eslint/restrict-template-expressions': ['error', { allowNumber: true }],
      '@typescript-eslint/no-unnecessary-condition': 'warn',     // finds impossible branches -> dead code
      '@typescript-eslint/switch-exhaustiveness-check': 'error',
      '@typescript-eslint/no-non-null-assertion': 'warn',
      '@typescript-eslint/consistent-type-imports': 'error',
      '@typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any': 'warn',
      'no-console': ['warn', { allow: ['warn', 'error'] }],     // route logging through your logger
      'eqeqeq': ['error', 'always', { null: 'ignore' }],
      'no-console': 'off',

      // ── React hooks: the official rules plus the compiler-derived ones
      'react-hooks/rules-of-hooks': 'error',
      'react-hooks/exhaustive-deps': 'warn',
      'react-hooks/immutability': 'error',                      // React Compiler rules (v6+)
      'react-hooks/purity': 'error',
      'react-hooks/refs': 'error',
      'react-hooks/set-state-in-effect': 'error',               // 🔴 catches the effect anti-pattern
      'react-hooks/set-state-in-render': 'error',

      // ── Testing: enforce the philosophy from 9.1.11 and 9.1.8
      'vitest/expect-expect': ['error', { assertFunctionNames: ['expect', 'screen.*', 'user.*'] }],
      'vitest/no-conditional-in-test': 'error',
      'vitest/no-standalone-expect': 'error',
      'vitest/prefer-lowercase-title': 'error',
      'vitest/require-top-level-describe': 'error',
      'vitest/no-focused-tests': 'error',
      'testing-library/no-testid-queries': 'warn',
      'testing-library/no-container': 'warn',
      'testing-library/no-node-access': 'warn',
      'testing-library/prefer-user-event': 'error',
      'testing-library/prefer-screen-queries': 'error',
      'testing-library/no-await-sync-events': 'error',
      'no-restricted-syntax': [
        'error',
        // Ban .only from ever merging.
        { selector: "CallExpression[callee.property.name='only']", message: 'No focused tests in committed code.' },
        { selector: "CallExpression[callee.property.name='skip']", message: 'No skipped tests in committed code. Track it in an issue.' },
        // Ban mocking your own domain.
        { selector: "CallExpression[callee.name='vi'][callee.property.name='mock']", message: 'Do not mock domain code. Extract a pure function.' },
      ],
    },
  },

  // Test files get test-specific rules and relaxations.
  {
    files: ['**/*.test.{ts,tsx}', '**/*.spec.{ts,tsx}', 'e2e/**/*.ts'],
    rules: {
      '@typescript-eslint/no-non-null-assertion': 'off',
      '@typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any': 'off',
      'vitest/no-conditional-in-test': 'off',
    },
  },
)
```

**TypeScript strictness -- the configuration and the reasoning for each flag.**

```jsonc
// tsconfig.json
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    // ── The strict family. All of it, on day one.
    "strict": true,                 // implies noImplicitAny, strictNullChecks, strictFunctionTypes,
                                    // strictBindCallApply, strictPropertyInitialization, noImplicitThis,
                                    // alwaysStrict, useUnknownInCatchVariables
    "noUncheckedIndexedAccess": true,   // 🔴 arr[i] is T | undefined. THE flag that finds real bugs.
    "exactOptionalPropertyTypes": true, // 🔴 { x?: number } does not accept { x: undefined }
    "noImplicitOverride": true,
    "noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature": true,
    "noImplicitReturns": true,          // a function must return on every path
    "noFallthroughCasesInSwitch": true,
    "noUnusedLocals": true,
    "noUnusedParameters": true,
    "allowUnreachableCode": false,
    "allowUnusedLabels": false,

    // ── Correctness
    "useUnknownInCatchVariables": true, // catch (e) is unknown -> you must narrow it
    "forceConsistentCasingInFileNames": true,
    "isolatedModules": true,
    "verbatimModuleSyntax": true,       // type-only imports must say `import type`
    "module": "NodeNext", "moduleResolution": "NodeNext",
    "target": "ES2023", "lib": ["ES2023", "DOM", "DOM.Iterable"],
    "resolveJsonModule": true,
    "skipLibCheck": true,               // the pragmatic exception: do not type-check node_modules

    // ── Emit (for the build tool, which should be esbuild/swc, not tsc)
    "noEmit": true,
    "incremental": true,
    "paths": { "@/*": ["./src/*"] }
  },
  "include": ["src", "test", "e2e", "scripts"],
  "exclude": ["dist", "node_modules", "coverage"]
}
```

**What each high-value flag catches, with examples.**

```ts
// noUncheckedIndexedAccess: the classic off-by-one that ships.
function firstChar(s: string): string {
  return s[0]            // 🔴 error: string | undefined
}
function firstChar(s: string): string {
  return s.charAt(0)     // ✅
}

// exactOptionalPropertyTypes: prevents a subtle spread bug.
interface Config { retries?: number }
const partial: Config = { retries: undefined }         // 🔴 error
const built: Config = { ...defaults, ...partial }      // with the flag off, `retries: undefined` overwrites the default
// The bug: a merge helper treats "key present with undefined" as "not present" and keeps 3; without the
// flag, tsc says nothing and you have a config that ignores your override.

// noImplicitOverride + noImplicitReturns: catches forgotten `return` in a switch.
function classify(n: number): 'low' | 'high' {
  switch (n) {
    case 0: return 'low'
    case 1: return 'high'
    // 🔴 falls through silently to undefined
  }
}

// useUnknownInCatchVariables: forces error handling instead of a guessed type.
try { await charge() } catch (e) {
  // e: unknown  -> e.message is a type error
  if (e instanceof PaymentDeclined) { /* ... */ }
  throw new Error('payment failed', { cause: e })
}
```

**Type-level testing -- a genuinely useful, underused technique.**

```ts
// src/auth/permissions.test-d.ts
import { describe, it, expectTypeOf } from 'vitest'
import { can } from './permissions'

describe('can() typing', () => {
  it('narrows the resource from the action', () => {
    expectTypeOf(can('order', 'read')).toEqualTypeOf<boolean>()
  })

  it('rejects an unknown action at compile time', () => {
    // @ts-expect-error -- this line MUST be a type error; if it compiles, the type is too loose
    can('order', 'destroy')
  })
})

// src/http/serializer.test-d.ts -- assert the wire format is exactly what you think it is
import { expectTypeOf } from 'vitest'
type OrderResponse = ReturnType<typeof serializeOrder>
expectTypeOf<OrderResponse>().toEqualTypeOf<{
  id: string; status: 'draft' | 'placed' | 'shipped' | 'cancelled';
  totalCents: number; currency: 'GBP' | 'USD' | 'EUR'; lines: readonly { sku: string; qty: number }[]
}>()
```

```bash
# Run type tests alongside unit tests.
vitest run --typecheck --typecheck.only
```

**Architectural static analysis -- the layer most teams skip, and the one that prevents rot.**

```js
// dependency-cruiser.config.mjs -- enforce the layering in the linter.
export default {
  forbidden: [
    // Domain must not depend on anything outside itself.
    {
      name: 'no-domain-dependencies',
      from: { path: '^src/domain/' },
      to: { path: '^(src/(application|infrastructure|http|ui)/|node_modules)' },
    },
    // Application may depend on domain, not on infrastructure.
    {
      name: 'no-application-to-infrastructure',
      from: { path: '^src/application/' },
      to: { path: '^src/infrastructure/' },
    },
    // Test-support must never be imported by production code.
    {
      name: 'no-test-support-in-prod',
      from: { path: '^src/' },
      to: { path: '^(test|test-support|e2e)/' },
    },
    // No cycles, anywhere.
    { name: 'no-circular', severity: 'error', from: {}, to: { circular: true } },
  ],
  options: {
    doNotFollow: { path: 'node_modules' },
    exclude: { path: '(^|/)node_modules/|^dist/|^coverage/' },
    tsPreCompilationDeps: true,
    enhancedResolveOptions: { exportsFields: ['exports'], conditionNames: ['import', 'types', 'default'] },
  },
}
```

```jsonc
// package.json — wire it in as a gate, in the same job as lint.
{
  "scripts": {
    "lint": "eslint . && depcruise src --config dependency-cruiser.config.mjs",
    "lint:fix": "eslint . --fix",
    "typecheck": "tsc --noEmit",
    "check": "pnpm lint && pnpm typecheck && pnpm test:unit"
  }
}
```

**When to use static analysis vs not.**

Always: lint, `tsc --noEmit`, and the dependency rules. They are the cheapest quality gates available (seconds, no runtime) and they find entire classes of bug (promises, nullability, hooks rules, import cycles) that tests find late and expensively.

Be pragmatic about: `no-explicit-any` (warn, not error, on a codebase with existing debt), `no-non-null-assertion` (warn), and formatting (use Prettier or Biome, not ESLint).

**Trade-offs.**

- **Type-aware linting is slow.** `projectService: true` makes lint 3-10x slower than the syntactic-only version. On a large codebase, budget 20-40s, and consider splitting: fast syntactic rules on save, type-aware rules in CI.
- **`@typescript-eslint` type-aware rules produce false positives on generic-heavy code** (`no-unnecessary-condition` is the worst offender; it will tell you a condition is impossible when a generic has not been instantiated). Use it as a `warn` and a gradual ratchet.
- **`strict` mode is a migration, not a switch.** Turning it on in a large codebase produces thousands of errors. The order that works: `noImplicitAny` + `strictNullChecks` first, then `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, then `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`, each as a separate PR with the error count ratcheting down.
- **`skipLibCheck: true` is a pragmatic compromise.** It skips type-checking `node_modules` .d.ts files, which is where the ecosystem's type errors live. Full checking is possible and will surface upstream bugs; most teams turn it off.
- **Lint rules are only as good as their adoption.** 400 rules that are all `warn` is 400 pieces of noise. Curate 20-30 rules that catch bugs, make them `error`, and turn everything else off.
- **Dependency rules prevent the rot that makes refactoring expensive** (a domain that imports a framework, a cycle between two modules that must be changed together). It is a small investment that pays off at the first big refactor.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Copying a 500-rule shared config and muting everything, so the linter has no signal.
2. Formatting rules in ESLint alongside a formatter, causing conflicts and slow runs.
3. `tsc` inside the Jest/Vitest transform instead of as a separate `typecheck` script, so type errors show up as test failures and every run is 5x slower.
4. `strict: false` in `tsconfig.json` with `"strict": true` accidentally omitted from a nested `tsconfig` in a sub-package.
5. `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` off, so `arr[0].id` is a potential production `TypeError`.
6. `any` used to silence an error, spreading through the codebase until the types are meaningless.
7. Not adding `@typescript-eslint/no-floating-promises`, which is the single rule that catches the most real bugs in an async codebase.
8. Allowing `eslint-disable` without a comment and an issue link, so suppressions accumulate invisibly.
9. No dependency-boundary rules, so the layering exists only in a diagram nobody enforces.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"Which TypeScript flags catch the most real bugs?"* -- `strictNullChecks` (the whole family), `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` (index access returns `T | undefined`), `exactOptionalPropertyTypes` (a spread cannot smuggle `undefined` over a default), `noImplicitReturns`, and `noImplicitOverride`. And outside tsc, `@typescript-eslint/no-floating-promises`.
- 🟡 *"How do you adopt `strict` on an existing codebase?"* -- Incrementally and ratcheted: `noImplicitAny` and `strictNullChecks` first (the bulk of the work), fix in themed PRs, then `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, then `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`. Add a CI check that the error count may not increase, so it never regresses. Do not do it in one PR.
- 🔴 *"What is `@typescript-eslint/no-floating-promises` and why does it matter?"* -- It errors when you call a promise-returning function and ignore the result. In an async codebase, that is how unhandled rejections, silently swallowed errors, and lost `await`s happen. It is type-aware, so it catches a bare `saveUser(x)` that a human reads as fine. It pairs with a setup-file trap on unhandled rejections (9.1.10).
- 🔴 *"ESLint takes 40 seconds. Is that acceptable?"* -- Not on every save. Split: fast syntactic rules with type-aware rules off run in ~2s in-editor, and the full type-aware set runs in CI and in a pre-commit hook. Or use the ESLint language server, which type-checks incrementally and feels instant. The rule set's value justifies 40s in CI; it does not justify 40s in the inner loop.
- ⚫ *"How do you stop lint suppressions accumulating?"* -- Require a comment and an issue link on every `eslint-disable`, run a monthly report of suppressions by rule, and ratchet: the count may only decrease. Better still, fix the false positives by configuring the rule (a typed union instead of a cast) rather than suppressing. Any suppression older than a quarter is a bug.

---

### 9.4.6 Code review practices as a quality mechanism

**Definition.**

**Code review** is the human (and increasingly machine) quality mechanism that catches semantic, architectural, security, and *requirement* defects that no automated tool can. It is also the most expensive quality mechanism in latency terms, and the one most often degraded into a rubber stamp. Its effectiveness depends entirely on the review being **specific, timely, evidence-seeking, and separated from authorship pressure**.

**Mechanics -- the review dimensions, and what each catches that automation cannot.**

| Dimension | The question | Catches | Tool cannot |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Correctness** | Does this do what the description says, for the edge cases? | logic errors, wrong boundary conditions, incorrect state transitions | see 9.2.10 |
| **Design** | Is this in the right place, at the right altitude? | wrong abstraction, leaky seams, duplication, over-engineering | see 9.4.5 |
| **Test adequacy** | Would these tests fail if the code were wrong? | weak assertions, missing negative paths, implementation-detail tests | see 9.1.8, 9.4.4 |
| **Security & privacy** | Could this leak, escalate, or be abused? | IDOR, missing authz, PII in logs, injection | see 9.4.3 |
| **Operability** | How does this fail at 3am? | missing logs, no metrics, no rollback, no feature flag | partly |
| **Compatibility** | Does this break a consumer? | API contract changes, migration without a path | see 9.2.7 |
| **Understandability** | Will the next person understand this in six months? | naming, dead code, unexplained cleverness | yes |

**Mechanics -- the practical protocol, and the anti-patterns.**

1. **Review the description and the tests first, then the diff.** If the tests do not demonstrate the requirement, the implementation is not reviewable yet. Most "bad reviews" are reviews of unclear intent.
2. **Under 400 lines.** Above that, the review is a skim. Split the PR. This is the single most effective rule in code review, and the one teams resist most.
3. **One concern per PR.** Refactor and behaviour change in the same commit is unreviewable: the reviewer cannot tell which lines are meant to change behaviour, so they either approve both or neither.
4. **Review within hours, not days.** Stale context makes a review a re-read. Time-box: first response within 4 working hours.
5. **Distinguish the three comment classes explicitly**, because conflating them is why reviews feel hostile:
   - **blocking** -- must change before merge (a bug, a security hole, a missing test for the requirement)
   - **suggestion** -- I would change this (style, naming, structure)
   - **question** -- I do not understand this (and that is a signal about the code, not the reviewer)
6. **Say why, not just what.** "`useUserQuery` instead of `useState` + `useEffect` here, because we already retry and cache on the server and duplicating that will drift" is actionable. "Use a hook" is not.
7. **Approve explicitly, including the things you checked.** "Approved: I've checked the authz path, the migration is forward-only, and the test would fail without the fix." This is what makes approval meaningful.
8. **The author responds to every blocking comment**, even if the response is "I disagree, because X" -- and then the reviewer decides. Unanswered threads are the number-one cause of stale PRs.
9. **Never rubber-stamp.** If you are reviewing 15 PRs a day and none of them made you think, something is wrong with the process, not the PRs.

```ts
// The reviewer-provable pattern: a PR whose tests are the specification.
test('a member with an expired card cannot complete checkout and is told to update the card', async ({ page, api }) => {
  // Arrange
  await api.seedMemberWithCard({ status: 'expired' })
  await page.goto('/checkout')

  // Act
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: /place order/i }).click()

  // Assert -- user-visible, and specific enough that a wrong implementation fails
  await expect(page.getByRole('alert')).toHaveText(/your card expired on 1 february 2026/i)
  await expect(page.getByRole('button', { name: /update card/i })).toBeVisible()
  expect(await api.countOrders()).toBe(0)                      // and no side effect
})

// The reviewer can verify three things from this alone:
//   1. The requirement (what the user sees when the card is expired).
//   2. The negative path (no order created).
//   3. The date is formatted for a human (a regression that unit tests would not catch).
```

**Mechanics -- automation as a first reviewer.** The most effective use of automation in review is to move the mechanical objections out of human hands.

```yaml
# .github/workflows/pr-checks.yml — what must be green before a human reviews.
jobs:
  checks:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
        with: { fetch-depth: 0 }
      - run: pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
      - run: pnpm lint
      - run: pnpm typecheck
      - run: pnpm test:unit -- --coverage
      - run: pnpm test:integration
        env: { TEST_DATABASE_URL: ${{ secrets.TEST_DATABASE_URL }} }
      - run: pnpm exec diff-cover coverage/lcov.info --compare-branch=origin/main --fail-under=90
      - run: pnpm exec depcruise src --config dependency-cruiser.config.mjs
      - run: pnpm exec osv-scanner scan source -r . --format table
      - name: PR description checks
        run: node scripts/pr-description-check.mjs
      - uses: actions/download-artifact@v4       # CodeQL + Semgrep SARIF
        if: failure()
        continue-on-error: true
```

```js
// scripts/pr-description-check.mjs -- a 40-line linter for the PR description.
// The highest-leverage review automation there is, because most unreviewable PRs
// are unreviewable because of the description, not the code.
const body = process.env.PR_BODY ?? ''
const problems = []

if (!/\b(why|problem|because|so that)\b/i.test(body)) problems.push('Explain WHY, not only what changed.')
if (!/(test|spec|manual|verified)/i.test(body)) problems.push('State how this was verified (tests, manual steps).')
if (/\b(should|maybe|probably|temporarily|for now)\b/i.test(body)) problems.push('Hedging found ("should", "temporarily"). Be definite or open a follow-up issue.')
if (/\+.*(console\.log|TODO|FIXME|\.only\()/.test(body)) problems.push('Debug code, TODO or focused test in the description snippets.')
if (body.length > 8000) problems.push('Description is very long: consider moving detail into a linked doc or the commit messages.')
if (problems.length) {
  console.error('PR description needs work:\n' + problems.map((p) => `  - ${p}`).join('\n'))
  process.exit(1)
}
```

**Mechanics -- review metrics worth tracking, and the ones that are actively harmful.**

| Metric | What it tells you | Healthy | Do not use |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PR size (files, lines) | reviewability | < 400 lines | as a target to game |
| Time to first review | responsiveness | < 4h | as a target that encourages rubber-stamping |
| Review rounds | how clear the PR was | 1-2 | as a personal metric (it incentivises trivial PRs) |
| Comment classification mix | whether reviews are substantive | mostly blocking + questions, few style nits | counting comments per reviewer |
| Defects found per review | the only real measure of review value | trending up as the team improves | per-reviewer league tables (gaming) |
| **Escape rate by tier** | whether your test tiers work | falling | -- |

The metric that actually matters is **defect escape by tier**: bugs that reached production and which tier *should* have caught them. If most escapes are E2E-tier bugs, your E2E suite is missing journeys; if most are unit-tier, your unit tests assert implementation or your coverage gates are theatre; if most are "nobody's job" (a config, a migration, a dependency), add a tier or a check.

**Mechanics -- the review techniques that raise the hit rate.**

- **Ask for the invariant, not the diff.** "What must be true for this to be correct?" gets a real answer; "Is this correct?" gets "LGTM".
- **Trace one value end to end.** Pick one entity and follow it from input to persistence to response to UI. Most serious review findings are found this way in ten minutes.
- **Read the test first and ask "would this fail if I broke the code?"** If you can break the code mentally and the test still passes, you have found the most valuable comment you can make.
- **Check the negatives.** Every PR should have at least one test that asserts something did *not* happen. If there is none, ask for one.
- **Check the boundaries.** Time, timezone, currency rounding, null vs undefined vs empty, the last element of an array, the first page of a paginated query, the maximum input length, the concurrent request.
- **Check the migration and the rollback.** For a schema change: is it forward-only? Is it reversible? Does the old code still work during the deploy window? Is it safe on a large table (lock duration)?
- **Check the observability.** What will you see in the dashboard when this breaks? What is the log line, the metric, the alert? What is the kill switch?
- **Security review for anything touching auth, input handling, or PII.** A 10-item checklist, applied every time: input validated at the boundary; output encoded for its context; authorisation checked server-side per object; no secrets in code; no PII in logs; no user-controlled redirects; no `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` on untrusted content; rate limits on the expensive endpoint; dependency risk reviewed; errors do not leak internals.

**When review is not the right mechanism.**

- **Style and formatting:** a formatter.
- **Types and nullability:** TypeScript.
- **Mechanical correctness:** tests.
- **Architectural drift:** dependency rules and a codeowners/ADR process.
- **Large, risky, hard-to-review changes (a framework migration, an auth rewrite):** split into a sequence of reviewable PRs each of which is green, or do a **pair-programming/design review first** and then a fast mechanical review of the implementation. A 6000-line PR cannot be reviewed by reading; it can only be reviewed by construction.

**Trade-offs.**

- **Review is the highest-latency gate in the pipeline** and the one most easily bypassed under deadline pressure. The mitigation is a fast first response and a hard rule that no one merges their own PR.
- **Reviewer capacity is the constraint.** In a team of 5, each person is reviewing 4 PRs a day; that is the real limit on quality, and it is an argument for fewer, smaller PRs and more automation, not for faster reviewing.
- **Rubber-stamping is worse than no review**, because it produces a false signal of quality. If you are approving without reading, the honest move is to say so and let the author find a real reviewer.
- **Author-blaming review culture destroys quality.** Reviews that attack the person rather than the code get the code fixed and the next author hides their uncertainty. Explicitly review the artefact, and reward the PR that surfaces a design problem.
- **Domain experts matter.** A security-sensitive or payments change reviewed by someone without the domain context will be approved with "LGTM" and no value. Route reviews to people who can actually evaluate the change.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Reviewing 1500-line PRs, which is a formality.
2. Mixing a refactor with a behaviour change, so the reviewer cannot see the behaviour change.
3. Style comments crowding out substance, until the reviewer stops reading.
4. Approving without a comment, so approval carries no information.
5. Blocking on preferences (naming, structure) that are not documented standards.
6. Writing the test *after* the review, so the reviewer reviewed untested code.
7. No one merges their own PR, but everyone "reviews" friends' quickly.
8. Resolving a thread without the author agreeing, which leaves the concern unaddressed in the code.
9. Reviewing the diff without reading the description, so the reviewer cannot judge the requirement.
10. Treating review as a gate to be passed rather than as the mechanism that transfers knowledge and catches design errors.

**Interviewer probes.**

- 🟢 *"What is code review for, given we have automated tests and CI?"* -- Catching the things that are not mechanical: whether the change solves the right problem, whether the design is right, whether the tests would actually fail if the code were wrong, whether the security and data-handling consequences are acceptable, and whether the next engineer can understand it. It is also the primary mechanism for knowledge transfer and for keeping architectural decisions honest.
- 🟡 *"What is your rule for PR size, and why?"* -- Under 400 changed lines and one concern. Above that, reviewers skim, and a skimmed review is worse than no review because it produces false confidence. Refactors and behaviour changes are separate PRs, always, so a reviewer can see exactly which lines change behaviour.
- 🔴 *"How do you review a large refactor?"* -- You do not review it as a diff. You require it to be structured as a sequence of behaviour-preserving PRs, each green, with the architecture and the plan agreed in a design doc or a pairing session beforehand. The code review then verifies the *steps*. If someone insists on one big PR, ask for the plan, review the plan, and then require the PR to be split before it merges.
