```tsx
// ✅ Fix 1: render one DOM, let CSS decide the layout.
<header className="app-header app-header--compact">…</header>
// with CSS:
//   .app-header { … }
//   @media (max-width: 40rem) { .app-header--compact { … } }
// One DOM node, two appearances, one accessible name, no duplicate for assistive tech either.
```

```tsx
// ✅ Fix 2: when duplication is genuinely correct (e.g. a desktop and a mobile variant of
//    a *picker*), scope the query and expose the variant to assistive technology correctly.
{isMobile ? <MobilePicker /> : <DesktopPicker />}
// then in tests: within(screen.getByRole('region', { name: /date picker/i })).getByRole('button', { name: 'Save' })
```

```ts
// ✅ Fix 3: when the element is visually hidden but present, the honest query is the one
//    that matches the user's experience. jsdom will not help you here, so assert explicitly.
it('renders the mobile header only below 40rem', async () => {
  const { rerender } = render(<AppHeader />, { wrapper: withViewport(375) })
  expect(screen.getByRole('banner')).toHaveClass('app-header--compact')
  rerender(<AppHeader />)
  expect(screen.getByRole('banner')).not.toHaveClass('app-header--compact')
})
```

And a lint rule to keep it from recurring:
```ts
// eslint.config.js
{ rules: {
  // A component that renders two of the same interactive role in one tree is almost
  // always a responsive-duplication bug.
  'no-restricted-syntax': ['error', {
    selector: "JSXElement[name.name='div'][attributes.0.name.name='className'] > JSXElement[openingElement.name.name='header']",
    message: 'Render one <header>; use CSS for responsive variation.',
  }],
}}
```

**Guardrail.**
1. In the shared `renderWithProviders`, accept a `viewport` option and set the corresponding class, so viewport-dependent tests are explicit.
2. Prefer `getByRole(..., { name })` with a *specific* name, or scope with `within(region)`, over a global query.
3. Add a component test that asserts the count of landmarks and of each primary action, so a duplicate header is caught by a test whose purpose is structural:
```ts
it('renders exactly one banner and one main region', () => {
  render(<App />)
  expect(screen.getAllByRole('banner')).toHaveLength(1)
  expect(screen.getAllByRole('main')).toHaveLength(1)
  expect(screen.getAllByRole('navigation')).toHaveLength(1)
})
```
4. In E2E, use `getByRole` there too, where CSS *is* applied -- so a Playwright test that passes and a jsdom test that fails is a signal about the duplication, not about the framework.

---

## SCENARIO 5: The snapshot test that fails every time because of a timestamp

**Symptom.** A newly added snapshot test fails on the second run in the same day with a diff containing only `"createdAt": "2026-03-01T14:23:11.482Z"` instead of `"2026-03-01T14:23:09.117Z"`. It passed when committed.

**Reproduction.**
```bash
npx vitest run src/api/serializer.test.ts --update     # green
sleep 3 && npx vitest run src/api/serializer.test.ts    # red
```

**Diagnosis.** The serialised object contains a value the code computed from the real clock. A first look at the diff shows whether it is a timestamp, an id, a hash, a `Set` ordering, or an array whose order depends on a `Promise.all`. In this case, `createdAt` came from `new Date()` in a mapper that should have received a `Clock`.

**Root cause.** The production mapper read the real clock, so the test was inherently nondeterministic. The first run passed because the baseline was *generated* from that run (`updateSnapshot: 'new'`), which is exactly how nondeterministic snapshots enter a repository.

**Fix, in two parts.**

1. **Remove the nondeterminism at the source**, which is the only real fix:
```ts
// Before
export function toOrderResponse(order: Order): OrderResponse {
  return { id: order.id, status: order.status, totalCents: order.totalCents, createdAt: new Date().toISOString() }
}

// After -- the clock is a parameter, not an ambient fact.
export function toOrderResponse(order: Order, now: Date): OrderResponse {
  return { id: order.id, status: order.status, totalCents: order.totalCents, createdAt: now.toISOString() }
}
```
```ts
it('serialises an order', () => {
  expect(toOrderResponse(orderFactory(), new Date('2026-03-01T09:00:00.000Z'))).toMatchSnapshot()
})
```

2. **Add a serializer as defence in depth**, so any *remaining* volatile value does not require a human:
```ts
expect.addSnapshotSerializer({
  test: (v) => typeof v === 'string' && /^\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}T[\d:.]+Z$/.test(v),
  print: () => '"<ISO-DATE>"',
})
```
Use a coarse grain for dates. `<ISO-DATE>` when the exact instant is irrelevant; `<DATE:2026-03-01>` when the day matters; a full value only when the instant *is* the behaviour under test.

**Guardrail.**
1. A lint rule or a review convention: no `new Date()`, `Date.now()`, `Math.random()`, or `crypto.randomUUID()` in code reachable from a snapshot test without an injected port.
2. In CI, `--update=false`, so a nondeterministic snapshot cannot regenerate itself into green.
3. A nightly job that re-runs the whole suite twice with different seeds; any snapshot that differs between the two runs fails, which catches nondeterminism the main run got lucky on.
4. Fail the build on obsolete snapshots, so `.snap` files do not accumulate entries nothing references:
```bash
npx vitest run --update=false 2>&1 | tee vitest.log
grep -qE '[0-9]+ snapshots? obsolete' vitest.log && { echo "::error::Obsolete snapshots: run 'npx vitest -u'"; exit 1; } || true
```

---

## SCENARIO 6: The E2E test that fails on the `click` with "element is not stable"

**Symptom.** `checkout.spec.ts` fails ~20% of the time with:
```
Error: locator.click: Timeout 30000ms exceeded.
  - attempting click action
  - element is visible, enabled and stable
  - scrolling into view
  - <button class="Place order…">…</button> intercepts pointer events
```
Or, more subtly, `element is not attached to the DOM`.

**Reproduction.**
```bash
npx playwright test e2e/checkout.spec.ts --repeat-each 20 --workers=1 --retries=0
npx playwright test e2e/checkout.spec.ts --repeat-each 20 --workers=4 --retries=0   # rate goes up
```
Then open the trace:
```bash
npx playwright show-trace e2e/.artifacts/*checkout*/trace.zip
```

**Diagnosis.** Two different root causes present identically.

**Root cause A: a re-render that replaces the node.** The `Place order` button is inside a component that re-renders when the cart query refetches. Playwright resolves the element, then the node is replaced, then it tries to click a detached handle. The trace's DOM snapshots show the same button in two different container elements between the actionability check and the click. The tell in the trace is a network request (a React Query refetch) firing immediately before the failure.

**Root cause B: an overlay intercepting pointer events.** A cookie banner, a toast, or a "prices updated" modal renders above the button. Playwright's hit test finds the overlay, not the button. The trace shows a `banner` or `dialog` element with a higher stacking order at the same coordinates.

**Fix A: stop the refetch from replacing the node, or wait for stability.**
```ts
// ✅ 1. Make the click resilient to re-render by targeting a stable container and
//      waiting for the network to settle first.
await page.waitForLoadState('networkidle')                    // crude, but reveals the problem
// Better: assert the state that precedes the action, so the refetch has happened.
await expect(page.getByTestId('cart-settled')).toHaveAttribute('data-settled', 'true')
await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Place order' }).click()

// ✅ 2. If a transient toast legitimately overlays, dismiss it and wait for it to leave.
const toast = page.getByRole('status', { name: /cart updated/i })
if (await toast.isVisible()) await toast.getByRole('button', { name: /dismiss/i }).click()
await expect(toast).toBeHidden()
```

**Fix B: make the overlay not intercept, and assert it is gone.**
```tsx
// The real product fix: a toast must not cover an interactive control.
<div role="status" className="toast toast--top-right" aria-live="polite">
  <span>Cart updated</span>
  <button onClick={dismiss} aria-label="Dismiss notification">×</button>
</div>
// CSS: pointer-events: none on the container, pointer-events: auto on the button,
// so the toast never blocks a click meant for the page underneath.
```
```ts
// And a regression test for the overlay class of bug, in the component tier:
it('a toast never intercepts clicks on content beneath it', () => {
  render(<><Toast message="Cart updated" /><button>Place order</button></>)
  const toast = screen.getByRole('status')
  expect(toast).toHaveStyle({ pointerEvents: 'none' })
})
```

**Guardrail.**
1. `trace: 'retain-on-failure'` in the Playwright config. This diagnosis took 20 minutes with a trace and would have taken a day without one.
2. An app-level "settled" signal (`data-settled="true"`) on every view that depends on fetched data, so tests can wait for a state rather than for a duration.
3. `expect` on a *state* immediately before every action that can be preceded by a refetch. This is the single highest-leverage practice in E2E.
4. A component test asserting `pointer-events` on overlays, which is impossible to test in E2E but trivial in jsdom.

---

## SCENARIO 7: The transaction-per-test suite that leaves data behind

**Symptom.** The integration suite is green on a clean database and fails on the second run with a unique-constraint violation on `User_email_key`, at the fixture creation step.

**Reproduction.**
```bash
pnpm test:integration      # green
pnpm test:integration      # red, immediately
```

**Diagnosis.** Transaction-per-test rollback is not covering some writes. The candidates, in likelihood order:

1. **Production code opened its own client.** A module-level `new PrismaClient()` somewhere in the call path writes outside the test's transaction, so the test's rollback does not undo it.
2. **Raw SQL on a separate connection.** A `pg` `Pool` created at module scope for a reporting query, a full-text search index maintenance, or a `pg_notify`.
3. **A non-transactional table.** Postgres sequences and some extensions (`pg_trgm` indexes are transactional, but a `MATERIALIZED VIEW` and some queue tables are not). If a fixture writes to one, the rollback leaves it.
4. **A worker thread or a background job** started by the application under test, holding its own pool.
5. **`$executeRawUnsafe('TRUNCATE')` somewhere** committing outside the test transaction.

The diagnosis step that finds it in one shot: assert the connection identity.
```ts
// Inside the test, inside the transaction.
const { rows } = await trx.$queryRawUnsafe<{ pid: number; xact: string }[]>('SELECT pg_backend_pid() AS pid, txid_current_if_assigned()::text AS xact')
const appPid = await appDb.$queryRawUnsafe<{ pid: number }[]>('SELECT pg_backend_pid() AS pid')
expect(appPid.rows[0]!.pid).toBe(rows[0]!.pid)     // the app must be ON the transactional connection
```
If the PIDs differ, the app has escaped the transaction. Also check `pg_stat_activity` from outside for connections in `state = 'idle in transaction'` after a test finishes.

**Root cause (in this case).** A `SearchIndexer` class created its own `pg.Pool` at module scope to write to a `search_documents` table outside the application transaction, on the assumption that indexing must not be rolled back. The write was outside the test transaction and therefore survived the rollback.

**Fix.**
```ts
// ✅ Inject the pool. In tests, it is the transactional client; in production, a real pool.
export class SearchIndexer {
  constructor(private readonly db: { query: (sql: string, params: unknown[]) => Promise<unknown> }) {}
  async reindex(entity: { id: string; tenantId: string; body: string }) {
    await this.db.query(
      `INSERT INTO search_documents (id, tenant_id, body) VALUES ($1, $2, $3)
       ON CONFLICT (id) DO UPDATE SET body = EXCLUDED.body, indexed_at = now()`,
      [entity.id, entity.tenantId, entity.body],
    )
  }
}
```
```ts
// Production composition root
const pool = new pg.Pool({ connectionString: process.env.DATABASE_URL, max: 10 })
container.register(SearchIndexer, new SearchIndexer(pool))

// Test harness: the same class, bound to the transactional client
const container = await createContainer({ env, dbOverride: { query: (sql, params) => trx.$queryRawUnsafe(sql, ...params) } })
```
Now the index write participates in the test transaction and rolls back with everything else.

**Guardrail.**
1. The explicit PID assertion above, once, in a dedicated test: *"every database write in a request happens on the request's connection"*. That is a load-bearing invariant and it deserves its own test.
2. A dependency-boundary lint rule: `src/infrastructure/**` may not `new` a `Pool` or `PrismaClient` at module scope. It must be constructed in the composition root and injected.
3. The idle-in-transaction leak assertion in `afterEach` (Scenario 3), which catches the *other* direction.
4. A CI hygiene job: run the integration suite twice in a row on the same database. A suite that only passes on a fresh database is broken, and this is the cheapest possible detector.
```yaml
- name: Integration suite (first run)
  run: pnpm test:integration
- name: Integration suite (second run, same database -- catches leaked state)
  run: pnpm test:integration
```

---

## SCENARIO 8: The MSW test that passes against a handler the real API never serves

**Symptom.** A checkout test passes with MSW stubbing `POST /api/payments/charge` returning `200 { id: 'ch_1' }`. In production, roughly 30% of charges are declined and the client treats every non-200 as success, so declined payments are recorded as paid. No test fails.

**Reproduction.** Point the test at a real sandbox, or read the provider's API reference. The discrepancy is visible immediately: the real API returns `402` with `{ error: { type: 'card_error', code: 'card_declined' } }`, and a `201` with `{ id, object, amount, status: 'succeeded', outcome: { type: 'authorized' } }`.

**Diagnosis.** This is a *fidelity* bug, not a logic bug, and it is invisible by construction. The diagnostic that exposes it is to make the fake's shape the real shape:
1. Capture real responses from the provider's sandbox (three: success, decline, 3DS challenge) and store them as fixtures.
2. Type the fake's return type against the provider's own TypeScript definitions, if they exist.
3. Generate the MSW handlers from the provider's OpenAPI schema, so a required field the handler omits is a type error.

**Root cause.** The handler was written from memory of what "a payment API" returns, not from the provider's actual contract. The client code had a branch that treated any 2xx as success, and no test ever produced a non-2xx from the fake.

**Fix, in three layers.**

1. **Record real fixtures and make the fake return them verbatim.**
```ts
// test/fixtures/provider/payments.sandbox.json  -- captured from the sandbox, sanitised
export const chargeSucceeded = {
  id: 'ch_3PxQ', object: 'charge', amount: 4200, amount_captured: 4200, currency: 'gbp',
  status: 'succeeded', captured: true, refunded: false,
  payment_method: 'pm_1',
  outcome: { network_status: 'approved_by_network', risk_level: 'normal', type: 'authorized', seller_message: 'Payment complete.' },
  created: 1_772_000_000, livemode: false, metadata: {},
} as const
export const chargeDeclined = {
  error: { type: 'card_error', code: 'card_declined', decline_code: 'generic_decline', message: 'Your card was declined.' },
} as const
```
2. **Type the client's mapping as a total function over the observed statuses**, so a new status is a compile error rather than a silent success:
```ts
const PaymentOutcomeSchema = z.discriminatedUnion('status', [
  z.object({ id: z.string(), status: z.literal('succeeded'), amount_captured: z.number() }),
  z.object({ id: z.string(), status: z.literal('pending') }),
])
type PaymentOutcome = z.infer<typeof PaymentOutcomeSchema>

export function mapCharge(raw: unknown): PaymentState {
  if (raw && typeof raw === 'object' && 'error' in raw) {
    const code = (raw as { error: { code?: string } }).error.code
    return { state: 'declined', code: code ?? 'unknown' }
  }
  const parsed = PaymentOutcomeSchema.safeParse(raw)
  if (!parsed.success) {
    // 🔴 An unrecognised shape must NOT be treated as success.
    return { state: 'unknown', reason: 'unrecognised_provider_response' }
  }
  return parsed.data.status === 'succeeded'
    ? { state: 'paid', txId: parsed.data.id }
    : { state: 'pending', txId: parsed.data.id }
}
```
3. **A nightly sandbox smoke test**, which is what keeps the fixtures honest:
```ts
it('the provider still returns the shapes our fixtures assume', async () => {
  const raw = await sandboxClient.charge({ amount: 100, currency: 'gbp', token: SANDBOX_TOKEN })
  expect(Object.keys(raw).sort()).toEqual(Object.keys(chargeSucceeded).sort())
  // If the provider adds a field we ignore, that is fine. If it removes one, this fails.
})
```

**Guardrail.**
1. Handlers live in one module imported by tests, Storybook, and dev, and are **derived from recorded fixtures** wherever the provider can be reached.
2. `onUnhandledRequest: 'error'`, so a handler with a wrong URL fails loudly.
3. **A test that asserts the client's behaviour for every documented provider outcome**, including the unknown-shape case, so a new status cannot fall through to success.
4. A reviewed registry of third-party contracts, each with: recorded fixtures, a schema, a nightly smoke test, and an owner. A third party with no entry in the registry is a third party you do not understand.

---

## SCENARIO 9: The test suite that got 5x slower after a framework upgrade

**Symptom.** After upgrading a query library, the component suite went from 50 seconds to 4 minutes, and 14 tests now time out. No test logic changed.

**Reproduction.**
```bash
pnpm test:component -- --reporter=verbose   # per-file timings in the output
# Vitest has no built-in slow-test report; generate one from the JSON report:
pnpm test:component -- --reporter=json --outputFile=component.json
node -e "const r=require('./component.json');
  r.testResults
    .map(f=>({f:f.name.replace(process.cwd(),''), ms:f.assertionResults.reduce((s,a)=>s+a.duration,0)}))
    .sort((a,b)=>b.ms-a.ms).slice(0,10).forEach(x=>console.log(String(x.ms).padStart(7), x.f))"
```

**Diagnosis.** Two questions: is it a *few* slow tests or *all* of them? Is it CPU or wall time?
- All tests equally slower by a constant factor: something per-test. A new per-test setup cost (a schema validation, a config reload, a new client construction) or a lost parallelism.
- A few tests disproportionately slower: a specific pattern is now expensive (N+1 awaits, a serialised query, a re-render per keystroke).
- CPU-bound (high process CPU, low I/O wait): the runner or a transform.
- Wall-time-bound with idle CPU: a fixed sleep or a retry backoff.

**Root cause (in this case).** Two causes from the upgrade:
1. The library's new version validates the whole query key on every `invalidateQueries` call, and three tests invalidate a large keyset in a loop. That is the few-slow-tests pattern.
2. The new version logs a deprecation warning per instantiation, and the test setup's `onConsoleLog` gate was serialising console writes through a reporter. That is the all-tests pattern.

**Fix.**
```ts
// 1. Replace the invalidation loop with a single invalidation. The loop was compensating
//    for a bug that the new version fixed by making invalidation recursive by default.
it('refreshes the order list after a status change', async () => {
  await api.updateOrderStatus('ord_1', 'shipped')
  // One invalidation, and the UI must reflect it.
  await waitFor(() => expect(screen.getByText('Shipped')).toBeVisible())
})

// 2. Silence the deprecation warning deliberately, once, with a reason and a tracking issue.
//    Do not disable the console gate globally.
const originalWarn = console.warn
beforeAll(() => {
  console.warn = (...args: unknown[]) => {
    if (String(args[0]).includes('[query] deprecated: sync option')) {
      deprecationWarnings.push(String(args[0]))
      return
    }
    originalWarn(...args)
  }
})
afterAll(() => { console.warn = originalWarn })

it('records every deprecation warning so we can remove the shim', () => {
  expect(deprecationWarnings).toEqual([])    // this test now FAILS, telling us exactly what to fix
})
```
The second change is the interesting one: instead of silencing the warning and forgetting, the suite now *fails* while the deprecation is live, and passes once the shim is removed. The warning became a TODO with a failure attached.

**Guardrail.**
1. A per-file duration report in CI, posted to the job summary, so a 3x regression is visible in the diff rather than felt.
2. A **duration budget** with an alert:
```ts
// scripts/check-suite-duration.mjs
const total = report.testResults.reduce((s, f) => s + f.assertionResults.reduce((a, x) => a + x.duration, 0), 0)
const budget = Number(process.env.UNIT_BUDGET_MS ?? 60_000)
if (total > budget) {
  console.error(`::error::Suite took ${(total / 1000).toFixed(0)}s, over the ${budget / 1000}s budget.`)
  process.exit(1)
}
```
3. A **baseline for the slow-test list**, with a check that new entries require an explicit `// @slow` comment and a justification.
4. A dependency upgrade policy: upgrade on a branch, run the suite, and diff the per-file durations. A 20% regression requires a written explanation before merge.

---

## SCENARIO 10: The E2E test that only fails in Firefox

**Symptom.** A checkout test passes in Chromium, fails 100% of the time in Firefox with `locator.click: element intercepts pointer events` on the submit button. WebKit passes.

**Reproduction.**
```bash
npx playwright test e2e/checkout.spec.ts --project=firefox --retries=0 --trace=on
npx playwright show-trace test-results/*firefox*/trace.zip
```

**Diagnosis.** Cross-browser-only failures are almost always one of five things. The trace's screenshot at the moment of failure tells you which:
1. **A different default box model or sizing.** Firefox's default `box-sizing` differs for some elements, or a `width: 100%` plus padding overflows differently, so the button is positioned differently.
2. **A different default `line-height`/`font-size`**, which changes text wrapping and therefore element height, which moves what overlaps.
3. **A scroll container difference.** Firefox scrolls a nested container where Chromium scrolls the page, so the sticky header covers the focused element (WCAG 2.4.11).
4. **A `<form>` submit difference**, or a `<button>` outside a form behaving differently.
5. **A real engine difference**: `Intl` output, a CSS feature Firefox does not support, `form` validation UI, or date parsing.

**Root cause (in this case).** A sticky header with `z-index: 10` and a `position: sticky` inside a page where the payment form's submit button sat at the very top of the viewport after scrolling. Chromium scrolled the page; Firefox scrolled an inner `<main>` element with its own overflow, so the sticky header ended up overlaying the button. The overlay was the header, not a toast.

**Fix.**
```css
/* ✅ 1. Never let a sticky element cover an interactive target. Reserve space with scroll-margin. */
.checkout-panel {
  scroll-margin-block: 5rem;                 /* clears the sticky header */
}
.sticky-header {
  position: sticky;
  top: 0;
  z-index: 10;
  /* Reserve a scroll-padding area on the scroll container. */
}

/* 2. Scroll the right container consistently across engines. */
html { scroll-padding-block: 5rem; }
main  { overflow: visible; }                 /* the page scrolls, not main */
```
```ts
// ✅ 3. Make the test scroll explicitly rather than relying on the engine's behaviour.
await page.getByRole('group', { name: /card details/i }).scrollIntoViewIfNeeded()
await expect(page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Place order' })).toBeInViewport()
await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Place order' }).click()
```
The `toBeInViewport` assertion is the important part: it converts "Playwright scrolled for me, sort of" into an explicit, checked precondition, and it catches the real bug class (an element hidden behind a sticky header) instead of papering over it with a forced click.

**Guardrail.**
1. **Run every E2E journey in all three engines on `main`, not just on PRs.** A Firefox-only bug found after release is a Firefox-only bug found too late.
2. A visual-regression baseline per platform, generated in a pinned container. Cross-engine layout drift is a visual signal, and it is much cheaper to see than to debug.
3. Never use `force: true` in a click. It is the E2E equivalent of a type cast: it silences a real overlap or hit-test problem.
4. A project-wide rule: after every user action, either assert a state or assert a visibility precondition. `click()` with nothing after it is where these bugs hide.

---

## SCENARIO 11: The test that is green but the feature is broken (the assertion roulette)

**Symptom.** `pricing.test.ts` is 100% covered, all green, and a promotion gives the wrong discount in production for orders above GBP 500. No test ever exercised a case where a tier is expired *and* a coupon is applied.

**Reproduction.** Mutation testing finds it in one run:
```bash
npx stryker run src/domain/pricing.ts --reporters clear-text,html
```
The report shows surviving mutants in the `expiredTier` branch, and one in the coupon-combination path. Every one of those mutants is a real bug that your tests cannot see.

**Diagnosis.** The tests were written branch-by-branch from the implementation, not behaviour-by-behaviour from the spec. Coverage was high because the code had 14 branches and the tests hit 14 of them, but they never *combined* them, and they never asserted a *negative*.

**Root cause.** Two specific defects in the suite:
1. **Each test set up one condition.** `it('applies no expired tier')`, `it('applies a coupon')` -- but never `it('does not apply a coupon to an expired tier')`. The combination is where the bug was.
2. **The assertions were weak.** `expect(result.discountCents).toBeGreaterThan(0)` instead of `expect(result).toEqual({ subtotalCents: 60000, discountCents: 12000, appliedCoupon: null, reason: 'tier_expired' })`.

**Fix, and it is short.**
```ts
// ✅ A combination table: the cases a human would never enumerate but production will.
it.each([
  // subtotal, coupon, tierExpired,  expectedDiscount, expectedCoupon, expectedReason
  [ 59999, 'WELCOME5', false,  0,      null,          'below_tier' ],
  [ 60000, null,        false, 12000,   null,          'tier_20' ],
  [ 60000, 'WELCOME5', false, 12000,   'WELCOME5',    'tier_20' ],       // the coupon does not stack
  [ 60000, null,        true,  6000,    null,          'tier_expired_20' ],
  [ 60000, 'WELCOME5', true,  3000,    null,          'tier_expired_20' ], // 🔴 the bug
  [ 250000,'WELCOME5', true,  62500,   null,          'max_discount_cap' ],
])(
  'subtotal=%i coupon=%s expired=%s -> discount=%i couponApplied=%s reason=%s',
  (subtotal, coupon, expired, discount, applied, reason) => {
    const result = applyDiscount({
      subtotalCents: subtotal as number,
      coupon: coupon as string | null,
      rules: { currency: 'GBP', tiers: [{ minSubtotalCents: 60000, percentOff: 20 }], maxDiscountPercent: 25, coupons: { WELCOME5: 5 } },
      now: expired ? new Date('2027-01-01') : new Date('2026-03-01'),
    })
    expect(result).toEqual({
      subtotalCents: subtotal,
      discountCents: discount,
      totalCents: (subtotal as number) - (discount as number),
      appliedCoupon: applied,
      reason,
    })
  },
)
```
That one table covers six behaviours, three of which no previous test touched, and it takes ten minutes to write.

**Guardrail.**
1. **Mutation testing in CI on changed files.** A surviving mutant in a changed file is a code review comment with evidence attached. This is the instrument that catches "green but wrong".
2. `expect(result).toEqual({...})` on small value objects rather than field-by-field assertions, so a *new* field forces a decision.
3. A review question that is asked every time: *"what test covers the combination of these two branches?"* If nobody can answer, the test does not exist.
4. Property-based tests for the general version of the same problem: for a large input space, assert invariants (monotonicity in `subtotalCents`, `discount <= maxDiscountPercent`, `total + discount === subtotal`, coupons never stack) rather than enumerating cases.

---

## SCENARIO 12: The pipeline that takes 45 minutes and developers have learned to batch

**Symptom.** CI takes 38-52 minutes per PR. Developers open one PR per day containing four features, review is perfunctory, and about 4% of PRs are reverted after merge. Nobody has raised this as a problem because "CI is slow" is normalised.

**Reproduction and diagnosis.** Get the data before you change anything.
```bash
# 1. Per-job durations for the last 50 runs.
gh run list --limit 50 --json databaseId,conclusion,createdAt,jobs \
  --jq '.[] | {id: .databaseId, at: .createdAt, jobs: [.jobs[] | {name, duration: (.startedAt)}]}' \
  > ci-history.json

# 2. Aggregate.
node -e "
const runs = require('./ci-history.json')
const totals = new Map()
for (const r of runs) for (const j of r.jobs || []) {
  totals.set(j.name, (totals.get(j.name) || 0) + (new Date(j.duration) - new Date(j.startedAt)) / 60000)
}
;[...totals.entries()].sort((a,b)=>b[1]-a[1]).forEach(([n,m])=>console.log(m.toFixed(1).padStart(8), 'min avg  ', n))
console.log('\nruns:', runs.length, ' failures:', runs.filter(r=>r.conclusion!=='success').length)
"
```
The result in this case:
```
  1123.4 min avg   Full E2E (all browsers, all shards)
  2210.7 min avg   E2E (shard 1/4 … 4/4)      <- 4 shards × ~9 min, but they start AFTER build + preview deploy
   890.3 min avg   Integration
   402.1 min avg   Verify
   310.6 min avg   Deploy the preview environment     <- serialised before E2E
   288.0 min avg   Install (pnpm + playwright browsers)
   141.0 min avg   Performance
   120.5 min avg   Security
```
And the critical-path analysis: E2E could not start until `build` (2 min) finished, *and* until the preview deploy (5 min) completed, *and* the browser install (7 min, uncached) completed, *and* the E2E global setup ran migrations on a 300k-row database.

**Root cause.** Four compounding problems:
1. **`retries: 2` in Playwright plus a 2.5% flake rate**, so the effective E2E time was 9 min x 1.5 on average, and one failing shard meant 12 minutes before a human saw anything.
2. **The preview environment was deployed per PR push**, including pushes that only changed a README.
3. **Playwright browsers were not cached**, so every job spent 7 minutes re-downloading 400MB of Chromium, Firefox and WebKit.
4. **The E2E suite had 180 tests, 60% of which were CRUD and validation-message checks** that belong in the component tier. And it ran Firefox and WebKit on every PR, not just on `main`.

**Fix, in priority order.**

```yaml
# 1. Cache the browsers. Saves 7 minutes per job.
- name: Cache Playwright browsers
  id: pw-cache
  uses: actions/cache@v4
  with:
    path: ~/.cache/ms-playwright
    key: pw-${{ runner.os }}-${{ hashFiles('pnpm-lock.yaml') }}-${{ hashFiles('playwright.config.ts') }}
    restore-keys: pw-${{ runner.os }}-${{ hashFiles('pnpm-lock.yaml') }}-
- run: pnpm exec playwright install --with-deps chromium
  if: steps.pw-cache.outputs.cache-hit != 'true'
```

```yaml
# 2. Reuse the preview environment per branch; redeploy only when the build or the
#    migrations change. A docs-only push now skips E2E entirely.
- name: Detect whether anything that affects the app changed
  id: scope
  run: |
    if git diff --quiet origin/main...HEAD -- src/ prisma/ package.json; then
      echo "changed=app"   >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
    elif git diff --quiet origin/main...HEAD; then
      echo "changed=none"  >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
    else
      echo "changed=docs"  >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
    fi
- run: npx acme/deploy-preview --if-changed ${{ steps.scope.outputs.changed }}
```

```ts
// 3. Move 108 tests down a tier. The CRUD and validation-message checks become component
//    tests; 4 E2E journeys remain.
test.describe('orders', () => {
  test('@smoke a customer places an order and sees it in their history', ...)      // 3 min
  test('a declined card does not create an order and preserves the input', ...)     // 40s
  test('a 3DS challenge completes the payment', ...)                               // 50s
  test('an expired session returns to sign-in with the basket intact', ...)         // 35s
  test('the checkout page has no axe violations', ...)                             // 25s
})
```

```yaml
# 4. E2E policy: Chromium on every user-facing PR; all three engines on main only.
e2e:
  needs: [build, scope]
  if: >-
    steps.scope.outputs.changed == 'app' &&
    (github.event_name == 'push' || contains(github.event.pull_request.labels.*.name, 'e2e'))
  strategy:
    fail-fast: false
    matrix: { shard: [1, 2] }          # 4 -> 2 shards: the suite is now 60 tests, not 180
  steps:
    - run: pnpm exec playwright test --project=chromium --shard=${{ matrix.shard }}/2
```

```yaml
# 5. Flake: fix, do not retry. Track it.
- uses: playwrightwright/test@...   # retries: 1 in CI, and a flake budget that fails the build
- run: node scripts/flake-budget.mjs test-results/e2e-results.json
```

**The result.** Wall clock 45 min -> **11 min** on a user-facing PR, 6 min on a docs change, and 2 min for the fast gate that actually blocks most mistakes. Revert rate fell because reviews happened within the hour rather than the next morning.

**Guardrail.**
1. **A pipeline duration budget**, posted to the job summary every run, with an alert if the p50 exceeds it. The budget is what stops the slow creep back.
2. Per-job duration history stored and charted, so a regression is a visual event, not a feeling.
3. A quarterly review of the *shape* of the suite: tests per tier, and the ratio of E2E to total. If E2E crosses 15% of the test count, that is a finding.
4. A rule that a PR which is "big because CI is slow" is a signal to split the PR, not to raise the timeout. Every timeout raised to make CI pass is a hidden decision about what your tests actually verify.

---

# PHASE 9 INTERVIEW Q&A (40 questions with model answers)

---

### Q1. 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** — What is a unit test, and how is it different from an integration test?

**Model answer.** A unit test exercises one cohesive piece of logic through its public surface, with every collaborator substituted so that it is deterministic, isolated and fast: no network, no real clock, no real database, no `Math.random`. An integration test crosses at least one *real* boundary -- a real database, a real HTTP server, a real queue -- and verifies that two or more components interoperate correctly.

The test I would run to settle any argument is: *can I run this with zero network, zero disk and zero database?* If yes, it is a unit test. The second test is: *would this still pass if I rewrote the implementation with different internals but identical observable behaviour?* If yes, it is testing behaviour, which is what I want; if no, it is coupled to implementation and will be a tax on every refactor.

I would add the nuance that "unit" is genuinely ambiguous -- function, class, module, or behaviour are four defensible definitions -- and that the productive framing is the *behavioural* one: a unit is one observable rule the system promises, regardless of how many functions implement it.

---

### Q2. 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** — Explain the AAA pattern and when you would stop using it.

**Model answer.** AAA is Arrange, Act, Assert: build the preconditions, do exactly one thing, then assert one logical outcome. Its value is navigability -- a stranger can find each phase in a glance -- and blame: a failing test names the phase it died in.

I would stop using it as a *comment convention* for tests that are two lines long. `expect(isValid(x)).toBe(true)` does not need three comment blocks. And I would never let an arrange helper contain the Act, because then the failure is attributed to the wrong place and the reader is misled.

The real limit is one Act per test. Two acts mean two behaviours, and if the second depends on the first's state you cannot assert the first in isolation. When I see a test called "checkout works" with fourteen assertions, I treat it as four tests that have not been separated yet.

---

### Q3. 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — What is a test double, and how do mock, stub, spy and fake differ?

**Model answer.** A test double is anything standing in for a real dependency. A **dummy** satisfies a signature and does nothing. A **stub** returns canned data so the code under test can run; you do not care how it was called. A **spy** delegates to the real implementation and records the calls. A **mock** returns canned data *and* you assert on how it was called. A **fake** is a working but deliberately simplified implementation with real semantics -- an in-memory repository, a clock you can advance, a payment gateway that declines a specific card.

The distinction that matters for quality is state verification versus behaviour verification. Prefer asserting on the *output* the code produced; use interaction assertions only when the interaction is genuinely the contract, like "publishes exactly one `order.paid` event". A mock-heavy suite is one where the mocks' return values have become the specification, and that specification is wrong the moment the interface changes.

My default is fakes, because they support state verification and survive refactors. I reach for mocks when I need to assert on an outward-facing event.

---

### Q4. 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — What is property-based testing, and when is it better than example tests?

**Model answer.** Property-based testing inverts the question. Instead of "does this function return the right thing for this input", you state invariants that must hold for *every* input, provide generators for the input domain, and let the framework shrink any counterexample to a minimal failing case. With fast-check the workflow is generate, assert, and on failure report a minimal input plus a reproducible seed.

I use it when the input space is large and the rule is a property rather than a case: parsers and serialisers (round-trip), allocators and splitters (conservation plus fairness -- a payment split must sum to the total with no share differing by more than one cent), reducers (associativity and identity), anything with money or dates or codecs. I write the example tests alongside it, because stakeholders care about specific cases ("GBP 100 gets 10% off") and a property is documentation only its author fully understands.

The cost is authoring: generators need thought about cross-field invariants, and I budget two to five times a single example test.

---

### Q5. 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — What does code coverage actually measure, and what does it miss?

**Model answer.** Coverage measures which executable constructs -- lines, branches, functions, statements -- your suite *executed*. It is a meta-metric about the test suite, not a measure of quality or correctness.

What it misses is the important part. It cannot see whether your test had any assertions, so `expect(f(1)).toBeDefined()` gives 100% coverage and zero signal. It cannot see whether your expectations are *correct*, which is the worst possible state to be in. It says nothing about interleavings and races, about performance, or about whether the deployed configuration is right. A function at 100% coverage may have all its `if` branches untested while every line ran.

So I use coverage for three things only: detecting dead code, finding untested error paths, and gating *new* uncovered lines in a PR with diff coverage. I do not use it as a target for engineers, because that reliably produces assertion-free code, and I pair it with mutation testing as the real quality signal.

---

### Q6. 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** — When is a snapshot test the right choice, and how do you stop it rotting?

**Model answer.** A snapshot test is right for large, deterministic, reviewable outputs where writing explicit assertions would be enormous and unmaintainable: generated OpenAPI documents and GraphQL schemas, serialised state, state-machine traces, translation key sets, and rendered pixels. The value is that a diff is a *change detector*: it tells you that everything you did not intend did not change.

The failure mode is noise, and noise is fatal. A snapshot nobody reads protects nothing. So: normalise every volatile field with a serializer -- dates, uuids, ids, hashes, `Error`s, and object key order; keep snapshots small and inline so the diff appears in the same review as the code; pair every snapshot with three to five explicit assertions on the invariants that actually matter; never auto-update in CI; and fail the build on obsolete entries so `.snap` files do not accumulate junk.

The rule I hold to: if a snapshot is too large to read in a PR diff, it should be replaced with explicit assertions.

---

### Q7. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What is TDD, and where does it *not* pay off?

**Model answer.** TDD is a discipline: write a failing test first, make it pass in the simplest way possible, then refactor with the test as a safety net, in small steps. The green step is deliberately throwaway -- I hardcode the return value first to prove my test can actually pass, which isolates a failure of the test from a failure of the implementation. That step feels like cheating and is the most misunderstood part.

It pays off where the domain has rules and edge cases, where the implementation will be rewritten, where a bug is expensive, and where you have continuous integration so the suite is trustworthy. It does not pay off when you are exploring an unknown design, when the unit is framework wiring whose value is in the integration, when UI layout is the deliverable, on a hard deadline with fixed scope, or in a team large enough that coordination dominates.

One nuance I would add: TDD's cost is paid in the first twenty percent of the work and recovered in the rest -- the evidence is strongest on debug time and API design quality rather than defect count. And bad tests make TDD actively harmful, because you end up with a green suite protecting the wrong behaviour, having *also* paid the cost of writing the test first.

---

### Q8. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What are the four ways an async test lies about time?

**Model answer.** One: the test finishes before the async work does -- a missing `await`, or an `await` on the wrong promise, so you assert on an unresolved value. The symptom is a test that passes even when the code is broken, and the fix is awaiting the returned promise plus `expect.assertions(n)` to prove you waited.

Two: real time is slower than the test's patience. A real `setTimeout(50)` in a test is a 1% flake rate on a loaded CI machine. The fix is to eliminate real time -- either inject a `Clock` into your own code, which I strongly prefer, or use fake timers for code you do not own.

Three: the classic one -- you advance the clock synchronously. `vi.advanceTimersByTime(1000)` fires the timer callbacks but any `await` inside them is queued as a microtask that has not run, so your assertion sees the state from *before* the callback completed. The fix is `await vi.advanceTimersByTimeAsync(1000)`, which drains the microtask queue between callbacks.

Four: leaked timers. The code schedules something and never clears it, so the test ends with a pending timer; Jest prints "worker failed to exit gracefully" and the next test is polluted. The fix is that production code must clear on teardown, and the test asserts `vi.getTimerCount() === 0`.

---

### Q9. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — How do you decide where the test seams go?

**Model answer.** Along the lines of *reasoning and effect*, not along lines of files. I inject a port when the thing is an effect -- I/O, the clock, randomness, the network, a message bus -- and when substituting it buys me determinism or a test of its own. I do not inject for its own sake; an interface with one implementation and one test is ceremony.

The specific seams I nearly always want are `Clock` and `Random`, because they are the two collaborators that most often lie, and injecting them turns time-based rules -- session expiry, retry backoff, scheduled jobs, token TTL -- into instant, exact, order-deterministic tests with no fake timers at all. Beyond that: a `PaymentGateway`, a `Mailer`, a repository, and an id generator.

A useful smell test: if I find myself mocking my own domain module, the seam is in the wrong place and the logic should be extracted to a pure function instead.

---

### Q10. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — When would you use transaction-per-test isolation, and what can you not test with it?

**Model answer.** Transaction-per-test is the highest-leverage decision in a Postgres-backed integration suite. You open a transaction before the test, bind your code to a client bound to that transaction, and roll back afterwards instead of deleting rows. It is ten to a hundred times faster than truncate-and-reseed, it preserves *real* transactional semantics during the test, and it makes tests order-independent and parallel-safe against one shared database for free.

What you cannot test with it: anything on a second connection or process. Background workers, because their writes are outside your transaction. Self-HTTP calls. Two-concurrent-writer scenarios, where neither actor sees the other's uncommitted data. Advisory locks and `LISTEN/NOTIFY`, which are session-scoped. `SERIALIZABLE` conflicts. And commit-visibility semantics.

There is one more gap worth naming: it hides whether your code commits correctly. If a repository's multi-statement write is not atomic, a transactional suite will not catch it, because the test's own rollback masks the partial write. You need one non-transactional test per repository that forces the second statement to fail and asserts the first did not land.

---

### Q11. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — Why is MSW better than mocking your own API client?

**Model answer.** Because MSW intercepts at the network boundary, so *your* code runs for real. Mocking `PaymentGateway.charge` means the URL, the headers, the auth, the payload serialisation, the timeout handling, the retry logic and the error mapping are all untested -- you have replaced exactly the code most likely to be wrong with a return value you chose.

MSW stubs the third party's HTTP API, so your adapter is under test. The two properties I hold onto: the handler's response shape must be a *recorded* real response, not something I invented from memory, and `onUnhandledRequest: 'error'` must be set, so a handler with a wrong URL fails immediately instead of silently producing a 404 from the wrong place.

The honest limitation is that MSW proves you speak the protocol you *think* the provider speaks. I close that gap with contract tests derived from the published spec, and a nightly suite against the provider's real sandbox that re-checks the recorded shapes.

---

### Q12. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What is consumer-driven contract testing, and when is it not worth it?

**Model answer.** The consumer's tests run against a mock provider generated from a pact, the pact is published to a broker, and the provider's CI replays every consumer pact against its real API before deploying. The genuinely novel part is that the consumer can build and test in parallel with no shared environment, and the provider learns *before* deploy which specific interaction is about to break.

The matcher choice is the real design decision, and I decide it by asking what the consumer's code does with the field. A field it branches on -- `status` -- gets equality or a regex enum, so a new enum value shows up as a contract change. A free-text display name gets `type` or a length regex, because pinning a value the consumer never reads makes the contract brittle for no benefit. A collection gets `eachLike`. A possibly-absent field gets `nullOrType`.

It is not worth it with one monolithic codebase, where an integration test is cheaper and stronger; when the "services" are really modules in one repo; or when the API churns so much you cannot afford the operational cost of a pact platform.

---

### Q13. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — How do you test authorisation properly?

**Model answer.** I test the negative path first, because almost every authorisation breach is a missing `return`, an `||` where `&&` was meant, a query that filters by `id` but not by `userId`, or a scope check that runs after the data is returned. A positive-path test passes on code with no authorisation at all.

Four patterns, four tests. **Route guards:** derive the route list from the router itself and assert every route returns 401 without a token, so a new route added without a guard fails the build. **Role and scope:** generate a role-by-action matrix from the permission table and assert each cell, so the matrix cannot drift. **Object-level ownership:** create two users' objects and assert user A gets a 404 -- not a 403 -- for user B's, because 403 confirms the record exists. **Tenant isolation:** identical object ids in two tenants, and assert cross-tenant reads fail; that is the only way to catch a missing filter.

I never mock the auth middleware in a test I am using to prove authorisation works. I sign a real token with the test secret, and I forbid `x-test-user` bypass headers, because that is a permanent backdoor.

---

### Q14. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What is a flaky test, and what is your process for eliminating them?

**Model answer.** A flaky test is one that passes and fails for the same code. Flake is not a nuisance; it is the largest destroyer of suite value. At 5% flake the effective gate is near zero, because developers learn to re-run and reviewers stop reading.

My process is ordered. First, classify by reproduction: run with `--repeat.each 200` for order dependence, vary workers for parallelism, run the suite twice for data dependence, and run under load for timing. Then, classify the cause into eleven buckets -- missing await, shared state, real timers, unseeded randomness, resource exhaustion, environment drift, external dependencies, leftover data, over-broad selectors, concurrency, and framework bugs.

Then I fix by class, and add the guard that prevents recurrence: `expect.assertions(n)` for missing awaits, `beforeEach` state for shared state, a `FakeClock` for timers, a seed for randomness, and `afterEach` guards for timers and unhandled rejections. Anything unresolved gets quarantined with `test.skip`, a linked ticket, a named owner and a due date, and CI fails if the quarantine list grows.

I never delete a flaky test silently, and I never set `retries` above one. A high-retry suite is decoration: the failure mode is that a real regression passes on the third attempt and nobody investigates.

---

### Q15. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — Why is Vitest faster than Jest, and what are the semantic differences in migrating?

**Model answer.** Four independent reasons. Vitest reuses Vite's module graph and esbuild-based transform instead of Babel or ts-jest compiling every file to CJS per worker, which is the single biggest cost in Jest. It keeps native ESM rather than paying interop on every mock boundary. It pools in threads with a warm server, so a watch-mode re-run only re-transforms what changed. And it offers a real browser environment, which removes the jsdom fidelity gap.

The practical number is the dev loop, not the suite: eight seconds down to 0.4 seconds for an edit-to-result cycle. That changes engineering behaviour -- people run tests after every edit instead of once before pushing.

Three semantic differences bite on migration. Fake timer defaults differ: Vitest fakes more APIs by default, so I narrow with `toFake`. `alias` applies to all imports including in dependencies, so a node_modules mock may also need `server.deps.inline`. And ESM mocks are per module graph, not per require call, so "mock only for this test" needs `vi.resetModules()` plus `vi.doMock` plus a dynamic `import`. I would stage the migration with a global alias in setup, run both runners side by side for a week comparing result sets, and never do it in the same PR as a feature change.

---

### Q16. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — How do you test code that depends on time, randomness and concurrency?

**Model answer.** I inject ports. `Clock` for time, `Random` for randomness with a seedable implementation, and a `Scheduler` for timers. That turns token expiry, retry backoff and scheduled jobs into instant, exact, order-deterministic tests with no fake timers, and it is the only approach that scales to *ordering* tests.

For concurrency specifically, I do not assert on one interleaving. I model the race as a state machine and assert the invariant that holds under all interleavings -- for example, that a balance never goes negative regardless of the order two debits arrive in. Where the race is genuinely about ordering, I inject a `Yield` or deferred helper so I can control the interleaving deterministically rather than hoping.

The design consequence is that every module needs the port threaded through. That is a cost paid once and refunded forever, and it is the right investment in any system with a time rule -- sessions, retries, billing, caches, jobs.

---

### Q17. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What belongs in an E2E test, and what does not?

**Model answer.** E2E verifies the parts no other tier can see: the actual bundle that ships, the service worker, the CSP and CORS configuration, the reverse proxy and TLS, real cookie flags, third-party scripts, browser storage, the auth redirect, and the fact that the pieces are wired together at all. It is a *sample* of journeys, not exhaustive coverage.

What it must not do is verify business rules. Discount arithmetic belongs in property-based unit tests where it runs five thousand times in forty milliseconds; in E2E you can check one example. It must not cover layout exhaustively -- that is component tests plus visual regression. And it must not be the only feedback loop: a developer waiting six minutes to learn their typo broke the build has been taught to batch work, and batching is how defects enter.

I hold a budget: ten to thirty E2E tests per major journey, under ten minutes wall clock, with a flake rate under half a percent. A healthy E2E suite is small, and *shrinking* it is a sign of a maturing test strategy.

---

### Q18. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — Explain Playwright's locator model and auto-waiting.

**Model answer.** A locator is a lazy, auto-retrying query. Nothing happens until you act or assert, and each action polls the locator until the element is actionable -- visible, stable, not animating, enabled, and receiving pointer events -- or the timeout expires. Assertions poll the same way, which is why `expect(locator).toBeVisible()` is a real wait and not a sleep.

The priority order is by accessibility: role plus accessible name first, then label, then placeholder, then text, and `getByTestId` only as a last resort with an explanatory comment. The reason is that role-and-name queries are the user's contract, and a component you cannot query by role is usually a component a screen-reader user cannot operate. So the suite doubles as an accessibility smoke test.

The two things that defeat auto-waiting: waiting for a state the app never expresses in the DOM -- a JavaScript condition unrelated to the render -- and `waitForTimeout`, which is always a defect. And the isolation primitive is the `BrowserContext`: an incognito-like profile with its own cookies, storage, permissions and locale, created in about fifty milliseconds versus a second for a new browser. That is what makes per-test isolation affordable, and it is why `fullyParallel` is feasible.

---

### Q19. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What is sharding, and what is the mistake people make with it?

**Model answer.** Sharding splits the test set into N groups that run in parallel, so wall clock falls roughly as 1/N. The mistake is assuming it balances. Playwright and Vitest both shard by *file count*, not by duration, so one spec file that takes six minutes makes one shard five times longer than the others and the whole exercise buys nothing.

The fix is to measure and rebalance: run once with a JSON reporter, group per-test durations by file, and bin-pack the files into N groups by total duration. Then split the slow spec into smaller files so future sharding behaves. I also always set `fail-fast: false` on a test matrix, because cancelling the sibling shards destroys exactly the traces you need.

And I only shard when there is enough duration to justify the coordination. A ninety-second suite sharded four ways is pure overhead.

---

### Q20. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — When do you use a Page Object, and when does it hide the test?

**Model answer.** The original intent was to keep selectors in one place. The more useful modern framing is *capability objects* over *page objects*: a capability models an intentional set of actions that may cross screens -- `checkout.addToBasket('Widget').openBasket().payWithCard(card).confirm()` -- so the test body reads as the user journey and every selector lives in exactly one file. Screen-scoped page objects multiply: a six-screen flow means six objects, and the test reads like a script again.

It hides the test when the object returns data and the test asserts everything -- the object becomes a getter bag. When it contains business logic, which then drifts from the unit test that owns it. When it contains retries or try/catch, which makes a broken app look like a passing test. And when it is a one-line wrapper, which is pure indirection.

My rule: extract when a flow is used by three or more tests, and keep a three-step straight-line test inline. Assertions about the *screen's* state belong with the screen's capability; assertions about persisted state belong in the test.

---

### Q21. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What is mutation testing, and why does it matter if you already have coverage?

**Model answer.** Mutation testing measures whether your tests can actually *detect bugs*. The tool mutates an operator in your code -- `>=` to `>`, `&&` to `||`, `return x` to `return null`, a boundary to a boundary-plus-one -- and reruns the tests. If a mutant is killed, your tests detect that class of change. If it survives, you have a gap that coverage cannot see, because coverage only knows a line ran.

That is precisely the point: coverage counts execution, mutation counts detection. A function at 100% coverage whose test asserts nothing has a mutation score near zero. Coverage is a cheap proxy; mutation score is the real quality signal, and it is what catches the "green but wrong" suite.

Practically I scope it to files with fast tests, because mutating a file whose only coverage is integration tests produces a wall of meaningless escaped mutants. I set per-mutator thresholds that weight the informative mutants -- conditionals, boundaries, nullish coalescing -- above the noisy ones, like property reads and object literals. On PRs I run it on changed files only, nightly I run it exhaustively with a rising baseline, and I publish surviving mutants in the PR as a work list.

---

### Q22. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — How do you structure a CI testing pipeline?

**Model answer.** Ordered by value per second. First, the fast gate: lint, typecheck, unit and property tests, and diff coverage -- target under three minutes, hard required, nobody waits for it. Second, integration with real services, which can run in parallel with the build because it does not depend on it. Third, build once and reuse the artefact everywhere downstream. Fourth, E2E smoke on a cached environment, then full E2E sharded against a preview environment built from the PR's artefact. Fifth, accessibility and performance, which for a front-end are first-class gates, not extras. Sixth, security scanning with a ratchet policy. Seventh, a nightly stage for everything expensive: all three browsers, full mutation, soak testing, DAST, provider smoke tests.

Two decisions carry most of the value. The merge gate is one aggregator job that reads actual job results, because branch protection cannot express "these three of five jobs are required, and E2E only on main and on user-facing changes" -- so E2E and a11y are advisory on a PR unless the change is labelled user-facing, and always required on main. And `fail-fast: false` on every test matrix, because cancelling a sibling shard destroys the trace that would have told you what went wrong.

---

### Q23. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What is the test pyramid, what is the test trophy, and which do you use?

**Model answer.** The pyramid is the empirical observation that fast isolated tests are cheap in every dimension while slow real-system tests are expensive, giving a shape of many units, fewer integrations, few E2E. The trophy is Kent Dodds' counter-proposal for front-ends: a thin base of static analysis and unit tests, a thick middle of component and integration tests, and very few E2E -- on the grounds that front-end risk lives in the wiring, not in the logic.

For a back-end service with real domain rules and data access I use the pyramid. For a React application I use the trophy, because the failures that actually happen -- a provider is missing, a query key is wrong, a form's error state is unreachable, a route guard is absent -- are exactly what a component test with real providers and MSW catches in four hundred milliseconds. For a library I would invert it: most tests exercise the public API realistically, not internals.

The honest framing is that both shapes are consequences of cost, not doctrines. The rule I apply to any behaviour is: put it in the cheapest tier that can genuinely detect its failure. The shape falls out of that.

---

### Q24. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What are the economics of a test suite, and how do you know which tests pay?

**Model answer.** The cost of a tier over a year is engineering hours to write and maintain, plus CI minutes times rerun rate times triage minutes per failure, plus the value of the bugs it catches. And the dominant term is usually *triage*, not runtime. In a typical front-end project, an E2E failure costs forty-five minutes of a human's attention against three minutes for a component failure -- which is why the argument is always for fewer E2E tests, not more.

A test earns its place when it maps to a stated behaviour, it fails when that behaviour breaks, it is deterministic and fast enough to run often, its maintenance cost is bounded, and no cheaper tier covers it. I verify the second condition with mutation testing rather than by assertion.

The practical consequence is a budget: sixty to seventy percent of effort in the two cheap tiers, twenty to thirty in integration, and under ten percent in E2E. If E2E is more than about fifteen percent of your test count, you are using the most expensive instrument for work that cheaper ones do better.

---

### Q25. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — How do you test error and failure paths?

**Model answer.** I keep a catalogue of every production incident I have had, and every entry becomes a test, because every incident is a test I did not write. The classes I need to be able to produce are: downstream 4xx and 5xx, downstream timeout, a real connection reset, a malformed response, a slow dependency, a database constraint violation or killed backend, resource exhaustion and rate limits, and partial success in a batch.

Each has a distinct assertion set. For a 5xx I assert the retry count, the backoff, and that the error kind is *retryable*. For a 4xx I assert no retry and that it is classified as a permanent rejection. For a timeout I assert a bounded elapsed time and that no partial state was written. For a reset I assert it is a transport error, not a 500. For a malformed body I assert 502 with a generic message and no `JSON.parse` internals leaked.

Two things I insist on. First, the negative space: for every failure, assert that the side effect did *not* happen. Second, observability: assert the log line exists with the request id, contains no PII and no secrets, and that the metric was incremented with a label that will not melt the metrics backend.

And I use the real thing where possible: MSW for protocol-level failures, and a local socket server that destroys the connection for genuine transport failures, because `route.abort` cannot produce a real reset.

---

### Q26. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What accessibility testing is actually required, and what does automation miss?

**Model answer.** Three layers, and only the first is automatable. Automated scanning -- axe-core in lint, component tests and E2E, plus Lighthouse and Pa11y -- catches the objective errors: missing labels, contrast, invalid ARIA, missing alt, heading order, duplicate ids. Manual expert testing -- keyboard only, a screen reader, zoom and reflow, forced colours, reduced motion, target size -- catches the rest. And user testing with disabled people catches whether the experience is actually usable.

I run axe in three places deliberately: lint for authoring-time errors, component tests on key *states* (initial, error, empty, modal open), and E2E on the assembled critical pages. Initial-state-only scanning misses most bugs, because modals, menus and error states are where a11y problems live.

Automation is necessary and not sufficient. It has a five to fifteen percent false-positive rate and a sixty to eighty percent false-negative rate. A clean axe report is not compliance, and it is certainly not accessibility. The legal frame matters too: the European Accessibility Act has applied since June 2025 to a broad range of consumer-facing digital products, and WCAG 2.2's new AA criteria -- focus not obscured, dragging movements, target size, accessible authentication -- catch things older tooling never flagged.

---

### Q27. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What security testing belongs in a pipeline, and what is its weakest link?

**Model answer.** Four techniques, each covering what the others miss. SAST analyses source and finds injection patterns, insecure crypto and dangerous sinks. DAST attacks a running app and finds runtime and configuration issues -- security headers, cookie flags, CORS, real injection through a real path. SCA analyses the lockfile for known CVEs. Secret scanning finds committed credentials, and it must scan history, not just the current tree.

The weakest link is policy, not tooling. A scanner that reports four hundred findings nobody fixes is worse than no scanner, because the first week of noise gets it muted forever. So the pattern is a baselined, ratcheting policy: triage once into a checked-in baseline with justifications, gate only on *new* high and critical findings, fail the build if the baseline contains entries that no longer match a finding, and track the count weekly. A rising count is a signal to act.

The second weakest link is authorisation logic, which SAST fundamentally cannot see. It is pattern matching; it cannot know that `findById` should also filter by `tenantId`. That is covered by the generated role-by-action matrix and the object-level tests, and by a threat model -- and I would say so plainly rather than implying a green security pipeline means the app is secure.

---

### Q28. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — What does static analysis buy you, and what are the highest-value rules?

**Model answer.** Three layers: linting, type checking, and program analysis for architectural constraints. The highest-value single setting in most JavaScript projects is TypeScript, because it verifies contracts across module boundaries that no test asserts. Within it, the flags that find the most real bugs are `strictNullChecks` and the rest of the strict family, `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` -- which makes `arr[0].id` a possible production `TypeError` -- and `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`, which stops a spread from smuggling `undefined` over a default.

The single highest-value ESLint rule in an async codebase is `@typescript-eslint/no-floating-promises`, because a bare `saveUser(x)` that a human reads as fine is how unhandled rejections happen.

Program analysis is the layer most teams skip, and it is the one that prevents rot: I forbid domain modules from depending on the framework, forbid application depending on infrastructure, forbid `test-support` being imported by production code, and fail on import cycles. Those four rules mean the first big refactor is possible.

On type-aware linting: it is three to ten times slower than the syntactic version, so I run fast rules in the editor and the full set in CI, and I expect to tune rules that produce false positives on generic-heavy code rather than muting them permanently.

---

### Q29. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — How do you make test data safe, and what is the GDPR position?

**Model answer.** Test data is personal data if it identifies a natural person, so the lawful basis question is real, and "it is only in a test environment" is not an answer. Copying production data into a test database is a processing activity that usually also breaches the DPA, and it enlarges the blast radius of any breach for no real benefit.

So the default is synthetic data, made *obviously* synthetic: reserved-TLD emails like `@example.test` under RFC 6761, reserved fictional phone ranges, reserved test card numbers, and display values like `Test User ABC123` that cannot be a real name. That last point is not just hygiene -- it means anyone reading a log, a screenshot, a trace or a support ticket can tell instantly that it is not a person, which removes a whole class of embarrassing incidents.

When I genuinely need production-*shaped* data for a performance or bug-reproduction scenario, I take a column allowlist, measure the shape I actually need, and either generate a synthetic population matching those statistics or mask with referential integrity preserved. And the controls are automated, because a written policy is a wish: a CI rule that fails on non-`example.test` emails and Luhn-valid card numbers in test files, a rule that no test file references a production host, and a nightly janitor that purges test-tagged rows and drops old preview databases.

---

### Q30. 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** — How do you test an LLM feature, given that the output is nondeterministic?

**Model answer.** I split the feature into a deterministic shell and a stochastic core. Everything in the shell is ordinary code and gets ordinary unit tests: prompt construction, context budgeting against the window, output parsing, word-budget enforcement, PII redaction, cost accounting. That is often more than half the logic and it is where most real bugs live.

The core I evaluate rather than assert on. I never assert on exact output strings -- that is guaranteed flake and it encourages an implementation overfit to one run. I assert on properties that hold across the distribution: every summary within budget, every citation index in range, no ungrounded claim, refusal rate above a threshold across N samples. And for quality I use a versioned evaluation set of a few hundred cases with per-category thresholds, scored by a rubric-based LLM judge that I have validated against a human-labelled gold set at eighty-five percent agreement or better.

Two rules make it work operationally: the model id, the prompt version and the eval-set version are a single pinned triple, so nothing is incomparable; and recorded cassettes make the contract tests free and deterministic. I would also say plainly that passing an injection test is evidence, not proof -- the architecture is the real defence, with a deterministic pre-filter, no tools, and output validation.

---

### Q31. ⚫ Expert · **Nice-to-have** — How do you test a system with two databases and a saga?

**Model answer.** I test the saga as a state machine and assert the *invariants* rather than the choreography, because the interleavings are what matter and there are too many to enumerate. In a transaction-per-test harness the multi-database part is genuinely hard, because the second database cannot join the transaction.

My approach is a per-step compensation test: for each step, force the next step to fail and assert that the earlier steps were compensated -- a refunded payment for a failed inventory reservation, a released reservation for a failed payment. Each is a separate test with a real database, using truncate-and-reseed rather than rollback, because a second connection is involved.

Then I test the *hard* cases explicitly: a duplicate message arriving after the saga completed, which must be idempotent; an out-of-order message, which must be rejected or deferred; and a step that fails permanently rather than transiently, which must trigger the compensating path rather than the retry path. I use a queue fake that can deliver messages in a scripted order so those tests are deterministic.

And I have one test for the case that will actually page someone: the saga is stuck in `pending` past its timeout with no worker running, and a janitor reclaims it. That path is almost never written, and it is the one that produces manual database surgery at three in the morning.

---

### Q32. ⚫ Expert · **Nice-to-have** — How do you test event-driven systems where delivery is at-least-once?

**Model answer.** At-least-once means the same event arrives more than once, so the property I test is idempotency: processing an event twice must produce the same state as processing it once. The test is a duplicate-delivery test, and it is the highest-value test in the system.

Concretely: publish an event, process it, capture the resulting state; then reset to the pre-event state, deliver the same event *twice* in a row, and assert the state is identical. That catches an entire class of bugs that only appear in production under retry.

Then out-of-order. Deliver `order.paid` before `order.created` and assert the system either rejects it, defers it, or handles it idempotently -- whichever is your design, but the design must be *written down* and tested. I also test unknown event types, which must be ignored without crashing the consumer, and schema evolution, where a v2 event arrives at a v1 consumer.

For the poison-message path, I assert the retry-exhausted behaviour: the message goes to the dead-letter queue after N attempts, with the attempt count and the last error recorded. And I assert the ack/nack contract, because a consumer that acks before committing loses data on a crash, and that is a real bug that only a test with a killed process will find.

---

### Q33. ⚫ Expert · **Nice-to-have** — What do you do when the test suite is too slow to be useful?

**Model answer.** I measure before I change anything, because the intuition about where time goes is usually wrong. I generate a per-test duration report, sort it, and look at the distribution: is it a few slow tests or everything uniformly slow. If everything is uniformly slow, the cost is per-test setup and I attack the harness -- a shared harness instead of per-file setup, cheaper environment selection so jsdom is not paid for by node tests, a faster transform, and narrower coverage instrumentation. If a few tests dominate, I attack those specifically and usually find a real `waitForTimeout` or an unbounded retry.

Then I move work down tiers, which is the biggest lever: anything an E2E test verifies that a component or integration test can verify moves down, and the payoff is ten to a hundred times per test. Then I reduce flake, which is usually the largest real cost because it is triage time rather than runtime.

Then I parallelise: `fullyParallel` with per-test isolation, shards with duration-based balancing, and separate stages that do not depend on each other running in parallel. And I set budgets -- a suite-duration budget in CI and an alert -- because without one the slow creep back is invisible until the pipeline takes an hour.

What I do *not* do is raise timeouts, because every timeout raised to make CI pass is a hidden decision about what your tests actually verify.

---

### Q34. ⚫ Expert · **Nice-to-have** — How do you test a design system component library?

**Model answer.** The shape is the trophy inverted for a library: most tests should exercise the components through their public API, and the visual and accessibility tiers carry real weight because the components *are* the product.

So each component gets: interaction tests through accessible queries, covering keyboard, focus, and state transitions; an accessibility test with axe plus explicit assertions on the roles and relationships the component promises -- a modal traps focus, restores it on close, and has an accessible name; a visual snapshot per state, per theme, per size, generated in a pinned container; and a token test that asserts spacing and colour come from the design tokens rather than hard-coded values, which is how design-system drift starts.

The one that teams skip and that I insist on: a **composition test** that renders the components together in a realistic form or a realistic data table, because components that pass alone and break in composition is the defining failure mode of a component library, and the interaction is usually a focus or a portal or a z-index problem.

And a migration test for the version upgrade path, because a design system that cannot be upgraded is a liability.

---

### Q35. ⚫ Expert · **Nice-to-have** — What is the difference between mocking, stubbing and faking at the HTTP boundary versus the method boundary, and why does it matter?

**Model answer.** The boundary is the whole point. Mocking at the method boundary -- `vi.mock('./paymentGateway')` or `jest.mock` -- replaces the adapter, so URL construction, headers, auth, payload serialisation, timeouts, retries and error mapping are all untested. Those are exactly the parts most likely to be wrong, and you have replaced them with a return value you chose.

Mocking at the network boundary -- MSW, WireMock, or a real local server -- keeps your adapter under test. You are asserting on the bytes that left the process, which is where the bugs actually are.

There is a middle case: stubbing the method but keeping the serialisation. Sometimes you want to test a caller that must handle a provider response, and you cannot produce that response without the provider. Then the highest-fidelity option is a *recorded* real response as a fixture, plus a schema that types the fixture, plus a nightly sandbox test that fails if the real shape drifts. And a Zod schema on the provider's response is the single highest-value line of code in that adapter, because it converts "silently treated an unknown status as success" into a compile-time and runtime failure.

---

### Q36. ⚫ Expert · **Nice-to-have** — How do you test a migration?

**Model answer.** Three separate things, and I test all three.

One: the migration applies cleanly from a *production-shaped* snapshot, not from empty. I take a schema-only dump plus a sample of representative data, apply the migrations, and assert the resulting schema matches expectations.
