A migration that only works on an empty database will fail in production, and it fails at the worst possible moment.

Two: the migration is *reversible*, or the irreversibility is deliberate and documented. I have a test that asserts each migration has a `down`, or is listed in an explicit `irreversible.json` with a reason. That file is a design document that gets reviewed.

Three: the application is compatible with both sides during the deploy window. This is the one teams miss entirely. I test the old code against the new schema and the new code against the old schema, because for the duration of a rolling deploy both combinations are live. A migration that renames a column will break the previous version of the app, and that is a real outage.

And in CI I never use `db push` or `synchronize`; the harness always runs the real migration chain, so the migration path is exercised on every single integration run.

---

### Q37. ⚫ Expert · **Nice-to-have** — How do you test caching?

**Model answer.** Caching has three independent behaviours and each needs its own test. First, does it hit: after a first request populates the cache, a second identical request must not reach the database. I assert that by counting queries or by spying on the data source, not by asserting on a response header, because the header is an implementation detail that lies.

Second, does it invalidate correctly. This is where the bugs are. I test every write path that should invalidate -- update, delete, and any bulk operation -- and assert the next read sees the new value. I also test the *selective* case: invalidating a user's cache must not evict a different user's, which is a real bug in tag-based invalidation.

Third, the failure modes: TTL expiry, an eviction storm under memory pressure, and a cache stampede, where N concurrent requests all miss and all hit the database. For the stampede I test that a single-flight or lock mechanism reduces concurrent origin requests to one, using a fake that counts simultaneous misses and asserts the count is bounded.

I also test the serialisation round trip explicitly, because a cached object that was serialised and deserialised must behave identically -- a `Date` that became a string, a class instance that became a plain object. That bug is invisible in development with an in-process cache and appears the moment you enable Redis.

---

### Q38. ⚫ Expert · **Nice-to-have** — How do you test a feature behind a feature flag?

**Model answer.** I treat the flag as a dimension, not a state, so I test the matrix. Every test that touches a flagged code path runs with the flag on and off, and asserts the *correct* behaviour in each, not merely that nothing crashed. Off should give the old behaviour; on should give the new; and there must be a defined state for "user has seen neither", because partial rollout means most users are in that state and it is rarely specified.

I test the three operational properties too. The default: what happens if the flag service is unreachable? The answer must be a defined, safe fallback, and I test it by making the flag client throw. The evaluation: I test that the flag is evaluated once per request rather than per render, because per-render evaluation is a performance bug and an inconsistency bug. And the kill switch: the test asserts that setting the flag to `false` restores the old path *without a deploy*, which is the whole reason the flag exists.

Structurally, I insist flags are read through one typed accessor, so `flag('new-checkout')` is the only way to read one. String literals scattered through the code are how flags become un-removable, and un-removable flags are the actual cost.

---

### Q39. ⚫ Expert · **Nice-to-have** — How do you test optimistic UI updates and rollback?

**Model answer.** Optimistic UI is where the interesting failures live, because there are four distinct states and a naive test only covers the first.

The four: the optimistic state immediately after the user acts, before any response; the confirmed state; the *rolled-back* state if the mutation fails; and the *reconciled* state if the server's response differs from what you assumed. I test all four, and the last two are the ones that are usually missing.

For rollback, I assert three things: the UI returned to the previous value, the user was *told* -- a status message, not a silent revert, because a value that silently changes back is a bug report waiting to happen -- and no partial state remains, so a list item is not left as a duplicate or an orphan.

For reconciliation, I have the server return a value different from the optimistic one -- a normalised name, a recomputed total, a server-assigned id -- and assert the UI ends up showing the *server's* value. That is a real class of bug and no happy-path test finds it.

The test mechanics: the mutation is slow enough to observe the optimistic state, which means a controllable deferred in the mock, not a sleep. And I use a fake timer or an explicit deferred so the intermediate state is asserted deterministically rather than raced.

---

### Q40. ⚫ Expert · **Nice-to-have** — What is your philosophy on deleting tests?

**Model answer.** Deleting a test is a claim: that the behaviour it verified is now verified elsewhere, or is no longer required. Both are legitimate, and both need evidence. So the default is not deletion, it is *relocation* -- moving a test down a tier when a cheaper tier can carry it -- and only deletion when the behaviour is genuinely gone.

Three things make a test a deletion candidate. It duplicates another test with no additional signal. It asserts an implementation detail that no longer exists, and the behaviour is covered by a behavioural test. Or it was added to chase a coverage number and mutation testing shows it kills no mutants.

Three rules I hold. You may not delete a test in the same PR that changes the behaviour, unless the replacement test is in that PR and named in the description. You may not delete a test to make a flaky suite green -- you quarantine it with a ticket and an owner. And every deletion must be justified by evidence: a mutation score comparison, or a pointer to the test that now covers the behaviour.

The metric I actually watch is *escape rate by tier*: bugs that reached production and which tier should have caught them. A tier that is quietly full of deleted tests shows up there first, which is a much better signal than test count.

---

# PHASE 9 RAPID-FIRE (60)

> One-line answers. Say the number, give the answer, move on.

1. 🟢 **`describe` vs `it`?** -- `describe` groups related tests and owns scoped hooks; `it` is one test with one behaviour. `test` is an alias for `it`.
2. 🟢 **`expect` alone does nothing?** -- Correct. It is lazy; nothing is verified until a matcher runs. Use `expect.assertions(n)` to prove you asserted.
3. 🟢 **`toBe` vs `toEqual`?** -- `toBe` is `Object.is` (identity, `NaN` equal, `-0` not); `toEqual` is deep structural and ignores `undefined` properties.
4. 🟢 **`toStrictEqual` adds what?** -- Class/`prototype` checks and `undefined`-sparseness checks that `toEqual` skips.
5. 🟢 **Jest hook order?** -- outer `beforeAll` -> inner `beforeAll` -> outer `beforeEach` -> inner `beforeEach` -> test -> inner `afterEach` -> outer `afterEach` -> inner `afterAll` -> outer `afterAll`.
6. 🟢 **Does `beforeAll` run per test?** -- No. Once per worker per file.
7. 🟡 **`it.only` risk?** -- Silently skips every other test in the run. Block it in ESLint and set `forbidOnly: true` in CI.
8. 🟡 **Best negative assertion?** -- `expect(spy).not.toHaveBeenCalled()` and friends -- asserting that something *did not* happen is the highest-value class in unit tests.
9. 🟡 **Why `userEvent` over `fireEvent`?** -- It dispatches the full event sequence (pointer, focus, key, input) and respects `disabled`/`pointer-events`, so it reproduces real failures.
10. 🟡 **Why `findBy*` over `getBy*` for async?** -- `findBy*` retries; `getBy*` throws immediately. Also use `waitFor`, not `waitForTimeout`.
11. 🟡 **`expect.hasAssertions()`?** -- Fails the test if zero assertions ran, catching a silently short-circuited path in a heavily-mocked test.
12. 🔴 **`clearMocks` vs `resetMocks` vs `restoreMocks`?** -- clear resets calls only; reset also wipes implementations (breaks `jest.fn(impl)`); restore restores spied originals.
13. 🔴 **Why did `resetMocks: true` break a suite?** -- It removes factory implementations, so configured mocks return `undefined` and failures surface far from the cause.
14. 🟢 **Vite: what is `vi`?** -- Vitest's API object: `vi.fn`, `vi.spyOn`, `vi.mock`, `vi.useFakeTimers`, `vi.stubEnv`, `vi.advanceTimersByTime`.
15. 🟢 **Why is Vitest faster?** -- Vite's esbuild transform and native ESM graph instead of Babel/ts-jest compiling to CJS, plus a warm server for watch mode.
16. 🟡 **Vitest's `vi.hoisted()`?** -- Creates a value initialised before hoisted `vi.mock` factories run, solving "mock out of variable scope".
17. 🔴 **Vitest vs Jest fake timer difference?** -- Vitest fakes more APIs by default (`performance`, `queueMicrotask`, `nextTick`); narrow with `toFake`.
18. 🟡 **`clearMocks: true` in Vitest?** -- `vi.clearAllMocks()` in config clears call history before every test.
19. 🔴 **Why does a test hang only in Vitest?** -- Fake timers faked the microtask pump, or a dependency is not inlined so its ESM interop differs. Use `advanceTimersByTimeAsync` and check `server.deps.inline`.
20. 🟡 **AAA expand?** -- Arrange, Act, Assert. One Act per test; the Act is the smallest possible call.
21. 🟡 **Test factory vs builder?** -- Factory: one call with an overridable object. Builder: fluent chaining when many fields vary across tests.
22. 🔴 **When does a factory become harmful?** -- When it can build states the real system forbids, becoming a second, wrong domain model. Derive its types from production types.
23. 🟢 **Property-based library?** -- fast-check. `fc.assert(fc.property(...))`; prints a shrunk counterexample plus a reproducible seed.
24. 🟢 **Round-trip property?** -- `decode(encode(x)) === x`. The first property to reach for on codecs, serialisers and URL builders.
25. 🟡 **Conservation property?** -- `sum(parts) === whole`. Essential for payment splitting, where a lost cent is a real defect.
26. 🔴 **Property tests flaky in CI?** -- Almost never flaky; they find real bugs. Fix the seed's counterexample as a deterministic regression test.
27. 🟢 **TDD cycle?** -- Red, Green, Refactor. Confirm red fails for the *expected* reason; green in the simplest way; refactor only while green.
28. 🟡 **Why hardcode in the green step?** -- To prove the harness and assertion work, isolating a test failure from an implementation failure.
29. 🔴 **Where does TDD not pay?** -- Exploratory spikes, UI layout work, throwaway prototypes, fixed-deadline throwaway code, and legacy code needing characterisation tests.
30. 🟢 **Coverage metrics?** -- Statement/line, branch, function. Istanbul is AST-based and precise; V8 is fast and coarser.
31. 🔴 **Biggest coverage trap?** -- The 100% gate, achieved with `toBeDefined()` assertions. It optimises the number, not the defect rate.
32. 🟡 **Diff coverage?** -- Gates that *changed* lines are covered, comparing against the base branch. Far better than an absolute global threshold.
33. 🟡 **Snapshot vs explicit assertions?** -- Snapshots detect change; explicit assertions detect correctness. Use both.
34. 🔴 **Never do this with snapshots?** -- `-u` in CI; snapshotting component DOM; leaving obsolete entries to rot.
35. 🟡 **Serialisers fix what?** -- Nondeterministic values: dates, uuids, ids, hashes, `Error`s, object key order, `Set`/`Map` iteration.
36. 🟢 **`advanceTimersByTime` vs `...Async`?** -- Sync fires callbacks only; async also drains the microtask queue. Async is what async code needs.
37. 🔴 **Four ways async tests lie about time?** -- Not awaiting; real time; sync timer advance; leaked timers/handles.
38. 🟡 **Injected `Clock` vs fake timers?** -- Injected clock for your own code always; fake timers only for third-party code you cannot change.
39. 🟢 **RTL query priority?** -- role (+name), label, placeholder, text, alt, testid. Testid is a last resort.
40. 🟡 **Why role queries?** -- They assert the user's contract, so a component you cannot query by role is usually one a screen reader user cannot use.
41. 🔴 **What never to mock in a component test?** -- Your own hooks, children, and validators. If a hook needs mocking to be testable, extract the logic to a pure function.
42. 🟡 **Component test done right?** -- Extract a pure view-model function and table-test it; keep 3-5 assertions on roles, names and visible text.
43. 🟢 **What is `renderHook`?** -- RTL's hook harness; assert on `result.current`, wrap your own mutations in `act`, use `waitFor` for async.
44. 🟡 **The common hook-test bug?** -- Asserting `result.current` right after an effect-driven update, before React commits. Fix with `act` or `waitFor`.
45. 🔴 **Must-test hook behaviour?** -- Cleanup/unsubscribe on unmount, abort signals on dependency change, and idempotency under StrictMode.
46. 🟢 **Unit vs integration boundary?** -- Does it run with zero network, disk and database? If not, at least integration.
47. 🟢 **Supertest?** -- Starts your app over a real HTTP socket so the router, middleware, body parser and cookie handling are all under test.
48. 🟡 **Why not call the handler directly?** -- You skip the HTTP layer, which is where content negotiation, header casing, CORS and cookie bugs live.
49. 🔴 **Never put in a test app?** -- A `x-test-user` bypass header. It is a permanent backdoor in production. Sign real tokens instead.
50. 🟡 **Transaction-per-test speed?** -- A `ROLLBACK` round trip instead of a `TRUNCATE`: 10-100x faster, real transaction semantics, parallel-safe.
51. 🔴 **Transaction-per-test cannot test?** -- Second connections/processes, background workers, self-HTTP, two concurrent writers, advisory locks, commit visibility.
52. 🟢 **Testcontainers?** -- Starts real dependencies in Docker for the run, waits for actual readiness, tears them down. Put it in `globalSetup`.
53. 🔴 **Container readiness?** -- Not "port open". Postgres logs "ready to accept connections" *twice*; use the service's own signal or a health endpoint.
54. 🟡 **MSW's key setting?** -- `onUnhandledRequest: 'error'` in tests (`'bypass'` in dev), so a typo'd handler URL fails loudly.
55. 🔴 **Biggest MSW flake?** -- Forgetting `server.resetHandlers()` in `afterEach`, so a per-test override leaks into the next test.
56. 🔴 **Pact matcher choice?** -- `equality` for fields the consumer branches on, `regex` for shapes, `type` for values it ignores, `nullOrType` for optional.
57. 🔴 **Pact's hardest part?** -- Provider state setup, which is hand-written test-only code that rots. Use in-process handlers where you can.
58. 🟢 **k6's key metrics?** -- `http_req_duration` (p95/p99), `http_req_failed`, plus your own business `Trend`/`Rate`/`Counter`.
59. 🔴 **k6 thresholds?** -- SLOs as hard gates, so the run is a test not a dashboard. Plus generator health (`vus`, `http_req_blocked`) so the numbers mean anything.
60. 🟢 **E2E rule of thumb?** -- 10-30 tests per major journey, under 10 minutes wall clock, under 0.5% flake. Fewer is a sign of maturity.

---

# PHASE 9 FINAL READINESS CHECKLIST

Use this as an honest self-audit. If you cannot tick a box, you know exactly what to build next.

## 9.1 Unit testing

- [ ] I can define "unit" behaviourally -- one observable rule -- rather than by file or class.
- [ ] My unit tests run with zero network, zero disk and zero database, in seconds.
- [ ] I use AAA with exactly one Act per test, and no arrange helper ever contains the Act.
- [ ] Factories and builders are deterministic: fixed ids, injected clock, no `Math.random()`.
- [ ] Factories derive their types from production types and live only in test code.
- [ ] I use state verification (assert the output) in preference to behaviour verification (assert the calls).
- [ ] When I do assert on calls, it is because the interaction is genuinely the contract -- an emitted event, a published message.
- [ ] Fakes are contract-tested against the real implementation at least once.
- [ ] I can name a property-based test I have written (round-trip, conservation, monotonicity, commutativity).
- [ ] I fix the seed on property tests in CI and promote any counterexample to a deterministic regression test.
- [ ] I can state where TDD paid off in a real project and where it did not.
- [ ] I know that coverage measures execution, not detection, and I gate on diff coverage rather than a global number.
- [ ] My suite uses `expect.assertions(n)` or `expect.hasAssertions()` in tests that mock heavily.
- [ ] Snapshots are serialiser-normalised, small, inline where possible, never auto-updated in CI, and paired with explicit invariant assertions.
- [ ] CI fails on obsolete snapshots.
- [ ] I never use `advanceTimersByTime` (sync) with promise-returning code.
- [ ] I inject a `Clock` into my own code rather than faking timers.
- [ ] My setup file turns unhandled rejections into failures and detects leaked timers.
- [ ] Component tests query by role and accessible name, never by class or `data-testid` (except a justified last resort).
- [ ] I extract pure view-model or reducer functions out of components and hooks rather than mocking my own logic.
- [ ] I test hooks' cleanup, abort and idempotency behaviour explicitly.

## 9.2 Integration testing

- [ ] My integration tests cross a real boundary (real database, real HTTP socket), not just a method call.
- [ ] The database is the same engine and major version as production, and I never use `db push`/`synchronize`.
- [ ] Migrations are applied for real in the test harness.
- [ ] Tests are isolated: transaction-per-test with rollback, or truncate-and-reseed where a second connection is involved.
- [ ] Integration tests can run in parallel against one database without interfering.
- [ ] I assert on the system of record (database rows, published events, cache entries), not only on the HTTP response.
- [ ] Every 4xx and 5xx path asserts the error *envelope*, not just the status code.
- [ ] I have a generated "every route requires auth" test.
- [ ] I have a generated role-by-action authorisation matrix.
- [ ] I have object-level tests asserting 404 (not 403) for another user's record.
- [ ] I have a tenant-isolation test with colliding ids.
- [ ] Idempotency is enforced by a database constraint, not a read-then-write.
- [ ] I have a cursor or offset pagination test that walks every page and asserts no row is dropped or duplicated.
- [ ] I never mock the auth middleware in a test that proves authorisation, and there is no bypass header.
- [ ] Containers start in `globalSetup`, use service-native readiness signals, and have a documented no-Docker fallback.
- [ ] Container images are pinned, and a scheduled job checks the newest minor version.
- [ ] I set `SET LOCAL statement_timeout` and `lock_timeout` inside test transactions.
- [ ] MSW is used at the network boundary with `onUnhandledRequest: 'error'` and `resetHandlers()` in `afterEach`.
- [ ] Third-party handler responses are recorded from a real sandbox, not invented.
- [ ] Unknown provider response shapes are treated as failures, not as success.
- [ ] I have a contract test where the pattern is valuable: pacts for cross-service APIs, schema validation for vendor responses.
- [ ] I have a failure-path suite covering 5xx, timeout, real connection reset, malformed body, rate limit and partial success.
- [ ] Every failure test asserts that no partial state was persisted.
- [ ] I test retry, circuit breaking, idempotency across retries, and observability (log, metric, no PII).
- [ ] There is a catalogue mapping past production incidents to tests.

## 9.3 End-to-end testing

- [ ] I can state what E2E is uniquely for, and what it must not be used for.
- [ ] My E2E suite is deliberately small (10-30 per major journey) and runs in under 10 minutes.
- [ ] The suite covers the money path, the auth path, and the top conversions -- not every screen.
- [ ] Setup happens through a test API, not through the UI.
- [ ] Every test creates namespaced data and purges it in a `finally`, so `fullyParallel` is safe.
- [ ] Assertions are on user-visible outcomes: roles, names, text, URLs -- never class names or internal state.
- [ ] There are no `waitForTimeout` calls in my E2E suite.
- [ ] Every action is preceded by an explicit state assertion, and followed by a user-visible assertion.
- [ ] Negative paths are covered: declined card, expired session, unavailable dependency, provider timeout.
- [ ] `trace: 'retain-on-failure'` is enabled, and traces are uploaded as CI artefacts with a retention policy.
- [ ] Tests are retried at most once, and a flake budget fails the build.
- [ ] Flaky tests are quarantined with a ticket, an owner and a due date -- never deleted, never blanket-retried.
- [ ] CI fails if the quarantine list grows.
- [ ] E2E runs against a production-like environment, not a localhost stack with a mocked API.
- [ ] I never run E2E against production, and load-test traffic cannot trigger real emails, charges or webhooks.
- [ ] The test-support API is gated, authenticated, and absent in production.
- [ ] Visual regression is scoped to a small set of critical states, with frozen time and randomness, masked dynamic regions, and pinned rendering environments.
- [ ] I have degraded-network journeys for slow, failed and offline, including the reconnect path.
- [ ] I test all three engines on `main` at least, and I know WebKit is not Safari.
- [ ] Sharding is balanced by measured duration, not just file count, and `fail-fast: false` is set.
- [ ] E2E is advisory on PRs unless the change is user-facing, and always required on `main`.
- [ ] The CI pipeline has a duration budget and a per-stage duration report.

## 9.4 Quality beyond tests

- [ ] I can name the four security testing techniques and what each finds, and I run all four.
- [ ] Security findings go through a baselined, ratcheting policy rather than a raw report.
- [ ] CI fails on new high/critical findings and on stale baseline entries.
- [ ] Secret scanning runs pre-commit and over full history, and I have a documented rotation procedure.
- [ ] I have a test asserting the role-by-action matrix and one asserting security headers and cookie flags.
- [ ] I know SAST cannot see authorisation logic, and I cover that gap with generated tests and a threat model.
- [ ] `strict` TypeScript is on, including `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` and `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`.
- [ ] `@typescript-eslint/no-floating-promises` is an error, not a warning.
- [ ] Typecheck runs as a separate step, not inside the test transform.
- [ ] Dependency-boundary rules are enforced: no cycles, domain does not depend on the framework, `test-support` never ships.
- [ ] Code review enforces: under 400 lines, one concern per PR, refactor separate from behaviour change.
- [ ] Reviews classify comments as blocking, suggestion, or question, and "why" is required with every "what".
- [ ] Approval is explicit about what was checked.
- [ ] Nobody merges their own PR, and no rubber-stamping.
- [ ] I track escape rate by tier: which bugs got past which tier.
- [ ] I have a test plan template mapping behaviours to tiers, written into PR descriptions.
- [ ] I build the seams (`Clock`, `Random`, ports) as part of the feature, before the code.
- [ ] Test data is obviously synthetic, enforced by a CI check, not a written policy.
- [ ] There is a retention and janitor job for test data, preview databases and artefacts.
- [ ] k6 runs a nightly regression with SLO thresholds as gates and correctness counters that must be zero.
- [ ] I have a short CI performance profile and a full nightly profile.
- [ ] Load-test results are correlated with real-user (RUM) data, and the RUM data wins when they disagree.
- [ ] Accessibility is tested in three tiers: lint, component states, and E2E on assembled pages.
- [ ] I do keyboard-only and screen-reader passes on critical journeys every release.
- [ ] I have done user testing with disabled people, at least annually and on any redesign of a critical flow.
- [ ] I can state that a clean axe report is necessary and not sufficient, and name the legal frame.
- [ ] Mutation testing runs on changed files per PR and exhaustively nightly, with a rising baseline.
- [ ] Surviving mutants appear in the PR as a work list.
- [ ] I can justify the shape of my suite with a cost model, and I know that triage time dominates.
- [ ] E2E is under roughly 15% of my test count.
- [ ] For AI features: the deterministic shell has full unit tests, the eval set is versioned with per-category thresholds, the judge is validated against humans, and the model and prompt versions are pinned.

---

# PHASE 9 COMPLETE

**You have reached the end of Phase 9.** Here is what you should now be able to do without hesitation.

**Define.** You can say precisely what a unit is -- one observable rule -- and where the substitution boundary sits, using the "zero network, zero disk, zero database" test and the "would it survive a rewrite?" test. You can name the five kinds of test double and explain state versus behaviour verification.

**Build.** You can write a unit test that is fast, deterministic and refactor-tolerant; a property-based test that shrinks a counterexample to a minimal input with a reproducible seed; a component test driven entirely by `user-event` and accessible queries, with MSW so your real adapter runs; a Supertest suite against a real database with transaction-per-test isolation and assertions on the system of record; a Testcontainers harness in `globalSetup` with service-native readiness; a Playwright suite with namespaced data, capability objects, traces, and a quarantined-flake registry; a k6 script whose thresholds are SLOs and whose correctness counters must be zero; a Pact contract whose matchers are chosen by what the consumer actually reads; and a mutation-testing configuration that tells you which of your tests are real.

**Diagnose.** You can classify a failure into one of eleven flake classes and get to a root cause with a procedure rather than a guess. You know that a hang with no error is fake timers or an unreachable state; that a CI-only database failure is the connection pool; that "multiple elements" is responsive DOM duplication plus jsdom not applying your CSS; that a green suite with a live bug is a surviving mutant; and that a 45-minute pipeline is usually uncached browsers, a per-push environment, and 60% too many E2E tests.

**Judge.** You can defend a suite's economics with numbers: authoring hours, CI minutes, and the triage minutes that dominate; the ten-to-thirty E2E tests and ten-minute budget that keep the suite runnable; the rule that a test earns its place by failing when the behaviour breaks, verified with mutation testing rather than coverage.

**Connect it forward.** Testing is not a phase. It is the feedback instrument that makes every other engineering decision -- the schema, the API contract, the queue, the flag, the prompt -- safe to make and safe to change. Phase 9 is the instrument; every subsequent phase is something you will want to be able to change with confidence.

**The one sentence to carry.** Push verification as far *down* the pyramid as it will honestly go, and use the slow, brittle tiers only for the things that genuinely cannot be proven any other way.

**What to read next.** Stryker's mutation testing documentation, because it will change what you consider a real test. The W3C WebDriver and WebDriver Bi-D specifications, if you touch Appium or real Safari. The Pact specification's matcher and generator semantics. k6's `thresholds` and `scenarios` executors, particularly `ramping-vus` and `constant-arrival-rate` for accurate open-model load. And your own production incident history, which is the highest-value testing document you own and the one nobody has written yet.


---

# PHASE 9: Testing Strategies

### Unit Testing ✅
- ✅ Jest, Assertion libraries, Mocking, Test coverage, Pure functions, Component testing, Hook testing
- ➕ Vitest, React Testing Library philosophy, Test doubles (mock, stub, spy, fake), AAA pattern, TDD red-green-refactor, Snapshot testing trade-offs, Testing async code and timers

### Integration Testing ✅
- ✅ API testing, Database seeding, Supertest, Endpoint validation, Service mocking, Network stubs, Error simulation
- ➕ MSW, Testcontainers, Contract testing (Pact), Test data factories, Testing auth flows

### End to End Testing ✅
- ✅ Cypress, Playwright, Selenium, Visual regression, User flows, Test environments, CI integration
- ➕ Flaky test causes and fixes, Page object model, Test pyramid vs trophy, Parallelization

### 🆕 Quality Beyond Tests (🟡)
- Performance and load testing (k6, JMeter), Accessibility testing, Security testing, Mutation testing awareness, Code review practices, Static analysis, Test strategy for a new feature
